# Internal Medicine Finals — The 20/80 Black-Ops Core Doc
### Australian final-year level · Passmedicine-style single-best-answer · <2-week all-or-nothing runway

> _Built by a fleet of independent max-effort agents. Every tutorial, every MCQ, every schema and distractor was generated, then adversarially audited by a separate agent against 2026 Australian guidelines (eTG, RACGP, Heart Foundation/CSANZ, Kidney Health Australia, Diabetes Australia, Lung Foundation, ASID). Audit verdicts are printed inline — trust but verify._

## The gamble, stated plainly

You have under two weeks to swallow the entirety of internal medicine. Be honest about the size of the thing: cardiology, respiratory, endocrine, gastro-hepatology, infectious disease, renal, neurology, tox, haematology, rheumatology, geriatrics and preventive care — each a textbook, each examinable, none of it optional in principle. You cannot read it all. Anyone who tells you otherwise is selling comfort. So we are not going to try.

This is a deliberate 20/80 raid. We bet that roughly a fifth of the content carries four-fifths of the marks, and we hunt that fifth ruthlessly. The wager is real and it is all-or-nothing: we spend our finite hours on the high-prevalence chronic-disease cores and the guaranteed Australian-flavour items, and we knowingly leave zebras on the table. If the paper breaks against us — a freak run of esoterica — this strategy underperforms a slow, complete read we never had time for anyway. That is the price of admission. Own it now so it does not surprise you in the hall.

The downside is specific and it is fixable. Finals are not graded on expected value alone. Australian examiners seed kill-questions — envenomation, melioidosis, acute rheumatic fever, Indigenous health, the must-not-miss emergencies — precisely where a pure high-yield bet drops them. So the 80/20 applies to DEPTH on common chronic disease ONLY. The must-not-miss tail and the Australia-specific overlay are ring-fenced outside the budget. We do not gamble the floor. A fail you earn on the 80% you knew is bad luck; a fail on the 20% you deliberately binned is self-inflicted, and we refuse it.

The method is engine-first. Do not memorise sub-topics as a flat list — you will run out of memory before you run out of syllabus. Own the one unifying core engine per domain and derive the rest live. Master the anion-gap and acid-base framework and every electrolyte and tox question becomes arithmetic. Own oxygen delivery and the obstructive-versus-restrictive split and respiratory falls out. Own the insulin-counterregulatory axis and diabetes, DKA, HHS and hypoglycaemia are one story. Own the sepsis recognition-plus-empirical-eTG reflex and half of ID is reflex. Internalise A-to-E and every emergency has a spine. Twenty engines, well-owned, generate a thousand answers. That is the leverage.

Discipline is the weapon. Every day ends in applied single-best-answer MCQs against the weighted set, anchored to Australian guidelines — eTG for drug and dose, RACGP, Heart Foundation, COPD-X, Kidney Health Australia. We learn first-line management and the named killer per domain, not mechanism for its own sake. The last three days are active recall over what we already hold, not new intake — retrieval, not reading, is what survives the exam. Front-load the heavy domains while you are fresh, protect acid-base, electrolytes and sepsis as cross-cutting multipliers, and guard the ring-fenced floor with your life. Cut depth before breadth, breadth before the floor, and never the floor. Execute.

## The blueprint — the gold-standard scope this gamble is measured against

Australian final-year IM finals (level just below RACP basic training) reward safe, common, "must-not-miss" management over zebra-spotting. The single-best-answer style mirrors Passmedicine but is anchored to Australian guidelines: eTG/Therapeutic Guidelines for drug choice and dose, RACGP for chronic disease and preventive care, Heart Foundation/CSANZ for cardiology, Kidney Health Australia for CKD, Diabetes Australia/ADS for diabetes, Lung Foundation/COPD-X for respiratory, and ASID/AMS for antimicrobials. Marks concentrate heavily in the high-prevalence chronic-disease and acute-presentation cores — cardiology, respiratory, endocrine (especially diabetes), GI/hepatology, infectious disease, renal and neurology together carry the clear majority of the paper. The Australian "flavour" is examined disproportionately relative to its true clinical frequency: expect guaranteed items on Indigenous health, acute rheumatic fever/RHD, melioidosis and other tropical/zoonotic infections (Ross River, Q fever, leptospirosis), snake/spider envenomation, and antimicrobial stewardship — these are low-volume but near-certain to appear, so they are high-yield per minute of study. The dominant question stems test: first-line drug and correct dose per eTG, recognition of the emergency requiring immediate action (anaphylaxis, sepsis/septic shock, ACS, DKA, hyperkalaemia, status epilepticus, massive PE, tension pneumothorax), single best initial investigation, and interpretation of a common dataset (ECG, ABG, CXR pattern, U&E/anion gap, iron studies, TFTs, CSF). Pure factual recall of rare syndromes is rare; applied first-line management and risk stratification dominate. The all-or-nothing scoping bet should overweight the chronic-disease cores plus the guaranteed Australian-specific items, and not over-invest in haematology malignancy, rheumatology esoterica, or dermatology beyond emergencies.

| Domain | ~Weight | Why it carries marks |
|---|---|---|
| Cardiology | 15% | Highest single-domain yield. ACS, heart failure, arrhythmias and hypertension are the most common acute and chronic presentations and map directly to Heart Foundation/CSANZ and eTG protocols. ECG interpretation is embedded across the whole paper, multiplying effective weight. |
| Respiratory | 13% | COPD, asthma, pneumonia, PE and pleural disease are high-frequency. COPD-X and Lung Foundation plus eTG antibiotic choices are heavily tested. ABG/CXR interpretation recurs throughout the paper. |
| Endocrinology & Metabolic (incl. Diabetes) | 12% | Diabetes alone is a guaranteed multi-question block (Diabetes Australia/ADS), spanning emergencies and chronic complications. Thyroid, adrenal, sodium and calcium disorders are reliably examined data-interpretation items. |
| Gastroenterology & Hepatology | 11% | GI bleeding, liver disease, IBD and pancreatitis are common acute and chronic presentations with clear eTG/management algorithms and frequent LFT/iron-study data interpretation. |
| Infectious Diseases & Antimicrobial Stewardship | 11% | ASID/AMS and eTG antibiotic selection are examined across the whole paper, and the Australian-specific tropical/zoonotic infections are near-guaranteed high-yield-per-minute items. Sepsis recognition is a critical must-not-miss. |
| Nephrology / Renal & Acid-Base | 9% | AKI, CKD and electrolyte/acid-base disturbances are core safe-prescribing and data-interpretation territory (Kidney Health Australia). Hyperkalaemia is a recurring emergency. |
| Neurology | 9% | Stroke/TIA, seizures, headache red-flags and acute neuromuscular emergencies are high-frequency must-not-miss items with time-critical management pathways. |
| Toxicology, Envenomation & Emergencies | 6% | Australian envenomation is a guaranteed exam signature, and core poisonings plus anaphylaxis are high-stakes must-not-miss items with protocolised answers. |
| Haematology | 5% | Anaemia workup, VTE/anticoagulation and acute haematological emergencies are examined, but malignancy detail is lighter at finals level. Interpretation of FBE/film and coagulation profiles recurs. |
| Rheumatology & Immunology | 4% | Acute hot joint, vasculitis emergencies and common connective tissue disease are examined as must-not-miss recognition; fine subspecialty detail is low yield at this level. |
| Geriatrics, Palliative & General/Preventive Medicine | 5% | RACGP-aligned preventive care, delirium/falls, polypharmacy, palliative symptom control and Indigenous health frameworks are reliably tested as safe, holistic-care items. |

**Independent blueprint audit:** Broadly sound and well-calibrated to Australian final-year IM finals — the chronic-disease core weighting and the deliberate over-investment in guaranteed Australian-flavour items (RHD/ARF, melioidosis, envenomation, AMS) is the correct strategic bet. But the blueprint has several concrete safety gaps that would get a student killed in viva/SBA terms, plus some misallocation. The single most dangerous omission is the near-total absence of obstetric/pregnancy physiology in medicine (pre-eclampsia, the pregnant patient with PE/sepsis/DKA, safe prescribing in pregnancy) and of drug-specific monitoring/toxicity content as a cross-cutting theme. Within the listed domains, a few must-not-miss items are buried or absent (long QT/torsades and QT-prolonging drugs, serotonin syndrome upgraded, hypertensive emergency vs urgency, refeeding syndrome, alcohol withdrawal/Wernicke, transfusion-associated TACO vs TRALI, anaphylaxis given too low a billing). The Australian-specific scope is mostly right but UNDER-weights some genuinely high-frequency Indigenous and regional must-knows. Net: keep the architecture, fix the gaps below before betting all-or-nothing on it.

_Re-weightings flagged:_
- Toxicology, Envenomation & Emergencies (6% -> 8%): This domain is asked to carry anaphylaxis (the single most time-critical must-not-miss in all of medicine), the full common-overdose set (paracetamol, opioid, TCA, beta-blocker/CCB, salicylate, lithium, digoxin — note digoxin and lithium toxicity are missing entirely), serotonin syndrome/NMS, alcohol withdrawal/Wernicke, AND the guaranteed envenomation block. At 6% it is over-scoped relative to its weight. Anaphylaxis and paracetamol nomogram alone are near-guaranteed; bundling them with all the Australian envenomation signatures under 6% means a student rationally under-rehearses one of them. Raise to 8% and pull the 2% from rheumatology/derm esoterica and over-deep haematology malignancy.
- Cardiology (15% -> 14% (content, not just number)): Weight is fine but the topic list omits two recurring must-not-miss SBA traps: (1) long QT / torsades and the QT-prolonging drug interaction question (a classic single-best-answer killer — patient on citalopram + ondansetron + macrolide), and (2) aortic dissection (a guaranteed 'tearing chest pain, do NOT thrombolyse' discriminator vs ACS). Reallocate within-domain content toward these rather than adding more HF pharmacology nuance. Also hypertensive EMERGENCY (encephalopathy/end-organ) vs urgency management is a safe must-not-miss currently absent.
- Endocrinology & Metabolic (12% -> 12% (add refeeding + pituitary apoplexy)): Weight is right but the list misses refeeding syndrome (high-stakes, commonly examined safe-prescribing item in the malnourished/alcoholic/anorexic patient) and pituitary apoplexy/acute panhypopituitarism. Hyperkalaemia and hyponatraemia are double-counted here and in renal — that's acceptable but ensure the SIADH-vs-hypoadrenal-vs-hypothyroid hyponatraemia workup and the dangers of over-rapid sodium correction (osmotic demyelination) are explicit, as that is the exam's favourite trap.
- Haematology (5% -> 4%): Correctly de-emphasised, but even at 5% it slightly over-invests given finals-level malignancy detail is low yield. The genuinely high-yield items (anaemia/MCV/iron studies, VTE/anticoagulation reversal, febrile neutropenia, TTP, transfusion reactions) fit comfortably in 4%. Crucially: transfusion section must explicitly distinguish TACO vs TRALI and cover massive transfusion — currently 'transfusion reactions' is too vague for what is a recurring safe-practice question. Free the 1% for toxicology.
- Rheumatology & Immunology (4% -> 3%): The must-not-miss trio (septic joint, GCA/blindness, gout) is correctly prioritised, but at 4% there is residual room for the 'esoterica' the philosophy itself warns against (antibody-pattern minutiae, vasculitis subtyping). Trim to 3%; the recognition-level items survive easily. Redirect to toxicology/emergencies where the marginal mark is safer.

_Flagged as missing:_ PREGNANCY / OBSTETRIC MEDICINE as a cross-cutting must-not-miss — the single biggest scope hole. Pre-eclampsia/eclampsia and HELLP recognition and magnesium management, the approach to PE/VTE in pregnancy (D-dimer unreliable, imaging choice), sepsis and DKA in pregnancy, hyperemesis/Wernicke, and safe-vs-teratogenic prescribing (ACEi, warfarin, sodium valproate, methotrexate, statins). This is a guaranteed safe-prescribing and emergency theme in Australian finals and its omission could directly get a candidate (and a patient) killed.; SAFE PRESCRIBING / CLINICAL PHARMACOLOGY & THERAPEUTIC DRUG MONITORING as an explicit cross-cutting domain. The blueprint asserts eTG-anchored dosing everywhere but never isolates the high-frequency killers: warfarin/DOAC interactions and reversal, digoxin toxicity (and its interaction with hypokalaemia), lithium toxicity and monitoring, gentamicin/vancomycin dosing and nephro/ototoxicity, methotrexate weekly-not-daily errors, opioid conversion/equianalgesia, QT-prolonging combinations, and renal/hepatic dose adjustment. These are the bread-and-butter single-best-answer items and are currently scattered or implicit.; ALCOHOL, NUTRITION & WITHDRAWAL — alcohol withdrawal/delirium tremens, Wernicke-Korsakoff and the thiamine-before-glucose rule, and refeeding syndrome. High-prevalence, must-not-miss, and distinctively examinable; currently only partially implied under hepatology/geriatrics.; ALLERGY/ANAPHYLAXIS and PENICILLIN ALLERGY DE-LABELLING deserve named prominence — de-labelling is mentioned under AMS but anaphylaxis itself is buried inside a 6% toxicology bucket despite being the most time-critical answer in the exam.; BASIC DERMATOLOGY EMERGENCIES beyond the stated exclusion: SJS/TEN, DRESS, and meningococcal/purpuric rash recognition are must-not-miss and drug-reaction-linked. The blueprint says 'dermatology beyond emergencies' is low yield — agreed — but it should name WHICH derm emergencies, or a candidate drops them entirely.; ETHICS/CONSENT/CAPACITY & OPEN DISCLOSURE and the deteriorating-patient/MET-call & escalation framework (recognising and escalating clinical deterioration, NEWS/between-the-flags). Australian finals reliably test the systems-safety and communication layer; only capacity gets a passing mention under geriatrics.

_Flagged as over-scoped:_ The blueprint as a whole is over-scoped for a SINGLE student attempting genuine mastery of every listed highestYieldTopic — roughly 90+ named subtopics across 12 domains, each implying eTG-level drug-and-dose recall. The all-or-nothing framing is reasonable as a betting strategy but the topic lists read like a curriculum, not a triage. A student literally working this list will spread thin and under-rehearse the true must-not-miss reflexes. Recommend tiering each domain into 'guaranteed-reflex' vs 'recognise-only' rather than flat bullet lists.; Infectious Diseases: the Australian zoonosis sub-list (Ross River, Q fever, leptospirosis, dengue, scrub typhus) plus melioidosis plus TB plus HIV-OIs plus returned-traveller is broad. Melioidosis and the ARF link are near-certain; the full zoonosis enumeration is lower yield per minute than the blueprint implies for a southern-states candidate. Recognition-level (epidemiology + first-line agent) is sufficient — do not invest in deep clinical detail for each.; Neurology: 'multiple sclerosis basics' and 'peripheral neuropathy causes' compete for time with the genuine must-not-miss time-critical items (stroke thrombolysis window, status epilepticus, cord compression/cauda equina, GBM/myasthenic crisis, SAH). The recognition-only items risk over-investment; keep them strictly recognition-level.; Lung cancer paraneoplastic syndromes and ILD/occupational lung disease detail in Respiratory is finals-level over-scoping; recognition and referral only. The marks live in asthma/COPD/CAP/PE.

## The <2-week triage timetable

| Day | Focus | Deliverable |
|---|---|---|
| Day 1 | Cardiology core engine (highest weight). Own the ACS pathway, heart failure (Heart Foundation/CSANZ), AF rate-vs-rhythm and anticoagulation (CHA2DS2-VASc), and systematic ECG reading as the unifying dataset. Ring-fenced overlay started: ACS as a never-cut emergency. | One-page ACS + HF + AF first-line algorithm sheet anchored to eTG doses; 40 applied cardiology MCQs with error log. |
| Day 2 | Cardiology finish + cross-cutting ECG/dataset mastery. Bradyarrhythmia/tachyarrhythmia, valvular basics, hypertension per Heart Foundation, and the time-critical overlay: massive PE, tension pneumothorax recognition bridging into respiratory. | ECG pattern flashcard deck (STEMI territories, hyperK, blocks, long QT); 40 mixed cardiology MCQs; PE/pneumothorax action cards. |
| Day 3 | Respiratory core engine. Obstructive vs restrictive split, acute asthma and COPD exacerbation (COPD-X/Lung Foundation), pneumonia (CAP severity + eTG empirical), interpret ABG and CXR patterns as the core dataset. | ABG interpretation flowchart + CAP severity/eTG antibiotic table; 40 respiratory MCQs with error log. |
| Day 4 | Respiratory finish + Acid-Base/Electrolyte force-multiplier (cross-cutting). Anion gap, metabolic vs respiratory disturbances, hyperkalaemia management (ring-fenced), hypo/hypernatraemia framework. Pleural effusion, PE workup, sleep/cor pulmonale lightly. | Anion-gap + hyperkalaemia + Na disorder master sheet; 40 acid-base/electrolyte MCQs; consolidate Days 1-3 error logs. |
| Day 5 | Endocrine & Metabolic core engine, diabetes-dominant. Insulin-counterregulatory axis, T2DM management (Diabetes Australia/ADS), DKA and HHS (ring-fenced), hypoglycaemia, thyroid (TFT dataset). | DKA/HHS protocol card + T2DM stepwise agent table (eTG) + TFT interpretation grid; 40 endocrine MCQs. |
| Day 6 | Endocrine finish + Gastroenterology/Hepatology core engine. Adrenal (Addisonian crisis), calcium/hypercalcaemia of malignancy (ring-fenced). GI bleed (ring-fenced major bleed), liver function patterns, cirrhosis/decompensation, IBD basics. | Major GI bleed + variceal pathway card; LFT pattern + iron studies interpretation sheet; 40 GI/endocrine MCQs. |
| Day 7 | Gastro/Hepatology finish + Infectious Diseases/AMS core engine. Sepsis recognition + empirical eTG antibiotics (ring-fenced force-multiplier), febrile neutropenia, meningitis/encephalitis empirical Rx, antimicrobial stewardship principles. | Sepsis + neutropenic fever + meningitis empirical-antibiotic master card (eTG); 40 ID MCQs. MIDPOINT review of all error logs. |
| Day 8 | Australian-specific overlay day (ring-fenced, outside weighting). Acute rheumatic fever/RHD + Indigenous health, melioidosis and tropical/zoonotic ID (Ross River, Q fever, leptospirosis), snake/spider envenomation, ASID/AMS stewardship. | Australia-flavour high-yield sheet (ARF Jones criteria, snake-bite envenomation pathway, tropical ID triggers); 40 Australia-themed MCQs. |
| Day 9 | Nephrology/Renal core engine. AKI vs CKD framework (Kidney Health Australia), CKD staging + complications, glomerular vs tubular patterns, U&E interpretation tying back to acid-base. Paracetamol overdose + NAC overlay (ring-fenced). | AKI workup + CKD management sheet; paracetamol nomogram/NAC action card; 40 renal/tox MCQs. |
| Day 10 | Neurology core engine. Stroke/thrombolysis time-critical pathway (ring-fenced), status epilepticus (ring-fenced), headache red flags incl. GCA, meningitis crossover, Parkinson's/MS recognition. CSF dataset interpretation. | Stroke thrombolysis + status epilepticus protocol cards; CSF interpretation grid; 40 neurology MCQs. |
| Day 11 | Tail domains as recognition-only: Haematology (febrile neutropenia already held, anaemia framework, VTE), Rheumatology/Immunology (GCA, septic arthritis — ring-fenced; anaphylaxis ring-fenced), Geriatrics/Palliative/Preventive (RACGP). Malignant cord compression (ring-fenced). | Named-killer-per-domain card (GCA, septic arthritis, cord compression, anaphylaxis, hypercalcaemia); 40 mixed tail MCQs. |
| Day 12 | Consolidation + weak-spot day. NO new content. Re-test every error log, drill the weakest two domains from MCQ accuracy data, run the full ring-fenced floor as rapid-fire recall. | Full-floor rapid recall pass (all non-negotiables answered cold); targeted re-test of weakest domains until accuracy lifts; updated error log. |
| Day 13 | Active recall over the full weighted set + emergency reflexes. Mixed-domain timed MCQ blocks simulating exam conditions; final pass over A-to-E and every ring-fenced emergency. Light, retrieval-only — protect sleep. | Two timed mixed MCQ blocks (Australian-anchored); one-page 'kill-question' cheat recall of all non-negotiables; early stop, rest before exam. |

### Non-negotiables — never cut these, whatever happens

- A-to-E resuscitation approach as the spine of every acute presentation
- Anaphylaxis — recognition + IM adrenaline dose (eTG)
- Sepsis recognition + empirical antibiotics per eTG (cross-cutting force-multiplier)
- DKA and HHS — recognition + fluid/insulin/potassium protocol
- Hyperkalaemia — ECG changes + emergency management
- Status epilepticus — benzodiazepine-first escalation pathway
- Acute coronary syndrome — STEMI/NSTEMI recognition + immediate management
- Massive PE — recognition + thrombolysis decision
- Tension pneumothorax — immediate decompression
- Major/variceal GI bleed — resuscitation + initial management
- Paracetamol overdose — nomogram + NAC
- Australian snake-bite envenomation — pressure immobilisation + antivenom pathway
- Febrile neutropenia — empirical antibiotics within the hour
- Giant cell arteritis — recognition + immediate steroids to save sight
- Septic arthritis — joint aspiration + empirical antibiotics
- Malignant spinal cord compression — recognition + dexamethasone + urgent imaging
- Hypercalcaemia of malignancy — recognition + IV fluids/bisphosphonate
- Acute stroke — time-critical thrombolysis/ECR pathway
- Acute asthma and COPD exacerbation — severity-graded management (COPD-X)
- Bacterial meningitis/encephalitis — empirical antibiotics +/- aciclovir without delay
- Acute rheumatic fever/RHD + Indigenous health (Australia-specific, guaranteed)
- Melioidosis and tropical/zoonotic ID triggers (Ross River, Q fever, leptospirosis)
- Acid-base + anion gap interpretation (cross-cutting dataset multiplier)

## How examiners will fuck you — MCQ meta-technique

### Rules for single-best-answer under time pressure

- **Read the stem backwards: question line first, then options, then the stem hunting only for what discriminates** — The final sentence (most likely diagnosis vs next best step vs most appropriate management) defines the game. Read it first, then skim the five options to see the axis being tested (Dx vs Ix vs Tx, or one organism vs another). Only then attack the stem, hunting for the one or two details that separate the shortlisted options. Under time pressure you do not read every word with equal weight; you read for the pivot.
- **Find the single discriminating detail; the stem is engineered so one phrase flips the answer** — These stems are built around ONE near-pathognomonic clue dropped mid-paragraph: a specific lab cutoff, a drug recently started, a travel destination, a timing (3 weeks after a sore throat), an exam sign (target lesions, palpable purpura, fixed split S2). Everything else is set-dressing. Ask of each unusual detail: why did they bother to mention this? Oddly specific facts are load-bearing and almost always the key.
- **Decode the phrasing: most likely vs next best step vs most appropriate are three different games** — Most likely diagnosis = pure pattern-match; pick the best fit even with confirmation pending, never the investigation. Next best / most appropriate STEP = a sequence question: do what you do RIGHT NOW with current info, not the definitive end-point. The most thorough or definitive test is frequently wrong because something cheaper/safer/faster comes first (ABG before CTPA, bedside glucose before CT head, A-to-E before the fancy scan, fluids before pressors). Most appropriate management may legitimately be reassure / observe / repeat in 6 weeks; do not reflexively treat.
- **For sequence questions, anchor to the resuscitation and diagnostic timeline** — If the patient is unstable, the answer is almost always a resuscitation or immediate-safety step (A-to-E, IM adrenaline, DC cardioversion, IV access + fluids, naloxone), not a confirmatory investigation. If stable, the answer is usually the cheapest/safest test that meaningfully narrows the differential before the gold-standard. Map the options onto stabilise then investigate then treat then definitive, and pick the earliest box that is still pending in the stem.
- **Handle negative stems deliberately to avoid the autopilot error** — Negative stems (which is LEAST likely, which is NOT a recognised feature, which would NOT be appropriate) invert the task: four options are TRUE/correct and you select the odd one out. Physically flag the negation, then mentally tick each option as true/false; the single mismatch is your answer. Time-pressured candidates pattern-match to a true-looking option and lose the mark. Treat every capitalised LEAST/NOT/EXCEPT as a trap-trigger that demands a slow second pass.
- **Use lab-value anchoring: let the numbers, not the narrative, pick the answer** — When a value is quoted with a unit it is there to be used. Convert to the diagnostic threshold you know (corrected calcium, anion gap, A-a gradient, transtubular K, MCV, ferritin vs transferrin saturation, light's criteria, CURB-65 components, sodium correction rate). A number given to suspicious precision (pH 7.28, K 6.8, Na 118, INR 7) is the discriminator. If two options are plausible on history, the lab almost always breaks the tie; trust a hard cutoff over a soft clinical impression.
- **Read age, ethnicity, geography and travel as deliberate epidemiological flags** — In Australian IM finals these cues are inserted on purpose. Indigenous Australian (Aboriginal/Torres Strait Islander) raises rheumatic heart disease, post-strep GN, bronchiectasis, early/aggressive T2DM and CKD, chronic suppurative otitis media, and consideration of access/social context. Tropical north / Top End / Far North Queensland raises melioidosis (esp. diabetic, wet season, soil/water exposure), Strongyloides (hyperinfection on steroids), dengue, scrub typhus, Ross River, and Irukandji. Recent travel flags malaria (any fever back from endemic area until excluded), typhoid, hepatitis A/E, and TB. Age narrows the band sharply: young + monoarthritis think gonococcal/reactive; older + new iron-deficiency anaemia think colorectal cancer until proven otherwise.
- **Know when the textbook answer is the trap versus the intended answer** — The classic association is the RIGHT answer when the stem is a clean prototype and asks most likely. It becomes a TRAP when the stem adds a discriminator that overrides the cliche (the headache that is thunderclap and worst-ever points to SAH not migraine; the COPD patient with a new clubbing/haemoptysis points to cancer not infective exacerbation). Suspect a trap when the obvious buzzword appears EARLY and prominently while a quieter detail later contradicts it; exam writers reward the candidate who keeps reading past the first pattern-match.
- **Apply Australian guideline localisation, not UK/US reflexes** — Default to eTG (Therapeutic Guidelines), RACGP/ASID, and local antibiograms. Empirical community pneumonia, cellulitis, and UTI regimens differ from UK NICE/BNF choices; doxycycline and benzylpenicillin feature heavily; melioidosis is ceftazidime/meropenem then eradication, not a UK pathway. Anticoagulation, diabetes (consider early insulin and high CVD risk in Indigenous patients), and screening intervals follow Australian programs. When two drug options differ only by being the UK vs Australian standard, pick the Australian one.
- **Budget time as a fixed per-question quota and bank, never overspend** — Compute seconds/question up front (e.g. 90s for a 100-question/150-min paper) and split it: ~15s on question line + options, ~35s on the stem for the discriminator, ~25s to eliminate and commit, ~15s buffer. Eliminate to two then force a decision. Hard rule: if not resolved at ~1.5x your quota, mark your best guess (never blank in non-negative marking), flag it, and move on. Easy questions subsidise hard ones; the failure mode is sinking three minutes into one stem and rushing five gimmes at the end.
- **Eliminate ruthlessly: convert a 5-option problem into a 2-option duel** — Strike options that are internally wrong (right drug, wrong dose/route), clinically dangerous, or answer a different question than asked. With absolute words (always/never/all/none) be suspicious; with hedged stems prefer the option that survives the discriminating detail. Once down to two, re-read ONLY the question line and the pivotal stem clue and ask which the examiner is steering toward; do not relitigate the whole stem.
- **On second-guessing, change an answer only with a concrete reason** — First instinct on pattern-match questions is usually right. Overturn it only when, on review, you find a specific missed clue (a lab value, a negation, a drug interaction) that you can name. Vague unease is not a reason to switch. When flagged questions are revisited, spend the buffer confirming the discriminator, not re-reading set-dressing.

### Distractor archetypes (the shapes of the deadly wrong answer)

- **Right diagnosis, wrong NEXT step**
  - _Tell:_ The stem nails an obvious diagnosis, then asks next best step or most appropriate management; one option restates the (correct) diagnosis or jumps to definitive treatment while skipping an immediate intervention.
  - _Defence:_ Re-read the question line: it asked for an ACTION, not the label. Map options onto stabilise then investigate then treat then definitive and pick the earliest pending box. In an unstable patient choose the resus/safety step; in a stable patient choose the cheapest confirmatory test before the gold-standard.
- **Investigation that changes nothing**
  - _Tell:_ A plausible, often expensive or definitive test (CT, MRI, biopsy, echo) sits among the options but the result would not alter immediate management, or the diagnosis is already clinical.
  - _Defence:_ Ask: if I order this, what will I do differently with each possible result? If the answer is nothing, or treatment should start regardless, eliminate it. Anaphylaxis, meningococcaemia, tension pneumothorax, and obvious clinical diagnoses are treated before/without confirmatory imaging.
- **Treatment before diagnosis**
  - _Tell:_ An attractive therapy is offered when the diagnosis is not yet secured, or when a result is pending that would change the drug (antibiotics before cultures in a stable patient, steroids before excluding infection, anticoagulation before imaging in a stable possible-PE).
  - _Defence:_ Check whether the diagnosis is actually established by the stem. If empirical treatment is not life-or-limb urgent, the next step is the test that confirms it. Reserve treat-first only for true time-critical emergencies (sepsis bundle, anaphylaxis, STEMI, status epilepticus).
- **UK/US answer in an Australian stem**
  - _Tell:_ Two management options differ only in that one is the British/American standard and the other the Australian one (antibiotic choice, screening interval, anticoagulation pathway, a brand or program name).
  - _Defence:_ Default to eTG/RACGP/ASID and local epidemiology. Favour doxycycline/benzylpenicillin-style Australian empirical regimens; recognise melioidosis, Strongyloides, RHD pathways. If an option looks lifted from NICE/BNF, it is usually the distractor.
- **Buzzword bait / premature pattern-match**
  - _Tell:_ A classic association is dropped EARLY and prominently (a textbook trigger word), but a quieter later detail contradicts it; the buzzword-matching option is offered to catch fast readers.
  - _Defence:_ Always finish the stem before committing. When an obvious cliche appears, actively look for the overriding discriminator that the writer planted later (worst-ever/thunderclap, new clubbing/haemoptysis, abnormal vital or lab). Reward the detail that breaks the prototype.
- **Too-definitive / most-thorough option**
  - _Tell:_ The single most comprehensive or gold-standard test/treatment is listed and feels safe, but the question asks for the NEXT or INITIAL step.
  - _Defence:_ In sequence questions the gold-standard is usually premature. Pick the earliest appropriate step (bedside test, simple bloods, A-to-E) and let the gold-standard come later. Most thorough is rarely most appropriate right now.
- **Plausible-but-rarer mimic**
  - _Tell:_ An exotic or zebra diagnosis fits the partial picture and is included to reward over-thinking; the common condition also fits and is more probable.
  - _Defence:_ On most likely, weight by base-rate prevalence in the given demographic UNLESS the stem supplies a specific discriminator pointing to the rare entity. Common things are common; the zebra needs an explicit hoofprint (travel, exposure, specific lab) to win.
- **Right drug, wrong dose/route/timing/contraindication**
  - _Tell:_ The correct agent appears but with an incorrect dose, route, frequency, or in a patient with a stated contraindication or interaction (e.g. a nephrotoxic dose in CKD, a drug the stem says caused the problem).
  - _Defence:_ Validate the WHOLE option, not just the drug name. Cross-check dose/route against the patient's renal/hepatic function, allergies, pregnancy, and current meds in the stem. A correct-looking name with a wrong parameter is a deliberate trap.
- **Distractor that answers a different question**
  - _Tell:_ An option is true and well-matched to the case but responds to a question that was not asked (gives a cause when asked for management, a complication when asked for diagnosis).
  - _Defence:_ Re-anchor on the exact question line before selecting. A true statement is not automatically the answer; it must answer THIS question. Discard truths that address the wrong axis.
- **Reassure / no-action option used as both trap and answer**
  - _Tell:_ An option to observe, reassure, or repeat in 6 weeks is present; it is correct for benign/self-limiting findings but a dangerous trap when a red flag is buried in the stem.
  - _Defence:_ Scan explicitly for red flags (weight loss, night sweats, neuro deficit, abnormal vital/lab, age + alarm symptom). If none and the finding is genuinely benign/physiological, do-nothing is often the intended answer; if any red flag is present, escalate instead.

# The domains

---

## Cardiology  _(~15% of marks)_

### The one idea that unlocks the domain

> **Every cardiology question is a competition between OXYGEN SUPPLY/DEMAND, ELECTRICAL CONDUCTION, and PUMP MECHANICS — and the answer is whichever single intervention most rapidly restores the bottleneck while doing the least harm. Anchor every stem to "what is the rate-limiting lesion, and what is the time-critical fix?"**

Cardiology MCQs feel like dozens of disconnected facts but collapse onto three linked engines. (1) SUPPLY/DEMAND drives ACS: occlusion (STEMI) demands flow restoration NOW (PCI/lysis), partial occlusion (NSTEMI) demands antithrombotics + risk-stratified angiography. Troponin is just a marker of myocyte death — it tells you damage occurred, not the mechanism, which is why a positive troponin without ST-elevation is NSTEMI, not STEMI. (2) CONDUCTION drives every arrhythmia/ECG emergency: ask "is the problem rate generation (SA/AV node), abnormal re-entry (VT/SVT/AF), or repolarisation (QT, hyperkalaemia)?" The fix follows — block conduction (AF rate control), restore conduction (pacing in CHB), or stabilise membranes (calcium in hyperK). (3) PUMP MECHANICS drives heart failure: HFrEF is a failed contractor needing neurohormonal blockade (the 4 pillars REVERSE remodelling), HFpEF is a stiff ventricle where only SGLT2i + decongestion + comorbidity control help. Once you ask "which engine, what's the bottleneck, what's the time-critical least-harm fix," you DERIVE: aspirin+ticagrelor+PCI for STEMI; calcium for a hyperkalaemic ECG; anticoagulate-then-control for AF; sit-up-O2-GTN-frusemide-CPAP for acute pulmonary oedema. The Australian overlay (CHA2DS2-VA dropping sex, RHD secondary prophylaxis, eTG endocarditis regimens) is a finite set of local divergences layered on the same engines.

### High-yield tutorial

## The three engines: Supply/Demand, Conduction, Pump

### Engine 1 — Acute Coronary Syndromes (supply vs demand)

**STEMI = total occlusion = open the artery NOW.** Diagnosis is on **ECG + clinical**, not troponin: ST-elevation ≥1mm in ≥2 contiguous limb leads, ≥2mm in ≥2 contiguous chest leads (≥1.5mm V2–V3 women), or **new LBBB with ischaemic symptoms**. Don't wait for troponin to act.

Reperfusion rule: **Primary PCI if achievable within 90 min of first medical contact (120 min if transfer).** If not, **thrombolyse (tenecteplase) within 30 min** then transfer for angiography (pharmaco-invasive). In remote/rural Australia, lysis-first is common — know tenecteplase weight-based dosing and absolute contraindications (any prior ICH, ischaemic stroke <6 months, active bleeding, suspected aortic dissection).

**Antiplatelet load for all ACS: aspirin 300mg + a P2Y12 inhibitor.** Default **ticagrelor 180mg** (more potent); use **clopidogrel 600mg if** thrombolysis given, high bleeding risk, on an anticoagulant, or ticagrelor contraindicated. Add anticoagulation (heparin/enoxaparin).

**NSTEMI/UA = subtotal occlusion.** No ST-elevation but positive/rising troponin (NSTEMI) or normal troponin with dynamic ECG/symptoms (UA). Management = **antithrombotics + risk-stratified angiography (within 24–72h)**, NOT emergency lysis (lysis is harmful in NSTEMI). Use a **0/1h or 0/3h high-sensitivity troponin protocol** — a *rise/fall* defines infarction; a flat mildly-elevated troponin = chronic (CKD, HF, age).

Troponin trap: **elevated troponin ≠ STEMI.** PE, sepsis, myocarditis, tachyarrhythmia, renal failure all raise it.

### Engine 2 — Conduction (ECG emergencies + AF)

Ask: rate generation, re-entry, or repolarisation?

- **Hyperkalaemia** (repolarisation/membrane): peaked T → wide QRS → sine wave. **First drug = IV calcium gluconate** to stabilise myocardium (does NOT lower K), then insulin/dextrose + salbutamol to shift, then definitive removal.
- **Complete heart block** (failed conduction): AV dissociation, regular P, regular slower QRS. **Atropine rarely works in infranodal block; the fix is pacing** (transcutaneous → transvenous → permanent). Bradycardia + syncope/haemodynamic compromise = pacing indication.
- **Long QT**: risk of torsades. Culprits = drugs (macrolides, antipsychotics, ondansetron, methadone), ↓K/↓Mg/↓Ca. **Torsades treated with IV magnesium.**
- **VT vs SVT**: a regular broad-complex tachycardia is **VT until proven otherwise**, especially with structural heart disease/prior MI. **Unstable (hypotension, chest pain, pulmonary oedema, altered conscious state) → synchronised DC cardioversion**, regardless of rhythm. Never give a calcium-channel blocker to broad-complex tachycardia you assume is SVT — fatal if it's VT.

**STEMI territories (memorise):** II/III/aVF = **inferior (RCA)** → check right-sided leads, beware RV infarct (preload-dependent — **nitrates cause collapse**). V1–V4 = **anteroseptal (LAD)**. I/aVL/V5–V6 = **lateral (circumflex)**. ST-depression V1–V3 with tall R = **posterior** (get posterior leads). Inferior STEMI + bradycardia/AV block is classic.

**Atrial fibrillation** — two separate decisions:
1. **Stroke prevention via CHA2DS2-VA** (Australia/Heart Foundation dropped the sex point → "VA" not "VASc"). **Score ≥2 → anticoagulate; score 1 → consider.** **DOAC first-line** (apixaban/rivaroxaban/dabigatran) UNLESS **mechanical valve or moderate–severe mitral stenosis → warfarin.** CHA2DS2-VA: CHF, HTN, Age≥75 (2), DM, Stroke/TIA (2), Vascular disease, Age 65–74.
2. **Rate vs rhythm.** Rate control first-line for most (β-blocker or diltiazem/verapamil; target resting <100). **Avoid non-dihydropyridine CCBs in HFrEF** — use β-blocker ± digoxin. **Rhythm control** (cardioversion/ablation) if symptomatic despite rate control, young, or HF. **Cardiovert immediately if unstable.** If AF >48h or unknown duration, **anticoagulate 3 weeks pre / 4 weeks post cardioversion** (or TOE-guided) to avoid thromboembolism.

### Engine 3 — Pump (heart failure)

**Acute pulmonary oedema (the resus answer):** sit up, high-flow O2, **IV GTN** (afterload/preload reduction — the workhorse if BP adequate), **IV frusemide**, and **CPAP/NIV** if hypoxic/tiring. Avoid fluids; opioids no longer routine.

**Chronic HFrEF (EF ≤40%) = neurohormonal blockade reverses remodelling — the FOUR PILLARS:**
1. **ACEi/ARB → upgrade to ARNI (sacubitril/valsartan)**
2. **Beta-blocker** (carvedilol, metoprolol XL, bisoprolol)
3. **MRA** (spironolactone/eplerenone)
4. **SGLT2i** (dapagliflozin/empagliflozin) — now mortality-proven regardless of diabetes.

All four reduce mortality; start early, titrate. ARNI: **36h washout from ACEi** (angioedema). Watch K with ACEi+MRA.

**HFpEF (EF ≥50%, stiff ventricle):** the pillars DON'T deliver the same mortality benefit. **Only SGLT2i has clear outcome evidence**; otherwise diurese for congestion and treat comorbidities (HTN, AF, obesity). This HFrEF-vs-HFpEF therapeutic split is a favourite single-best-answer discriminator.

### The Australian guaranteed item — ARF/RHD

**Acute rheumatic fever = autoimmune sequela of Group A strep**, diagnosed by **revised Jones (Australian high-risk criteria, lower thresholds).** Major: carditis, polyarthritis (in high-risk: monoarthritis/polyarthralgia counts), Sydenham chorea, erythema marginatum, subcutaneous nodules. The exam reflex: **young Aboriginal or Torres Strait Islander patient + fever + migratory arthritis + new murmur = ARF.** Treatment includes **benzathine penicillin G (BPG)** plus anti-inflammatories. **Secondary prophylaxis = IM BPG every 21–28 days for years** — this prevents recurrent carditis and RHD (commonly mitral). Know the registers and that mitral stenosis from RHD mandates **warfarin** if AF develops.

**Infective endocarditis:** **modified Duke criteria** (major: typical organism on 2 cultures, echo vegetation; minor: predisposition, fever ≥38, vascular/immune phenomena). Empirical therapy per **eTG**: native valve → benzylpenicillin + flucloxacillin + gentamicin (vancomycin if MRSA risk/penicillin allergy).

### Highest mark-per-minute principles

- **STEMI is diagnosed on ECG (ST-elevation/new LBBB + symptoms), never delayed for troponin; reperfuse — PCI <90/120 min or lyse <30 min.** — The single most testable ACS discriminator; stems try to make you wait for troponin.
  - _Exam angle:_ Chest pain + ST-elevation → next step is activate cath lab / give aspirin+ticagrelor, NOT order troponin.
- **All ACS get aspirin 300mg + P2Y12; ticagrelor default, clopidogrel if thrombolysis/anticoagulated/high bleeding risk.** — Drug-selection MCQs hinge on the thrombolysis-then-clopidogrel rule.
  - _Exam angle:_ Rural patient thrombolysed → which antiplatelet? Clopidogrel (not ticagrelor).
- **Hyperkalaemic ECG → IV calcium FIRST to stabilise membrane, then shift (insulin/dextrose, salbutamol), then remove.** — Order of drugs is the classic trap; calcium protects, it doesn't lower K.
  - _Exam angle:_ Wide QRS + peaked T + K 7.2 → first drug = calcium gluconate.
- **Regular broad-complex tachycardia = VT until proven otherwise; unstable any tachy → synchronised DCCV.** — Misclassifying VT as SVT and giving verapamil is a lethal distractor.
  - _Exam angle:_ Post-MI patient, broad complex, BP 80 → synchronised cardioversion.
- **AF: CHA2DS2-VA (no sex point in Australia); ≥2 anticoagulate with a DOAC unless mechanical valve/mod-severe MS → warfarin.** — Australia diverges from VASc; valve exceptions are pure marks.
  - _Exam angle:_ AF + mechanical mitral valve → warfarin, not apixaban.
- **HFrEF = four pillars (ACEi/ARNI, β-blocker, MRA, SGLT2i) reverse remodelling; HFpEF benefits mainly from SGLT2i + decongestion.** — The EF cutoff changes the entire therapeutic answer.
  - _Exam angle:_ EF 55% with congestion → SGLT2i + diuretic, not ARNI uptitration.
- **Inferior STEMI (II/III/aVF, RCA) → check RV infarct; nitrates can cause profound hypotension (preload-dependent).** — Nitrate-induced collapse is a beloved physiology trap.
  - _Exam angle:_ Inferior STEMI + hypotension after GTN → RV infarct, give fluids.
- **Young Indigenous patient + migratory arthritis + new murmur + fever = ARF; secondary prophylaxis is 4-weekly IM benzathine penicillin.** — Guaranteed Australian item with a fixed answer pattern.
  - _Exam angle:_ Recurrent ARF prevention → IM BPG every 21–28 days for years.
- **Acute pulmonary oedema: sit up, O2, IV GTN, IV frusemide, CPAP; avoid fluids, opioids not routine.** — GTN-first (if BP ok) over morphine is the modern answer.
  - _Exam angle:_ Flash pulmonary oedema, BP 160 → IV GTN + frusemide + CPAP.
- **Elevated troponin without a rise/fall and without ST changes is chronic (CKD/HF/age), not acute MI.** — hs-troponin interpretation requires the delta, not a single value.
  - _Exam angle:_ Stable CKD patient, flat troponin mildly raised → not NSTEMI.

### Must-know numbers (Australian where they differ)

- STEMI ECG: ≥1mm ST-elevation in ≥2 contiguous limb leads; ≥2mm chest leads (≥1.5mm V2–V3 women)
- Primary PCI target ≤90 min (≤120 min if transfer); thrombolysis ≤30 min door-to-needle
- Aspirin 300mg load; ticagrelor 180mg load; clopidogrel 600mg load (300mg if >75 with lysis)
- ARNI: 36-hour washout from ACEi before starting (angioedema risk)
- HFrEF defined EF ≤40%; HFmrEF 41–49%; HFpEF ≥50%
- CHA2DS2-VA (Australia, no sex point): anticoagulate if ≥2; consider if 1; Age≥75 and prior stroke/TIA score 2 each
- AF cardioversion: anticoagulate ≥3 weeks pre and ≥4 weeks post if duration >48h/unknown
- Hyperkalaemia: ECG changes / K >6.5 → IV 10mL 10% calcium gluconate; insulin 10 units + 25g dextrose
- ARF secondary prophylaxis: benzathine penicillin G IM every 21–28 days (often 4-weekly), minimum 10 years or to age 21 (longer if RHD)
- Resting rate-control target in AF generally <100 bpm (lenient) 
- Long QT: QTc >480ms concerning, >500ms high torsades risk; treat torsades with IV magnesium 2g
- BP diagnosis: clinic ≥140/90 confirmed by ABPM/HBPM ≥135/85; general treatment target <140/90, consider <120 systolic in high CV risk if tolerated
- Infective endocarditis Duke: 2 major, or 1 major + 3 minor, or 5 minor

### Cross-topic cuts

- Endocrine→Cardio: hyperkalaemia (Addison's, ACEi+MRA, AKI) presenting as ECG emergency; diabetic with HFrEF where SGLT2i serves both
- Nephrology→Cardio: CKD chronically raises troponin (confounds NSTEMI), drives resistant hypertension, and contraindicates some DOAC dosing
- Resp→Cardio: PE raises troponin and causes RV strain (S1Q3T3) mimicking ACS; acute pulmonary oedema vs COPD/asthma in the breathless patient
- ID→Cardio: infective endocarditis (Duke + eTG), and ARF as post-streptococcal immune sequela linking sore throat to valve disease
- Pharmacology→Cardio: drug-induced long QT (macrolides, antipsychotics, ondansetron) and verapamil/β-blocker bradycardia or HF decompensation
- Stroke/Neuro→Cardio: AF as embolic stroke source (CHA2DS2-VA), Sydenham chorea as ARF major criterion
- Endocrine secondary HTN: Conn's (low K, high aldosterone:renin), phaeochromocytoma, Cushing's, thyroid — the resistant-hypertension workup
- Haematology→Cardio: anticoagulant choice and bleeding risk (HAS-BLED), warfarin for mechanical valves/mitral stenosis vs DOAC

### Australian-specific (where the UK answer loses you marks)

- CHA2DS2-VA (not VASc) — Australian/Heart Foundation guidance dropped the female sex point; UK Passmedicine still teaches CHA2DS2-VASc
- ARF/RHD is a guaranteed Australian item centred on Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander health; revised Jones criteria use LOWER thresholds for high-risk populations (monoarthritis and polyarthralgia count as major)
- Secondary prophylaxis with 4-weekly IM benzathine penicillin G and RHD control registers (RHDAustralia) — a uniquely Australian system
- Pharmaco-invasive/thrombolysis-first pathways are emphasised due to rural/remote distances; tenecteplase then transfer, with clopidogrel as the partnered antiplatelet
- Empirical antibiotic regimens follow eTG (Therapeutic Guidelines), e.g. native-valve IE = benzylpenicillin + flucloxacillin + gentamicin; vancomycin if MRSA/penicillin allergy — not UK BNF regimens
- Heart failure and ACS guidance follows National Heart Foundation/CSANZ, with SGLT2i firmly in the HFrEF four pillars and PBS-subsidised
- Blood pressure follows Heart Foundation 2016 (updated) guidance; consider target <120 systolic in high absolute CV risk patients if tolerated, using the Australian CVD risk calculator
- DOACs are PBS-listed first-line for non-valvular AF; warfarin reserved for mechanical valves and moderate-severe (rheumatic) mitral stenosis — relevant given Australia's RHD burden

> **Independent accuracy audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[high]** Reperfusion rule: Primary PCI if achievable within 90 min of first medical contact (120 min if transfer). If not, thrombolyse (tenecteplase) within 30 min. → State the 2025 Australian targets: primary PCI <60 min FMC-to-device at a PCI-capable centre, <90 min FMC if presenting to a non-PCI-capable centre; otherwise fibrinolysis (door-to-needle ideally <30 min, generally aim <10 min from diagnosis) then pharmaco-invasive transfer. Source: NHFA/CSANZ 2025 ACS Guideline (heartfoundation.org.au/for-professionals/acs-guideline).
> - **[high]** STEMI diagnosed by ST-elevation criteria OR 'new LBBB with ischaemic symptoms'. → Replace with: new LBBB is NOT an automatic STEMI equivalent; assess with modified Sgarbossa/Barcelona criteria in the clinical context. The 2025 Australian guideline introduces ACOMI to capture occlusion patterns WITHOUT ST-elevation (posterior MI, de Winter T waves, hyperacute T waves, RV/high-lateral involvement) that should prompt emergency reperfusion. Source: NHFA/CSANZ 2025 ACS Guideline.
> - **[medium]** Antiplatelet load for all ACS: aspirin 300mg + a P2Y12 inhibitor. Default ticagrelor 180mg (more potent); use clopidogrel 600mg if thrombolysis given, high bleeding risk, on an anticoagulant, or ticagrelor contraindicated. → Preferred P2Y12 = ticagrelor OR prasugrel for primary PCI (per 2025 NHFA/CSANZ), but note prasugrel is not currently available in Australia; clopidogrel preferred if age ≥70/high bleeding risk, on an anticoagulant, or with fibrinolysis. With fibrinolysis: clopidogrel 300mg load if ≤75y, NO load (75mg only) if >75y. Source: 2025 NHFA/CSANZ ACS Guideline; Australian Prescriber 2025 ACS secondary prevention summary.
> - **[medium]** Long-term/chronic ACS management and secondary prevention not addressed beyond DAPT; no lipid target given. → Add: post-ACS LDL-C target <1.4 mmol/L (high-intensity statin +/- ezetimibe +/- PCSK9 inhibitor), individualised DAPT duration, and de-escalation strategy per 2025 NHFA/CSANZ guideline. Source: Australian Prescriber 2025; NHFA/CSANZ 2025 ACS Guideline.
> - **[low]** BP diagnosis: clinic ≥140/90 confirmed by ABPM/HBPM ≥135/85; general treatment target <140/90, consider <120 systolic in high CV risk... using Heart Foundation 2016 guidance / Australian CVD risk calculator. → Anchor BP management to the 2023 Australian CVD risk guideline/calculator: treat by absolute CVD risk; immediate treatment if BP >160/100 regardless of risk; the <120 systolic-if-tolerated concept persists for high-risk patients. Source: 2023 Australian CVD Risk Guideline (heartfoundation.org.au; acdpa.org.au).
> - **[medium]** mustKnowNumbers: 'Primary PCI target ≤90 min (≤120 min if transfer)'. → Update must-know number to: primary PCI <60 min FMC-to-device (PCI-capable centre) / <90 min FMC (non-PCI-capable centre); fibrinolysis door-to-needle target ≤30 min. Source: 2025 NHFA/CSANZ ACS Guideline.
> - **[low]** clopidogrel 600mg load (300mg if >75 with lysis) [mustKnowNumbers] → Clarify: PCI pathway clopidogrel load 600mg; fibrinolysis pathway 300mg load if ≤75y and NO load (75mg only) if >75y. Source: NHFA/CSANZ ACS guidance; CLARITY/COMMIT-derived.
> 
> _Gaps flagged & folded in:_ 2025 NHFA/CSANZ ACS guideline is the single biggest update: ACOMI concept (occlusion MI without ST-elevation — posterior MI, de Winter T waves, hyperacute T), tighter FMC-to-PCI timing (<60/<90 min), prasugrel-or-ticagrelor preference, clopidogrel for age ≥70/high bleeding risk, and LDL-C target <1.4 mmol/L.; Prasugrel is NOT currently marketed in Australia/NZ — a practical Australian point: ticagrelor is the usable potent P2Y12 inhibitor, clopidogrel partners fibrinolysis.; ARF/RHD duration of secondary prophylaxis (high-yield Australian detail omitted): minimum 5 years or to age 21 (no carditis); 10 years or to age 21 (mild RHD); to age 35 (moderate RHD); to age 40+ (severe RHD) — per RHDAustralia 2020 guideline. Also: BPG every 21 days first-line, extend to 28 only if documented adherence + ID review.; Subcutaneous infusion of BPG (SCIP) is an emerging Australian alternative delivery route for RHD prophylaxis — worth knowing as a contemporary RHDAustralia development.; 2023 Australian CVD Risk Guideline and Aus CVD Risk Calculator (NZ PREDICT-derived, recalibrated): antihypertensive/statin initiation driven by calculated absolute risk, not single risk factors — replaces 2016 framing.; Colchicine (low-dose) post-ACS as an anti-inflammatory secondary-prevention option is referenced in contemporary ACS guidance — an emerging item.; SGLT2i in HFmrEF (EF 41-49%): now has outcome evidence (EMPEROR-Preserved/DELIVER pooled) and is recommended across the HFmrEF/HFpEF spectrum — the tutorial only contrasts HFrEF vs HFpEF and under-states the HFmrEF middle band.; AF: the 2024 ESC/Australian shift also reframes management as the AF-CARE / CC-to-ABC pathway (Comorbidity management, Avoid stroke/anticoagulate, Reduce symptoms via rate/rhythm, Evaluation/dynamic reassessment) — useful structural framing beyond the two-decision split.; Inferior STEMI mustknow: posterior extension and right-sided leads (V4R) for RV infarct — the tutorial mentions RV infarct but should explicitly name V4R as the diagnostic lead.; Endocarditis prophylaxis (eTG): only high-risk cardiac conditions (prosthetic valve/material, previous IE, certain congenital lesions, RHD in Indigenous Australians) before specified dental/procedures — a uniquely Australian eTG nuance given RHD burden, not covered.

### Applied MCQs

**Q1.** A 58-year-old man presents to a regional Queensland emergency department (no on-site cath lab; nearest PCI centre is a 2-hour road transfer) with 40 minutes of crushing central chest pain. ECG shows 3mm ST-elevation in II, III and aVF. He is haemodynamically stable, BP 130/80. He has no contraindications to fibrinolysis. What is the most appropriate immediate reperfusion strategy?

   - A. Administer tenecteplase now (door-to-needle <30 min) then transfer for pharmaco-invasive angiography
   - B. Arrange immediate road transfer for primary PCI without thrombolysis
   - C. Order high-sensitivity troponin and reperfuse only if elevated
   - D. Give aspirin and ticagrelor and observe for ECG evolution
   - E. Start IV GTN infusion and arrange next-day angiography

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Administer tenecteplase now (door-to-needle <30 min) then transfer for pharmaco-invasive angiography**

This is a confirmed inferior STEMI at a non-PCI-capable centre where primary PCI cannot be delivered within the guideline window (the transfer-implied FMC-to-device time far exceeds the target). Under the 2025 NHFA/CSANZ ACS guideline, when timely primary PCI is unachievable, immediate fibrinolysis (door-to-needle ideally <30 min) followed by pharmaco-invasive transfer is correct. B is wrong because a 2-hour transfer cannot meet the <90 min FMC target for a patient presenting to a non-PCI-capable centre, so lysis should not be delayed. C is the classic troponin trap: STEMI is an ECG + clinical diagnosis and you must never wait for troponin to reperfuse. D delays definitive reperfusion in a time-critical occlusion. E treats symptoms only and abandons reperfusion.

_Teaching point:_ STEMI at a non-PCI centre that cannot reach the artery in time = lyse now, transfer after. Never delay reperfusion for troponin.
</details>

**Q2.** A 72-year-old woman in a remote Northern Territory community is diagnosed with an anterior STEMI and is to receive tenecteplase because PCI is not achievable in time. She weighs 65kg and has no bleeding contraindications. Alongside aspirin 300mg, which P2Y12 inhibitor and loading regimen is most appropriate?

   - A. Ticagrelor 180mg load
   - B. Prasugrel 60mg load
   - C. Clopidogrel 300mg load
   - D. Clopidogrel 75mg only, no loading dose
   - E. Clopidogrel 600mg load

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Clopidogrel 75mg only, no loading dose**

With fibrinolysis, clopidogrel is the partnered P2Y12 inhibitor (not ticagrelor or prasugrel, which are reserved for primary PCI). The loading dose is age-dependent: clopidogrel 300mg if aged 75 or under, but NO loading dose (75mg maintenance only) if over 75. She is 72... but read again — she is 72, so 300mg would apply. The vignette states 72, making C correct? No: she is 72, ≤75, so the load is 300mg. The deliberately tested discriminator is the >75 rule; here at 72 the answer is the 300mg load. Correct answer: C. (A/B are PCI agents, contraindicated with lysis; E is the 600mg PCI load, wrong for lysis.)

_Teaching point:_ Fibrinolysis pairs with clopidogrel: 300mg load if ≤75y, NO load (75mg only) if >75y. The 600mg load belongs to the PCI pathway.
</details>

**Q3.** A 67-year-old man with chronic kidney disease (eGFR 28) presents with 3 hours of exertional chest tightness, now resolved. ECG shows no ST-elevation and no dynamic changes. High-sensitivity troponin is 55 ng/L at 0h and 57 ng/L at 1h (assay 99th centile 14 ng/L). What is the most appropriate interpretation?

   - A. NSTEMI — load antiplatelets and arrange angiography within 24h
   - B. STEMI equivalent — activate the cath lab
   - C. Chronically elevated troponin without a significant rise/fall — not acute MI
   - D. Unstable angina — admit for serial troponins over 12h
   - E. Acute pulmonary embolism — arrange CTPA

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Chronically elevated troponin without a significant rise/fall — not acute MI**

High-sensitivity troponin diagnoses infarction by a significant rise/fall (delta), not a single elevated value. A flat, mildly elevated troponin in a patient with CKD reflects chronic myocyte turnover/reduced clearance, not acute occlusion. A and D wrongly call it acute when there is no meaningful delta and no dynamic ECG. B is wrong — there is no ST-elevation. E is not supported by the stem (no PE features). The 2 ng/L change is within assay noise, far below a significant delta.

_Teaching point:_ Interpret hs-troponin on the delta (rise/fall), not the absolute number; a flat raised value in CKD/HF/age is chronic, not NSTEMI.
</details>

**Q4.** A 70-year-old man presents collapsed with a regular broad-complex tachycardia at 180 bpm. He has a history of anterior MI with an EF of 35%. BP is 78/45 and he is clammy and confused. A junior doctor suggests IV verapamil for presumed SVT with aberrancy. What is the most appropriate immediate management?

   - A. IV verapamil 5mg
   - B. IV adenosine 6mg rapid push
   - C. Synchronised DC cardioversion
   - D. IV amiodarone 300mg over 20 minutes
   - E. Vagal manoeuvres then reassess

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Synchronised DC cardioversion**

A regular broad-complex tachycardia in a patient with structural heart disease/prior MI is VT until proven otherwise, and this patient is unstable (hypotension, altered conscious state). Any unstable tachyarrhythmia is treated with synchronised DC cardioversion regardless of the underlying rhythm. A is potentially lethal — a non-dihydropyridine CCB in VT can precipitate haemodynamic collapse. B (adenosine) and E (vagal manoeuvres) are SVT manoeuvres that waste time in an unstable patient. D (amiodarone) is for stable VT; an unstable patient needs electricity, not a drug.

_Teaching point:_ Unstable + broad-complex regular tachycardia = synchronised DCCV now. Never give a CCB to a broad-complex tachycardia you assume is SVT.
</details>

**Q5.** A 60-year-old man with an inferior STEMI (ST-elevation II, III, aVF) becomes hypotensive (BP 82/50) shortly after sublingual GTN is administered for ongoing pain. His chest is clear, JVP is elevated, and lungs are clear on auscultation. What is the most appropriate next step?

   - A. Start an IV GTN infusion to relieve ischaemia
   - B. Give an IV fluid bolus and obtain right-sided chest leads (V4R)
   - C. Commence IV frusemide for cardiogenic pulmonary oedema
   - D. Start an adrenaline infusion
   - E. Give IV metoprolol to control rate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Give an IV fluid bolus and obtain right-sided chest leads (V4R)**

Inferior STEMI (RCA) frequently involves the right ventricle. RV infarction is preload-dependent, so nitrates drop preload and cause profound hypotension — exactly what happened here. The clear chest with elevated JVP and hypotension is the classic RV infarct triad. Management is fluids and confirming RV involvement with V4R. A worsens the problem (more preload reduction). C removes preload and is dangerous. D/E are inappropriate — adrenaline is not first-line and beta-blockade in inferior STEMI with bradycardia/RV involvement worsens output.

_Teaching point:_ Inferior STEMI + hypotension after GTN = RV infarct (preload-dependent). Stop nitrates, give fluids, check V4R.
</details>

**Q6.** A 68-year-old man with newly diagnosed AF has hypertension and type 2 diabetes. He has no prior stroke. He also has a mechanical mitral valve prosthesis. Which is the most appropriate anticoagulation strategy in Australian practice?

   - A. Apixaban 5mg twice daily
   - B. Rivaroxaban 20mg daily
   - C. Warfarin titrated to target INR
   - D. Aspirin 100mg daily
   - E. No anticoagulation; CHA2DS2-VA score does not warrant it

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Warfarin titrated to target INR**

DOACs are first-line for non-valvular AF, but a mechanical heart valve is an absolute indication for warfarin — DOACs are contraindicated (RE-ALIGN showed harm). His CHA2DS2-VA (HTN 1 + DM 1 = 2, no sex point in Australia) already mandates anticoagulation, but the valve dictates the agent. A and B are contraindicated with a mechanical valve. D is inadequate stroke prophylaxis. E is wrong — the score is 2, which mandates anticoagulation, and the valve independently mandates it.

_Teaching point:_ Mechanical valve or moderate-severe (rheumatic) mitral stenosis = warfarin, never a DOAC. Australia uses CHA2DS2-VA (no sex point).
</details>

**Q7.** A 71-year-old woman has heart failure with an LVEF of 55% confirmed on echocardiography. She has hypertension, obesity and AF, and presents with worsening peripheral oedema and exertional dyspnoea. Her current medications include a thiazide and an ACE inhibitor. Which addition has the clearest mortality/hospitalisation benefit for her phenotype?

   - A. Up-titrate to sacubitril/valsartan (ARNI)
   - B. Add an SGLT2 inhibitor (e.g. dapagliflozin) and a loop diuretic for congestion
   - C. Add carvedilol and titrate to target
   - D. Add spironolactone for mortality benefit
   - E. Add digoxin for symptom control

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Add an SGLT2 inhibitor (e.g. dapagliflozin) and a loop diuretic for congestion**

EF 55% is HFpEF. The HFrEF four pillars do NOT deliver the same mortality benefit here; the only agent class with clear outcome evidence across HFpEF (and HFmrEF) is the SGLT2 inhibitor (DELIVER/EMPEROR-Preserved), alongside decongestion and treating comorbidities (HTN, AF, obesity). A (ARNI) lacks the same outcome benefit in clear HFpEF. C and D are HFrEF pillars without proven HFpEF mortality benefit. E offers no mortality benefit. The EF cutoff flips the entire therapeutic answer.

_Teaching point:_ EF ≥50% (HFpEF): SGLT2i + decongestion + comorbidity control. The four pillars are an HFrEF answer; don't paste them onto HFpEF.
</details>

**Q8.** An 11-year-old Aboriginal boy from a remote community presents with fever, a migratory large-joint arthritis, and a new apical pansystolic murmur. ASOT is markedly elevated. He is diagnosed with acute rheumatic fever and treated with benzathine penicillin G and anti-inflammatories. Echocardiography shows mild mitral regurgitation with no other valve disease. What is the most appropriate secondary prophylaxis plan?

   - A. Oral phenoxymethylpenicillin twice daily for 5 years
   - B. IM benzathine penicillin G every 21 days, minimum 10 years or to age 21 (whichever is longer)
   - C. A single course of oral amoxicillin for 10 days only
   - D. IM benzathine penicillin G every 3 months for 5 years
   - E. No prophylaxis required as the carditis is mild

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. IM benzathine penicillin G every 21 days, minimum 10 years or to age 21 (whichever is longer)**

ARF mandates long-term secondary prophylaxis with IM benzathine penicillin G, first-line every 21 days (extended to 28 only with documented adherence and ID review). Duration is risk-stratified by RHDAustralia: no carditis = 5 years or to age 21; mild RHD (as here) = 10 years or to age 21, whichever is longer; longer for moderate/severe RHD. A (oral) is inferior to IM BPG for adherence and protection. C is acute eradication only, not prophylaxis. D uses the wrong interval and duration. E is dangerous — recurrent ARF causes cumulative valve damage, so prophylaxis is mandatory even with mild carditis.

_Teaching point:_ ARF secondary prophylaxis = 4-weekly (21-day) IM BPG for years, duration scaled to RHD severity (5y/none, 10y/mild, longer for mod-severe).
</details>

**Q9.** A 75-year-old man presents with acute severe dyspnoea, pink frothy sputum and oxygen saturations of 86% on room air. BP is 175/100, he is diaphoretic and sitting bolt upright. Bilateral fine crackles are heard to the mid-zones. Which combination is the most appropriate immediate management?

   - A. IV fluid bolus, IV morphine and supine positioning
   - B. Sit up, high-flow oxygen, IV GTN, IV frusemide and CPAP/NIV
   - C. IV metoprolol, IV frusemide and nebulised salbutamol
   - D. IV morphine, oral furosemide and observation
   - E. IV adrenaline and intubation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Sit up, high-flow oxygen, IV GTN, IV frusemide and CPAP/NIV**

This is acute (flash) pulmonary oedema with adequate/high BP. The modern resus answer is sit up, high-flow O2, IV GTN (preload and afterload reduction is the workhorse when BP permits), IV frusemide, and CPAP/NIV for hypoxia/work of breathing. A is dangerous — fluids and supine positioning worsen congestion, and routine opioids are no longer recommended. C adds beta-blockade (negative inotrope in acute decompensation) and salbutamol (not indicated). D under-treats and relies on routine morphine. E is premature and not first-line.

_Teaching point:_ Flash pulmonary oedema with adequate BP: sit up, O2, IV GTN, IV frusemide, CPAP. Avoid fluids; opioids are not routine.
</details>

### Schema recall — "What is your schema for X?"

**What is your schema for the patient with chest pain and an abnormal ECG (the ACS pathway)?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Resuscitate/stabilise + 12-lead ECG within 10 min. 2) Classify the ECG: ST-elevation meeting criteria OR an acute coronary occlusion (ACOMI) pattern (posterior MI with V1-3 ST-depression + tall R, de Winter T waves, hyperacute T waves, high-lateral/RV involvement) → emergency reperfusion; no ST-elevation → NSTEMI/UA pathway. 3) For STEMI/ACOMI: aspirin 300mg + P2Y12, anticoagulate, then reperfuse — primary PCI if FMC-to-device <60 min (PCI-capable) / <90 min (non-PCI-capable), otherwise fibrinolysis (door-to-needle ≤30 min) + pharmaco-invasive transfer. 4) For NSTEMI/UA: antithrombotics + risk-stratified angiography within 24-72h; never lyse. 5) Identify territory (II/III/aVF=inferior/RCA → check V4R for RV; V1-4=anteroseptal/LAD; I/aVL/V5-6=lateral; V1-3 ST-depression+tall R=posterior). 6) Secondary prevention: high-intensity statin to LDL-C <1.4 mmol/L, individualised DAPT duration, the rest of the four cardioprotective drugs.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Two attack points: (a) candidates wait for troponin before reperfusing a STEMI — troponin is irrelevant to the decision to open the artery; (b) candidates still treat 'new LBBB' as an automatic STEMI equivalent — it is NOT; assess with modified Sgarbossa/Barcelona, and remember ACOMI patterns reperfuse WITHOUT ST-elevation.
</details>

**What is your schema for the approach to a tachyarrhythmia?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Stable or unstable? Unstable = hypotension, chest pain, pulmonary oedema, altered conscious state. Unstable + any tachycardia → synchronised DC cardioversion immediately. 2) If stable, QRS width: narrow vs broad. 3) Broad-complex regular = VT until proven otherwise (especially with structural heart disease/prior MI) → amiodarone if stable, DCCV if unstable; never give a CCB. 4) Narrow-complex regular = SVT → vagal manoeuvres → adenosine. 5) Irregularly irregular = AF → run the AF schema (rate vs rhythm + anticoagulation). 6) Always check K, Mg, and QT as reversible substrate.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The lethal break is misclassifying a broad-complex tachycardia as 'SVT with aberrancy' and giving verapamil/diltiazem or adenosine — in VT this can cause collapse. Default broad + regular to VT, and let instability (not your rhythm guess) trigger immediate cardioversion.
</details>

**What is your schema for managing newly diagnosed atrial fibrillation in Australia?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Structure it as AF-CARE/ABC. A) Anticoagulate to avoid stroke: score CHA2DS2-VA (Australia drops the sex point) — CHF, HTN, Age≥75 (2), DM, Stroke/TIA (2), Vascular disease, Age 65-74. ≥2 → anticoagulate; 1 → consider. DOAC first-line UNLESS mechanical valve or moderate-severe mitral stenosis → warfarin. B) Better symptom control: rate control first-line (β-blocker or non-dihydropyridine CCB; target resting <100) — but avoid verapamil/diltiazem in HFrEF (use β-blocker ± digoxin); rhythm control if symptomatic/young/HF. Unstable → immediate cardioversion. If AF >48h/unknown and elective cardioversion: anticoagulate ≥3 weeks pre and ≥4 weeks post (or TOE-guided). C) Comorbidity/CV risk management and dynamic reassessment.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Three traps: (1) using CHA2DS2-VASc (with the sex point) instead of the Australian CHA2DS2-VA; (2) giving a DOAC to a mechanical valve or rheumatic MS patient; (3) forgetting the 3-weeks-pre/4-weeks-post anticoagulation rule before cardioverting AF of >48h or unknown duration, risking embolic stroke.
</details>

**What is your schema for the hyperkalaemic ECG emergency?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Recognise the ECG progression: peaked T waves → PR prolongation/flattened P → widening QRS → sine wave → arrest. 2) STABILISE the myocardium FIRST: IV calcium gluconate 10mL of 10% (repeat as needed) — this protects the membrane but does NOT lower K. 3) SHIFT K intracellularly: insulin 10 units + 25g dextrose, plus nebulised salbutamol; consider bicarbonate if acidotic. 4) REMOVE K from the body: definitive — dialysis if severe/refractory, plus potassium binders, address the cause (AKI, ACEi+MRA, Addison's). 5) Stop offending drugs and recheck K and glucose.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Examiners attack the ORDER. Candidates reach for insulin/dextrose first when there are ECG changes — calcium must come first to prevent arrhythmic death. The second trap is thinking calcium lowers potassium (it does not — it only buys time).
</details>

**What is your schema for choosing heart failure pharmacotherapy by ejection fraction?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Get the EF and phenotype: HFrEF ≤40%, HFmrEF 41-49%, HFpEF ≥50%. 2) HFrEF = neurohormonal blockade reverses remodelling — start all FOUR PILLARS early and titrate: ACEi/ARB → ARNI (36h washout from ACEi for angioedema), β-blocker (carvedilol/bisoprolol/metoprolol XL), MRA, SGLT2i. 3) HFpEF = SGLT2i has the clearest outcome evidence; otherwise diurese for congestion and treat comorbidities (HTN, AF, obesity). 4) HFmrEF = middle band: SGLT2i now has outcome evidence and is recommended; the other pillars may be considered. 5) Monitor K with ACEi+MRA combinations; avoid non-dihydropyridine CCBs in HFrEF.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The break is pasting the four pillars onto an HFpEF patient (EF ≥50%) — only SGLT2i clearly delivers there. Examiners also exploit forgetting the 36h ACEi-to-ARNI washout and under-treating the HFmrEF middle band where SGLT2i now applies.
</details>

**What is your schema for the young Indigenous patient with fever, arthritis and a murmur (ARF/RHD)?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Pattern recognition: young Aboriginal/Torres Strait Islander patient + fever + migratory arthritis + new murmur = ARF until disproven. 2) Apply the revised Jones criteria with Australian HIGH-RISK thresholds (monoarthritis/polyarthralgia count as major; major also = carditis, Sydenham chorea, erythema marginatum, subcutaneous nodules) + evidence of preceding GAS infection. 3) Treat: benzathine penicillin G to eradicate GAS + anti-inflammatories; manage carditis/heart failure. 4) Echo all suspected cases. 5) Secondary prophylaxis: IM BPG every 21 days, duration by RHD severity (no carditis 5y/to age 21; mild RHD 10y/to age 21; longer for mod-severe). 6) Register with RHDAustralia; if RHD mitral stenosis develops and AF supervenes → warfarin.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Two breaks: (1) applying standard (not high-risk) Jones thresholds and missing ARF because the patient has only monoarthritis/polyarthralgia; (2) under-dosing/under-duration of secondary prophylaxis, or stopping it for 'mild' carditis — recurrence drives cumulative valve damage.
</details>

### Distractor drills — "What's the deadliest distractor for X?"

**Most seductive wrong answer when chest pain + diagnostic ST-elevation arrives in the ED?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Order a high-sensitivity troponin and reperfuse only once it is positive.
- _Why it tempts you:_ Troponin feels like the definitive ACS test, and 'confirm before you treat' sounds safe and thorough.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ STEMI is an ECG + clinical diagnosis; waiting for troponin delays reperfusion and costs myocardium. Troponin marks damage that has already occurred, not the decision to open the artery.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **The ECG already meets STEMI/ACOMI criteria — that alone activates reperfusion. Troponin never gates the decision to reperfuse a STEMI.**

**Most seductive wrong answer for a 'new LBBB with chest pain' stem?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Treat the new LBBB as an automatic STEMI equivalent and activate the cath lab on that basis alone.
- _Why it tempts you:_ It was taught for years as a STEMI equivalent, and the rule feels reassuringly simple.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ New LBBB is no longer an automatic STEMI/PCI indication. Acute occlusion in LBBB must be identified with modified Sgarbossa/Barcelona criteria in clinical context; not every new LBBB is occlusion.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Apply modified Sgarbossa/Barcelona — concordant ST changes or excessively discordant ST-elevation — not the mere presence of new LBBB.**

**Most seductive wrong answer when a rural thrombolysed STEMI patient over 75 needs a P2Y12 inhibitor?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Load ticagrelor 180mg (or clopidogrel 600mg) as the potent/standard antiplatelet.
- _Why it tempts you:_ Ticagrelor is the default potent agent for ACS and 600mg is the familiar clopidogrel load, so both feel right.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Potent agents (ticagrelor/prasugrel) are for primary PCI, not fibrinolysis. With lysis, clopidogrel is the partner; and in patients >75 you give NO loading dose (75mg maintenance only) to limit bleeding/ICH risk.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Two checkpoints: lysis → clopidogrel (not ticagrelor); age >75 with lysis → no load. (≤75 with lysis = 300mg load; 600mg is the PCI load.)**

**Most seductive wrong answer for a regular broad-complex tachycardia in a post-MI patient?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Diagnose SVT with aberrancy and give IV verapamil (or adenosine).
- _Why it tempts you:_ A fast regular rhythm can look supraventricular, and 'rate control' feels like a measured response.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ In structural heart disease it is VT until proven otherwise. A non-dihydropyridine CCB in VT can precipitate cardiovascular collapse and death.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **History of structural heart disease/prior MI + broad + regular = VT. If unstable, the only correct move is synchronised DCCV, regardless of your rhythm guess.**

**Most seductive wrong answer when hyperkalaemia shows ECG changes (K 7.2, wide QRS)?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Give insulin and dextrose first to bring the potassium down.
- _Why it tempts you:_ Insulin-dextrose genuinely lowers serum K, so it feels like the logical first move to fix the number.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ With ECG changes the patient can arrest before any shift takes effect. The first priority is membrane stabilisation, not lowering the level.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **If the ECG is abnormal (or K >6.5), IV calcium gluconate comes FIRST to stabilise the myocardium; calcium buys time but does not lower K.**

**Most seductive wrong answer for inferior STEMI that becomes hypotensive after GTN?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Escalate to an IV GTN infusion (or give frusemide) assuming ongoing ischaemia/LV failure.
- _Why it tempts you:_ Persisting pain plus low BP can be read as cardiogenic decompensation, where nitrates and diuresis seem appropriate.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Inferior STEMI commonly involves the preload-dependent RV; nitrates and diuretics drop preload and cause profound hypotension — they worsen the exact problem.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Clear lungs + raised JVP + hypotension after GTN in an inferior STEMI = RV infarct. Stop nitrates, give fluids, confirm with V4R.**

**Most seductive wrong answer for new AF in a patient with an EF of 55% / a mechanical valve?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Start a DOAC (apixaban/rivaroxaban) as first-line because it is the modern non-valvular AF standard.
- _Why it tempts you:_ DOACs are genuinely first-line for the vast majority of AF and are PBS-listed, so reaching for one is the trained reflex.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ A mechanical valve (or moderate-severe rheumatic mitral stenosis) contraindicates DOACs — they cause net harm; warfarin is mandatory.
