- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Scan the stem for 'mechanical valve' or 'rheumatic/mitral stenosis'. Either word flips the answer from DOAC to warfarin.**

### High-end review pearls

- 2025 NHFA/CSANZ ACS timing (memorise the new numbers, not the old 90/120): primary PCI <60 min FMC-to-device at a PCI-capable centre, <90 min FMC if presenting to a non-PCI-capable centre; otherwise lyse (door-to-needle ≤30 min) and transfer.
- ACOMI is the new high-yield concept: occlusion can exist WITHOUT ST-elevation — posterior MI (V1-3 ST-depression + tall R), de Winter T waves (upsloping ST-depression + tall T in precordials), and hyperacute T waves all warrant emergency reperfusion. If you see these, think 'open the artery', not 'NSTEMI'.
- Prasugrel-or-ticagrelor is the guideline-preferred potent P2Y12 for primary PCI — but prasugrel is NOT marketed in Australia/NZ, so in practice ticagrelor is the usable potent agent and clopidogrel partners fibrinolysis. A classic Australian 'practice vs guideline' trap.
- Clopidogrel loading by pathway: PCI = 600mg; fibrinolysis = 300mg if ≤75y, and NO load (75mg only) if >75y. Mixing these up is a guaranteed mark lost.
- Post-ACS LDL-C target is now <1.4 mmol/L (and ≥50% reduction): high-intensity statin ± ezetimibe ± PCSK9 inhibitor. If you only quoted DAPT, you missed the lipid mark.
- If you see clopidogrel as 'the only PBS-listed P2Y12 monotherapy', remember de-escalation: high-bleeding-risk patients can switch from ticagrelor to clopidogrel at ~1 month — DAPT duration is individualised (1-3 months high bleeding risk, up to >12 months high ischaemic risk).
- Aunt Minnie: clear lungs + raised JVP + hypotension after a nitrate in an inferior STEMI = RV infarction. The fix is fluids, and the diagnostic lead is V4R.
- If you see an Aboriginal or Torres Strait Islander child + fever + migratory arthritis + new murmur, think ARF — and apply the HIGH-RISK revised Jones thresholds where monoarthritis and polyarthralgia count as major.
- ARF prophylaxis duration ladder (RHDAustralia): no carditis = 5y or to age 21; mild RHD = 10y or to age 21; moderate RHD = to age 35; severe RHD = to age 40+. BPG every 21 days first-line (extend to 28 only with documented adherence + ID review). Subcutaneous infusion (SCIP) is an emerging Australian delivery route.
- Australia uses CHA2DS2-VA (no sex point) — UK Passmedicine still teaches CHA2DS2-VASc. If a stem hinges on the female sex point, the Australian answer diverges.
- Mechanical valve or moderate-severe (rheumatic) mitral stenosis = warfarin, full stop — DOACs are contraindicated. Given Australia's RHD burden this exception is heavily tested.
- Hyperkalaemia mantra: STABILISE (calcium) → SHIFT (insulin/dextrose, salbutamol) → REMOVE (dialysis, binders). Calcium protects the membrane; it does not lower the potassium.
- HF by EF flips the whole answer: EF ≤40% = four pillars; EF ≥50% = SGLT2i + decongestion + comorbidities; EF 41-49% (HFmrEF) now also has SGLT2i outcome evidence (DELIVER/EMPEROR-Preserved pooled) — don't leave the middle band empty.
- ARNI rule: 36-hour washout from an ACE inhibitor before starting sacubitril/valsartan to avoid angioedema. Watch potassium with any ACEi + MRA combination.
- Elevated troponin without a rise/fall and without ST changes = chronic (CKD, HF, age), not NSTEMI. hs-troponin is interpreted on the delta, never a single value.
- Flash pulmonary oedema with adequate BP: sit up, O2, IV GTN (the workhorse), IV frusemide, CPAP. Avoid fluids; routine morphine is out. GTN-first over morphine is the modern answer.
- eTG endocarditis (not BNF): native valve empirical = benzylpenicillin + flucloxacillin + gentamicin; vancomycin if MRSA risk/penicillin allergy. IE prophylaxis is reserved for high-risk cardiac conditions only (prosthetic valve/material, prior IE, certain congenital lesions, and RHD in Indigenous Australians) before specified procedures.
- BP/CVD risk has moved on from the 2016 guideline: initiation is driven by the 2023 Australian CVD Risk Calculator (absolute risk), but treat BP >160/100 regardless of calculated risk. The <120 systolic-if-tolerated concept persists for high-risk patients.
- Cross-topic Aunt Minnie: S1Q3T3 + raised troponin + RV strain + breathlessness = PE mimicking ACS, not an MI — chase the CTPA, not the cath lab.

> **Independent item audit — verdict: major-fixes**
> - **[high]** appliedMCQ 2 (72yo NT woman, anterior STEMI, tenecteplase, which P2Y12 load?): The item is internally broken and self-contradicting. The 'answer' field states 'D. Clopidogrel 75mg only, no loading dose', but the explanation argues the answer is actually C (300mg load) because the patient is 72 (<=75). So the machine-readable answer (D) DIRECTLY CONTRADICTS the explanation's stated conclusion (C). Worse, the explanation is written as stream-of-consciousness ('she is 72... but read again... No: she is 72... making C correct? No:...'), which is unusable as an exam rationale. By the stated rule (300mg if <=75, no load if >75), a 72yo should get clopidogrel 300mg = option C. So the correct answer is C, and the answer field (D) is simply WRONG. → Set answer to 'C. Clopidogrel 300mg load'. Rewrite the explanation cleanly: 'With fibrinolysis the P2Y12 partner is clopidogrel (not ticagrelor/prasugrel, which are PCI agents). Loading is age-dependent: 300mg if <=75y, no load (75mg only) if >75y. She is 72 (<=75), so the correct regimen is a 300mg load = C. D is the >75y regimen and is wrong here; A/B are PCI agents contraindicated with lysis; E is the PCI 600mg load.' Alternatively, if the intent was to test the >75 rule, change the stem age to 'A 79-year-old woman' and keep answer D — but pick ONE; right now the item is incoherent.
> - **[high]** appliedMCQ 1 explanation + highEndExtras (2025 NHFA/CSANZ PCI timing numbers): The layer repeatedly asserts NEW timing targets of 'primary PCI <60 min FMC-to-device at a PCI-capable centre, <90 min FMC if presenting to a non-PCI-capable centre' and tells candidates to 'memorise the new numbers, not the old 90/120'. This conflates two different things. The actual 2025 NHFA/CSANZ (and ANZCOR 14.3) decision threshold for choosing PPCI vs fibrinolysis remains 120 minutes FMC-to-device (PPCI preferred if achievable <=120 min; otherwise lyse). The <60 min and <90 min figures are wire-crossing QUALITY targets WITHIN a PCI-capable system (<60 min for direct presenters, <90 min for inter-hospital transfers) — they are NOT the PPCI-vs-lysis cutoff, and '<90 min FMC if presenting to a non-PCI-capable centre' is not a real guideline threshold. As written, the layer instructs candidates to discard the correct 120-min number as 'old', which is a teaching error. → Correct to: 'PPCI is preferred if FMC-to-device can be achieved within 120 minutes; otherwise give fibrinolysis (door-to-needle <=30 min, or prehospital) followed by pharmaco-invasive transfer. Within a PCI-capable pathway, audit/quality targets are wire crossing <60 min for direct presenters and <90 min for transfers.' Remove the instruction to forget the 120-min threshold. MCQ1's answer (lyse now, transfer) stays correct because a 2-hour transfer clearly exceeds 120 min — but fix the rationale's stated numbers.
> - **[medium]** appliedMCQ 7 explanation (HFpEF, EF 55%, SGLT2i) + schemaRecall HF schema + highEndExtras: The stem asks for the agent with 'clearest mortality/hospitalisation benefit' and the explanation claims SGLT2i delivers 'clear outcome evidence' implying mortality benefit. In DELIVER and EMPEROR-Preserved (and their pooled analysis), the benefit in HFpEF is driven almost entirely by reduced HF hospitalisation; the effect on cardiovascular death was a non-significant trend (HR ~0.88-0.90, p~0.08) and all-cause mortality was neutral. Stating or implying a MORTALITY benefit for SGLT2i in HFpEF overstates the evidence. The answer (B, SGLT2i) is still correct as the single best class, but the rationale's mortality framing is inaccurate. → Reword to: 'SGLT2 inhibitors are the only class with clear OUTCOME (predominantly HF-hospitalisation) benefit in HFpEF; their effect on mortality is a non-significant trend.' Since the stem says 'mortality/hospitalisation benefit', either keep that phrasing (defensible) but ensure the explanation specifies the benefit is hospitalisation-driven, not mortality.
> - **[low]** appliedMCQ 7 option B (bundled answer): Option B bundles two interventions ('SGLT2 inhibitor AND a loop diuretic for congestion'). The discriminating element is the SGLT2i; the loop diuretic is symptomatic and has no mortality/hospitalisation outcome evidence. Bundling a non-outcome intervention into the 'clearest outcome benefit' answer muddies single-best-answer cleanliness and could let a sharp candidate argue the loop diuretic dilutes the outcome claim. → Make option B the clean single agent ('Add an SGLT2 inhibitor, e.g. dapagliflozin') so the outcome claim maps to one agent. Decongestion can be mentioned in the explanation rather than baked into the option.
> - **[low]** highEndExtras bullet on prasugrel ('prasugrel is NOT marketed in Australia/NZ'): Currency issue. Prasugrel was withdrawn for years, but the TGA ARTG now lists multiple registered generic prasugrel products (PRASUGREL AJS, PRASUGREL LUPIN, PRASUGREL SCP, APO-PRASUGREL). So the blanket statement 'not marketed in Australia' is outdated/likely incorrect as of 2026. This does not change any MCQ answer (prasugrel is still wrong with lysis), but the 'practice vs guideline' teaching point rests on a possibly false premise. → Soften to: 'Prasugrel availability in Australia has been intermittent (withdrawn, now available as generics); in practice ticagrelor is the commonly used potent agent, and clopidogrel partners fibrinolysis.' Verify current ARTG/PBS status before asserting non-availability.
> - **[low]** schemaRecall ACS schema + AF schema (CHA2DS2-VA threshold wording): Minor precision issue. The AF schema says 'score >=2 -> anticoagulate; 1 -> consider', which is correct per ESC 2024 CHA2DS2-VA. However MCQ6's explanation and the schema both state the patient's score of 2 'mandates' anticoagulation — fine — but the layer should be consistent that a score of 1 is 'consider' (Class IIa), not 'recommend'. This is internally consistent here, so it is a low-priority watch item, not an error. → No change required; just ensure any future score-of-1 stems use 'consider' not 'recommend'. Flagging for completeness only.
> - **[low]** appliedMCQ 4 distractor D (amiodarone) vs answer C (DCCV): Clinically sound but worth a note: option D says 'IV amiodarone 300mg over 20 minutes' for an UNSTABLE patient. The explanation correctly says unstable VT needs electricity. This is a strong, deadly-trap distractor (good). No correctness problem — answer C is unambiguously the single best answer. No fix needed. → No change. Item is clean and the distractor is genuinely seductive (the 'amiodarone for VT' reflex).
> 
> _Quality notes:_ Distractor quality is generally HIGH. The deadliest traps are well chosen and not strawmen: 'wait for troponin before reperfusing a STEMI' (MCQ1/C, drill 1), 'verapamil/adenosine for broad-complex regular tach' (MCQ4/A-B, drill 4), 'escalate GTN in inferior STEMI hypotension' (MCQ5, drill 6), 'DOAC for mechanical valve' (MCQ6, drill 7), and 'four pillars pasted onto HFpEF' (MCQ7). These are exactly the high-yield reflexes that kill marks/patients.; MCQ2 is the standout failure: it is internally self-contradicting (answer field D vs explanation arguing C) and the explanation reads as unedited thinking-aloud. This is the single most important fix — as shipped it is unusable and would confuse or mislead.; MCQ1 answer is correct but the rationale teaches WRONG timing numbers and actively tells candidates to discard the correct 120-min threshold. High-yield 'memorise the new numbers' framing is dangerous when the numbers are wrong.; Single-best-answer integrity is otherwise clean: I checked whether ARNI (option A) could be defensibly correct in MCQ7 — PARAGON-HF was neutral in overt HFpEF (EF 55% > median 57%), so SGLT2i remains the single best answer. No accidental also-correct distractor found in MCQ7 (aside from the bundling cosmetic issue).; MCQ3 (chronic troponin without delta in CKD) is clean and well-constructed; the 2 ng/L change being within assay noise and absence of dynamic ECG correctly excludes NSTEMI. Distractors A and D are appropriately seductive.; MCQ6 (mechanical valve) and MCQ8 (ARF secondary prophylaxis, RHDAustralia 21-day BPG, mild RHD 10y/to age 21) and MCQ9 (flash APO: sit up/O2/GTN/frusemide/CPAP, avoid fluids and routine opioids) are all clinically sound per current Australian guidance.; No item is too easy for a high-stakes applied layer; difficulty is well calibrated. The main risk is not ambiguity but the two factual errors (MCQ2 answer key, MCQ1/extras timing numbers) plus the overstated HFpEF mortality claim.; Recommend a final consistency pass to ensure the machine-readable 'answer' field always matches the explanation's concluded letter — MCQ2 proves this is not currently guaranteed.

---

## Respiratory  _(~13% of marks)_

### The one idea that unlocks the domain

> **Every respiratory problem is a question about GAS EXCHANGE failing at one of two interfaces — ventilation (moving air, a PUMP problem → CO2 retention/type 2 failure) or oxygenation/diffusion-perfusion (a LUNG problem → hypoxia with low/normal CO2/type 1 failure) — and the ABG plus A-a gradient tells you which interface is broken, which then dictates the entire management algorithm and disposition.**

Once you frame the lung as a pump (chest wall, respiratory drive, airways resistance) feeding an exchange membrane (alveolar-capillary unit), every sub-topic becomes derivable rather than memorised. Acute asthma and COPD exacerbations are pump-failure-from-airflow-obstruction: a rising/normalising CO2 in a tiring asthmatic is the engine reading that triggers ICU, not a reassuring sign. Pneumonia, PE and ILD break the membrane → type 1 failure with a WIDE A-a gradient (V/Q mismatch or shunt), distinguishing them from hypoventilation (narrow A-a gradient, e.g. opioid overdose or COPD CO2 narcosis). The A-a gradient is the single discriminator between "lung problem" and "pump/drive problem" — that one calculation routes PE vs sedation, pneumonia vs neuromuscular weakness. Severity scoring (CORB for pneumonia, the Australian asthma severity table, Wells for PE) all exist to quantify how close the system is to total exchange failure, driving the same decision: how aggressive and where (home vs ward vs ICU). Acid-base interpretation closes the loop: it tells you whether failure is acute (uncompensated, dangerous) or chronic (compensated, the patient's baseline). Owning gas-exchange physiology lets you predict the ABG before you see it, recognise which numbers are red flags, and select therapy that targets the broken interface — bronchodilators/steroids for the pump, oxygen/antibiotics/anticoagulation/drainage for the membrane.

### High-yield tutorial

## The engine: pump vs membrane, read off the ABG

Classify every breathless patient first. **Type 1 respiratory failure = PaO2 <60 mmHg with normal/low PaCO2 → the membrane/V-Q is broken** (pneumonia, PE, oedema, ILD, early asthma). **Type 2 = PaO2 <60 with PaCO2 >45 → the pump has failed** (COPD, exhausted asthma, opioids, neuromuscular). The discriminator is the **A-a gradient = [FiO2×(760−47)−PaCO2/0.8] − PaO2** (room air ≈ 150 − PaCO2/0.8 − PaO2). **Normal A-a = (age/4)+4.** A *wide* gradient with hypoxia = lung/V-Q/shunt problem; a *normal* gradient with hypoxia = pure hypoventilation (drive/pump). This one number separates "give oxygen + treat lung" from "support ventilation/reverse the cause."

## Acute asthma (Australian Asthma Handbook)

Severity is clinical, not the peak flow alone. **Life-threatening: silent chest, cyanosis, poor effort, exhaustion, confusion, bradycardia, SpO2 <90%, or a normalising/rising PaCO2.** **A normal or high CO2 in acute asthma is an emergency** — the asthmatic should be hyperventilating (low CO2); a "normal" CO2 means they're tiring → call ICU. Treatment is **salbutamol (continuous/back-to-back via spacer or neb), ipratropium for severe, systemic steroids (prednisolone 1 mg/kg up to 50 mg), controlled O2 to SpO2 92–96%, and IV magnesium 2 g for severe/life-threatening.** Escalate to IV salbutamol/aminophylline/ICU if no response. Discharge needs a written **asthma action plan**, technique check, and step-up of preventer.

**Chronic stepwise (adults):** the modern Handbook favours **ICS-formoterol** as both preventer and reliever (**AIR / MART**) rather than SABA-only. Step up: as-needed ICS-formoterol → low-dose maintenance ICS-formoterol → medium dose → add-on/refer. **SABA-only treatment is no longer first-line** — a high SABA refill rate signals poor control and risk of death.

## COPD (COPD-X)

**Diagnosis requires post-bronchodilator FEV1/FVC <0.7** (fixed/irreversible obstruction) plus risk (smoking). Acute exacerbation (AECOPD): **controlled oxygen titrated to SpO2 88–92%** (over-oxygenation abolishes hypoxic drive and worsens V-Q → CO2 narcosis), **salbutamol + ipratropium, prednisolone 30–50 mg for 5 days, antibiotics if increased sputum purulence/volume + dyspnoea** (amoxicillin or doxycycline, eTG). **Persistent respiratory acidosis (pH <7.35, rising CO2) despite therapy → NIV (BiPAP).** Long-term: inhaler escalation LAMA/LABA → triple therapy (add ICS) if exacerbations/eosinophilia. **Long-term oxygen therapy improves survival only if PaO2 ≤55 mmHg (or ≤59 with cor pulmonale/polycythaemia), used ≥15 h/day, in non-smokers.**

## Pneumonia (eTG + CORB)

**CORB = Confusion, O2 sat ≤90%, RR ≥30, BP <90 systolic.** Australia uses **CORB/SMART-COP** rather than CURB-65. **0–1 mild = home; ≥2 = admit; severe → IV + cover atypicals.** eTG empirical: **mild CAP = amoxicillin (+ doxycycline or clarithromycin if atypical suspected); moderate = benzylpenicillin IV + doxycycline; severe = ceftriaxone + azithromycin.** **Aspiration** (alcohol, stroke, reduced GCS) → anaerobic cover (amoxicillin-clavulanate or add metronidazole). Atypicals (*Mycoplasma, Legionella, Chlamydophila*) need a **tetracycline or macrolide** — beta-lactams don't cover them.

## PE (Wells → D-dimer → CTPA)

**Wells: <4 (or "PE unlikely") → D-dimer; negative D-dimer excludes, positive → CTPA. Wells ≥4 ("PE likely") → straight to CTPA**, skip D-dimer. **D-dimer is for ruling OUT, never ruling in.** **Massive PE = haemodynamic instability (SBP <90 / shock) → thrombolysis (alteplase)**, not just anticoagulation. Stable PE → **DOAC (apixaban/rivaroxaban) first-line**; use LMWH in pregnancy and active cancer. ABG classically shows **type 1 failure + respiratory alkalosis (low CO2 from tachypnoea) + wide A-a gradient.** ECG: sinus tachycardia (commonest); S1Q3T3 is classic but rare.

## Pleural effusion & pneumothorax

**Light's criteria → exudate if any one: pleural/serum protein >0.5, pleural/serum LDH >0.6, or pleural LDH >2/3 upper normal serum.** Transudate = pressure (HF, cirrhosis, nephrotic); exudate = leak (infection, malignancy, PE). **Tension pneumothorax is a CLINICAL diagnosis — never wait for imaging.** Signs: hypotension, distended neck veins, tracheal deviation AWAY, absent breath sounds. **Immediate decompression: large-bore cannula 2nd ICS mid-clavicular line (or 5th ICS anterior axillary in modern ATLS), then chest drain.**

## Lung cancer & paraneoplastic

**Small cell → SIADH (hyponatraemia), ectopic ACTH (Cushing), Lambert-Eaton.** **Squamous cell → PTHrP (hypercalcaemia).** Clubbing, weight loss, haemoptysis, new chronic cough. **Any unexplained hypercalcaemia or hyponatraemia + smoker = think lung cancer.** **ILD/occupational:** asbestos → pleural plaques + mesothelioma; silica/coal → upper-zone fibrosis; IPF → basal reticulation, restrictive spirometry (FEV1/FVC normal/high, low FVC, reduced DLCO).

### Highest mark-per-minute principles

- **In acute asthma, a normal or rising PaCO2 is a marker of exhaustion and impending arrest — escalate to ICU, do not be reassured.** — The single most common asthma 'trap' MCQ; tests whether you understand the asthmatic should be hyperventilating with LOW CO2.
  - _Exam angle:_ ABG given with 'normal' CO2 in a struggling asthmatic; best answer is call ICU/anaesthetics, not 'continue current management'.
- **Calculate the A-a gradient to separate lung disease (wide) from hypoventilation/drive failure (normal).** — Derives the whole type 1 vs type 2 split and routes PE/pneumonia vs opioid/COPD narcosis.
  - _Exam angle:_ Hypoxic patient with normal A-a → cause is hypoventilation (opioids, sedation); wide A-a → PE/pneumonia/shunt.
- **Target SpO2 88–92% in COPD/AECOPD, 92–96% in asthma/most acute illness.** — Over-oxygenating COPD causes CO2 narcosis via V-Q mismatch and lost hypoxic drive — a classic Australian exam point.
  - _Exam angle:_ COPD patient becomes drowsy after high-flow O2 → cause is hypercapnia; titrate O2 down, consider NIV.
- **Wells ≥4 (PE likely) → CTPA directly; Wells <4 → D-dimer first. D-dimer only rules OUT.** — The entire PE diagnostic algorithm hinges on correctly gating D-dimer vs imaging.
  - _Exam angle:_ High-Wells patient with a 'negative D-dimer' distractor — correct answer is still CTPA.
- **Haemodynamically unstable PE (SBP <90/shock) = thrombolysis; stable PE = DOAC.** — Separates massive from non-massive PE and the only scenario where you lyse.
  - _Exam angle:_ Hypotensive PE → alteplase, not 'commence apixaban'.
- **Use CORB/SMART-COP (not CURB-65) for Australian CAP severity and admission.** — Australian-specific scoring; eTG aligns antibiotic choice to severity tier.
  - _Exam angle:_ Best-severity-tool question or 'which antibiotic' keyed to mild vs severe CAP.
- **Light's criteria: any one positive = exudate; tells you to investigate for infection/malignancy/PE.** — Most reliable way to classify effusions and direct workup.
  - _Exam angle:_ Pleural fluid numbers given; classify and choose next step (transudate=treat HF; exudate=culture/cytology).
- **Tension pneumothorax is clinical — decompress immediately, never image first.** — Time-critical; choosing 'CXR' loses the mark and the patient.
  - _Exam angle:_ Hypotension + deviated trachea + absent breath sounds post-procedure → needle decompression.
- **Match paraneoplastic syndrome to histology: SCLC→SIADH/ACTH/LEMS; SCC→hypercalcaemia (PTHrP).** — Reliable two-step MCQ linking biochemistry to tumour type.
  - _Exam angle:_ Smoker with hyponatraemia (SIADH) → small cell; hypercalcaemia → squamous.
- **COPD diagnosis = post-bronchodilator FEV1/FVC <0.7 (irreversible); asthma is reversible obstruction.** — Spirometry pattern is the definitional discriminator examiners love.
  - _Exam angle:_ Spirometry numbers given; identify obstructive irreversible (COPD) vs reversible (asthma) vs restrictive (ILD).

### Must-know numbers (Australian where they differ)

- Type 1 failure: PaO2 <60 mmHg (8 kPa) with normal/low PaCO2
- Type 2 failure: PaO2 <60 mmHg with PaCO2 >45 mmHg (6 kPa)
- Normal A-a gradient = (age/4)+4 mmHg; room air A-a = 150 − PaCO2/0.8 − PaO2
- O2 targets: COPD/CO2-retainers 88–92%; asthma/acute illness 92–96%
- Acute asthma prednisolone 1 mg/kg up to 50 mg; IV magnesium 2 g for severe/life-threatening
- COPD diagnosis: post-bronchodilator FEV1/FVC <0.7
- AECOPD prednisolone 30–50 mg for 5 days; NIV trigger pH <7.35 with rising CO2
- LTOT criteria: PaO2 ≤55 mmHg, or ≤59 with cor pulmonale/polycythaemia; ≥15 h/day
- CORB: Confusion, O2 sat ≤90%, RR ≥30, SBP <90 — ≥2 = admit/severe
- Wells PE: ≥4 = PE likely → CTPA; <4 → D-dimer
- Massive PE thrombolysis threshold: SBP <90 mmHg or shock
- Light's criteria exudate: pleural/serum protein >0.5, pleural/serum LDH >0.6, or pleural LDH >2/3 upper limit serum
- Needle decompression: 2nd ICS mid-clavicular (or 5th ICS anterior axillary line, modern ATLS)
- DOAC first-line for stable PE/DVT; LMWH preferred in pregnancy and active cancer

### Cross-topic cuts

- Hyponatraemia ↔ Endocrine/Renal: SCLC SIADH presents as confusion/seizure with low Na — overlaps the electrolyte domain; correct fluid restriction vs hypertonic saline.
- Hypercalcaemia ↔ Endocrine: squamous lung cancer PTHrP vs primary hyperparathyroidism vs myeloma — same biochemistry workup engine.
- PE ↔ Cardiology: right heart strain, S1Q3T3, raised troponin/BNP in submassive PE; differentiating from ACS and decompensated HF.
- Acid-base ↔ everything: the same ABG engine interprets DKA, salicylate, COPD narcosis and PE alkalosis — a unifying cross-domain skill.
- Pleural effusion ↔ Cardiology/Hepatology/Renal: transudates come from HF, cirrhosis, nephrotic syndrome — Light's links respiratory to systemic disease.
- Pneumonia sepsis ↔ ID/Sepsis: CORB/SMART-COP feeds the sepsis recognition and antibiotic timing pathway; lactate and qSOFA overlap.
- Cor pulmonale ↔ Cardiology: chronic hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction → RV failure links COPD/ILD to right-sided heart failure and oedema.
- Anticoagulation ↔ Haematology: DOAC choice, reversal, and provoked vs unprovoked VTE duration overlap clotting/bleeding management.

### Australian-specific (where the UK answer loses you marks)

- Severity scoring: Australia teaches CORB and SMART-COP for CAP, not the UK's CURB-65 — examiners expect the Australian tool.
- Antibiotics follow eTG (Therapeutic Guidelines), not BNF/NICE: mild CAP amoxicillin ± doxycycline/clarithromycin; severe ceftriaxone + azithromycin.
- Asthma management follows the Australian Asthma Handbook (National Asthma Council): ICS-formoterol AIR/MART regimens, SABA-only no longer first-line, written asthma action plans emphasised.
- COPD follows the COPD-X guidelines (Lung Foundation Australia) rather than GOLD — terminology and the COPD-X acronym (Confirm, Optimise, Prevent, Develop, manage eXacerbations) may be tested.
- PBS authority prescribing affects real-world inhaler/DOAC access and may frame stems; triple-therapy ICS combos are PBS-listed for COPD with exacerbations.
- Occupational lung disease has Australian salience: re-emergent silicosis from engineered (artificial) stone benchtops is a current Australian exam favourite; coal workers' pneumoconiosis in Queensland.
- Oxygen saturation targets per Thoracic Society of Australia and NZ (TSANZ): 88–92% for CO2-retainers, 92–96% otherwise.
- Mesothelioma and asbestos exposure carry specific Australian medicolegal/compensation context (Wittenoom legacy).

> **Independent accuracy audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[high]** Pneumonia eTG empirical: severe → ceftriaxone + azithromycin. → Severe CAP (eTG): benzylpenicillin IV + gentamicin IV + azithromycin IV/oral is first-line; ceftriaxone (or cefotaxime) + azithromycin is the alternative for penicillin allergy / aminoglycoside contraindication. Note directed therapy and renal dosing for gentamicin. _(Therapeutic Guidelines (eTG), Antibiotic — Community-acquired pneumonia in adults (high-severity), March 2025 update; tg.org.au)_
> - **[low]** Moderate CAP = benzylpenicillin IV + doxycycline. → Moderate CAP (eTG): benzylpenicillin IV (step down to oral amoxicillin on improvement) + doxycycline OR clarithromycin for atypicals. _(Therapeutic Guidelines (eTG), Antibiotic — moderate-severity CAP, 2025)_
> - **[medium]** Aspiration (alcohol, stroke, reduced GCS) → anaerobic cover (amoxicillin-clavulanate or add metronidazole). → Treat aspiration pneumonia like CAP for severity; add specific anaerobic cover (metronidazole) ONLY for lung abscess, empyema, necrotising pneumonia, putrid sputum or severe periodontal disease — not routinely. _(Therapeutic Guidelines Antibiotic March 2025 update; Australian Prescriber, 'Aspiration pneumonia and pneumonitis')_
> - **[medium]** Needle decompression: 2nd ICS mid-clavicular line (or 5th ICS anterior axillary in modern ATLS). → Adult tension pneumothorax (ATLS 10th ed): decompress at the 5th (or 4th) ICS in the anterior/mid axillary line as first choice, using a long (5–8 cm) cannula; 2nd ICS MCL is the older/secondary option. Definitive treatment is a chest drain. _(ATLS 10th edition (2018), needle thoracostomy site change to 4th/5th ICS anterior axillary line)_
> - **[low]** Acute asthma … IV magnesium 2 g for severe/life-threatening. → Acute asthma (Australian Asthma Handbook v3): IV magnesium sulfate 0.2 mmol/kg (max 10 mmol) over 20 min for severe/life-threatening not responding to bronchodilators. _(Australian Asthma Handbook v3 (National Asthma Council), 16 Sep 2025 — ED management, adults/adolescents)_
> - **[low]** systemic steroids (prednisolone 1 mg/kg up to 50 mg) [in acute asthma adults]. → Acute asthma in adults (Handbook): oral prednisolone 37.5–50 mg daily (within 1 h of presentation), continue 5–10 days. The 1 mg/kg up to a cap is the paediatric regimen. _(Australian Asthma Handbook v3, 2025 — systemic corticosteroids in acute asthma (adults vs children))_
> - **[low]** AECOPD … antibiotics if increased sputum purulence/volume + dyspnoea (amoxicillin or doxycycline, eTG). → AECOPD antibiotics (COPD-X/eTG): indicated with increased sputum purulence plus increased volume and/or dyspnoea; doxycycline 200 mg day 1 then 100 mg daily, or amoxicillin, for ~5 days. _(COPD-X X2.2 Optimise treatment (May 2025); eTG Antibiotic — acute exacerbations of COPD)_
> 
> _Gaps flagged & folded in:_ IV corticosteroid in severe CAP: 2025 eTG/Australian update recommends IV hydrocortisone within 24 h of diagnosis for critically unwell adults with high-severity CAP requiring high-flow oxygen or mechanical ventilation (based on recent RCT evidence). This is a current, high-yield Australian addition entirely omitted from the pneumonia section.; Engineered (artificial) stone silicosis and the world-first national ban (effective 1 July 2024; import ban 1 Jan 2025): the tutorial mentions re-emergent silicosis as 'an exam favourite' in australianSpecifics but the actual tutorial body does not flag the ban or the screening/notifiable-disease context. Silicosis is a NOTIFIABLE occupational disease in all states; high-resolution CT (upper-zone nodular fibrosis, progressive massive fibrosis) and serial spirometry are used for surveillance.; SMART-COP detail: the tutorial names SMART-COP but never lists components. High-yield to know — Systolic BP, Multilobar CXR, Albumin low, Respiratory rate, Tachycardia, Confusion, Oxygen low, pH low; a score predicting need for intensive respiratory/vasopressor support (IRVS). CORB75 (adds age >=75) is a simpler validated variant.; PE risk stratification beyond 'massive vs stable': submassive/intermediate-risk PE (RV strain on echo/CTPA + raised troponin/BNP, but normotensive) is NOT routinely thrombolysed — anticoagulate with close monitoring and reserve reperfusion for deterioration. The tutorial's binary massive=lyse / stable=DOAC omits the intermediate-risk group, a common MCQ nuance.; PESI / sPESI score for outpatient PE management: low-risk PE (sPESI 0) may be managed as outpatient with a DOAC — increasingly examined in Australian practice and relevant to disposition (the tutorial's unifying theme is disposition).; Influenza/COVID-19 and antiviral context for CAP: in Australia, consider oseltamivir for influenza-associated pneumonia in season and note that empirical CAP regimens do not cover viral causes; relevant to ASID/eTG respiratory infection pathways.; Asthma-COPD: the tutorial covers AIR/MART well but omits that as-needed budesonide-formoterol (AIR-only) is now Treatment Level 1 even for mild asthma — SABA-only reliever without any ICS is no longer recommended at any step (a key v3 2025 change worth stating explicitly in the chronic section).; Pulmonary rehabilitation and smoking cessation as the highest-yield non-pharmacological COPD interventions (COPD-X 'O' Optimise and 'P' Prevent): pulmonary rehab improves quality of life and reduces admissions; these are frequently tested 'best next step' answers and absent from the COPD section.; Vaccination (Prevent): annual influenza, age/risk-based pneumococcal (per the Australian Immunisation Handbook), and COVID-19 vaccination are core COPD/chronic-respiratory management and an Australian exam staple — not mentioned.; NIV specifics: COPD-X/TSANZ trigger NIV for respiratory acidosis pH <7.35 with hypercapnia persisting after initial therapy; intubation/IMV if pH <7.25 or NIV failure. The tutorial gives the NIV trigger but not the lower pH threshold that escalates to invasive ventilation.; Idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis antifibrotic therapy (pirfenidone, nintedanib) is PBS-listed in Australia and slows FVC decline — relevant to the ILD paragraph which currently stops at the spirometry pattern.; Home oxygen now governed by the TSANZ 2024 adult home oxygen therapy guideline (Respirology) — worth citing as the current Australian source for LTOT rather than older references; criteria in the tutorial (PaO2 <=55, or <=59 with cor pulmonale/polycythaemia, >=15 h/day) remain correct.

### Applied MCQs

**Q1.** A 24-year-old uni student in Melbourne is brought to ED by ambulance with a severe asthma exacerbation. On arrival she is sitting forward, too breathless to speak in full sentences, RR 32, SpO2 89% on room air, HR 128. She is given continuous nebulised salbutamol, ipratropium, IV magnesium and oral prednisolone. Forty minutes later she is quieter and appears calmer, RR has 'settled' to 18, and she is drowsy. ABG on 8 L/min O2: pH 7.30, PaCO2 48 mmHg, PaO2 78 mmHg, HCO3 23. What is the single most appropriate next step?

   - A. Continue current nebulised therapy and reassess in 30 minutes
   - B. Reassure the patient and family that she is improving and reduce oxygen
   - C. Urgently call ICU/anaesthetics for likely intubation
   - D. Switch to as-needed budesonide-formoterol and plan discharge with an action plan
   - E. Give a second dose of IV magnesium sulfate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Urgently call ICU/anaesthetics for likely intubation**

A severe asthmatic should be hyperventilating with a LOW PaCO2. A 'normalising/rising' CO2 (here 48 mmHg) with drowsiness, a falling RR and a quietening chest signals exhaustion and impending respiratory arrest, not improvement. This is a life-threatening exacerbation requiring urgent ICU/anaesthetic involvement for ventilatory support. A (continue and reassess) and B (reassure) are the seductive traps that mistake exhaustion for recovery. D treats this as a discharge scenario. E is reasonable adjunctive therapy but does not address the imminent arrest the CO2 is screaming about — definitive airway/ICU care takes priority.

_Teaching point:_ In acute asthma a normal or rising PaCO2 with a tiring patient is an emergency: the engine reading that triggers ICU, never reassurance.
</details>

**Q2.** A 71-year-old man with known COPD presents to a regional Queensland ED with increased dyspnoea and purulent sputum. He is started on controlled oxygen via Venturi mask, back-to-back salbutamol/ipratropium, prednisolone and doxycycline. Two hours later he is more drowsy. ABG: pH 7.28, PaCO2 78 mmHg, PaO2 71 mmHg, HCO3 34, SpO2 96%. His oxygen had been uptitrated by a nurse because his sats 'looked low' earlier. What is the most appropriate next step?

   - A. Intubate and commence invasive mechanical ventilation immediately
   - B. Titrate oxygen down to a target SpO2 of 88–92% and commence NIV (BiPAP)
   - C. Increase oxygen further to correct any residual hypoxia
   - D. Give IV sodium bicarbonate to correct the acidosis
   - E. Add IV aminophylline and repeat the ABG in 4 hours

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Titrate oxygen down to a target SpO2 of 88–92% and commence NIV (BiPAP)**

This is type 2 failure with a persistent respiratory acidosis (pH 7.28, rising CO2) after initial therapy — and over-oxygenation (SpO2 96%) has worsened V/Q matching and blunted respiratory drive, causing CO2 narcosis. The correct moves are to titrate O2 back to 88–92% and start NIV (the COPD-X/TSANZ trigger is pH <7.35 with hypercapnia despite therapy). C worsens the narcosis. D bicarbonate doesn't treat ventilatory failure. A is premature — invasive ventilation is reserved for pH <7.25 or NIV failure; NIV is the correct first escalation here. E delays definitive ventilatory support.

_Teaching point:_ Persistent respiratory acidosis in AECOPD = NIV; over-oxygenation drives the hypercapnia, so titrate O2 to 88–92% before reaching for the tube.
</details>

**Q3.** A 58-year-old woman presents to a Sydney ED 5 days post knee replacement with sudden pleuritic chest pain and dyspnoea. HR 110, BP 122/78, SpO2 93% room air. She has unilateral calf swelling and a recent immobilisation. Her two-level Wells score is calculated at 6 ('PE likely'). A junior doctor orders a D-dimer which returns mildly elevated, and suggests this 'confirms' the PE. What is the single most appropriate next step?

   - A. Commence therapeutic apixaban on the basis of the positive D-dimer
   - B. Repeat the D-dimer in 6 hours to confirm the trend
   - C. Arrange CTPA
   - D. Commence thrombolysis with alteplase
   - E. Arrange a ventilation-perfusion (V/Q) scan as first-line

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Arrange CTPA**

With Wells ≥4 ('PE likely'), D-dimer should be skipped entirely and the patient goes straight to CTPA. D-dimer only rules OUT (in low-probability patients) and NEVER rules in — its result here is irrelevant. A treats a positive D-dimer as diagnostic, the classic trap. D thrombolysis is wrong: she is haemodynamically stable (no shock/SBP <90), so this is not massive PE. E V/Q is a fallback (e.g. renal impairment/contrast allergy), not first-line when CTPA is available. B wastes time on a test that cannot confirm.

_Teaching point:_ Wells ≥4 → straight to CTPA; D-dimer is a rule-OUT test only and a positive result never confirms PE.
</details>

**Q4.** A 66-year-old man presents to a Perth ED with fever, productive cough and confusion. Obs: RR 32, SpO2 88% on room air, BP 84/52, HR 118. CXR shows multilobar consolidation. He is intubated for high-flow oxygen requirement. Which empirical antibiotic regimen best matches current Australian Therapeutic Guidelines (eTG) for high-severity community-acquired pneumonia?

   - A. Oral amoxicillin plus doxycycline
   - B. Benzylpenicillin IV plus gentamicin IV plus azithromycin
   - C. Ceftriaxone IV plus azithromycin
   - D. Benzylpenicillin IV plus doxycycline
   - E. Amoxicillin-clavulanate IV plus metronidazole

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Benzylpenicillin IV plus gentamicin IV plus azithromycin**

For high-severity CAP, current eTG (2025) recommends benzylpenicillin IV + gentamicin IV + azithromycin as the PREFERRED empirical regimen. C (ceftriaxone + azithromycin) is the seductive distractor — it is the eTG ALTERNATIVE, reserved for penicillin hypersensitivity or where an aminoglycoside is contraindicated, not the default. A is a mild-CAP/home regimen — inappropriate for a shocked, intubated patient. D (benzylpenicillin + doxycycline) is the moderate-severity regimen. E (anaerobic-heavy) is not indicated without abscess/empyema/necrotising features.

_Teaching point:_ Severe CAP first-line in Australia is benzylpenicillin + gentamicin + azithromycin; ceftriaxone + azithromycin is only the penicillin-allergy alternative.
</details>

**Q5.** A 79-year-old nursing-home resident with prior stroke and impaired swallow is admitted with a right lower lobe pneumonia after a witnessed aspiration event. He has moderate-severity CAP by CORB. His sputum is not putrid, and there is no cavitation, empyema or severe dental disease on assessment. The admitting RMO wants to add metronidazole 'because all aspiration pneumonia needs anaerobic cover'. What is the most appropriate antibiotic approach?

   - A. Add metronidazole routinely for anaerobic cover
   - B. Treat according to CAP severity without routine anaerobic cover
   - C. Switch to amoxicillin-clavulanate alone and stop all other agents
   - D. Use ceftriaxone plus metronidazole as standard aspiration cover
   - E. Withhold all antibiotics as this is a chemical pneumonitis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Treat according to CAP severity without routine anaerobic cover**

Current eTG/Australian Prescriber guidance is that routine anaerobic cover for aspiration pneumonia is overestimated. Anaerobes are not major pathogens, so added metronidazole is reserved for lung abscess, empyema, necrotising pneumonia, putrid sputum or severe periodontal disease — none present here. Treat as per CAP severity. A and D add unnecessary anaerobic cover (the outdated trap). C drops appropriate atypical/standard cover. E is wrong because there is established consolidation/infection requiring treatment, not pure pneumonitis.

_Teaching point:_ Aspiration pneumonia is treated by CAP severity; add anaerobic cover ONLY for abscess, empyema, necrotising pneumonia, putrid sputum or severe periodontal disease.
</details>

**Q6.** A 22-year-old tall thin man develops sudden dyspnoea during a subclavian central line insertion on the ward. He becomes acutely hypotensive (BP 76/40), tachycardic, with distended neck veins, tracheal deviation to the left, and absent breath sounds on the right. What is the single most appropriate immediate action?

   - A. Arrange an urgent portable chest X-ray to confirm the diagnosis
   - B. Insert a large intercostal chest drain in the right 5th ICS mid-axillary line
   - C. Immediate needle decompression at the right 5th ICS in the anterior/mid-axillary line
   - D. Immediate needle decompression at the right 2nd ICS mid-clavicular line
   - E. Commence high-flow oxygen and observe for 15 minutes

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Immediate needle decompression at the right 5th ICS in the anterior/mid-axillary line**

Tension pneumothorax is a CLINICAL diagnosis — the hypotension, distended neck veins, contralateral tracheal deviation and absent breath sounds mandate immediate decompression without imaging. Per ATLS 10th edition the PRIMARY adult site is the 4th/5th ICS in the anterior/mid-axillary line using a long cannula (the 2nd ICS MCL had high failure from chest-wall thickness), making D the outdated/secondary-site trap. A wastes critical time imaging. B (definitive chest drain) follows decompression but is slower to set up than needle decompression for an arresting patient. E delays life-saving treatment.

_Teaching point:_ Tension pneumothorax = decompress on clinical signs alone; modern ATLS first-choice site is the 4th/5th ICS anterior/mid-axillary line, not the 2nd ICS MCL.
</details>

**Q7.** A 63-year-old man who has worked for 8 years cutting and polishing engineered (artificial) stone benchtops presents with progressive exertional dyspnoea and dry cough. High-resolution CT shows upper-zone nodular fibrosis with areas of progressive massive fibrosis. Spirometry shows a restrictive pattern with reduced DLCO. Which statement is most accurate regarding his condition in the Australian context?

   - A. The obstruction is reversible and he should be trialled on ICS-formoterol
   - B. This is a notifiable occupational disease and engineered stone has been banned nationally
   - C. The expected spirometry pattern is obstructive with FEV1/FVC <0.7
   - D. Pleural plaques and mesothelioma are the characteristic findings
   - E. The fibrosis characteristically affects the lung bases first

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. This is a notifiable occupational disease and engineered stone has been banned nationally**

This is engineered-stone silicosis — silica causes UPPER-zone nodular fibrosis with progressive massive fibrosis, and a RESTRICTIVE pattern (low FVC, reduced DLCO), so C and E are wrong. Silicosis is a notifiable occupational disease in all Australian states, and engineered stone was banned nationally (use ban from 1 July 2024, import ban from 1 January 2025) — a world-first. A is wrong: this is irreversible fibrosis, not reversible airflow obstruction. D (pleural plaques/mesothelioma) describes ASBESTOS-related disease, the classic seductive mix-up.

_Teaching point:_ Silica → upper-zone fibrosis + restriction + notifiable disease (engineered stone now banned); asbestos → pleural plaques + mesothelioma — don't swap them.
</details>

**Q8.** A 60-year-old long-term smoker presents with weight loss, a new chronic cough and confusion. Bloods show serum Na 118 mmol/L with low serum and high urine osmolality, euvolaemia, and normal renal/thyroid/adrenal function. A CXR shows a central hilar mass. Which tumour type and mechanism best explain the biochemistry?

   - A. Squamous cell carcinoma producing PTHrP
   - B. Small cell carcinoma producing ectopic ADH (SIADH)
   - C. Adenocarcinoma producing PTHrP
   - D. Small cell carcinoma producing ectopic ACTH
   - E. Squamous cell carcinoma producing ectopic ADH

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Small cell carcinoma producing ectopic ADH (SIADH)**

Euvolaemic hyponatraemia with low serum osmolality, inappropriately concentrated urine and normal endocrine/renal axes is SIADH — classically caused by small cell lung cancer (a central tumour in a smoker) producing ectopic ADH. A and C link PTHrP to hypercalcaemia, not hyponatraemia (PTHrP is the SQUAMOUS cell / hypercalcaemia association — the seductive cross-wire). D (ectopic ACTH) is a real SCLC syndrome but causes Cushing's/hypokalaemic alkalosis, not isolated hyponatraemia. E mis-assigns ADH to squamous histology.

_Teaching point:_ Smoker + euvolaemic hyponatraemia (SIADH) → small cell (ectopic ADH); smoker + hypercalcaemia → squamous (PTHrP). Match the biochemistry to the histology.
</details>

### Schema recall — "What is your schema for X?"

**What is your schema for the acutely breathless patient with an ABG in front of you?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Is there hypoxia? PaO2 <60 mmHg = respiratory failure. 2) Look at PaCO2: normal/low (<45) with hypoxia = TYPE 1 (membrane/V-Q problem); high (>45) with hypoxia = TYPE 2 (pump/ventilation problem). 3) Calculate the A-a gradient (room air ≈ 150 − PaCO2/0.8 − PaO2; normal = age/4 + 4). WIDE gradient = lung disease (pneumonia, PE, oedema, ILD, shunt); NORMAL gradient = pure hypoventilation (opioids, sedation, neuromuscular, CO2 narcosis). 4) Read the pH/HCO3 to decide acute (uncompensated, dangerous) vs chronic (compensated, baseline). 5) Target therapy at the broken interface: pump → bronchodilators/ventilatory support; membrane → O2/antibiotics/anticoagulation/drainage.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Students skip the A-a gradient and so cannot separate a hypoxic PE (wide gradient, type 1) from opioid hypoventilation (normal gradient). The other failure point is misreading a 'normal' CO2 in a tiring asthmatic as reassuring rather than as a marker of exhaustion.
</details>

**What is your schema for grading and escalating an acute asthma exacerbation?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Severity is CLINICAL, not peak flow alone. 2) Screen for LIFE-THREATENING features: silent chest, cyanosis, poor respiratory effort, exhaustion, confusion, bradycardia, SpO2 <90%, or a normalising/rising PaCO2. 3) Treat all: controlled O2 to SpO2 92–96%, continuous/back-to-back salbutamol via spacer or neb, ipratropium for severe, oral prednisolone 37.5–50 mg (adult flat dose) within 1 hour. 4) Severe/life-threatening not responding → IV magnesium sulfate 0.2 mmol/kg (max 10 mmol) over 20 min. 5) No response → IV salbutamol/aminophylline + ICU. 6) Discharge needs written asthma action plan, inhaler technique check, and step-up of preventer (ICS-formoterol).

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The killer point is the CO2: examiners give a 'normal' CO2 in a struggling asthmatic and the wrong move is 'continue current therapy'. Also students quote the paediatric 1 mg/kg prednisolone dose for an adult — the adult Handbook dose is a flat 37.5–50 mg.
</details>

**What is your schema for the PE diagnostic pathway?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Assess pre-test probability with two-level Wells. 2) Wells <4 ('PE unlikely') → D-dimer; if negative, PE excluded; if positive → CTPA. 3) Wells ≥4 ('PE likely') → straight to CTPA, skip D-dimer entirely. 4) Once confirmed, risk-stratify: haemodynamically UNSTABLE (SBP <90/shock) = massive → thrombolysis (alteplase). 5) Stable but RV strain + raised troponin/BNP = submassive/intermediate → anticoagulate with close monitoring, reserve reperfusion for deterioration. 6) Stable, low-risk → DOAC (apixaban/rivaroxaban); LMWH in pregnancy and active cancer; sPESI 0 may go home.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Two attack points: (a) using a D-dimer in a high-Wells patient and treating a positive result as confirmatory (D-dimer rules OUT only); (b) forgetting the intermediate/submassive group exists and either lysing a stable patient or under-monitoring them.
</details>

**What is your schema for classifying and working up a pleural effusion?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Tap it and apply LIGHT'S CRITERIA — it's an EXUDATE if ANY ONE is positive: pleural/serum protein >0.5, pleural/serum LDH >0.6, or pleural LDH >2/3 of the upper limit of normal serum LDH. 2) TRANSUDATE (none positive) = pressure problem → heart failure, cirrhosis, nephrotic syndrome → treat the systemic cause. 3) EXUDATE = a leak → infection, malignancy, PE → send fluid for culture, cytology, pH, glucose; investigate for the source. 4) Use pleural pH/glucose to flag complicated parapneumonic effusion/empyema needing drainage.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Students require all three criteria for an exudate — wrong; only ONE positive makes it an exudate. The second slip is stopping at classification without naming the next investigation (culture/cytology for exudates).
</details>

**What is your schema for managing an AECOPD and deciding on antibiotics, NIV and disposition?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Controlled O2 titrated to SpO2 88–92% (avoid over-oxygenation/CO2 narcosis). 2) Back-to-back salbutamol + ipratropium. 3) Prednisolone 30–50 mg for 5 days. 4) Antibiotics ONLY with ≥2 Anthonisen cardinal features — increased purulence (most predictive) plus increased volume and/or dyspnoea: doxycycline 200 mg day 1 then 100 mg daily, or amoxicillin, ~5 days. 5) ABG: persistent respiratory acidosis pH <7.35 with rising CO2 despite therapy → NIV (BiPAP). 6) pH <7.25 or NIV failure → intubation/IMV. 7) Address Optimise/Prevent: pulmonary rehab, smoking cessation, vaccination, LTOT if PaO2 ≤55 (or ≤59 with cor pulmonale/polycythaemia) ≥15 h/day.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The over-oxygenation trap (sats 'looked low', uptitrated O2 → narcosis). Also confusing the NIV threshold (pH <7.35) with the intubation threshold (pH <7.25), and giving antibiotics for breathlessness alone without purulence.
</details>

**What is your schema for choosing the right CAP antibiotic by Australian severity tool?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Score severity with CORB (Confusion, O2 sat ≤90%, RR ≥30, BP/SBP <90) or SMART-COP. 2) MILD (0–1, home): amoxicillin ± doxycycline OR clarithromycin if atypical suspected. 3) MODERATE (admit): benzylpenicillin IV (step down to oral amoxicillin on improvement) + doxycycline OR clarithromycin. 4) SEVERE/high-severity: benzylpenicillin IV + gentamicin IV + azithromycin (PREFERRED); ceftriaxone + azithromycin only for penicillin allergy/aminoglycoside contraindication. 5) Critically unwell on high-flow O2/ventilation: add IV hydrocortisone within 24 h. 6) Atypicals need a tetracycline or macrolide — beta-lactams don't cover them.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Defaulting to ceftriaxone + azithromycin for severe CAP — that is the eTG ALTERNATIVE, not first-line. Also using CURB-65 instead of CORB/SMART-COP, and forgetting atypicals are uncovered by penicillins.
</details>

### Distractor drills — "What's the deadliest distractor for X?"

**Most seductive wrong answer when a struggling acute asthmatic's ABG shows a 'normal' PaCO2?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ 'Continue current management and reassess' / 'reassure — the patient is improving'
- _Why it tempts you:_ A normal number looks reassuring, and the patient often appears calmer and quieter with a slower respiratory rate — it reads like recovery.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ A severe asthmatic should be hyperventilating with a LOW CO2. A normal or rising CO2 means the patient is tiring and can no longer maintain ventilation — this is impending respiratory arrest, not improvement.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **In a severe exacerbation, a CO2 that is 'normal' (let alone rising) plus drowsiness/falling RR/quiet chest = exhaustion → call ICU now.**

**Most seductive wrong answer for empirical antibiotics in severe/high-severity CAP in an eTG-keyed Australian question?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Ceftriaxone IV + azithromycin
- _Why it tempts you:_ It is widely used, covers typicals and atypicals, and is genuinely an eTG-listed severe-CAP regimen — so it 'feels' textbook-correct.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ It is the eTG ALTERNATIVE regimen, reserved for penicillin hypersensitivity or where an aminoglycoside is contraindicated — not the default first-line.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **The PREFERRED severe-CAP regimen is benzylpenicillin + gentamicin + azithromycin; ceftriaxone replaces it only when there's penicillin allergy or you can't give an aminoglycoside.**

**Most seductive wrong answer when a high-Wells PE patient has a 'mildly positive D-dimer'?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ 'The positive D-dimer confirms PE — commence apixaban'
- _Why it tempts you:_ A raised D-dimer in a clinically likely PE feels like confirmation, and starting anticoagulation feels decisive and safe.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ D-dimer rules OUT only and never rules IN; with Wells ≥4 the D-dimer should never have been ordered, and you still need imaging to confirm before committing to treatment duration/risk.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Wells ≥4 → CTPA regardless of D-dimer; a positive D-dimer changes nothing because it cannot confirm PE.**

**Most seductive wrong answer for the needle decompression site in adult tension pneumothorax?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ 2nd intercostal space, mid-clavicular line
- _Why it tempts you:_ It is the classic, most-memorised landmark drilled for decades and still appears in older texts and mnemonics.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ The 2nd ICS MCL has high failure rates because adult chest-wall thickness often exceeds standard cannula length; ATLS 10th edition moved the primary site.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Modern ATLS first-choice is the 4th/5th ICS in the anterior/mid-axillary line with a long (5–8 cm) cannula; 2nd ICS MCL is now secondary.**

**Most seductive wrong answer when a COPD patient desaturates and oxygen 'looks low'?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Increase the oxygen to fully correct the hypoxia (aim SpO2 >95%)
- _Why it tempts you:_ Treating hypoxia by giving more oxygen is intuitive and feels safe — and the sats number improves immediately.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Over-oxygenating a CO2-retainer worsens V/Q matching and blunts the hypoxic drive, driving CO2 narcosis, drowsiness and worsening acidosis.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Target SpO2 88–92% in COPD/CO2-retainers; if drowsy with rising CO2 despite controlled O2, the answer is NIV — not more oxygen.**

**Most seductive wrong answer for classifying a pleural effusion by Light's criteria?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ 'It's only an exudate if all three Light's criteria are met'
- _Why it tempts you:_ Three named criteria invite the assumption that you need all of them, and partially-positive numbers look like a transudate.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ An effusion is an exudate if ANY ONE of the three criteria is positive — requiring all three massively under-calls exudates and misdirects the workup.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **One positive criterion = exudate → investigate for infection/malignancy/PE (culture, cytology).**

**Most seductive wrong answer when an occupational stone-benchtop worker has upper-zone fibrosis?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Pleural plaques and mesothelioma (asbestos-related disease)
- _Why it tempts you:_ Both are dust/occupational lung diseases with overlapping 'dusty trade' histories, so the asbestos associations spring to mind.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Engineered (artificial) stone causes SILICOSIS — upper-zone nodular fibrosis ± progressive massive fibrosis with a restrictive pattern; pleural plaques/mesothelioma are ASBESTOS, not silica.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Silica = upper-zone fibrosis + restriction + notifiable (engineered stone now banned); asbestos = pleural plaques + mesothelioma (lower zones/pleura).**

### High-end review pearls

- If you see a tiring asthmatic with a 'normal' PaCO2, think impending arrest — the CO2 should be low; a normal one is the engine reading that triggers ICU.
- Aunt-minnie: smoker + euvolaemic hyponatraemia = SCLC/SIADH; smoker + hypercalcaemia = squamous/PTHrP. Two-step biochemistry-to-histology link the examiners love.
- D-dimer is a RULE-OUT test only. If you ever 'confirm' a PE with a positive D-dimer, you've fallen for the trap — Wells ≥4 goes straight to CTPA.
- O2 targets are a two-number reflex: 88–92% for COPD/CO2-retainers, 92–96% for asthma and most acute illness. Getting this 'wrong' direction is a classic Australian mark-loser.
- NIV trigger vs intubation trigger in AECOPD: pH <7.35 → NIV; pH <7.25 (or NIV failure) → intubate. Don't conflate the two thresholds.
- Severe CAP first-line in Australia is benzylpenicillin + gentamicin + azithromycin — ceftriaxone + azithromycin is ONLY the penicillin-allergy alternative. New 2025 add: IV hydrocortisone within 24 h for critically unwell high-severity CAP on high-flow O2/ventilation.
- Aspiration pneumonia does NOT routinely need metronidazole — anaerobic cover only for abscess, empyema, necrotising pneumonia, putrid sputum or severe periodontal disease.
- Tension pneumothorax is a clinical diagnosis: hypotension + distended neck veins + trachea deviated AWAY + absent breath sounds → decompress immediately. Modern ATLS site is 4th/5th ICS anterior/mid-axillary line, NOT 2nd ICS MCL.
- Light's criteria: ANY ONE positive = exudate. Requiring all three is the commonest misremembering.
- Engineered (artificial) stone silicosis is THE current Australian occupational exam favourite: upper-zone nodular fibrosis/PMF on HRCT, restrictive spirometry, notifiable disease, national ban (use 1 July 2024, import 1 Jan 2025). Wittenoom asbestos legacy carries the mesothelioma/medicolegal angle.
- Australian chronic asthma: SABA-only reliever is no longer recommended at ANY step — as-needed budesonide-formoterol (AIR) is Treatment Level 1 even for mild asthma. A high SABA refill rate is a death-risk marker.
- ILD doesn't stop at the spirometry pattern: IPF in Australia has PBS-listed antifibrotics (pirfenidone, nintedanib) that slow FVC decline — a high-end 'next step' answer.
- COPD-X 'best next step' sleepers: pulmonary rehab and smoking cessation are the highest-yield non-pharmacological interventions, plus vaccination (influenza, pneumococcal, COVID). LTOT improves survival only if PaO2 ≤55 (or ≤59 with cor pulmonale/polycythaemia), ≥15 h/day, in non-smokers.
- Submassive/intermediate-risk PE (normotensive but RV strain + raised troponin/BNP) is NOT routinely lysed — anticoagulate and monitor closely; reserve reperfusion for deterioration. Massive (SBP <90/shock) = alteplase. sPESI 0 = candidate for outpatient DOAC.
- The A-a gradient is the master discriminator: hypoxia + WIDE gradient = lung/V-Q/shunt (PE, pneumonia, oedema); hypoxia + NORMAL gradient = pure hypoventilation (opioids, sedation, neuromuscular). One calculation routes PE vs sedation.
- SMART-COP components (predicts need for intensive respiratory/vasopressor support): Systolic BP low, Multilobar CXR, Albumin low, Respiratory rate high, Tachycardia, Confusion, Oxygen low, pH low. CORB75 adds age ≥75 to CORB.

> **Independent item audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[medium]** MCQ2 (AECOPD, ABG pH 7.28) — option A 'Intubate immediately' vs answer B 'titrate O2 + NIV': The stem describes a patient who is 'more drowsy' with pH 7.28/CO2 78 and over-oxygenation. The explanation correctly says NIV is first escalation and intubation is reserved for pH <7.25 or NIV failure. This is defensible (pH 7.28 > 7.25, so NIV first). However the stem buries a real safety caveat: NIV requires the patient to be able to protect their airway and cooperate. A patient with worsening drowsiness/obtundation from CO2 narcosis is borderline for NIV vs intubation, and many examiners/guidelines say a depressed conscious state is a relative contraindication to NIV. The single-best-answer is still B (titrate O2 first — the narcosis is O2-driven and reversible, give NIV a trial), but the 'drowsy' descriptor makes A more defensible than the item admits. → Soften the conscious-state descriptor to 'mildly drowsy but rousable and cooperative' to cleanly exclude airway-protection concerns and keep B unambiguously single-best. Add a line to the explanation acknowledging that a deteriorating GCS/inability to protect airway would flip the answer toward intubation.
> - **[low]** MCQ1 (asthma) — distractor E 'second dose IV magnesium' / schemaRecall asthma step 4: The explanation calls a second dose of IV magnesium 'reasonable adjunctive therapy.' Repeat/second-dose IV magnesium is NOT an established recommendation in the Australian Asthma Handbook (single dose 0.2 mmol/kg, max 10 mmol). Calling a non-recommended second dose 'reasonable' is a factual softening that could mislead, and weakens E as a clean wrong answer (the real reason E is wrong is that it does not address the impending arrest, not that it is 'reasonable but lower priority'). → Reword the explanation: E is wrong because repeat IV magnesium is not standard and, regardless, does nothing for the impending respiratory arrest the rising CO2 signals — the patient needs airway/ICU. Avoid endorsing a second magnesium dose as 'reasonable.'
> - **[low]** MCQ4 severe CAP — explanation/teaching omit corticosteroid, but distractor set is otherwise clean: Not an error in the keyed answer (B benzylpenicillin + gentamicin + azithromycin is correct first-line per eTG). Minor internal-consistency gap: the highEndExtras and schemaRecall both flag 'add IV hydrocortisone within 24 h for critically unwell high-severity CAP,' yet this intubated, shocked patient is the exact scenario — the MCQ deliberately scopes to antibiotics only, which is fine, but a sharp candidate may be momentarily distracted looking for a steroid option. The stem is clean because it explicitly asks 'which empirical antibiotic regimen.' → No change required to the answer. Optionally add one clause to the explanation noting steroids are adjunctive and outside the antibiotic question being asked, to pre-empt confusion.
> - **[low]** MCQ3 (PE) — option E 'V/Q scan as first-line' framing: The keyed answer C (CTPA) is correct for Wells >=4. Distractor E says V/Q is the fallback for renal impairment/contrast allergy — true — but the patient is 5 days post knee replacement with no stated renal/allergy issue, so E is appropriately wrong. Minor: V/Q is also less interpretable with any baseline lung abnormality; the item is sound. No correctness problem, flagging only that E is a slightly weaker (more obviously wrong) distractor than the others. → Optional: strengthen E by adding a plausible hook (e.g., 'a colleague suggests V/Q to avoid contrast') to make it less of a strawman, though it is acceptable as is.
> - **[low]** schemaRecall PE pathway — 'massive PE = thrombolysis (alteplase)' without naming embolectomy fallback: Minor completeness gap, not an error: for massive/high-risk PE the schema names systemic thrombolysis but omits surgical/catheter embolectomy when thrombolysis is contraindicated or fails. For a high-end exam schema this is a known 'next step' the schema claims to cover ('reserve reperfusion for deterioration'). → Add: 'if thrombolysis contraindicated or fails -> catheter-directed therapy or surgical embolectomy.'
> - **[low]** MCQ7 silicosis — option E 'fibrosis affects bases first' as distractor vs MCQ as a whole: No correctness error — B is the single best answer and is well-verified (engineered stone use ban 1 July 2024, import ban 1 Jan 2025; silicosis notifiable; silica causes upper-zone fibrosis/PMF, restrictive pattern). The item bundles multiple true/false facts into options, which is fine. Note only: option B is a compound claim ('notifiable AND banned nationally') — both clauses are true, so it holds, but compound options carry a small risk if either clause were ever contested. → No change needed; both clauses of B are verified true. Keep as is.
> 
> _Quality notes:_ Clinical accuracy is high. The four highest-stakes factual claims all verified against 2026 Australian sources: (1) eTG high-severity CAP first-line = benzylpenicillin + gentamicin + azithromycin, with ceftriaxone + azithromycin as the penicillin-allergy alternative; (2) routine anaerobic cover for aspiration pneumonia is NOT recommended (only abscess/empyema/necrotising/putrid sputum/severe periodontal disease) per Australian Prescriber/eTG; (3) ATLS 10th edition primary needle-decompression site = 4th/5th ICS anterior/mid-axillary line, not 2nd ICS MCL; (4) adult Australian Asthma Handbook prednisolone = flat 37.5-50 mg, and engineered stone bans 1 July 2024 (use) / 1 Jan 2025 (import).; Distractors are genuinely seductive, not strawmen, on the highest-yield items: MCQ1 'continue/reassess' (mistaking exhaustion for recovery) and MCQ4 'ceftriaxone+azithromycin' (a real eTG regimen, just the alternative) are exactly the deadliest traps. MCQ6 '2nd ICS MCL' and MCQ8 PTHrP/ADH cross-wiring are excellent discriminators.; All eight MCQs are clean single-best-answer with unambiguous stems — no double-barrelled or genuinely ambiguous keys found. The CO2-narcosis logic chain (asthma rising CO2; COPD over-oxygenation) is correct and consistently applied, matching the validated tutorial core idea about pump vs membrane failure.; Weakest distractors: MCQ3 option E (V/Q first-line) and MCQ2 option D (sodium bicarbonate) lean toward strawman territory — clearly wrong to most candidates — but are acceptable as the 'obviously wrong' anchors in an otherwise tight set. None of the items is too easy overall; difficulty is well-pitched for a high-end Australian medical exam.; One soft-language issue to fix (not a key error): MCQ1's explanation calls a second IV magnesium dose 'reasonable adjunctive therapy' — repeat-dose magnesium is not an established Australian recommendation, so this endorsement should be removed even though E remains correctly the wrong answer.; One borderline single-best-answer risk: MCQ2 ('more drowsy' AECOPD) — NIV vs intubation hinges on conscious state/airway protection. Answer B (titrate O2 + NIV) is correct because the narcosis is O2-driven and pH 7.28 > 7.25, but tightening the conscious-state wording to 'rousable and cooperative' would remove the only defensible argument for option A and make it cleanly single-best.

---

## Endocrinology & Metabolic (incl. Diabetes)  _(~12% of marks)_

### The one idea that unlocks the domain

> **Every endocrine/metabolic answer is derived from two coupled questions: (1) is the hormone/electrolyte HIGH or LOW, and (2) is the lesion PRIMARY (gland) or CENTRAL/SECONDARY (pituitary–hypothalamus) — then you apply feedback logic and volume/osmolality status to the same handful of axes (HPA, HPT, glucose-insulin, ADH-water, calcium-PTH).**

Endocrinology looks like dozens of disconnected diseases but is really one engine: negative feedback loops plus fluid/osmolality balance. If you fix the axis and the direction, the labs fall out automatically. Hypothyroid + HIGH TSH = primary (gland failing, pituitary screaming); hypothyroid + LOW/normal TSH = central. Same template solves adrenal (low cortisol + high ACTH = Addison/primary; low cortisol + low ACTH = pituitary), calcium (high Ca + high/inappropriate PTH = primary hyperparathyroidism; high Ca + suppressed PTH = malignancy/granuloma), and gonadal axes. For water/sodium, the engine is osmolality + volume status: sodium disorders are WATER problems, so you ask "is the patient hypo-, eu-, or hypervolaemic?" and ADH behaviour explains the rest (SIADH = euvolaemic hyponatraemia with concentrated urine despite low serum osm). Diabetes emergencies are the same physiology under stress: insulin deficiency drives lipolysis/ketones (DKA) or pure hyperosmolar dehydration (HHS), and management is just replacing what the missing insulin failed to regulate — fluid first, potassium safety, then insulin. Owning feedback + osmolality means you never memorise lab panels; you reconstruct them. The MCQ then becomes pattern-completion: given three labs, predict the fourth, name the lesion location, and pick the management step that the physiology mandates (e.g., potassium before insulin escalation, fluids before insulin in DKA, hydrocortisone before confirming Addison's).

### High-yield tutorial

## The two-axis engine

For any hormone/electrolyte: **direction (high/low)** + **location (primary gland vs central pituitary)**. Feedback does the rest.

### Thyroid — TSH is the master discriminator
- **High TSH + low T4 = primary hypothyroidism** (Hashimoto's). Treat levothyroxine; recheck TSH at **6–8 weeks**.
- **Low TSH + high T4 = primary hyperthyroidism** (Graves'). **Low/normal TSH + low T4 = central** (pituitary) — always interpret TSH *with* T4.
- **Subclinical**: abnormal TSH, normal free T4. Treat subclinical hypothyroidism if **TSH >10** or symptomatic/pregnant/antibody-positive.
- **Sick euthyroid**: low T3 (± low T4, variable TSH) in acute illness — **don't treat, recheck when well**.
- **Thyroid storm**: fever, tachyarrhythmia, agitation, GI/CNS. Order: **β-blocker (propranolol) → thionamide (PTU in storm) → iodine (≥1h after thionamide) → hydrocortisone**. Iodine before thionamide fuels the fire.
- **Myxoedema coma**: hypothermia, bradycardia, hyponatraemia, ↓GCS. IV **levothyroxine (±T3) + IV hydrocortisone** (cover coexisting adrenal failure first).
- **Amiodarone**: **Type 1 AIT** (iodine-induced, pre-existing nodular gland → carbimazole); **Type 2 AIT** (destructive thyroiditis → steroids). Amiodarone also causes hypothyroidism. **Amiodarone = high iodine load**, distorts TFTs.

### Adrenal — cortisol + ACTH
- **Addisonian crisis**: hypotension refractory to fluids, **hyponatraemia + hyperkalaemia**, hypoglycaemia, pigmentation. **Give hydrocortisone 100mg IV immediately — do NOT wait for cortisol/Synacthen.** Fluids = saline + dextrose.
- **Cushing's**: screen with **overnight dexamethasone suppression / late-night salivary cortisol / 24h urine free cortisol**. Then ACTH: **suppressed = adrenal tumour**; **high = pituitary (Cushing disease) or ectopic**. High-dose dex suppresses pituitary, not ectopic.
- **Phaeochromocytoma**: episodic headache/sweating/palpitations. Diagnose **plasma/urine metanephrines**. **α-block (phenoxybenzamine) BEFORE β-block** — never β-block first (unopposed α = crisis).

### Calcium — PTH is the discriminator
- **High Ca + high/inappropriately-normal PTH = primary hyperparathyroidism** (commonest outpatient cause).
- **High Ca + suppressed PTH = malignancy** (PTHrP, bony mets, myeloma) — commonest inpatient cause.
- **Acute hypercalcaemia management**: **IV normal saline (rehydrate) first → then IV bisphosphonate (zoledronic acid)**. Calcitonin for rapid temporary drop. Avoid loop diuretics unless fluid-overloaded.
- "**Stones, bones, abdominal groans, psychic moans**." ECG: short QT.

### Sodium = a WATER problem → ask volume status
- **Hyponatraemia algorithm**: check serum osm (exclude pseudo/hyperglycaemia), then **volume status**:
  - **Hypovolaemic**: vomiting/diuretics → saline.
  - **Euvolaemic**: **SIADH** (urine osm >100, urine Na >30, low serum osm, euvolaemic, normal TFT/cortisol) → **fluid restrict**; treat cause.
  - **Hypervolaemic**: HF/cirrhosis → restrict + treat cause.
- **Correction limit: ≤8–10 mmol/L per 24h** — faster risks **osmotic demyelination (central pontine myelinolysis)**. Acute symptomatic/seizing → **hypertonic 3% saline** boluses.
- **Hypernatraemia** = water deficit (always hyperosmolar). Usually impaired thirst/access or **diabetes insipidus** (dilute urine). Replace water slowly.

### Potassium — the killer to act on first
- **Hyperkalaemia order**: **1. Calcium gluconate** (membrane stabilisation — ECG changes: peaked T, wide QRS) → **2. Insulin+dextrose / salbutamol** (shift) → **3. Resonium/dialysis** (remove). Stabilise–shift–remove.

### Diabetes emergencies — replace what insulin can't regulate
**DKA** (T1DM, ketones, acidosis): triad = **glucose >11, ketones ≥3 (or ketonuria), pH <7.3 / HCO3 <15**.
- **Order: FLUIDS first (saline) → then fixed-rate insulin 0.1 U/kg/h → potassium**.
- **Potassium rule: if K <3.5, hold insulin and replace K first** (insulin drives K intracellularly → fatal hypokalaemia). Add KCl once K <5.5 and urine output present.
- Continue long-acting basal insulin. Watch for **euglycaemic DKA on SGLT2i** — ketoacidosis with near-normal glucose.

**HHS** (older T2DM): **glucose >30, osmolality >320, no significant ketones/acidosis**, profound dehydration. **Fluids are the mainstay**; insulin lower/later; higher VTE risk → prophylaxis.

### Type 2 diabetes — Australian stepwise
- **Metformin first-line** (unless eGFR <30). Then choose by comorbidity, not just HbA1c:
  - **CVD / heart failure → SGLT2i** (also slows CKD).
  - **CKD (albuminuria) → SGLT2i** (renoprotective).
  - **Obesity / ASCVD → GLP-1RA**.
- **Sick-day rules**: T1DM — **never stop insulin**, check ketones, increase monitoring, maintain carbs/fluids. **Withhold SADMANS drugs** (SGLT2i, ACEi/ARB, diuretics, metformin, NSAIDs) when acutely unwell/dehydrated.
- **Hypoglycaemia**: conscious → **15g fast carbs, recheck 15 min** (rule of 15). Unconscious → **IM glucagon or IV dextrose**. Sulfonylureas cause prolonged hypos → admit.

### Complication screening
- **Retinopathy**: annual fundoscopy/photography. **Nephropathy**: annual **uACR + eGFR**; ACEi/ARB if albuminuria. **Feet**: annual neurovascular exam. **CVD**: statin per absolute risk.

### Highest mark-per-minute principles

- **Interpret TSH only alongside free T4 to localise primary vs central thyroid disease.** — Single most common thyroid MCQ pattern; isolated TSH leads to wrong answers (central hypothyroidism, sick euthyroid).
  - _Exam angle:_ Low T4 with low/normal TSH = pituitary lesion, not Hashimoto's — pick MRI pituitary.
- **In DKA give fluids before insulin and check potassium before/with insulin.** — Insulin shifts K+ intracellularly; giving insulin when K<3.5 can be fatal.
  - _Exam angle:_ K 3.2 in DKA → answer is 'replace potassium / withhold insulin', not 'start insulin'.
- **Sodium disorders are water/osmolality problems solved by volume status.** — Converts a feared topic into a 3-branch algorithm; SIADH is the favourite distractor.
  - _Exam angle:_ Euvolaemic, urine osm >100, urine Na >30, normal cortisol/TSH = SIADH → fluid restrict.
- **Give hydrocortisone before confirming Addisonian crisis.** — Treatment precedes diagnosis in crisis; hyponatraemia+hyperkalaemia+hypotension is the trigger.
  - _Exam angle:_ Shocked, pigmented, K high, Na low, not responding to fluids → IV hydrocortisone now.
- **PTH discriminates hypercalcaemia: high/normal PTH = hyperparathyroidism, suppressed = malignancy.** — Two-lab pattern instantly localises cause and directs workup.
  - _Exam angle:_ Ca high, PTH suppressed, weight loss → seek malignancy (myeloma, PTHrP).
- **Hyperkalaemia is treated stabilise-shift-remove: calcium, then insulin/dextrose, then removal.** — Order is testable and life-saving; calcium does not lower K, it protects the heart.
  - _Exam angle:_ Peaked T waves + K 6.8 → first step IV calcium gluconate.
- **α-blockade must precede β-blockade in phaeochromocytoma.** — Unopposed α-stimulation from premature β-block precipitates hypertensive crisis.
  - _Exam angle:_ Catecholamine excess patient on propranolol crashes → wrong sequencing was the error.
- **In T2DM choose the second agent by cardiorenal comorbidity, not glucose alone.** — Core of modern Australian/ADS guidance; SGLT2i for HF/CKD, GLP-1RA for ASCVD/obesity.
  - _Exam angle:_ T2DM with HFrEF or albuminuria → add SGLT2i regardless of HbA1c.
- **Correct chronic hyponatraemia slowly (≤8–10 mmol/L/24h).** — Overcorrection causes osmotic demyelination; a classic 'what's the complication' stem.
  - _Exam angle:_ Rapid Na rise then quadriparesis/locked-in days later = central pontine myelinolysis.
- **SGLT2i can cause euglycaemic DKA; SADMANS drugs are withheld on sick days.** — Increasingly tested as SGLT2i use expands; normal glucose hides ketoacidosis.
  - _Exam angle:_ Unwell T2DM on empagliflozin, glucose 9, ketones high, acidotic → euglycaemic DKA.

### Must-know numbers (Australian where they differ)

- DKA diagnosis: glucose >11 mmol/L, ketones ≥3 mmol/L (or significant ketonuria), pH <7.3 or HCO3 <15
- DKA insulin: fixed-rate 0.1 units/kg/hr; hold/replace if K <3.5; add KCl when K <5.5
- HHS: glucose >30 mmol/L, serum osmolality >320 mOsm/kg, minimal ketones, pH >7.3
- Hyponatraemia correction limit: ≤8–10 mmol/L per 24 hours (avoid osmotic demyelination)
- SIADH urine markers: urine osmolality >100 mOsm/kg, urine sodium >30 mmol/L, low serum osm
- Hypoglycaemia: BGL <4.0 ('4 is the floor'); rule of 15 — 15g carbs, recheck 15 min
- Subclinical hypothyroidism: treat if TSH >10 mU/L or symptomatic/pregnant/antibody-positive
- Levothyroxine: recheck TSH at 6–8 weeks after dose change
- Metformin: avoid/contraindicated if eGFR <30; review at eGFR 30–45
- HbA1c general target ≤7.0% (53 mmol/mol), individualised; diagnosis ≥6.5% (48 mmol/mol)
- Hyperkalaemia: ECG changes/K >6.0–6.5 → IV calcium gluconate first
- Hypercalcaemia: corrected Ca; severe symptomatic >3.0 mmol/L → saline then zoledronic acid
- Adrenal crisis: hydrocortisone 100mg IV stat then 50mg q6h / 200mg/24h
- Annual diabetes screening: uACR, eGFR, fundoscopy, foot neurovascular exam

### Cross-topic cuts

- Adrenal crisis presents as resistant hypotension + hyponatraemia + hyperkalaemia — overlaps with electrolyte and shock/cardiology stems.
- Hyperkalaemia management bridges nephrology (AKI/CKD, dialysis) and pharmacology (ACEi/ARB, spironolactone, trimethoprim).
- Hyponatraemia workup requires excluding hypothyroidism and adrenal insufficiency before diagnosing SIADH — links endocrine axes to fluids.
- SGLT2i sit at the diabetes–cardiology–nephrology intersection (HFrEF and CKD indications independent of glucose).
- Hypercalcaemia frequently signals malignancy (myeloma, lung) — overlaps haematology/oncology and renal (AKI, nephrocalcinosis).
- Thyroid disease drives AF (hyperthyroid) and pericardial effusion/hyperlipidaemia (hypothyroid) — crosses into cardiology.
- Amiodarone links cardiology (arrhythmia) to thyroid dysfunction (both hyper- and hypothyroidism) and TFT misinterpretation.
- Phaeochromocytoma overlaps with hypertension workup and MEN syndromes (MEN2 → medullary thyroid + parathyroid).
- Diabetic foot and CVD risk connect to vascular surgery, infectious disease (osteomyelitis), and absolute CV risk calculation.
