- Pituitary lesions unify central hypothyroidism, secondary adrenal insufficiency, and diabetes insipidus — one structure, multiple axes.

### Australian-specific (where the UK answer loses you marks)

- Type 2 diabetes pharmacotherapy follows Diabetes Australia / ADS (Australian Diabetes Society) algorithm and the RACGP 'Management of T2DM' handbook, not NICE; cardiorenal-first second-agent selection.
- PBS criteria govern SGLT2i and GLP-1RA subsidy — exam may frame access/eligibility; many GLP-1RA listings restricted and supply-affected.
- Glucose and HbA1c reported in mmol/L and mmol/mol (HbA1c often dual-reported with %); UK Passmedicine uses mg/dL — convert.
- Australian absolute CVD risk assessed via the 2023 Australian CVD risk calculator (AusCVDRisk), incorporating diabetes, not QRISK.
- Sick-day management plans and 'SADMANS' drug withholding are emphasised by NDSS/Diabetes Australia patient resources.
- DKA/HHS managed per local hospital protocols broadly aligned with ADS position statements (fixed-rate insulin, VRIII), similar to JBDS but Australian-badged.
- National Diabetes Services Scheme (NDSS) provides subsidised consumables (strips, CGM) — relevant to ongoing management questions.
- Thyroid and diabetes screening intervals follow RACGP Red Book / Diabetes Australia annual cycle of care (uACR, eGFR, eyes, feet, HbA1c).
- Hypoglycaemia threshold taught as BGL <4.0 mmol/L ('4 is the floor') consistent with Australian/UK practice — confirm SI units in stems.
- Carbimazole is the standard first-line thionamide in Australia (PTU reserved for first-trimester pregnancy and thyroid storm).

> **Independent accuracy audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[high]** Obesity / ASCVD → GLP-1RA (listed as a parallel second-agent choice alongside SGLT2i, by comorbidity). → State that PBS criteria (from June 2024) require trial/failure of, or contraindication/intolerance to, an SGLT2i before a GLP-1 RA is subsidised. Frame SGLT2i as the usual first add-on (CVD/HF/CKD), with GLP-1 RA where SGLT2i is unsuitable or glycaemic/weight targets are unmet, plus where ASCVD/obesity dominate. Source: PBS PBAC PSD July 2024, 'PBS restriction changes to type 2 diabetes mellitus medicines'; ADS Australian T2D Glycaemic Management Algorithm (current update 2026).
> - **[medium]** Correction limit: ≤8–10 mmol/L per 24h. → Use ≤8 mmol/L per 24h as the standard ceiling (some references ≤6), and ≤4–6 mmol/L/24h in high-risk patients. Add the caveat that volume repletion in hypovolaemic hyponatraemia can trigger a brisk water diuresis and overcorrection — monitor urine output/Na closely and consider desmopressin to re-lower if overshooting. Source: eTG / European hyponatraemia guidance; 2024 ODS meta-analyses.
> - **[low]** Thyroid storm thionamide: 'PTU in storm'. → State PTU is preferred in thyroid storm (blocks peripheral conversion) but carbimazole is the routine Australian first-line thionamide for non-crisis Graves'; either is acceptable in storm. Source: eTG Endocrinology; ATA 2016.
> - **[low]** HbA1c general target ≤7.0% (53 mmol/mol). → Keep ≤53 mmol/mol (7.0%) as the general default but explicitly note individualised targets: ≤6.5% where achievable safely (lifestyle/metformin), and ≤8.0% in frailty/recurrent hypoglycaemia/limited life expectancy. Source: ADS/RACGP General Practice Management of T2D.
> - **[low]** DKA insulin: 'continue long-acting basal insulin' and fixed-rate 0.1 U/kg/h. → Add that an initial IV insulin bolus is not routinely recommended, and that Australian centres follow local protocols broadly aligned with ADS position statements (fixed-rate IV insulin, continue basal). Source: WACHS Adult DKA Guideline; ADS commentary MJA 2024.
> - **[low]** Metformin: 'avoid/contraindicated if eGFR <30; review at eGFR 30–45.' → No change required — matches RACGP General Practice Management of T2D. (eGFR <30 contraindicated; 30–45 reduce dose, do not initiate.)
> 
> _Gaps flagged & folded in:_ PBS access/subsidy rules as a distinct exam axis: GLP-1 RA requires prior SGLT2i failure/contraindication (June 2024 change); SGLT2i and GLP-1 RA each have specific PBS authority criteria. Australian exams test eligibility, not just efficacy.; Diabetes in pregnancy / gestational diabetes: Australian diagnostic OGTT thresholds (fasting ≥5.1, 1h ≥10.0, 2h ≥8.5 mmol/L per ADIPS), metformin/insulin use, and the postpartum OGTT at 6–12 weeks — high-yield and Australia-specific (ADIPS criteria).; Annual Cycle of Care / NDSS: the structured Diabetes Australia annual cycle of care (HbA1c, BP, lipids, uACR, eGFR, eyes, feet, weight, smoking, self-care/mental health review) and NDSS-subsidised consumables and CGM eligibility — frequently tested in Australian GP-style stems.; Subsidised CGM expansion: NDSS fully subsidised CGM for all people with type 1 diabetes (and defined groups) — relevant to ongoing T1DM management questions.; Driving and diabetes: Austroads 'Assessing Fitness to Drive' standards for hypoglycaemia/insulin and commercial vs private licences — a recurring Australian medico-legal exam point absent from the tutorial.; Hypothyroidism in pregnancy: trimester-specific TSH targets and need to increase levothyroxine ~30% on confirming pregnancy — high-yield, links to the central-vs-primary thyroid framework.; Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander health: earlier and more frequent diabetes/CKD screening, higher absolute CVD risk (and AusCVDRisk reclassification considerations) — an Australian exam theme not represented.; Primary aldosteronism (Conn's): increasingly emphasised as an under-diagnosed secondary hypertension cause; aldosterone:renin ratio screening — fits the high/low + primary/central engine and is commonly tested.; CVD risk tool specificity: use the 2023 Australian CVD risk calculator (AusCVDRisk / cvdcheck.org.au), NOT QRISK/Framingham; people with diabetes are assessed with diabetes-specific variables, and many are automatically high-risk — statin/BP threshold at ≥10% 5-year risk. The tutorial mentions AusCVDRisk in specifics but the tutorial body still says only 'statin per absolute risk' without the tool or threshold.; Adrenal crisis sick-day/stress dosing rules for known Addison's (double/triple oral hydrocortisone, IM hydrocortisone for vomiting) and steroid emergency card — a practical Australian management point beyond the acute IV hydrocortisone bolus.

### Applied MCQs

**Q1.** A 58-year-old man in Perth presents to ED unwell for 3 days with vomiting and lethargy. He has type 2 diabetes managed with metformin and empagliflozin. Obs: HR 108, BP 104/64, RR 26. Venous gas: pH 7.21, HCO3 12 mmol/L, glucose 9.4 mmol/L, blood ketones 4.8 mmol/L. Which single statement best explains his presentation?

   - A. He cannot have DKA because his glucose is below 11 mmol/L
   - B. This is euglycaemic DKA precipitated by the SGLT2 inhibitor; treat with fluids, IV insulin and dextrose, and withhold the SGLT2i
   - C. This is hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state and the priority is a stat insulin bolus
   - D. The acidosis is most likely lactic acidosis from metformin and the SGLT2i is irrelevant
   - E. He has a high anion-gap acidosis from starvation alone and needs only oral carbohydrate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. This is euglycaemic DKA precipitated by the SGLT2 inhibitor; treat with fluids, IV insulin and dextrose, and withhold the SGLT2i**

Ketones 4.8 with pH 7.21 and HCO3 12 is ketoacidosis. SGLT2 inhibitors cause euglycaemic DKA — glucose can be near-normal (<11) because urinary glucose loss masks the hyperglycaemia, but lipolysis and ketogenesis proceed. Management is fluids + fixed-rate IV insulin WITH dextrose co-infusion (because glucose is already low) and withholding the SGLT2i. A is the classic trap — the DKA diagnostic glucose threshold does not apply when an SGLT2i is on board. C is wrong: HHS requires glucose >30, osmolality >320 and minimal ketones/no acidosis. D is wrong: metformin lactic acidosis would not produce ketones 4.8, and the SGLT2i is the actual driver. E underplays a life-threatening acidosis.

_Teaching point:_ In an unwell diabetic on an SGLT2 inhibitor, check ketones regardless of glucose — euglycaemic DKA is defined by ketoacidosis with normal/near-normal glucose, and is a SADMANS sick-day drug to withhold.
</details>

**Q2.** A 44-year-old woman is admitted after a road accident. Routine bloods show corrected calcium 3.4 mmol/L. She reports months of fatigue, constipation and back pain, with 6 kg unintentional weight loss. PTH is 0.4 pmol/L (low). Renal function is normal and she is alert. After IV normal saline rehydration, what is the most appropriate next pharmacological step, and what does the PTH tell you?

   - A. PTH is suppressed → primary hyperparathyroidism; arrange parathyroidectomy
   - B. PTH is suppressed → likely malignancy; give IV zoledronic acid and investigate for myeloma/solid tumour
   - C. PTH is high → malignancy; start cinacalcet
   - D. Give IV calcium gluconate to stabilise the myocardium first
   - E. Start a loop diuretic immediately as first-line to lower calcium

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. PTH is suppressed → likely malignancy; give IV zoledronic acid and investigate for myeloma/solid tumour**

Hypercalcaemia with a SUPPRESSED PTH means the parathyroid is appropriately switched off — the calcium is coming from a non-PTH source, most often malignancy (PTHrP, bony mets, myeloma). The weight loss and back pain reinforce this. Management is saline rehydration FIRST, then a bisphosphonate (zoledronic acid). A is internally contradictory — primary hyperparathyroidism has HIGH/inappropriately-normal PTH, not suppressed. C misreads the PTH (it is low, not high) and cinacalcet is for hyperparathyroidism. D confuses hypercalcaemia with hyperkalaemia — calcium gluconate is for cardiac membrane stabilisation in hyperkalaemia, not for high calcium. E is wrong: loop diuretics are not first-line and are reserved for fluid overload after rehydration.

_Teaching point:_ PTH is the single discriminator in hypercalcaemia: high/inappropriately-normal = primary hyperparathyroidism (outpatient), suppressed = malignancy (inpatient). Always rehydrate with saline before giving a bisphosphonate.
</details>

**Q3.** A 71-year-old woman with cirrhosis is found to have serum sodium 116 mmol/L. She is clinically euvolaemic. Serum osmolality 248 mOsm/kg, urine osmolality 420 mOsm/kg, urine sodium 52 mmol/L. TSH and 9am cortisol are normal. She is asymptomatic. What is the most appropriate management?

   - A. 3% hypertonic saline boluses to rapidly normalise the sodium
   - B. Diagnose SIADH; fluid restrict and treat the underlying cause, correcting sodium by no more than ~8 mmol/L in 24 hours
   - C. Aggressive 0.9% saline infusion to raise sodium quickly
   - D. Demeclocycline as immediate first-line
   - E. No action needed as the sodium is within an acceptable range for cirrhosis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Diagnose SIADH; fluid restrict and treat the underlying cause, correcting sodium by no more than ~8 mmol/L in 24 hours**

Euvolaemic hyponatraemia + low serum osm + urine osm >100 + urine Na >30 + normal TFT/cortisol = SIADH. She is asymptomatic, so no hypertonic saline is indicated — treatment is fluid restriction and treating the cause. Crucially, chronic hyponatraemia must be corrected slowly: the standard ceiling is ≤8 mmol/L per 24h (and ≤4–6 in high-risk patients such as this one with liver disease) to avoid osmotic demyelination. A is wrong: 3% saline is reserved for acute symptomatic/seizing patients, and rapid correction here risks central pontine myelinolysis. C is wrong: giving isotonic saline in SIADH can paradoxically worsen hyponatraemia and overcorrect unpredictably. D is not first-line. E is dangerously complacent at 116.

_Teaching point:_ Before diagnosing SIADH, exclude hypothyroidism and adrenal insufficiency. Correct chronic hyponatraemia by ≤8 mmol/L/24h (≤4–6 if high-risk: liver disease, alcohol, malnutrition, very low Na).
</details>

**Q4.** A 29-year-old woman with type 1 diabetes presents with vomiting and abdominal pain. Glucose 28 mmol/L, ketones 5.2 mmol/L, pH 7.18, HCO3 10. Initial potassium is 3.2 mmol/L. She is passing urine. After commencing IV 0.9% saline, what is the most appropriate next step?

   - A. Start fixed-rate IV insulin 0.1 U/kg/hr immediately
   - B. Give an IV insulin bolus then commence the fixed-rate infusion
   - C. Withhold insulin and replace potassium first, then start insulin once K is rising/≥3.5
   - D. Give IV sodium bicarbonate to correct the acidosis
   - E. Add 40 mmol KCl to the fluids and start insulin simultaneously regardless of the potassium level

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Withhold insulin and replace potassium first, then start insulin once K is rising/≥3.5**

In DKA total-body potassium is depleted even when serum K looks normal; here serum K is already LOW at 3.2. Insulin drives K intracellularly and can precipitate fatal hypokalaemia/arrhythmia. The rule: if K <3.5, hold insulin and replace potassium first. A is dangerous — starting insulin at K 3.2 can kill. B is wrong on two counts: a routine insulin bolus is no longer recommended (increases hypo/hypokalaemia risk), and insulin should not start yet anyway. D — bicarbonate is not routinely given in DKA. E starts insulin too early; KCl is added once K is <5.5 with urine output, but insulin still waits until K ≥3.5.

_Teaching point:_ In DKA the order is fluids → check/replace potassium → insulin. If K <3.5, potassium replacement precedes insulin. No routine insulin bolus.
</details>

**Q5.** A 62-year-old man with type 2 diabetes (HbA1c 8.2% / 66 mmol/mol on maximal metformin) has heart failure with reduced ejection fraction and an eGFR of 58 with a uACR of 12 mg/mmol (albuminuria). His CVD risk is high. In the Australian setting, which add-on agent is the most appropriate next choice?

   - A. A GLP-1 receptor agonist, because obesity and ASCVD favour it as first add-on
   - B. An SGLT2 inhibitor, for its heart-failure and renoprotective benefits and as the PBS-default add-on
   - C. A sulfonylurea, as the cheapest way to lower HbA1c
   - D. Basal insulin, since HbA1c is above target
   - E. A DPP-4 inhibitor, as it is weight-neutral and well tolerated

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. An SGLT2 inhibitor, for its heart-failure and renoprotective benefits and as the PBS-default add-on**

This patient has HFrEF plus albuminuric CKD — the textbook indication for an SGLT2 inhibitor, which reduces HF hospitalisation and slows CKD progression independent of glucose. Critically for Australian exams, since June 2024 PBS rules require trial/failure of, or contraindication/intolerance to, an SGLT2i BEFORE a GLP-1 RA is subsidised, so an SGLT2i is the usual first add-on. A is the seductive trap — clinically a GLP-1 RA suits obesity/ASCVD, but in Australia you generally cannot start it ahead of an SGLT2i on the PBS. C and E ignore the cardiorenal indication and add nothing for HF/CKD. D is premature and risks hypoglycaemia/weight gain before exploiting the cardiorenal agent.

_Teaching point:_ In Australian T2DM, pick the second agent by cardiorenal comorbidity AND PBS access: SGLT2i is the default add-on for HF/CKD/CVD; GLP-1 RA generally requires prior SGLT2i failure/contraindication for subsidy (June 2024 PBS rule).
</details>

**Q6.** A 38-year-old man with episodic headache, palpitations and drenching sweats has a BP of 200/118 during an episode. Plasma metanephrines are markedly elevated and CT shows a 4 cm adrenal mass. The intern has already started him on propranolol for the tachycardia and his BP has worsened. What is the key management principle that was breached?

   - A. Calcium-channel blockade should always precede surgery
   - B. Alpha-blockade (e.g. phenoxybenzamine) must be established BEFORE any beta-blockade
   - C. Beta-blockade should have been combined with a thionamide
   - D. He should have had IV hydrocortisone before any antihypertensive
   - E. Surgery should have been performed emergently before any medical blockade

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Alpha-blockade (e.g. phenoxybenzamine) must be established BEFORE any beta-blockade**

This is a phaeochromocytoma. Beta-blockade given before alpha-blockade leaves alpha-mediated vasoconstriction unopposed, precipitating a hypertensive crisis — exactly what happened. The rule is alpha-block first (phenoxybenzamine), then beta-block for tachycardia once alpha-blockade is established. A is wrong: alpha-blockade, not calcium-channel blockade, is the foundational step. C confuses phaeo with thyroid storm. D is irrelevant — there is no adrenal insufficiency here. E is dangerous: operating on an unblocked phaeo risks fatal intraoperative catecholamine surge.

_Teaching point:_ Phaeochromocytoma: alpha before beta, always. Premature beta-blockade causes unopposed alpha vasoconstriction and hypertensive crisis.
</details>

**Q7.** A 26-year-old woman at 26 weeks gestation has a 75 g OGTT performed in Melbourne. Results: fasting 5.3 mmol/L, 1-hour 9.2 mmol/L, 2-hour 8.7 mmol/L. Using Australian (ADIPS) criteria, what is the correct interpretation and initial step?

   - A. Normal OGTT — no further action
   - B. Gestational diabetes (fasting ≥5.1 and 2-hour ≥8.5 met); commence diet and lifestyle, add metformin/insulin if targets unmet
   - C. Overt pre-existing type 2 diabetes; commence insulin immediately and stop all oral agents
   - D. Impaired glucose tolerance only; repeat the OGTT at 36 weeks
   - E. Gestational diabetes but treatment is deferred until after delivery

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Gestational diabetes (fasting ≥5.1 and 2-hour ≥8.5 met); commence diet and lifestyle, add metformin/insulin if targets unmet**

ADIPS diagnostic thresholds for GDM on a 75 g OGTT are fasting ≥5.1, 1-hour ≥10.0, 2-hour ≥8.5 mmol/L — any one value meeting threshold is diagnostic. Here fasting (5.3) and 2-hour (8.7) both exceed thresholds, so this is GDM. First-line is medical nutrition therapy and lifestyle, escalating to metformin and/or insulin if glycaemic targets are not met. A misreads the thresholds. C is wrong — the values are diagnostic of GDM, not overt diabetes, and insulin is not automatically first-line. D ignores that diagnostic criteria are already met. E is dangerous — GDM needs active management during pregnancy. (Remember the postpartum OGTT at 6–12 weeks.)

_Teaching point:_ ADIPS GDM thresholds: fasting ≥5.1, 1h ≥10.0, 2h ≥8.5 mmol/L — any single abnormal value diagnoses GDM. Manage with lifestyle first, then metformin/insulin; arrange a postpartum OGTT at 6–12 weeks.
</details>

**Q8.** A 55-year-old woman with known Addison's disease on hydrocortisone and fludrocortisone develops gastroenteritis with vomiting and cannot keep oral medication down. She rings the diabetes/endocrine helpline. Which advice is most appropriate?

   - A. Continue her usual oral hydrocortisone dose and push oral fluids
   - B. Double or triple her oral hydrocortisone; if vomiting, she needs IM hydrocortisone and urgent review
   - C. Stop hydrocortisone temporarily until the vomiting settles to avoid overdose
   - D. Switch to fludrocortisone only until she can tolerate food
   - E. Take an extra dose of fludrocortisone and continue normal hydrocortisone

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Double or triple her oral hydrocortisone; if vomiting, she needs IM hydrocortisone and urgent review**

Sick-day rules for established adrenal insufficiency: during intercurrent illness, oral glucocorticoid (hydrocortisone) is doubled or tripled to mimic the physiological stress response. If the patient is vomiting and cannot absorb oral steroid, she must receive IM/IV hydrocortisone and seek urgent care, as she is at risk of adrenal crisis. A under-doses for stress. C is potentially lethal — stopping steroid during illness precipitates crisis. D and E misunderstand the axis: fludrocortisone is mineralocorticoid replacement; the stress response requires increased glucocorticoid (hydrocortisone), not extra fludrocortisone.

_Teaching point:_ Addison's sick-day rules: stress = double/triple oral hydrocortisone; vomiting/unable to absorb = IM hydrocortisone + urgent review. Never stop steroids when unwell. (Patients carry a steroid emergency card.)
</details>

### Schema recall — "What is your schema for X?"

**What is your schema for interpreting any thyroid or adrenal axis abnormality (primary vs central)?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Step 1: Identify the AXIS (HPT thyroid, HPA adrenal). Step 2: Determine DIRECTION of the end-organ hormone — is T4/cortisol HIGH or LOW? Step 3: Read the PITUITARY hormone (TSH / ACTH) ALONGSIDE the end-organ hormone, never alone. Step 4: Apply feedback logic. Low T4 + HIGH TSH = primary gland failure (Hashimoto's); low T4 + LOW/normal TSH = central (pituitary) — image the pituitary. Low cortisol + HIGH ACTH = primary (Addison's, with pigmentation); low cortisol + LOW ACTH = secondary/pituitary. Step 5: Cross-check for combined deficits (a pituitary lesion knocks out multiple axes at once).

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Students interpret TSH or ACTH in isolation and miss CENTRAL disease. The classic attack: low T4 with a low/normal (not high) TSH — they reflexively call it Hashimoto's and prescribe levothyroxine instead of imaging the pituitary. The discriminator is always reading the pituitary hormone in the SAME breath as the end-organ hormone.
</details>

**What is your schema for the approach to hyponatraemia?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Step 1: Confirm it's true hypotonic hyponatraemia — check SERUM OSMOLALITY (exclude pseudohyponatraemia and hyperglycaemia/mannitol = high osm). Step 2: Assess VOLUME STATUS clinically — hypovolaemic, euvolaemic, or hypervolaemic. Step 3: Branch. HYPOVOLAEMIC (vomiting, diuretics, addisonian): urine Na helps localise → give 0.9% saline. EUVOLAEMIC: think SIADH (urine osm >100, urine Na >30, low serum osm) — but FIRST exclude hypothyroidism and adrenal insufficiency → fluid restrict. HYPERVOLAEMIC (HF, cirrhosis, nephrotic): fluid restrict + treat cause. Step 4: Decide acuity/symptoms — seizing/comatose = 3% hypertonic saline boluses; otherwise correct slowly. Step 5: Cap correction ≤8 mmol/L/24h (≤4–6 if high-risk).

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Two failure points. (1) Diagnosing SIADH without excluding cortisol and TSH abnormalities — examiners plant a normal-looking euvolaemic patient who actually has hypothyroidism or Addison's. (2) Overcorrection: in hypovolaemic hyponatraemia, once volume is repleted ADH switches off, triggering a brisk water diuresis and overshoot — monitor urine output/Na and consider desmopressin to re-lower.
</details>

**What is your schema for managing DKA?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Step 1: Confirm diagnosis — glucose >11 (or any glucose if on SGLT2i), ketones ≥3, pH <7.3 or HCO3 <15. Step 2: FLUIDS first — IV 0.9% saline to restore circulating volume. Step 3: POTASSIUM check — if K <3.5, withhold insulin and replace K first; add KCl to fluids once K <5.5 with urine output. Step 4: INSULIN — fixed-rate IV infusion 0.1 U/kg/hr, NO routine bolus; continue the patient's long-acting basal insulin. Step 5: Add DEXTROSE when glucose falls (or from the outset in euglycaemic DKA) to allow continued insulin while clearing ketones. Step 6: Monitor ketones/pH/K, find and treat the precipitant.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The lethal error is starting insulin before checking/replacing potassium when K is low — insulin drives K intracellularly and causes fatal hypokalaemia. Examiners give a normal-or-low K (e.g. 3.2) to see if you reach for insulin. Second trap: giving an insulin bolus (no longer recommended). Third: missing euglycaemic DKA on an SGLT2i because glucose is <11.
</details>

**What is your schema for hyperkalaemia management?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Step 1: Confirm it's real (exclude haemolysed sample) and get an ECG. Step 2: STABILISE the myocardium — IV calcium gluconate if ECG changes (peaked T, widened QRS) or K >6.0–6.5; calcium does NOT lower potassium, it protects the heart. Step 3: SHIFT potassium intracellularly — insulin + dextrose (and/or nebulised salbutamol). Step 4: REMOVE potassium from the body — resonium / loop diuretics if volume-replete / dialysis if refractory or in renal failure. Step 5: Stop the cause — withhold ACEi/ARB, spironolactone, trimethoprim; treat AKI.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Candidates pick insulin/dextrose as the FIRST step because it 'lowers' potassium, forgetting that membrane stabilisation with calcium comes first when there are ECG changes. The other attack: thinking calcium gluconate reduces the serum K (it does not) — it only buys time. Remember: stabilise → shift → remove.
</details>

**What is your schema for working up hypercalcaemia?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Step 1: Confirm with CORRECTED calcium (adjust for albumin). Step 2: Measure PTH — the single discriminator. Step 3: Branch. HIGH or inappropriately-normal PTH = primary hyperparathyroidism (commonest outpatient cause; consider FHH if urine calcium low). SUPPRESSED PTH = non-PTH source → malignancy (PTHrP, bony mets, myeloma), granulomatous disease, vitamin D excess. Step 4: If suppressed PTH, hunt the malignancy (myeloma screen, imaging). Step 5: Manage acutely — IV 0.9% saline to rehydrate FIRST, then IV bisphosphonate (zoledronic acid); calcitonin for rapid temporary effect; avoid loop diuretics unless fluid-overloaded.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Two failure points: (1) ordering the bisphosphonate before rehydrating with saline; (2) misreading PTH — a suppressed PTH with hypercalcaemia is appropriate physiology pointing AWAY from the parathyroid toward malignancy, but students wrongly call it hyperparathyroidism. The discriminator is whether PTH is appropriately suppressed (malignancy) or inappropriately high/normal (primary HPT).
</details>

**What is your schema for choosing second-line therapy in Australian type 2 diabetes?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Step 1: Confirm metformin is first-line and tolerated (avoid if eGFR <30; reduce dose 30–45). Step 2: Choose the SECOND agent by CARDIORENAL COMORBIDITY, not HbA1c alone. HF / established CVD / CKD with albuminuria → SGLT2 inhibitor (cardio- and renoprotective). Obesity / ASCVD where weight loss dominates → consider GLP-1 RA. Step 3: Apply the PBS ACCESS filter — since June 2024 a GLP-1 RA is subsidised only after trial/failure of, or contraindication/intolerance to, an SGLT2i; so SGLT2i is the default add-on. Step 4: Individualise the HbA1c target — general ≤53 mmol/mol (7.0%), tighter ≤6.5% if safely achievable, relaxed ≤8.0% in frailty/hypo-prone/limited life expectancy. Step 5: Bundle the annual cycle of care and CVD risk (AusCVDRisk, statin/BP if ≥10% 5-year risk).

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The exam trap is offering GLP-1 RA as the 'obesity/ASCVD' answer when an SGLT2i has not been tried — clinically reasonable but fails the PBS access rule. Students also quote a flat 7.0% target without individualisation tiers, and forget that SGLT2i benefits are independent of glucose.
</details>

**What is your schema for the crashing, shocked patient you suspect is in adrenal crisis?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Step 1: Recognise the pattern — hypotension REFRACTORY to fluids, hyponatraemia + HYPERkalaemia, hypoglycaemia, pigmentation, often a precipitating illness. Step 2: TREAT BEFORE CONFIRMING — give IV hydrocortisone 100 mg stat immediately; do NOT wait for cortisol/Synacthen results. Step 3: Resuscitate — IV 0.9% saline + dextrose (for hypoglycaemia). Step 4: Continue hydrocortisone 50 mg q6h (≈200 mg/24h). Step 5: Identify and treat the precipitant. Step 6: Once stable, confirm the diagnosis (random/Synacthen-stimulated cortisol, ACTH) and arrange education + steroid emergency card + sick-day plan.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The fatal hesitation is delaying hydrocortisone to 'confirm' the diagnosis with a cortisol or Synacthen test — in crisis, treatment precedes diagnosis. The trigger pattern examiners use is the combination hyponatraemia + hyperkalaemia + fluid-refractory hypotension.
</details>

### Distractor drills — "What's the deadliest distractor for X?"

**In an unwell diabetic on an SGLT2 inhibitor with high ketones and acidosis, what is the deadliest distractor?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ 'Glucose is only 9 mmol/L, so this cannot be DKA — exclude it.'
- _Why it tempts you:_ The DKA diagnostic triad is drilled as glucose >11, so a normal/low glucose feels reassuring and lets you discard the most dangerous diagnosis. The numbers superficially 'don't fit' DKA.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ SGLT2 inhibitors cause EUGLYCAEMIC DKA — urinary glucose loss keeps serum glucose near-normal while lipolysis and ketogenesis run unchecked. The acidosis and ketones are diagnostic regardless of glucose; missing it is fatal.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Ketones + acidosis define the DKA, NOT glucose. If a patient is on an SGLT2i and acidotic with raised ketones, it is euglycaemic DKA until proven otherwise — withhold the SGLT2i and treat with fluids, insulin and dextrose.**

**When hypercalcaemia presents with a suppressed PTH, what is the most seductive wrong answer?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ 'High calcium → primary hyperparathyroidism → refer for parathyroidectomy.'
- _Why it tempts you:_ Primary hyperparathyroidism is the commonest cause of hypercalcaemia in the community, so it is the reflex association with any high calcium, and surgery feels like the definitive answer.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Primary hyperparathyroidism has HIGH or inappropriately-normal PTH. A SUPPRESSED PTH means the parathyroid is correctly switched off and the calcium is coming from elsewhere — usually malignancy (PTHrP, myeloma, bony mets). Operating on the parathyroid is the wrong organ entirely.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **The PTH level. Suppressed PTH = malignancy workup; high/inappropriately-normal PTH = primary hyperparathyroidism. Read the PTH before naming the cause.**

**In a euvolaemic hyponatraemic patient, what is the favourite trap distractor?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ 'Euvolaemic + concentrated urine = SIADH → fluid restrict' (without checking the other axes).
- _Why it tempts you:_ SIADH is the poster-child of euvolaemic hyponatraemia and the urine markers (urine osm >100, urine Na >30) fit perfectly, so candidates lock on immediately.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ SIADH is a diagnosis of EXCLUSION — hypothyroidism and adrenal insufficiency (secondary hypoadrenalism) produce an identical euvolaemic hyponatraemic picture with concentrated urine. Calling SIADH without normal TSH and cortisol misses a treatable, potentially lethal endocrine cause.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Normal TFTs AND normal cortisol must be documented before SIADH is diagnosed. If either is abnormal, that is your answer, not SIADH.**

**In DKA with a low-normal potassium, what is the lethal distractor?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ 'Patient is acidotic and hyperglycaemic — start the insulin infusion now.'
- _Why it tempts you:_ Insulin is THE definitive treatment for DKA and feels like the urgent priority; delaying it seems like under-treating.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Insulin shifts potassium intracellularly. With a serum K already low (e.g. 3.2) and total-body potassium depleted, insulin precipitates severe hypokalaemia and fatal arrhythmia. Fluids and potassium replacement must come first.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Check the potassium before insulin: if K <3.5, withhold insulin and replace potassium first. Fluids → potassium → insulin, always in that order.**

**In a phaeochromocytoma patient who is tachycardic and hypertensive, what is the catastrophic distractor?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ 'Start a beta-blocker (e.g. propranolol) to control the heart rate and blood pressure.'
- _Why it tempts you:_ Beta-blockade is the intuitive answer for tachycardia and hypertension and is correct in most hypertensive patients, so it feels safe.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ In catecholamine excess, beta-blockade removes beta-mediated vasodilation and leaves alpha-mediated vasoconstriction UNOPPOSED, precipitating a hypertensive crisis. The patient gets worse, not better.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Alpha-blockade (phenoxybenzamine) must be established BEFORE any beta-blocker in phaeochromocytoma. Beta first = unopposed alpha = crisis.**

**In hyperkalaemia with ECG changes, what is the common distractor for the first step?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ 'Give insulin and dextrose first — it lowers the potassium.'
- _Why it tempts you:_ Insulin/dextrose genuinely reduces serum potassium and is a core treatment, so it feels like the most direct fix for a high number.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ It does not protect the heart in the minutes that matter. With ECG changes (peaked T waves, wide QRS), the immediate threat is a malignant arrhythmia — you must stabilise the myocardium FIRST with IV calcium gluconate. Calcium doesn't lower K but it buys survival time.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **ECG changes (or K >6.0–6.5) → IV calcium gluconate FIRST. Order is stabilise (calcium) → shift (insulin/salbutamol) → remove (resonium/dialysis).**

**In a patient with low free T4, what is the seductive distractor that mislocates the lesion?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ 'Low T4 → hypothyroid → it's Hashimoto's → start levothyroxine and recheck TSH in 6–8 weeks.'
- _Why it tempts you:_ Primary hypothyroidism is far commoner than central, and 'low T4 = give thyroxine' is a heavily drilled reflex, so candidates skip past the TSH.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ If the TSH is LOW or inappropriately normal alongside the low T4, this is CENTRAL (pituitary/hypothalamic) hypothyroidism, not Hashimoto's. The pituitary needs imaging, other axes (especially cortisol) must be checked, and giving thyroxine before cortisol in coexisting adrenal failure can precipitate crisis.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Read TSH WITH T4. Low T4 + HIGH TSH = primary (treat thyroxine). Low T4 + LOW/normal TSH = central → MRI pituitary and assess the rest of the axis.**

### High-end review pearls

- Aunt-minnie: shocked, pigmented patient with hyponatraemia + HYPERkalaemia + fluid-refractory hypotension = Addisonian crisis. Give IV hydrocortisone 100 mg now; do not wait for cortisol/Synacthen.
- If you see hyponatraemia + hyperkalaemia together, think ADRENAL (low aldosterone) before anything else — it is the electrolyte fingerprint of mineralocorticoid deficiency.
- Always interpret TSH alongside free T4. Isolated TSH is the single biggest source of wrong thyroid answers (central hypothyroidism and sick euthyroid both fool you).
- If you see an acidotic, ketotic diabetic with a near-normal glucose, think SGLT2 inhibitor → euglycaemic DKA. Ketones and pH make the diagnosis, not glucose.
- PTH is the discriminator in hypercalcaemia: suppressed PTH points to malignancy (inpatient), high/normal PTH to primary hyperparathyroidism (outpatient). Never name the cause before reading the PTH.
- Order rules that win marks: fluids before insulin (DKA); calcium before insulin (hyperkalaemia); saline before bisphosphonate (hypercalcaemia); alpha before beta (phaeo); hydrocortisone before confirming (Addison); potassium before insulin if K <3.5 (DKA); iodine at least 1 h AFTER thionamide (thyroid storm).
- Australian PBS trap (June 2024): a GLP-1 RA is subsidised only after SGLT2i trial/failure or contraindication/intolerance — so SGLT2i is the default add-on even when obesity/ASCVD would clinically favour a GLP-1 RA. Exams test access, not just efficacy.
- Correct chronic hyponatraemia by ≤8 mmol/L per 24h (≤4–6 in high-risk: low Na <115, alcohol, malnutrition, liver disease, hypokalaemia). Overshoot → osmotic demyelination (locked-in/quadriparesis days later).
- Overcorrection trap: in hypovolaemic hyponatraemia, restoring volume switches OFF ADH → brisk water diuresis → unintended rapid rise in sodium. Watch urine output; consider desmopressin to re-lower if overshooting.
- HHS vs DKA one-liner: HHS = older T2DM, glucose >30, osm >320, minimal ketones, no acidosis, profound dehydration → fluids are the mainstay, insulin lower/later, give VTE prophylaxis. DKA = ketones + acidosis → fluids then fixed-rate insulin, no routine bolus.
- Thyroid storm thionamide nuance: PTU is preferred IN STORM because it also blocks peripheral T4→T3 conversion, but carbimazole is the routine Australian first-line for non-crisis Graves'. PTU also for first-trimester pregnancy.
- Amiodarone wrecks the thyroid both ways: Type 1 AIT (iodine-induced, nodular gland → carbimazole) vs Type 2 AIT (destructive thyroiditis → steroids); it also causes hypothyroidism and distorts TFTs via its iodine load.
- Hypothyroidism in pregnancy: increase levothyroxine by ~30% as soon as pregnancy is confirmed, and aim for trimester-specific TSH targets — a high-yield, frequently missed point.
- ADIPS GDM thresholds on 75 g OGTT: fasting ≥5.1, 1h ≥10.0, 2h ≥8.5 mmol/L (any one diagnostic). Always arrange the postpartum OGTT at 6–12 weeks.
- Use the Australian CVD risk calculator (AusCVDRisk / cvdcheck.org.au), NOT QRISK/Framingham. People with diabetes are assessed with diabetes-specific variables and many are automatically high-risk; treat at ≥10% 5-year risk.
- Conn's (primary aldosteronism) is the under-diagnosed secondary hypertension: think of it with hypertension + hypokalaemia (or resistant hypertension) and screen with the aldosterone:renin ratio.
- MEN links to never forget: MEN1 = parathyroid + pancreas + pituitary; MEN2 = medullary thyroid + phaeochromocytoma + parathyroid. A phaeo in a young patient should prompt a hunt for the syndrome.
- Sulfonylurea hypoglycaemia is PROLONGED and recurrent — admit these patients even after they wake up; a one-off dextrose bolus is not enough.
- Sick-day SADMANS drugs to withhold when acutely unwell/dehydrated: Sulfonylureas/SGLT2i, ACEi/ARB, Diuretics, Metformin, ARBs, NSAIDs, SGLT2i. In T1DM never stop insulin — check ketones and increase monitoring instead.
- Addison's sick-day stress dosing: double/triple oral hydrocortisone for illness; IM hydrocortisone if vomiting/unable to absorb; carry a steroid emergency card. Stopping steroids when unwell precipitates crisis.

> **Independent item audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[high]** MCQ7 (GDM / 26-week OGTT) and teaching point, plus the highEndExtras line on ADIPS thresholds, and schemaRecall implicit: The MCQ uses the OLD 2014 ADIPS/IADPSG thresholds (fasting >=5.1, 1h >=10.0, 2h >=8.5). The ADIPS 2025 consensus, published in the MJA on 23 June 2025 (BEFORE the stated exam date of 2026-06-28), RAISED the thresholds to fasting >=5.3, 1h >=10.6, 2h >=9.0 mmol/L (intentionally, to reduce over-diagnosis). Under the NEW criteria the worked case (fasting 5.3, 1h 9.2, 2h 8.7) still meets fasting (5.3 = threshold) but NO LONGER meets the 1h or 2h criteria, so the explanation's reasoning ('2-hour 8.7 exceeds threshold') is now WRONG, and the case is borderline (diagnostic on fasting alone). This is the single most dated item for a 2026 Australian exam. → Update to ADIPS 2025 thresholds (fasting >=5.3, 1h >=10.6, 2h >=9.0). Either (a) rewrite the stem values so the diagnosis is unambiguous under the new criteria (e.g. fasting 5.5 or 2h 9.4), or (b) keep the case but correct the explanation to state diagnosis rests on the fasting value (5.3) alone and that 8.7 is now BELOW the 2h threshold. Acknowledge a transitional period exists (some labs still report old criteria) but the exam-correct current standard is ADIPS 2025.
> - **[low]** MCQ1 (euglycaemic DKA) stem and label: The stem labels glucose 9.4 mmol/L as the euglycaemic case, but 9.4 is essentially a normal glucose and the answer/explanation correctly call it euglycaemic DKA — fine clinically. However option A says 'glucose below 11' and the explanation says glucose 'can be near-normal (<11)'. The defining feature is acidosis+ketones irrespective of glucose; the <11 framing is acceptable but slightly reinforces a numeric anchor. Minor: HHS distractor C states 'glucose >30' — Australian/most criteria use >30 for HHS but the precise ketone/osmolality framing in the explanation is fine. → Optional polish: in explanation, reframe to 'glucose is normal or only mildly elevated; the diagnosis is made on ketones + acidosis regardless of glucose' rather than anchoring on <11. No change to the answer key — B is unambiguously the single best answer.
> - **[low]** MCQ4 / DKA schema (potassium threshold for withholding insulin): States the cut-off as K <3.5 (withhold insulin). International sources vary: ADA/US uses <3.3 mmol/L; JBDS/UK (which Australian practice generally follows) effectively withholds and replaces aggressively when K is low and uses the 3.5 framing. The 3.5 threshold is defensible for an Australian exam but is not universally the number — an adversarial candidate could argue 3.3. → Defensible as-is for Australian/JBDS-aligned practice. Optionally add a parenthetical '(some guidelines use <3.3; JBDS/Australian practice withholds until K >=3.5)' to pre-empt the dispute. Not a scoring error.
> - **[low]** MCQ5 (T2DM add-on) explanation — PBS rule precision: States GLP-1 RA 'generally requires prior SGLT2i failure/contraindication for subsidy.' The actual June 2024 PBS rule is more precisely: a GLP-1 RA is not subsidised in COMBINATION with an SGLT2i UNLESS the SGLT2i is for a different indication (HF/CKD) and there was no clinically meaningful glycaemic response. The simplified version is adequate but slightly overstates it as a blanket 'must fail SGLT2i first' rule. → The answer (B, SGLT2i) is unambiguously correct given HFrEF + albuminuric CKD — this is the right cardiorenal choice on clinical grounds ALONE, independent of PBS. Optionally soften the PBS wording to 'a GLP-1 RA is generally not PBS-subsidised in combination with an SGLT2i unless the SGLT2i is for a non-glycaemic indication without adequate glycaemic response.' Answer key unaffected.
> - **[low]** schemaRecall 'thyroid/adrenal axis' and distractorDrill 'low free T4' — central hypothyroidism: Repeatedly states central hypothyroidism shows 'low T4 + LOW/normal TSH.' This is correct, but the more common and exam-classic finding is a low/inappropriately-NORMAL TSH (TSH is frequently in the normal range, occasionally mildly elevated due to bioinactive TSH, only sometimes frankly low). Stating 'LOW' first may mislead candidates into expecting a clearly suppressed TSH. → Reorder to emphasise 'low T4 with an INAPPROPRIATELY normal (or low) TSH' as the central-disease fingerprint, since an unexpectedly normal TSH is the commoner trap than a frankly low one.
> - **[low]** highEndExtras — thyroid storm iodine timing line: States 'iodine at least 1 h AFTER thionamide.' Correct principle (block synthesis before giving iodine substrate to avoid Jod-Basedow), and the commonly quoted interval is ~1 hour. This is accurate but is not directly tested by any MCQ in the set — it is an orphan high-yield fact with no anchoring item. → No correction needed for accuracy. Consider adding a thyroid storm MCQ to anchor this and the PTU-vs-carbimazole point, which are currently only in extras.
> 
> _Quality notes:_ Distractors are genuinely seductive across the set, not strawmen. The strongest traps: MCQ1 option A (glucose <11 'excludes' DKA) and MCQ5 option A (GLP-1 RA for obesity/ASCVD — clinically reasonable but fails PBS access) are exactly the deadly real-world errors. MCQ3 option C (isotonic saline worsening SIADH) and MCQ6 option A (CCB before surgery) are also well-constructed near-misses.; MCQs are clean single-best-answer. No item has two defensibly-correct options. In MCQ2 the 'internally contradictory' distractor A (suppressed PTH labelled hyperparathyroidism) is a slightly soft strawman — a savvy candidate spots the contradiction instantly — but the seductive pull of the reflex 'high calcium = parathyroidectomy' association is preserved, so it works.; A few items are on the easier side for a postgraduate cohort: MCQ8 (Addison's sick-day) and MCQ6 (alpha-before-beta) test single well-drilled rules with obvious distractors. They reinforce core safety rules but are unlikely to discriminate top candidates. MCQ4 (DKA potassium order) and MCQ1 (euglycaemic DKA) are the best discriminators.; No ambiguous stems. Volume status, biochemistry and context are sufficiently specified in every case. MCQ7 is the only stem whose 'correct' interpretation is now time-sensitive (ADIPS 2025) and should be updated.; The schemas are clinically sound and well-sequenced (order rules: fluids-before-insulin, calcium-before-insulin, saline-before-bisphosphonate, alpha-before-beta, treat-before-confirm in Addisonian crisis are all correct). The hyponatraemia correction caps (<=8/24h, <=4-6 high-risk) match current 2024-2026 guidance.; Coverage is strong but two high-yield areas referenced only in extras (thyroid storm/PTU-vs-carbimazole; amiodarone thyroid; Conn's; MEN syndromes) have no anchoring MCQ. Consider promoting at least thyroid storm and primary aldosteronism to full items, as both are frequently tested in Australian endocrine exams.

---

## Gastroenterology & Hepatology  _(~11% of marks)_

### The one idea that unlocks the domain

> **Every GI/hepatology answer is derived by FIRST localising the lesion (where in the gut tube or the liver's structural-functional axis the problem sits) and SECOND asking "what is the single time-critical decision that changes mortality NOW" — because finals reward the resuscitation/stratification step, not the diagnosis.**

Most GI vignettes are not testing whether you can name the disease — the stem usually hands you the diagnosis. They test the NEXT action under a clock. If you internalise two engines, you derive almost every answer. Engine 1 (anatomical-functional localisation): bilirubin/ALT/ALP/GGT patterns localise liver injury to hepatocellular vs cholestatic; the gut tube localises bleeding (haematemesis = above ligament of Treitz; melaena = upper/proximal; haematochezia = lower) and malabsorption (proximal small bowel = iron/folate/coeliac; terminal ileum = B12/bile salts/Crohn's). Engine 2 (mortality-first triage): in every acute GI presentation ask "ABC and what kills in the next hour?" — variceal bleed needs terlipressin + antibiotics BEFORE the scope; acute severe colitis needs IV steroids + VTE prophylaxis + day-3 rescue planning; SBP needs tap-then-antibiotics-then-albumin; severe pancreatitis needs fluids + organ-support escalation. Owning these means when a stem gives ascites + fever, you reflexively derive "diagnostic paracentesis first" rather than memorising it. The same engine sorts chronic problems: Child-Pugh and decompensation features tell you transplant/prognosis trajectory; LFT pattern + ferritin/transferrin saturation derives haemochromatosis without recall. Because Australian finals layer epidemiology (hepatitis B/C demographics, NBCSP age cut-offs, alcohol thresholds) onto these engines, knowing WHERE and WHAT-KILLS-NOW lets you reason rather than recall, which is the only viable strategy under a 20/80 time gamble.

### High-yield tutorial

# Gastroenterology & Hepatology — The Engine

## 1. Read the liver as TWO axes, not a list of diseases

Interpret LFTs by **pattern, not single values**:

- **Hepatocellular** = ALT/AST dominant. ALT >1000 → ischaemic, viral, drug (paracetamol), autoimmune. **AST:ALT >2:1 with GGT up = alcohol** (mnemonic: "A Scotch and Tonic").
- **Cholestatic** = ALP + GGT dominant. Raised ALP with **normal GGT = bone** (Paget's, mets), not liver. Then split cholestasis: dilated ducts on USS = obstruction (stone, tumour) → ERCP/MRCP; non-dilated = intrahepatic (PBC → **anti-mitochondrial Ab**, drugs, PSC → assoc. UC).
- **Synthetic function** = bilirubin, albumin, INR. These, not transaminases, tell you how sick the liver actually is. **A normal ALT does not mean a healthy liver** (burnt-out cirrhosis has near-normal ALT).

**Haemochromatosis** (high Australian prevalence, *HFE* C282Y homozygous): screen with **transferrin saturation first** (>45% abnormal, the most sensitive early marker) then ferritin. Order genetics if iron studies abnormal. Triad to spot: diabetes + arthropathy (2nd/3rd MCP) + bronze skin + deranged LFTs. Manage with **venesection**; target ferritin 50–100.

## 2. Upper GI bleed — variceal vs peptic is a fork, not a guess

Resuscitate first: **2 large-bore cannulae, restrictive transfusion (target Hb 70, or 80 if cardiac/active)** — over-transfusing varices raises portal pressure and worsens bleeding.

- **Suspected variceal** (known CLD, stigmata): give **terlipressin + IV antibiotics (ceftriaxone) BEFORE endoscopy** — antibiotics independently reduce mortality. Endoscopic **band ligation** within 12h. Refractory → Sengstaken/TIPS.
- **Peptic ulcer**: **PPI**, endoscopy within 24h. Pre-endoscopy PPI is optional, not mortality-changing. Post-scope: high-dose IV PPI if high-risk stigmata (active bleeding, visible vessel).
- **Scoring**: **Glasgow-Blatchford** pre-endoscopy (0–1 = consider discharge); **Rockall** post-endoscopy for rebleeding/mortality.

## 3. Decompensated chronic liver disease

Decompensation = **jaundice, ascites, variceal bleed, encephalopathy**. Quantify with **Child-Pugh (ABCDE: Albumin, Bilirubin, Coagulation/INR, Distension/ascites, Encephalopathy)**.

- **SBP**: ascites + fever/abdo pain/deteriorating. **Diagnostic paracentesis BEFORE antibiotics**; **ascitic neutrophils >250 = SBP** regardless of culture. Treat with cefotaxime/ceftriaxone **+ IV albumin** (cuts hepatorenal syndrome and mortality). Recurrent → norfloxacin prophylaxis.
- **Hepatic encephalopathy**: precipitated by infection, GI bleed, constipation, diuretics, sedatives. Treat the precipitant; **lactulose first-line** (titrate to 2–3 soft stools/day), **rifaximin** add-on for recurrence.
- **Alcohol**: assess with **Maddrey's discriminant function ≥32** for severe alcoholic hepatitis → consider corticosteroids. Always give **thiamine before glucose** (Wernicke prevention).

## 4. IBD — the comparison table is examined relentlessly

| | UC | Crohn's |
|---|---|---|
| Distribution | Continuous, rectum→proximal, colon only | Mouth→anus, **skip lesions**, terminal ileum |
| Depth | Mucosal | **Transmural** → fistulae, strictures |
| Histology | Crypt abscesses | **Non-caseating granulomas** |
| Smoking | Protective | **Worsens** |
| Antibody | pANCA | ASCA |

**Acute severe UC** (Truelove & Witts: **>6 bloody stools/day + systemic features** — fever, tachycardia, anaemia, ESR>30): admit, **IV hydrocortisone + VTE prophylaxis** (paradoxically prothrombotic despite bleeding), AXR for toxic megacolon. **Day 3 review**: if **>8 stools/day or CRP>45 + 3–8 stools** → rescue **infliximab or ciclosporin**; failure → colectomy. Never give antimotility agents (precipitate megacolon).

## 5. Acute pancreatitis

Causes: **GET SMASHED** — Gallstones and Ethanol dominate. Diagnose with **lipase >3× upper limit**. Severity by **organ failure (modified Glasgow/APACHE)** not pain. **Aggressive fluid resuscitation** is the core early intervention; analgesia; treat cause. **Antibiotics are NOT routine** — only for infected necrosis. ERCP only if cholangitis/obstruction. Look for hypocalcaemia, hyperglycaemia.

## 6. Malabsorption, coeliac, iron-deficiency

**Iron-deficiency anaemia (microcytic, low ferritin)** in any adult = **investigate the gut for malignancy** until proven otherwise. **Postmenopausal woman or any man with IDA → colonoscopy + gastroscopy** (this is a classic single-best-answer). Coeliac screen: **anti-tissue transglutaminase (tTG-IgA) + total IgA** (IgA deficiency causes false negatives); confirm with **duodenal biopsy on a gluten-containing diet** (villous atrophy). Coeliac causes **proximal** malabsorption → iron/folate; terminal ileal disease → **B12** and bile salts.

## 7. Dysphagia, GORD, colorectal screening

- **Progressive dysphagia for solids→liquids + weight loss = oesophageal cancer until excluded → urgent endoscopy**. Intermittent solids+liquids from onset = motility (achalasia: "bird's beak").
- **GORD red flags** (dysphagia, weight loss, anaemia, vomiting, age>50 new onset) → **endoscopy**. **Barrett's** (metaplasia) → surveillance.
- **NBCSP (Australia)**: **iFOBT every 2 years, now from age 45 to 74**; positive iFOBT → **colonoscopy**.

### Highest mark-per-minute principles

- **In suspected variceal bleeding, terlipressin AND IV antibiotics (ceftriaxone) are given before endoscopy.** — Antibiotics independently reduce mortality in cirrhotic GI bleeds — examiners test that you give them up front, not after the scope.
  - _Exam angle:_ Stem: known cirrhosis + haematemesis. Distractor 'urgent endoscopy' is the trap; correct = drugs + antibiotics first, then band ligation within 12h.
- **In ascites + fever/deterioration, do diagnostic paracentesis BEFORE antibiotics; neutrophils >250 = SBP and mandates albumin.** — Tap-first sequencing and the 250 cut-off plus albumin co-administration are the precise testable points.
  - _Exam angle:_ Single-best-answer 'next step' = ascitic tap, not 'start IV cefotaxime'.
- **Iron-deficiency anaemia in any man or postmenopausal woman demands bidirectional endoscopy to exclude GI malignancy.** — This is the most repeated GI single-best-answer in finals — links haematology to bowel cancer.
  - _Exam angle:_ Tired man, microcytic anaemia, low ferritin → answer is colonoscopy + gastroscopy, not oral iron.
- **Acute severe colitis needs IV steroids + VTE prophylaxis, with a day-3 decision point for rescue therapy or colectomy.** — VTE prophylaxis despite bloody diarrhoea is counter-intuitive and heavily tested; so is the Truelove-Witts/day-3 logic.
  - _Exam angle:_ Distractor is 'hold anticoagulation'; correct includes prophylactic LMWH plus infliximab/ciclosporin if no day-3 response.
- **LFT pattern (hepatocellular vs cholestatic) plus GGT localises injury before any imaging.** — Lets you derive alcohol (AST:ALT>2, GGT up), bone (ALP up, GGT normal), and obstruction without recall.
  - _Exam angle:_ Raised ALP normal GGT = bone source — classic trap distractor steering you to liver imaging.
- **Transferrin saturation is the first and most sensitive screen for haemochromatosis, before ferritin and genetics.** — High Australian prevalence makes this a favourite; sequence of tests is the tested detail.
  - _Exam angle:_ Diabetes + arthropathy + deranged LFTs → order iron studies (transferrin sat) first, then HFE genotyping.
- **Restrictive transfusion (Hb target 70) in GI bleeding, especially variceal.** — Over-transfusion worsens variceal bleeding via portal pressure — counter-intuitive and examined.
  - _Exam angle:_ Stable variceal bleed with Hb 75 → do NOT transfuse; correct answer withholds blood.
- **Lipase >3x ULN diagnoses pancreatitis; severity is organ failure, and antibiotics are not routine.** — The 'no prophylactic antibiotics' rule and fluid-first management are reliable distractor-killers.
  - _Exam angle:_ Severe pancreatitis stem with 'commence IV antibiotics' distractor — correct = aggressive fluids.
- **Coeliac serology is tTG-IgA WITH total IgA, confirmed by duodenal biopsy on gluten-containing diet.** — IgA deficiency false-negatives and 'stay on gluten before biopsy' are the trap points.
  - _Exam angle:_ Patient already gluten-free → answer is reintroduce gluten before testing, or check total IgA.
- **NBCSP uses iFOBT 2-yearly from 45-74; a positive test mandates colonoscopy.** — Australian-specific age change (now from 45) is a guaranteed epidemiology mark.
  - _Exam angle:_ 47-year-old positive iFOBT → next step colonoscopy, not repeat FOBT.

### Must-know numbers (Australian where they differ)

- Restrictive transfusion threshold in GI bleed: Hb 70 g/L (80 if active cardiac ischaemia)
- SBP diagnosis: ascitic PMN/neutrophils >250 cells/mm3
- Lipase for pancreatitis: >3x upper limit of normal
- Transferrin saturation abnormal: >45% (screen for haemochromatosis); ferritin venesection target 50-100
- Maddrey discriminant function >=32 = severe alcoholic hepatitis (consider steroids)
- Child-Pugh components: Albumin, Bilirubin, INR, Ascites, Encephalopathy
- Truelove & Witts severe colitis: >6 bloody stools/day + systemic upset (HR>90, temp>37.8, Hb<105, ESR>30)
- Acute severe colitis day-3 rescue trigger: >8 stools/day OR CRP>45 with 3-8 stools
- Glasgow-Blatchford 0-1 = low risk, consider outpatient management
- NBCSP Australia: iFOBT every 2 years, age 45-74
- Variceal endoscopy timing within 12h; non-variceal/peptic within 24h
- AST:ALT ratio >2:1 suggests alcoholic liver disease
- Lactulose titrated to 2-3 soft stools per day in encephalopathy
- Coeliac: confirm on gluten-containing diet; check total IgA alongside tTG-IgA

### Cross-topic cuts

- Iron-deficiency anaemia bridges Haematology and GI — same workup answer (bidirectional endoscopy) appears in both blocks.
- Hepatic encephalopathy and SBP overlap with Infectious Disease and Renal (hepatorenal syndrome, albumin for prevention).
- Alcohol thread links GI (cirrhosis, pancreatitis), Neurology (Wernicke — thiamine before glucose) and Psychiatry (withdrawal).
- Haemochromatosis crosses Endocrine (diabetes), Rheumatology (arthropathy), Cardiology (restrictive cardiomyopathy) and Hepatology.
- PSC links Hepatology to IBD (UC association) and to cholangiocarcinoma risk.
- Coeliac disease bridges to Dermatology (dermatitis herpetiformis), Haematology (iron/folate), and bone (osteoporosis).
- Variceal bleeding ties Hepatology to Emergency/resuscitation and transfusion medicine (restrictive strategy).
- Pancreatitis crosses to Metabolic medicine (hypertriglyceridaemia, hypercalcaemia causes) and Endocrine (new diabetes).
- Colorectal cancer screening connects GI to Public Health/preventive medicine (NBCSP) and Genetics (Lynch, FAP).
- Hepatitis B/C links to Infectious Disease, sexual health, and pharmacology (DAAs for HCV cure).

### Australian-specific (where the UK answer loses you marks)

- NBCSP now invites iFOBT every 2 years from age 45 (lowered from 50) up to 74 — UK uses FIT from 50-74; the age threshold is the Australian exam point.
- Hepatitis B epidemiology in Australia is concentrated in migrant populations from Asia-Pacific, Sub-Saharan Africa, and in Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples — screen these groups (HBsAg, anti-HBc, anti-HBs).
- Hepatitis C is highly curable with direct-acting antivirals fully PBS-subsidised and prescribable in primary care in Australia — refer/treat rather than just monitor; injecting drug use is the dominant transmission route.
- Hereditary haemochromatosis (HFE C282Y) has notably high prevalence in Australians of Northern European/Celtic ancestry — iron studies and genotyping feature heavily.
- Alcohol guidance follows NHMRC: no more than 10 standard drinks/week and no more than 4 on any one day; an Australian standard drink = 10 g alcohol (differs from UK units).
- Terlipressin, ceftriaxone, and PBS-listed DAAs reflect Australian formulary choices; ceftriaxone/cefotaxime are standard for SBP and variceal prophylaxis.
- Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples have higher rates of chronic liver disease and hepatocellular carcinoma — culturally informed screening is examinable.
- Hepatocellular carcinoma surveillance with 6-monthly ultrasound is offered to at-risk cirrhotic and chronic hepatitis B patients within Australian practice.

> **Independent accuracy audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[medium]** Restrictive transfusion (target Hb 70, or 80 if cardiac/active) — over-transfusing varices raises portal pressure. Must-know number: 'Hb 70 g/L (80 if active cardiac ischaemia)'. → State: restrictive threshold transfuse at Hb <70 g/L (target 70-90); in patients with established cardiovascular/ischaemic heart disease use a higher threshold of <80 g/L (target ~100). Drop 'active' from the cardiac-threshold clause. Source: Villanueva NEJM 2013; ESGE/international consensus on non-variceal UGIB; consistent with Australian National Blood Authority Patient Blood Management Guidelines (Module 3 - Critical Bleeding/Massive Transfusion and Module on medical patients).
> - **[medium]** Maddrey's discriminant function >=32 for severe alcoholic hepatitis -> consider corticosteroids. → Specify oral prednisolone 40 mg/day for severe alcoholic hepatitis (Maddrey DF >=32 or hepatic encephalopathy), after excluding active infection/GI bleeding; assess response with the Lille score at day 7 and stop if Lille >=0.45. Note STOPAH: short-term benefit only, no long-term mortality reduction. Source: STOPAH NEJM 2015; eTG (Therapeutic Guidelines) Gastrointestinal - alcoholic hepatitis.
> - **[medium]** Haemochromatosis: screen with transferrin saturation first (>45% abnormal) then ferritin. Order genetics if iron studies abnormal. → State: order fasting transferrin saturation and ferritin; proceed to HFE genotyping (C282Y/H63D) when transferrin saturation is raised (>45%) AND ferritin is elevated. Ferritin is an acute-phase reactant, so an isolated high ferritin with normal transferrin saturation is usually not haemochromatosis. Source: RACGP 'Hereditary haemochromatosis - Diagnosis and management'; Australian Prescriber 'Testing for HFE-related haemochromatosis'.
> - **[medium]** Recurrent SBP -> norfloxacin prophylaxis. → For secondary SBP prophylaxis in Australia, use trimethoprim+sulfamethoxazole (co-trimoxazole) daily, or ciprofloxacin as an alternative, per eTG (Therapeutic Guidelines - Gastrointestinal/Antibiotic). Also offer primary prophylaxis in patients with low-protein ascites + advanced disease or after a variceal bleed. Source: eTG Australia.
> - **[low]** Acute severe UC day-3 rescue: infliximab or ciclosporin; failure -> colectomy. → List rescue options as infliximab OR a JAK inhibitor (tofacitinib/upadacitinib) OR ciclosporin, with colectomy if rescue fails or for toxic megacolon/perforation. Note upadacitinib's rapid onset makes it attractive in ASUC. Source: contemporary IBD literature 2024-2025; PBS UC listings; GESA/ECCO guidance.
> - **[low]** AST:ALT >2:1 with GGT up = alcohol (mnemonic 'A Scotch and Tonic'). ALT >1000 -> ischaemic, viral, drug, autoimmune. → Add: AST:ALT >2 suggests alcohol OR advanced cirrhosis/fibrosis of any aetiology; alcoholic hepatitis transaminases are typically only moderately elevated (AST usually <300-500), so ALT >1000 points away from alcohol toward ischaemic/viral/drug/autoimmune. Source: standard hepatology (consistent with eTG/RACGP LFT interpretation).
> - **[low]** Hepatocellular carcinoma surveillance with 6-monthly ultrasound (Australian specifics). → State 6-monthly ultrasound PLUS AFP for at-risk groups, citing the 2025 Australian HCC surveillance guidelines (George et al, MJA 2025), and list the CHB age/ethnicity cut-offs. Source: MJA 2025 Australian HCC surveillance guidelines.
> - **[low]** Coeliac confirm with duodenal biopsy on a gluten-containing diet (villous atrophy). → Add HLA-DQ2/DQ8 typing as an adjunct (a negative result effectively excludes coeliac and is useful in patients already gluten-free); emphasise serology AND biopsy must be on a gluten-containing diet. Biopsy remains the adult gold standard in Australia (no-biopsy paediatric pathway differs). Source: RACGP/GESA coeliac guidance.
> 
> _Gaps flagged & folded in:_ Hepatitis B management/eligibility: omits the Australian framework for who needs antiviral therapy (entecavir/tenofovir, both PBS s85), the phases of CHB, and that ~9/10 Australians with CHB are from migrant/Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander populations and many are undiagnosed. Examinable: HBsAg/anti-HBc/anti-HBs interpretation table and that CHB itself (not just cirrhosis) warrants HCC surveillance per age/ethnicity.; Hepatitis C is curable and the tutorial's australianSpecifics correctly notes GP/nurse-practitioner PBS prescribing of pangenotypic DAAs (glecaprevir/pibrentasvir 8wk, sofosbuvir/velpatasvir 12wk) with >95% cure - but the main tutorial body never mentions HCV at all. Add a line: any anti-HCV positive -> HCV RNA to confirm active infection -> treat (do not just monitor).; MASLD/MAFLD (formerly NAFLD): completely absent despite being the commonest liver disease in Australia. Should cover the nomenclature change to MASLD, non-invasive fibrosis staging (FIB-4 first-line, then transient elastography/FibroScan, with FIB-4 <1.3 effectively excluding advanced fibrosis), and that metabolic risk + fibrosis assessment drives referral. High-yield 2026 exam material.; Iron-deficiency anaemia workup: tutorial says bidirectional endoscopy but omits coeliac serology (tTG-IgA) as a mandatory parallel investigation in IDA, and omits that menstruating premenopausal women still need GI workup if no clear gynae cause or if symptoms/age suggest. Add coeliac screen to the IDA algorithm.; Variceal primary prophylaxis: tutorial covers acute variceal bleeding but omits PRIMARY prevention - non-selective beta-blocker (carvedilol now preferred, or propranolol) OR endoscopic band ligation for medium/large varices; and secondary prophylaxis = NSBB + repeat banding. Commonly examined.; Hepatorenal syndrome (HRS-AKI): mentioned only in passing under SBP/albumin. Should state terlipressin + albumin as treatment and the diagnostic concept (functional renal failure, diagnosis of exclusion after volume challenge), since it links to the cross-topic Renal cut.; Paracetamol overdose / N-acetylcysteine: the commonest cause of acute liver failure and ALT >1000 in Australia is paracetamol, yet NAC and the Australian nomogram/treatment line (and King's College Criteria for transplant referral) are not mentioned. High-yield ED/tox crossover.; Cholangitis (ascending) Charcot triad/Reynolds pentad and the ERCP-for-biliary-sepsis rule: tutorial mentions ERCP for cholangitis but never defines the syndrome or the antibiotics + urgent biliary drainage imperative. Worth an explicit line.; Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander health: tutorial notes higher CLD/HCC rates but does not connect to actionable screening (CHB prevalence, alcohol, HCC surveillance enrolment) in a way that scores Australian cultural-safety marks. Could add a concrete screening checklist.; Refeeding/alcohol-withdrawal bundle: 'thiamine before glucose' is noted, but the Australian alcohol-withdrawal management (symptom-triggered benzodiazepine regimens, e.g. diazepam, and high-dose parenteral thiamine for established/at-risk Wernicke) and the NHMRC 10/4 standard-drink thresholds belong together as an examinable Aus-specific bundle.; Hereditary CRC syndromes / surveillance: NBCSP covers average risk, but the tutorial omits that a strong family history or Lynch/FAP changes screening to colonoscopy-based pathways (and Category 1/2/3 family-history risk stratification per Cancer Council/NHMRC) - a classic Australian exam discriminator from the population iFOBT program.

### Applied MCQs

**Q1.** A 58-year-old man with known alcoholic cirrhosis presents to a Melbourne ED with a large-volume haematemesis. He is drowsy but protecting his airway. HR 118, BP 92/58. Two large-bore cannulae are sited and crystalloid started. His Hb returns at 74 g/L. He has no known ischaemic heart disease. Endoscopy can be arranged within 6 hours. What is the single most important next step?

   - A. Transfuse 2 units packed red cells to a target Hb of 100 g/L
   - B. Commence IV terlipressin and IV ceftriaxone
   - C. Proceed immediately to endoscopic band ligation
   - D. Insert a Sengstaken-Blakemore tube
   - E. Start high-dose IV pantoprazole infusion

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Commence IV terlipressin and IV ceftriaxone**

In a cirrhotic with suspected variceal bleed, vasoactive therapy (terlipressin) plus prophylactic antibiotics (ceftriaxone) are given BEFORE endoscopy — antibiotics independently reduce mortality and rebleeding, and terlipressin reduces portal pressure. A is wrong: with Hb 74 and no IHD, a restrictive threshold applies (transfuse only below 70, target 70-90); over-transfusion raises portal pressure and worsens variceal bleeding (target 100 is for established cardiovascular disease, not this man). C is the classic trap — banding is correct but comes AFTER drugs/antibiotics, within 12h. D is rescue therapy for refractory/uncontrolled bleeding, not first-line. E (PPI) is for peptic ulcer, not varices.

_Teaching point:_ Suspected variceal bleed = terlipressin + ceftriaxone BEFORE the scope; banding within 12h. Restrictive transfusion (threshold 70) unless established IHD.
</details>

**Q2.** A 49-year-old woman with cirrhosis secondary to autoimmune hepatitis presents with a 2-day history of worsening abdominal distension, low-grade fever (37.9C) and confusion. She has tense ascites. Bloods: WCC 13.2, creatinine 95 (baseline 70). What is the most appropriate immediate next step?

   - A. Start empirical IV cefotaxime
   - B. Give IV human albumin 20%
   - C. Perform a diagnostic ascitic tap
   - D. Commence oral lactulose
   - E. Arrange urgent CT abdomen

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Perform a diagnostic ascitic tap**

Ascites plus fever/deterioration mandates diagnostic paracentesis BEFORE antibiotics — the ascitic neutrophil count (>250 cells/mm3 = SBP) drives the diagnosis regardless of culture, and tapping first avoids losing the diagnosis. A is the seductive trap: antibiotics are essential but the tap comes first so you do not blunt the diagnostic yield. B (albumin) is given alongside antibiotics once SBP is confirmed (reduces hepatorenal syndrome and mortality) — not the immediate first action. D treats encephalopathy but the precipitant (likely SBP) must be diagnosed. E delays definitive diagnosis.

_Teaching point:_ Ascites + fever/deterioration = diagnostic tap FIRST; neutrophils >250 = SBP; then cefotaxime/ceftriaxone PLUS albumin.
</details>

**Q3.** A 67-year-old man presents to his Brisbane GP with 3 months of fatigue. He has no GI symptoms. FBE shows Hb 104 g/L, MCV 76 fL. Ferritin is 8 microg/L. He takes no NSAIDs and has no overt bleeding. What is the most appropriate next investigation?

   - A. Commence oral ferrous sulfate and recheck FBE in 3 months
   - B. Anti-tissue transglutaminase IgA only
   - C. Bidirectional endoscopy (gastroscopy and colonoscopy)
   - D. iFOBT
   - E. CT colonography

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Bidirectional endoscopy (gastroscopy and colonoscopy)**

Iron-deficiency anaemia (microcytic, low ferritin) in any adult man or postmenopausal woman demands bidirectional endoscopy to exclude GI malignancy — this is the single most repeated GI finals answer. A is dangerous: treating empirically with iron masks an occult colorectal cancer. B (coeliac serology) should be sent in PARALLEL as part of the IDA workup, but it is not the definitive next step that excludes malignancy. D (iFOBT) is a screening tool for asymptomatic average-risk patients — useless here where you already have proven IDA; you go straight to scope. E (CT colonography) is second-line if colonoscopy is incomplete/contraindicated.

_Teaching point:_ Proven IDA in a man or postmenopausal woman = bidirectional endoscopy to exclude GI cancer; send coeliac serology in parallel. Never just give iron.
</details>

**Q4.** A 26-year-old woman is admitted with a 5-day flare of ulcerative colitis: 9 bloody stools/day, HR 104, temp 38.0C, Hb 98 g/L, CRP 80. AXR shows no colonic dilatation. She is started on IV hydrocortisone. Which additional intervention is most important on admission?

   - A. Prophylactic low-molecular-weight heparin
   - B. Loperamide to reduce stool frequency
   - C. Oral mesalazine
   - D. Empirical IV ciprofloxacin and metronidazole
   - E. Immediate infliximab infusion

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Prophylactic low-molecular-weight heparin**

Acute severe UC is a prothrombotic state despite the bloody diarrhoea, and VTE prophylaxis with LMWH is mandatory — the counter-intuitive 'anticoagulate the bleeding patient' point is heavily examined. B is contraindicated: antimotility agents can precipitate toxic megacolon. C: oral mesalazine has no role in acute severe colitis (IV steroids are the backbone). D: antibiotics are not routine unless infection/toxic megacolon is suspected. E: infliximab is RESCUE therapy decided at the day-3 review if she fails steroids (>8 stools/day, or CRP>45 with 3-8 stools), not an admission-day reflex.

_Teaching point:_ Acute severe UC: IV steroids + VTE prophylaxis on admission; NO antimotility agents; day-3 review triggers rescue (infliximab / JAK inhibitor / ciclosporin) or colectomy.
</details>

**Q5.** A 70-year-old woman has a routine health check. She is asymptomatic. ALP is 280 U/L (ref <110), with normal ALT, AST, GGT and bilirubin. She has chronic lower back pain. What is the most likely source of the raised ALP?

   - A. Primary biliary cholangitis
   - B. Choledocholithiasis
   - C. Bone (e.g. Paget's disease or metastases)
   - D. Alcoholic liver disease
   - E. Primary sclerosing cholangitis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Bone (e.g. Paget's disease or metastases)**

Raised ALP with a NORMAL GGT points to a bone source, not the liver — GGT rises with hepatobiliary cholestasis but not with bone turnover. The clinical context (older woman, back pain) fits Paget's or metastases. A (PBC), B (stones), and E (PSC) are all cholestatic liver causes that would elevate GGT alongside ALP — the normal GGT excludes them. D (alcohol) gives an AST:ALT >2 with raised GGT, not isolated ALP. The seductive trap is to order liver imaging for an 'abnormal LFT' when the GGT has already localised the source to bone.

_Teaching point:_ Isolated raised ALP with normal GGT = bone, not liver. Check GGT before reaching for biliary imaging.
</details>

**Q6.** A 52-year-old man of Celtic ancestry attends his Perth GP with fatigue, polyarthralgia of the 2nd and 3rd MCP joints, and recently diagnosed type 2 diabetes. LFTs show mildly raised ALT. Fasting transferrin saturation is 62% and ferritin is 940 microg/L. What is the most appropriate next step?

   - A. Liver biopsy
   - B. HFE genotyping for C282Y and H63D
   - C. Commence venesection immediately
   - D. Repeat ferritin in 6 months
   - E. Reassure — ferritin is an acute phase reactant

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. HFE genotyping for C282Y and H63D**

The classic triad (diabetes + 2nd/3rd MCP arthropathy + deranged LFTs) with a RAISED transferrin saturation AND raised ferritin is the Australian trigger to proceed to HFE genotyping. A (biopsy) is reserved for staging fibrosis when ferritin is very high (>1000) or LFTs are markedly abnormal, not the immediate next step. C: you confirm the diagnosis genetically and assess iron burden before committing to lifelong venesection. D delays a probable diagnosis. E is the seductive distractor — ferritin IS an acute-phase reactant, BUT here the transferrin saturation is also raised (62%), which is what distinguishes true iron overload from inflammatory hyperferritinaemia.

_Teaching point:_ Genotype for haemochromatosis only when BOTH fasting transferrin saturation (>45%) AND ferritin are raised. Isolated high ferritin with normal transferrin sat is usually inflammation, not iron overload.
</details>

**Q7.** A 41-year-old woman has a severe alcoholic hepatitis flare. Bilirubin 280, INR 1.9, Maddrey discriminant function 48. Active infection and GI bleeding have been excluded. She is started on oral prednisolone 40 mg daily and given thiamine. At day 7 the Lille score is 0.60. What is the most appropriate management decision?

   - A. Continue prednisolone for a full 28-day course
   - B. Switch prednisolone to IV hydrocortisone
   - C. Stop prednisolone as she is a non-responder
   - D. Add pentoxifylline
   - E. Increase prednisolone to 60 mg daily

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Stop prednisolone as she is a non-responder**

A Lille score >=0.45 at day 7 identifies a steroid non-responder; continuing steroids confers no benefit and adds infection risk, so they should be stopped. A is wrong because steroids are only continued if Lille indicates response (<0.45); STOPAH showed at best a borderline 28-day benefit and none at 90 days/1 year. B: prednisolone (not prednisone or hydrocortisone) is the specific oral agent because it needs no hepatic conversion — switching is illogical. D: pentoxifylline did not show benefit over steroids in modern trials. E: dose escalation is not the strategy; the Lille score governs continuation.

_Teaching point:_ Severe alcoholic hepatitis: oral prednisolone 40 mg after excluding sepsis/GI bleed, then Lille at day 7 — stop if Lille >=0.45. Benefit is short-term only.
</details>

**Q8.** A 33-year-old man is brought to a regional Victorian ED 18 hours after a staggered paracetamol overdose. He now has RUQ pain. ALT is 4200 U/L, INR 2.4, bilirubin rising, creatinine 180. Which single intervention most directly reduces his mortality?

   - A. Oral activated charcoal
   - B. IV N-acetylcysteine
   - C. IV vitamin K
   - D. Urgent gastroscopy
   - E. IV proton pump inhibitor

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. IV N-acetylcysteine**

Paracetamol is the commonest cause of acute liver failure and ALT >1000 in Australia; IV N-acetylcysteine is the antidote and reduces mortality even when started late, and is given regardless of nomogram in established hepatotoxicity. A (charcoal) is only useful within ~1-2 hours of ingestion — far too late at 18 hours. C (vitamin K) will not correct a synthetic-failure coagulopathy and the rising INR is a prognostic marker (King's College Criteria) you do not want to mask. D and E are irrelevant to hepatocellular failure. This patient meets the threshold for discussion with a liver transplant unit.

_Teaching point:_ ALT >1000 + coagulopathy in Australia = think paracetamol; give IV NAC even late, and screen against King's College Criteria for transplant referral.
</details>

**Q9.** A 47-year-old asymptomatic man returns a positive immunochemical faecal occult blood test (iFOBT) through the National Bowel Cancer Screening Program. He has no family history of bowel cancer. What is the most appropriate next step?

   - A. Repeat the iFOBT in 2 years
   - B. Repeat the iFOBT in 3 months
   - C. Colonoscopy
   - D. CT colonography
   - E. Reassurance, as he is below the standard screening age of 50

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Colonoscopy**

A positive iFOBT mandates colonoscopy — it is the definitive investigation and should not be repeated or delayed. E is the seductive trap built on outdated knowledge: the Australian NBCSP now invites screening from age 45 (lowered from 50) to 74, so a 47-year-old is squarely within the program and his positive result is valid and actionable. A and B (repeating the test) inappropriately delay diagnosis of a potential malignancy. D (CT colonography) is reserved for incomplete or contraindicated colonoscopy.

_Teaching point:_ Positive iFOBT = colonoscopy, never repeat FOBT. Australian NBCSP now screens 2-yearly from age 45 to 74.
</details>

### Schema recall — "What is your schema for X?"

**What is your schema for interpreting deranged liver function tests?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>
