Step 1 — Decide the PATTERN, not single values. Hepatocellular = ALT/AST dominant; Cholestatic = ALP/GGT dominant. Step 2 — If ALP raised, check GGT: raised GGT = hepatobiliary cholestasis; normal GGT = bone (Paget's, mets). Step 3 — If hepatocellular, gauge magnitude: ALT >1000 = ischaemic/viral/drug (paracetamol)/autoimmune; AST:ALT >2 with raised GGT = alcohol OR advanced cirrhosis of any cause (and alcoholic hepatitis AST is usually <300-500, so very high ALT argues against alcohol). Step 4 — If cholestatic, image: dilated ducts on USS = extrahepatic obstruction (stone/tumour) -> MRCP/ERCP; non-dilated = intrahepatic (PBC -> anti-mitochondrial Ab; PSC -> assoc UC; drugs). Step 5 — Assess SYNTHETIC function separately (bilirubin, albumin, INR) — these tell you how sick the liver is; a normal ALT does NOT mean a healthy liver (burnt-out cirrhosis).

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Two attack points: (1) candidates reflexively order liver imaging for a raised ALP without checking GGT, missing the bone source; (2) candidates equate a normal ALT with a healthy liver, forgetting that cirrhosis can have near-normal transaminases and that synthetic markers (INR/albumin/bilirubin) are the true severity gauge.
</details>

**What is your schema for the acute upper GI bleed?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Step 1 — Resuscitate: ABC, 2 large-bore cannulae, crossmatch, restrictive transfusion (transfuse below Hb 70, target 70-90; higher threshold below 80 / target ~100 only if established cardiovascular disease). Step 2 — Risk-stratify pre-endoscopy with Glasgow-Blatchford (0-1 = consider outpatient). Step 3 — Fork: is it VARICEAL (known CLD, stigmata) or PEPTIC? Step 4a — Variceal: terlipressin + IV ceftriaxone BEFORE the scope, then band ligation within 12h; refractory -> Sengstaken/TIPS. Step 4b — Peptic: endoscopy within 24h, high-dose IV PPI if high-risk stigmata (active bleeding/visible vessel) post-scope. Step 5 — Post-endoscopy Rockall for rebleeding/mortality; address precipitant (stop NSAIDs, H. pylori, secondary varices prophylaxis with NSBB + banding).

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Examiners attack the SEQUENCE in variceal bleeds — 'urgent endoscopy' is offered as the trap when the mortality-changing answer is drugs + antibiotics first. They also attack the transfusion threshold: over-transfusing a stable variceal bleed (Hb 75) worsens portal pressure, so withholding blood is correct. Misremembering '80 if active bleeding' (it is 80 only for established cardiac disease) loses the mark.
</details>

**What is your schema for suspected spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (SBP)?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Step 1 — Trigger: any cirrhotic with ascites who develops fever, abdominal pain, encephalopathy, or unexplained deterioration/renal impairment. Step 2 — Diagnostic paracentesis BEFORE antibiotics. Step 3 — Diagnose on ascitic neutrophil (PMN) count >250 cells/mm3 — this defines SBP regardless of culture result. Step 4 — Treat: IV cefotaxime or ceftriaxone PLUS IV albumin (albumin cuts hepatorenal syndrome and mortality). Step 5 — Prevent: secondary prophylaxis with trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole (co-trimoxazole) or ciprofloxacin in Australia (NOT norfloxacin); primary prophylaxis in low-protein ascites with advanced disease or post-variceal bleed.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The killer is sequencing — candidates 'start IV cefotaxime' as the next step when the correct next step is the diagnostic tap. The second attack is forgetting albumin co-administration. The Australian-specific trap is quoting norfloxacin for prophylaxis, which is dated/deregistered — use co-trimoxazole or ciprofloxacin.
</details>

**What is your schema for acute severe ulcerative colitis (ASUC)?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Step 1 — Diagnose severity with Truelove & Witts: >6 bloody stools/day PLUS a systemic feature (HR>90, temp>37.8, Hb<105, ESR>30). Step 2 — Admit; baseline AXR to exclude toxic megacolon; stool cultures/C. difficile. Step 3 — Treat: IV hydrocortisone + VTE prophylaxis with LMWH (prothrombotic despite bleeding); NEVER antimotility agents. Step 4 — Day-3 review: if >8 stools/day, OR CRP>45 with 3-8 stools -> medical rescue with infliximab OR a JAK inhibitor (upadacitinib/tofacitinib) OR ciclosporin. Step 5 — Failure of rescue, or toxic megacolon/perforation -> colectomy.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Two attacks: (1) candidates 'hold anticoagulation' because the patient is bleeding — wrong, VTE prophylaxis is mandatory; (2) candidates reach for infliximab on day 1 — rescue is a structured day-3 decision, not a reflex. Forgetting that JAK inhibitors (upadacitinib) are now established rescue options dates the answer.
</details>

**What is your schema for investigating iron-deficiency anaemia (IDA)?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Step 1 — Confirm true IDA: microcytic (low MCV) anaemia with low ferritin (ferritin is the key — but it is an acute-phase reactant, so low ferritin is definitive whereas normal ferritin with inflammation does not exclude it). Step 2 — Risk-stratify by group: any adult man or postmenopausal woman = GI malignancy until proven otherwise. Step 3 — Bidirectional endoscopy (gastroscopy + colonoscopy). Step 4 — Send coeliac serology (tTG-IgA + total IgA) IN PARALLEL — coeliac is a common occult cause. Step 5 — Premenopausal menstruating women still need GI workup if no clear gynae cause, or if symptoms/age raise concern. Step 6 — If both scopes and coeliac negative -> consider small bowel imaging/capsule.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The trap answer is 'commence oral iron and review' — this masks an occult colorectal cancer. The second miss is forgetting parallel coeliac serology. The third is wrongly reassuring a premenopausal woman without considering GI sources when the history doesn't fit gynaecological loss.
</details>

**What is your schema for diagnosing coeliac disease?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Step 1 — Ensure the patient is on a gluten-containing diet for BOTH serology and biopsy (gluten-free state causes false negatives). Step 2 — First-line serology: anti-tissue transglutaminase IgA (tTG-IgA) PLUS total IgA (IgA deficiency causes false-negative tTG). Step 3 — If tTG positive (or high clinical suspicion), confirm in adults with duodenal biopsy showing villous atrophy — the adult gold standard. Step 4 — HLA-DQ2/DQ8 typing as an adjunct: high negative predictive value, useful in patients already gluten-free or with equivocal serology (a negative result effectively excludes coeliac). Step 5 — Localise consequences: proximal small-bowel involvement -> iron/folate deficiency; associations include dermatitis herpetiformis and osteoporosis.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The classic trap is the patient who has ALREADY started a gluten-free diet — testing now gives false negatives, so the answer is reintroduce gluten before serology/biopsy, OR use HLA-DQ2/DQ8 to exclude. Second attack: forgetting total IgA, so an IgA-deficient coeliac is falsely called negative.
</details>

### Distractor drills — "What's the deadliest distractor for X?"

**In a cirrhotic with haematemesis, what is the most seductive wrong 'next step' answer?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Proceed immediately to urgent endoscopy / band ligation.
- _Why it tempts you:_ Endoscopy IS the definitive treatment for varices, and 'urgent endoscopy' feels like the decisive, hospital-grade action under a clock — it is also genuinely part of management within 12h.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Antibiotics (ceftriaxone) independently reduce mortality and vasoactive terlipressin reduces portal pressure — both must be given BEFORE the scope. Choosing endoscopy first skips the mortality-changing pharmacological step.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Ask 'what changes mortality in the next hour?' — it is the antibiotic + terlipressin bundle, not the timing of the scope. Drugs before scope.**

**For ascites + fever in a cirrhotic, what is the deadliest distractor?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Start empirical IV cefotaxime immediately.
- _Why it tempts you:_ SBP is a sepsis emergency and 'early antibiotics save lives' is drummed into every student, so reaching for cefotaxime feels both correct and urgent.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ You must perform the diagnostic ascitic tap FIRST — the neutrophil count >250 defines SBP, and giving antibiotics before tapping can blunt the diagnostic yield and the decision to give albumin.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **The single-best-answer 'next step' is always the diagnostic tap; antibiotics + albumin follow once the PMN count confirms SBP.**

**For iron-deficiency anaemia in an older adult, what is the most seductive trap?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Commence oral iron and recheck the blood count in 3 months.
- _Why it tempts you:_ It is cheap, non-invasive, treats the anaemia, and 'trial of iron' is standard GP practice for many anaemias — it feels proportionate and patient-friendly.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ In any man or postmenopausal woman, IDA is colorectal/upper GI malignancy until proven otherwise; empiric iron masks an occult cancer and delays a curable diagnosis.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Demographic + proven IDA (low ferritin) = bidirectional endoscopy. The presence of a malignancy-risk demographic overrides the temptation to treat empirically.**

**In acute severe ulcerative colitis, what is the counter-intuitive distractor?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Withhold/hold anticoagulation because the patient is passing bloody stools.
- _Why it tempts you:_ It seems physiologically obvious — you do not anticoagulate someone who is visibly bleeding from the bowel; giving heparin feels like adding fuel to fire.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Active IBD is strongly prothrombotic; VTE (DVT/PE) is a leading cause of death in ASUC, so prophylactic LMWH is mandatory despite the rectal bleeding.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **The bleeding is mucosal/luminal, not a systemic coagulopathy — luminal bleeding does not contraindicate prophylactic-dose LMWH. Always give VTE prophylaxis in ASUC.**

**With a raised ALP, what is the classic distractor that sends you down the wrong path?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Arrange liver/biliary imaging (USS or MRCP) for the 'abnormal LFT'.
- _Why it tempts you:_ ALP is on the 'liver panel', so a raised ALP reads as a cholestatic liver problem demanding biliary imaging.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ If the GGT is normal, the source is bone (Paget's, metastases), not the liver — imaging the biliary tree wastes time and misdirects.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Check the GGT: raised GGT = hepatobiliary; normal GGT = bone. GGT is the discriminator that localises the ALP.**

**In a patient with a high ferritin, what is the seductive overcall?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Diagnose hereditary haemochromatosis and proceed straight to HFE genotyping / venesection on the ferritin alone.
- _Why it tempts you:_ Ferritin is the headline iron-overload marker and a very high number screams haemochromatosis, especially in someone of Celtic ancestry.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Ferritin is an acute-phase reactant — it rises with inflammation, infection, malignancy, alcohol and metabolic syndrome far more commonly than with iron overload. Genotyping requires a raised TRANSFERRIN SATURATION as well.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Transferrin saturation: only when fasting transferrin sat is raised (>45%) AND ferritin is elevated do you genotype. Isolated high ferritin with normal transferrin sat is not haemochromatosis.**

**In severe acute pancreatitis, what is the deadliest distractor?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Commence empirical broad-spectrum IV antibiotics.
- _Why it tempts you:_ The patient looks septic (SIRS, fever, organ dysfunction) and 'severe + unwell = antibiotics' is a deeply ingrained reflex.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Prophylactic antibiotics do not improve outcomes in acute pancreatitis and are only indicated for proven infected necrosis (or concurrent cholangitis) — the early SIRS is inflammatory, not infective.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **The core early intervention is aggressive fluid resuscitation, not antibiotics. Reserve antibiotics for infected necrosis or cholangitis specifically.**

**In a patient already on a gluten-free diet being tested for coeliac, what is the trap?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Reassure that negative tTG-IgA and normal duodenal biopsy exclude coeliac disease.
- _Why it tempts you:_ Negative serology plus normal histology looks like a clean, definitive negative result.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Both tTG and biopsy normalise on a gluten-free diet, producing false negatives — a negative test in a gluten-avoiding patient does not exclude coeliac.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Was the patient eating gluten? If gluten-free, either reintroduce gluten before testing OR use HLA-DQ2/DQ8 (a negative genotype effectively excludes coeliac).**

### High-end review pearls

- If you see haematemesis in a known cirrhotic, think 'terlipressin + ceftriaxone BEFORE the scope' — the antibiotic is the mortality lever, not the band.
- Aunt Minnie: diabetes + 2nd/3rd MCP arthropathy + bronze skin + deranged LFTs in a Celtic-ancestry Australian = haemochromatosis. Confirm with raised transferrin sat AND ferritin, then HFE genotype.
- Never-forget one-liner: raised ALP + NORMAL GGT = bone, not liver. The GGT is your localiser before any imaging.
- If you see ascites + fever, your hand reaches for the paracentesis needle, not the antibiotic bag. Tap first, neutrophils >250 = SBP, then cefotaxime + albumin.
- A normal ALT does NOT mean a healthy liver — burnt-out cirrhosis runs near-normal transaminases. Synthetic function (INR, albumin, bilirubin) is the true severity gauge.
- Australian trap: NBCSP now screens from age 45 (not 50) to 74, 2-yearly iFOBT. A positive iFOBT always means colonoscopy, never a repeat FOBT.
- Australian trap: do NOT quote norfloxacin for SBP prophylaxis — it is deregistered here. Use co-trimoxazole or ciprofloxacin.
- Restrictive transfusion threshold trap: transfuse below Hb 70 (target 70-90); the higher threshold (below 80, target 100) is for ESTABLISHED cardiovascular disease, NOT for 'active bleeding'. Over-transfusing varices worsens portal pressure.
- Severe alcoholic hepatitis: oral PREDNISOLONE (not prednisone — no hepatic conversion needed), only after excluding sepsis/GI bleed, then Lille score at day 7 — stop if Lille >=0.45. STOPAH: short-term benefit only.
- If you see ALT >1000 + rising INR in an Australian ED, think paracetamol — give IV N-acetylcysteine even late, and screen against King's College Criteria for transplant referral.
- AST:ALT >2 is NOT pathognomonic for alcohol — it also flips in advanced cirrhosis of ANY cause. And alcoholic hepatitis AST is usually <300-500, so a very high ALT argues AGAINST simple alcohol.
- Lipase >3x ULN diagnoses pancreatitis; severity is defined by organ failure (Glasgow/APACHE), not pain. Antibiotics are NOT routine — only for infected necrosis or cholangitis. Fluids first.
- Acute severe UC is prothrombotic — give VTE prophylaxis despite the bloody diarrhoea, and NEVER give antimotility agents (toxic megacolon risk). Day-3 review drives rescue vs colectomy.
- ASUC rescue list for 2026: infliximab OR JAK inhibitor (upadacitinib — rapid onset, increasingly first-choice) OR ciclosporin. Quoting only infliximab/ciclosporin now looks dated.
- Coeliac: tTG-IgA must be paired with total IgA (IgA deficiency = false negative), and BOTH serology and biopsy must be on a gluten-containing diet. HLA-DQ2/DQ8 negative effectively excludes coeliac — useful in the already-gluten-free patient.
- Hepatitis C in Australia is curable in primary care: any anti-HCV positive -> check HCV RNA to confirm active infection -> treat with PBS pangenotypic DAAs (glecaprevir/pibrentasvir 8wk or sofosbuvir/velpatasvir 12wk), >95% cure. Do not just monitor.
- MASLD (the renamed NAFLD) is Australia's commonest liver disease — stage fibrosis with FIB-4 first (FIB-4 <1.3 effectively excludes advanced fibrosis), then FibroScan. Metabolic risk + fibrosis drives referral.
- HCC surveillance in Australia (George et al, MJA 2025) = 6-monthly ultrasound PLUS AFP — for all cirrhotics and for non-cirrhotic chronic hepatitis B by age/sex/ethnicity (e.g. Asian men >40, Asian women >50, African >20, Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander).
- Charcot triad (RUQ pain + fever + jaundice) = ascending cholangitis; add hypotension + confusion = Reynolds pentad. Management is IV antibiotics PLUS urgent biliary drainage (ERCP) — biliary sepsis needs the duct decompressed.
- Hepatorenal syndrome (HRS-AKI) is functional renal failure and a diagnosis of exclusion after a volume challenge with albumin — treat with terlipressin + albumin.
- Alcohol bundle: thiamine BEFORE glucose (Wernicke prevention), symptom-triggered diazepam for withdrawal, and NHMRC limits = no more than 10 standard drinks/week and no more than 4 in a day (1 Aus standard drink = 10 g alcohol).

> **Independent item audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[low]** Q1 (variceal haematemesis) — answer B, terlipressin + ceftriaxone; and stem framing: The stem describes a drowsy patient with large-volume haematemesis and explicitly states he is 'protecting his airway,' then asks for the 'single most important next step.' Answer B is correct per guidelines, but a purist examiner could argue airway/aspiration risk is the true priority in a drowsy variceal bleeder (these patients frequently need intubation for airway protection before endoscopy). The stem pre-empts this by saying he protects his airway, which is acceptable, but it slightly blunts the realism. More importantly, the explanation states the restrictive target is '70-90' and that 'target 100 is for established cardiovascular disease' — this conflates two things: the Villanueva/ESGE restrictive strategy transfuses below 70 with target 70-90; the higher threshold for cardiovascular disease is below 80, not a 'target of 100.' The option A wording ('target Hb 100') is fine as a distractor, but the explanation should not imply 100 is an evidence-based target even in IHD. → Tighten the explanation: in established CVD the threshold is ~Hb 80 (target up to ~100 is pragmatic, not trial-defined). Keep answer B. Optionally add an explicit clause that airway is secured/patient protecting it (already present) to keep single-best-answer clean.
> - **[low]** Q7 (alcoholic hepatitis, Lille 0.60) — answer C and explanation: Answer C (stop prednisolone, non-responder) is correct: Lille >=0.45 at day 7 = non-responder, discontinue steroids. This is well supported. However the explanation's claim that prednisolone is chosen over prednisone 'because it needs no hepatic conversion' is presented as established fact. This is the conventional exam rationale but is a theoretical/unproven advantage (prednisolone is preferred but never demonstrated superior; STOPAH used prednisolone). Stating it as the reason a switch is 'illogical' overstates the evidence. → Soften to: 'prednisolone is the standard agent (preferred over prednisone as it avoids the theoretical issue of impaired hepatic conversion); switching to IV hydrocortisone is not indicated.' Answer unchanged.
> - **[low]** highEndExtras — HCC surveillance line ('6-monthly ultrasound PLUS AFP'): The George et al MJA 2025 Australian guidelines recommend 6-monthly ultrasound WITH OR WITHOUT AFP, and explicitly note that adding AFP may not be incrementally cost-effective over ultrasound alone. The extra states ultrasound 'PLUS AFP' as if AFP is mandatory, which overstates the recommendation. → Reword to '6-monthly ultrasound (with or without AFP)' to match the 2025 Australian consensus wording.
> - **[low]** Q9 / highEndExtras — NBCSP screening age phrasing: The eligible screening age was lowered to 45 from 1 July 2024 (people 45-49 must opt in/request a kit; 50-74 are auto-invited). The item and teaching points correctly say 'from age 45 to 74' and 'lowered from 50,' which is accurate. No error in the age itself, but the explanation says the 47-year-old is 'squarely within the program' without noting 45-49-year-olds must actively request the kit rather than being auto-invited — a minor completeness gap, not an error affecting the answer. → Optionally add that 45-49 year-olds opt in by request while 50-74 are automatically invited. Answer C (colonoscopy for positive iFOBT) is fully correct and unaffected.
> - **[low]** Q4 (ASUC) — answer A (prophylactic LMWH) as 'most important additional intervention': Answer A is defensible and the VTE-prophylaxis-despite-bleeding teaching point is genuinely high-yield. But 'most important' is a slight value judgement: stool culture/C. difficile testing and the day-3 review structure are also critical, and an examiner could argue confirming/excluding C. difficile is equally 'important on admission.' The provided options do not include stool testing, so among the listed options A is clearly the single best answer (B, C, D, E are all wrong/contraindicated). The item is clean as written; the only risk is the superlative 'most important' if a candidate over-thinks infection screening. → No change required; optionally reword stem to 'which listed additional intervention is mandatory on admission' to remove any ambiguity. Distractors are strong.
> - **[low]** schemaRecall — SBP schema, secondary prophylaxis agents: The schema states secondary SBP prophylaxis in Australia uses co-trimoxazole or ciprofloxacin and that norfloxacin is 'dated/deregistered.' Norfloxacin is indeed no longer marketed in Australia, so this is correct. Minor note: it is worth being explicit that this is the Australian-specific deviation from international (EASL) guidance which still lists norfloxacin first-line — otherwise a candidate using international texts may think the schema is wrong. → Add a one-line note that international guidelines still cite norfloxacin first-line but it is unavailable in Australia, hence co-trimoxazole/ciprofloxacin. No factual change.
> 
> _Quality notes:_ Overall this is a strong, exam-realistic set. Every stated correct answer is genuinely the single best answer per 2026 Australian/contemporary guidelines; I found no answer that is outright wrong and no distractor that is accidentally also-correct.; Distractors are genuinely seductive, not strawmen. The strongest traps are well-chosen: 'urgent endoscopy/banding first' in the variceal bleed (Q1), 'start cefotaxime before tapping' in SBP (Q2), 'trial of oral iron' in IDA (Q3), 'reassure, below screening age' exploiting the outdated age-50 cutoff (Q9), and 'ferritin is an acute-phase reactant' in haemochromatosis (Q6, where the raised transferrin sat is the discriminator). These are the real deadly traps candidates fall for.; The verified facts all hold: restrictive transfusion threshold 70 / target 70-90 (Villanueva/ESGE); Lille >=0.45 at day 7 = non-responder, stop steroids; Oxford/Travis day-3 criteria (>8 stools, or CRP>45 with 3-8 stools); HFE genotyping requires raised TSAT (>45%) AND ferritin; bidirectional endoscopy for IDA in men/postmenopausal women with parallel coeliac serology; positive iFOBT always mandates colonoscopy.; No item is too easy in a way that wastes a question — even the 'obvious' ones (Q8 NAC, Q9 colonoscopy) carry a genuine examined nuance (NAC works even late / give regardless of nomogram in established hepatotoxicity; the age-45 NBCSP change).; Single-best-answer integrity is clean across all 9 MCQs: in each, the four distractors are individually defensible reasons a candidate might pick them but each has a clear discriminating flaw, and only one option survives.; Minor over-confidence in two explanations (prednisolone-no-hepatic-conversion stated as fact; AFP implied mandatory in HCC surveillance) — these are teaching-nuance overstatements, not answer-changing errors.; The schemaRecall and distractorDrills are well-aligned with the MCQs and reinforce the same high-yield discriminators (GGT localises ALP; transferrin sat localises ferritin; tap-before-antibiotics; drugs-before-scope; VTE prophylaxis despite luminal bleeding). The added severe-pancreatitis distractor (no prophylactic antibiotics) and gluten-free coeliac trap are both correct and high-yield even though no dedicated MCQ covers them.

---

## Infectious Diseases & Antimicrobial Stewardship  _(~11% of marks)_

### The one idea that unlocks the domain

> **Every ID answer is derived from one chain: SOURCE → likely ORGANISM(S) → narrowest eTG-correct DRUG, modulated by host (immunocompromise, allergy, region) and severity (does sepsis force broad-spectrum/IV now?). Own "anatomical source predicts microbiology predicts empirical therapy," and you can reconstruct almost any single-best-answer without memorising drug lists.**

Passmedicine-style ID MCQs rarely test a free-floating fact; they test whether you can run the source→bug→drug engine under constraints. Once you internalise that the SITE of infection dictates the probable organisms (skin = Staph/Strep; urinary = E. coli/Gram-negatives; meningitis = pneumococcus/meningococcus/Listeria-if-extremes; lung = pneumococcus/atypicals), the empirical antibiotic falls out almost deterministically because eTG is built on exactly this logic. The same engine then absorbs the modifiers the examiner layers on: SEVERITY escalates you (oral flucloxacillin for cellulitis → IV, add vancomycin if MRSA risk or septic; add meropenem if life-threatening). HOST shifts the differential (neutropenia → Pseudomonas cover with piperacillin-tazobactam; HIV with low CD4 → opportunistic organisms; >50/pregnant/immunosuppressed → Listeria cover with benzylpenicillin). GEOGRAPHY rewrites the bug list (tropical north + sepsis/pneumonia + diabetes/alcohol → melioidosis → ceftazidime/meropenem, NOT standard CAP cover). ALLERGY narrows your options and is itself a stewardship test (de-label non-severe penicillin allergy; true severe allergy avoids beta-lactams). Stewardship is the meta-layer: choose the NARROWEST effective agent, the SHORTEST evidence-based duration, de-escalate on cultures, and source-control. If you can state the source, name the two or three bugs, and apply severity/host/geography/allergy modifiers, you will derive the keyed answer even on a drug you have never explicitly revised, because the examiner constructed the question from the same engine.

### High-yield tutorial

## The engine: SOURCE → BUG → DRUG, bent by severity, host, geography, allergy

**Step 1 — Sepsis is a time test, not a diagnosis test.** If the stem describes a febrile, tachycardic, hypotensive (or lactate >2) patient, the keyed action is almost always **first-hour resuscitation**: take **blood cultures BEFORE antibiotics**, give **broad-spectrum IV antibiotics within 1 hour**, **30 mL/kg crystalloid** for hypotension/lactate ≥4, measure lactate, monitor urine output. **Do not delay antibiotics for the LP or imaging.** "Most important next step" in septic shock = antibiotics + fluids, not a CT. Vasopressor of choice = **noradrenaline** once fluid-replete.

**Step 2 — Map source to bug to eTG drug.**
- **Cellulitis (non-purulent):** Strep > Staph → **flucloxacillin** (oral di/flucloxacillin if mild; IV if systemic). Penicillin allergy → **cephalexin** (non-severe) or **clindamycin/lincomycin** (severe).
- **Purulent/abscess or MRSA risk:** cover **MRSA** → **trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole** or **doxycycline** (community), **vancomycin** if severe/IV.
- **Necrotising fasciitis:** surgical emergency. **Urgent debridement** + **meropenem + vancomycin + clindamycin** (clinda for toxin suppression). Pain out of proportion, rapid progression, systemic toxicity → operate, don't await imaging.
- **Bone/joint (septic arthritis, osteo):** S. aureus dominant → **flucloxacillin** (add **vancomycin** if MRSA risk); aspirate/sample before antibiotics where feasible.
- **UTI/pyelonephritis:** E. coli → **trimethoprim** (cystitis), **gentamicin ± ampicillin** or **ceftriaxone** (pyelo). ESBL → **meropenem**.
- **CAP:** severity-stratified (CORB/SMART-COP). Mild → **amoxicillin** + **doxycycline**; severe → **benzylpenicillin + gentamicin + azithromycin** or **ceftriaxone + azithromycin**. In the **tropical wet season** with severe CAP/sepsis → **add meropenem for melioidosis**.

**Step 3 — Meningitis decision tree.** Suspected bacterial meningitis: **dexamethasone + empirical antibiotics immediately; do not delay for LP/CT.** Empirical = **ceftriaxone** (covers pneumococcus + meningococcus); **add benzylpenicillin/amoxicillin for Listeria if >50, pregnant, immunocompromised, neonate.** CT before LP only if focal deficit, seizures, GCS drop, or immunocompromise. **CSF read:** bacterial = neutrophils, high protein, LOW glucose (<50% serum); viral = lymphocytes, normal-ish glucose; TB/fungal = lymphocytes, very high protein, very low glucose. Encephalitis (altered mental state + fever) → **empirical aciclovir for HSV**, don't wait for PCR.

**Step 4 — Geography rewrites the bug list (the guaranteed regional item).** **Melioidosis (Burkholderia pseudomallei):** northern Australia (Top End, FNQ), **wet season**, **soil/water exposure**, risk factors **diabetes, hazardous alcohol, chronic lung/renal disease, Indigenous**. Presents as severe pneumonia, sepsis, abscesses (lung, liver, splenic, prostatic). **Intensive phase = ceftazidime OR meropenem (meropenem if neuro/severe) ≥2 weeks; eradication phase = trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole 3+ months.** This is the highest-probability single regional MCQ — if the stem is "diabetic in Darwin, wet season, severe pneumonia/sepsis," the answer is **ceftazidime/meropenem**, not ceftriaxone.

**Other Australian zoonoses (pattern-match):**
- **Q fever (Coxiella burnetii):** abattoir/farm/livestock exposure → **doxycycline**; vaccine-preventable (Q-Vax, screen first).
- **Leptospirosis:** freshwater/mud, floods, farming → fever + **conjunctival suffusion** + myalgia + jaundice/AKI (Weil's) → **doxycycline/benzylpenicillin**.
- **Ross River virus:** polyarthralgia + rash, post-mosquito, lingering arthritis → supportive only.
- **Scrub typhus (Orientia):** northern Aus/SE Asia, **eschar** + fever → **doxycycline**.
- **Dengue:** returned traveller/FNQ, fever + retro-orbital pain, **thrombocytopenia**, warning signs → supportive, **avoid NSAIDs**.

**Step 5 — Host modifiers.** **Febrile neutropenia** = emergency → **piperacillin-tazobactam** (anti-Pseudomonal) within an hour. **HIV:** suspect on recurrent zoster, oral candidiasis, weight loss, unusual/opportunistic infection; **CD4 <200 → PJP** (cough, exertional desaturation, raised LDH) → **TMP-SMX + steroids if PaO2 <70**; **CD4 <50 → MAC, CMV, cryptococcus.** Always test HIV in TB. **TB / returned traveller:** stepwise febrile-traveller approach — **malaria thick/thin films first** (kills fastest, treatable), then enteric fever, dengue, etc., by incubation and exposure. TB = **RIPE** (rifampicin, isoniazid, pyrazinamide, ethambutol) 2 months then RI 4 months.

**Step 6 — Stewardship is the meta-rule.** Choose the **narrowest** agent that covers the likely bug; **de-escalate** when cultures return; use the **shortest** evidence-based course; pursue **source control** (drain the abscess, remove the line). **Penicillin allergy de-labelling:** most "allergy" labels are non-severe — a low-risk history (childhood rash, no anaphylaxis/SJS) can be **de-labelled / direct oral challenged**, restoring first-line beta-lactams and reducing broad-spectrum overuse. **Resistance flags:** **MRSA → vancomycin**; **ESBL Gram-negatives → meropenem**; avoid carbapenems unless indicated to protect against **CRE**. The examiner rewards the answer that is correct AND narrowest.

### Highest mark-per-minute principles

- **In sepsis: cultures, then antibiotics within 1 hour, then fluids — never delay antibiotics for LP or imaging** — Single highest-frequency 'next best step' item; one rule answers many stems
  - _Exam angle:_ Septic shock + 'most appropriate immediate action' → IV broad-spectrum antibiotics + 30 mL/kg fluid; noradrenaline if still hypotensive
- **Source dictates organism dictates eTG empirical drug** — Lets you derive the keyed antibiotic without memorising every regimen
  - _Exam angle:_ Cellulitis→flucloxacillin; pyelo→gentamicin±amoxicillin; meningitis→ceftriaxone(±benzylpen for Listeria)
- **Tropical north + wet season + diabetic/alcohol + severe pneumonia/sepsis = melioidosis** — Guaranteed Australian regional item with a non-obvious answer
  - _Exam angle:_ Answer is ceftazidime or meropenem (NOT ceftriaxone); eradication with TMP-SMX months
- **Listeria cover (benzylpenicillin/amoxicillin) added to ceftriaxone at extremes of age, pregnancy, immunocompromise** — Classic distractor — ceftriaxone alone misses Listeria
  - _Exam angle:_ 70-year-old with meningitis → ceftriaxone PLUS benzylpenicillin + dexamethasone
- **Necrotising fasciitis is clinical and surgical — operate, don't image** — Tests recognition of pain-out-of-proportion and refusal to delay
  - _Exam angle:_ Rapidly spreading, systemic toxicity → urgent debridement + meropenem+vancomycin+clindamycin
- **CD4 count predicts the opportunistic infection** — One number generates the whole HIV differential
  - _Exam angle:_ <200 PJP (TMP-SMX + steroids if PaO2<70); <100 toxoplasma/cryptococcus; <50 MAC/CMV
- **Doxycycline is the answer for most Australian intracellular zoonoses** — Collapses Q fever, leptospirosis, scrub typhus, rickettsia into one drug
  - _Exam angle:_ Eschar→scrub typhus→doxy; abattoir→Q fever→doxy; floodwater+jaundice→lepto→doxy/penicillin
- **Penicillin allergy de-labelling and narrowest-spectrum choice are stewardship answers** — Stewardship questions reward the narrow, de-escalated, shortest-course option
  - _Exam angle:_ Non-severe childhood rash → de-label/oral challenge → use flucloxacillin not vancomycin
- **Febrile neutropenia needs anti-Pseudomonal cover within an hour** — High-stakes emergency with a specific drug
  - _Exam angle:_ Post-chemo fever + neutrophils <0.5 → piperacillin-tazobactam immediately
- **Febrile returned traveller — exclude malaria first** — Sequencing rule; malaria kills fastest and is treatable
  - _Exam angle:_ Fever after the tropics → thick and thin films before chasing dengue/typhoid

### Must-know numbers (Australian where they differ)

- Sepsis bundle: blood cultures before antibiotics; broad-spectrum IV antibiotics within 1 hour; fluid bolus 30 mL/kg for hypotension or lactate ≥4 mmol/L
- Lactate >2 mmol/L = hypoperfusion flag; ≥4 = mandatory aggressive resuscitation
- CSF bacterial: neutrophils, protein >1 g/L, glucose <50% of serum (CSF:serum ratio <0.5); viral: lymphocytes, normal glucose
- Listeria cover triggers: age >50, neonate, pregnancy, immunocompromise
- Melioidosis: intensive phase ceftazidime/meropenem ≥2 weeks (≥4 if deep-seated/neuro), eradication TMP-SMX 3–6 months
- HIV CD4 <200 → PJP prophylaxis/treatment; PJP steroids if PaO2 <70 mmHg or A-a gradient >35
- Febrile neutropenia = neutrophils <0.5 x10^9/L (or <1.0 falling) + fever ≥38.0–38.3°C
- TB therapy: RIPE 2 months intensive + rifampicin/isoniazid 4 months continuation (6 months total, drug-sensitive)
- Cellulitis duration typically 5–7 days (eTG), reassess; uncomplicated cystitis trimethoprim 3 days
- Dexamethasone 10 mg IV with/before first antibiotic dose in suspected bacterial meningitis
- Penicillin allergy: ~90% of labels are not true allergy and are de-labellable

### Cross-topic cuts

- Sepsis ↔ Critical care/shock: distinguishing septic (warm, vasodilated, noradrenaline) from cardiogenic/hypovolaemic shock
- Meningitis ↔ Neurology: SAH vs meningitis on LP (xanthochromia), encephalitis vs first-seizure workup, raised ICP contraindicating LP
- HIV ↔ Respiratory: PJP vs bacterial CAP vs TB on CXR and desaturation pattern; LDH
- Melioidosis/zoonoses ↔ Endocrine: diabetes as the dominant risk factor; alcohol-related liver disease
- ESBL/MRSA ↔ Renal: vancomycin and gentamicin dosing/nephrotoxicity and therapeutic monitoring
- Febrile neutropenia ↔ Haematology/Oncology: chemotherapy timing, tumour lysis overlap
- Q fever ↔ Cardiology: chronic Q fever endocarditis with culture-negative endocarditis
- Leptospirosis/dengue ↔ Renal/Haematology: AKI, thrombocytopenia, DIC
- TB/HIV ↔ Public health & Indigenous health: notification, contact tracing, screening migrants

### Australian-specific (where the UK answer loses you marks)

- Empirical antibiotics follow Therapeutic Guidelines (eTG), not UK BNF/NICE: flucloxacillin/di(cloxacillin) for cellulitis, gentamicin-based regimens used more freely for pyelonephritis and severe sepsis
- Melioidosis (Burkholderia pseudomallei) is a uniquely northern-Australian exam item — absent from UK Passmedicine; ceftazidime/meropenem then TMP-SMX
- Australian zoonoses examiners love: Ross River/Barmah Forest virus polyarthritis, Q fever (Q-Vax vaccine, abattoir workers), leptospirosis (floods/farming), scrub typhus and rickettsial spotted fevers (eschar), dengue in FNQ/returned travellers
- Severe CAP in tropical Australia adds melioidosis (meropenem) and consider Acinetobacter; gentamicin+benzylpenicillin+azithromycin is a standard eTG severe-CAP option
- Indigenous Australians carry disproportionate burden: rheumatic heart disease, bronchiectasis, melioidosis, invasive Strep/Staph, higher community-associated MRSA, crusted (Norwegian) scabies, trachoma, and higher TB and STI rates — answers often hinge on this epidemiology
- Strongyloides stercoralis screening before immunosuppression/steroids in at-risk (Indigenous, tropical, returned travellers) to prevent hyperinfection
- Acute rheumatic fever / RHD prophylaxis (benzathine penicillin) and acute post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis remain high-burden in remote communities — a Strep skin/throat complication angle
- Penicillin allergy de-labelling is an active Australian stewardship priority (AURA/national programs); direct oral challenge for low-risk histories
- HIV: notifiable; PJP and cryptococcal disease as AIDS-defining presentations follow the same CD4 logic but with Australian testing/PrEP context
- Antimicrobial stewardship governed by NSQHS Standards and AURA surveillance; CRE and ESBL containment emphasised

> **Independent accuracy audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[high]** Sepsis bundle: '30 mL/kg crystalloid for hypotension/lactate ≥4' and 'fluid bolus 30 mL/kg for hypotension or lactate ≥4 mmol/L' (stated as a must-know number and repeated in highYieldPrinciples). → Reframe as: give a 250-500 mL crystalloid bolus (balanced fluid e.g. Hartmann's/Plasma-Lyte preferred over saline), reassess after each bolus, titrate to perfusion/lactate, and move early to noradrenaline rather than continuing large fixed-volume fluids. Source: Sepsis Australia / Australian Sepsis Network Clinical Management guidance; ARISE-FLUIDS (EMA 2024-2025).
> - **[high]** UTI/pyelonephritis: 'E. coli → trimethoprim (cystitis)' and must-know number 'uncomplicated cystitis trimethoprim 3 days'. Trimethoprim presented as the default first-line for cystitis. → First-line empirical cystitis is now nitrofurantoin 100 mg 6-hourly for 5 days (eTG March 2025); trimethoprim 300 mg daily for 3 days is now a second-line/conditional option. Source: tg.org.au Antibiotic March 2025 update.
> - **[medium]** Bone/joint and cellulitis directed therapy implicitly anchored on flucloxacillin as the sole first-line for S. aureus ('S. aureus dominant → flucloxacillin'; cellulitis → flucloxacillin). → State cefazolin as an equal first-line alternative to flucloxacillin for MSSA directed therapy; note cefalexin now acceptable for paediatric cellulitis/impetigo. Source: eTG Antibiotic March 2025.
> - **[medium]** UTI/pyelonephritis: 'gentamicin ± ampicillin or ceftriaxone (pyelo)' presented as the standard pyelonephritis approach. → Add the severity split: non-pregnant, non-septic pyelonephritis can be oral first-line; reserve gentamicin (+ amoxicillin/ampicillin in pregnancy) for sepsis/severe disease. Source: eTG Antibiotic March 2025.
> - **[low]** 'Vasopressor of choice = noradrenaline once fluid-replete' and other sepsis bundle elements presented as a US-style fixed 1-hour bundle. → Keep noradrenaline as first-line; clarify that in Australian practice vasopressors are started early rather than only after a large fixed fluid volume. Source: Australian Sepsis Network; ARISE-FLUIDS.
> - **[low]** Necrotising fasciitis empirical regimen: 'meropenem + vancomycin + clindamycin'. → State: vancomycin + (piperacillin-tazobactam or meropenem) + (clindamycin or lincomycin). Source: eTG Antibiotic, necrotising soft tissue infection.
> - **[low]** 'Febrile neutropenia = neutrophils <0.5 x10^9/L (or <1.0 falling) + fever ≥38.0-38.3°C' and treatment with piperacillin-tazobactam within an hour. → Optionally tighten to the common Australian trigger (single temp ≥38.5°C, or ≥38.0°C sustained ≥1 hr) and note cefepime/ceftazidime as beta-lactam alternatives. Source: eTG Antibiotic, febrile neutropenia.
> - **[low]** Melioidosis intensive phase 'ceftazidime OR meropenem (meropenem if neuro/severe) ≥2 weeks' and must-know '≥4 if deep-seated/neuro'. → State intensive phase is a MINIMUM of 2 weeks but frequently 4-8 weeks in Australian practice depending on site (neuro, bone, deep collections); ceftazidime first-line, meropenem if ICU/neurological/severe; eradication TMP-SMX 3-6 months. Source: Australian (NT/Royal Darwin) melioidosis guidelines; eTG.
> 
> _Gaps flagged & folded in:_ eTG March 2025 dosing-frequency change: in adults with community-acquired sepsis WITHOUT septic shock/ICU, REDUCED-frequency dosing of flucloxacillin and cefazolin is now recommended; septic shock/ICU patients need MORE frequent dosing for adequate exposure. This is a current, examinable Australian-specific change the tutorial omits entirely.; Cefazolin = equal first-line to flucloxacillin for MSSA (CAMERA-2/SNAP), with lower AKI risk than high-dose IV flucloxacillin — a stewardship/safety point now central to Australian S. aureus management.; Balanced crystalloids (Hartmann's/Plasma-Lyte) preferred over 0.9% saline for resuscitation (PLUS-trial era / Australian practice) — the tutorial says '30 mL/kg crystalloid' without specifying balanced fluid.; Acute rheumatic fever / RHD secondary prophylaxis specifics: benzathine benzylpenicillin G IM every 21-28 days, and the 2020 Australian ARF/RHD guideline (RHDAustralia) Jones criteria including a high-risk-population category for Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people — high-yield remote-health item only glancingly mentioned.; Crusted (Norwegian) scabies management in Indigenous/immunocompromised patients: combination oral ivermectin PLUS topical (benzyl benzoate/permethrin) plus keratolytics, public-health notification in some jurisdictions — a recurrent Australian exam item not in the tutorial body.; Invasive group A Strep / APSGN and acute post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis as skin-sore complications in remote communities, and the role of impetigo treatment (single-dose oral co-trimoxazole programs) — Australian-specific Strep skin disease angle.; Japanese encephalitis virus (JEV): since 2022 JEV is now established/notifiable across mainland southeastern Australia (Murray-Darling basin), with a national vaccination program for at-risk groups — a NEW post-2022 Australian arbovirus the tutorial omits while listing older arboviruses.; Buruli ulcer (Mycobacterium ulcerans): endemic and rising in coastal Victoria (Mornington/Bellarine Peninsulas) — painless nodule/ulcer, treated with rifampicin + clarithromycin; a uniquely Australian mycobacterial item absent from the zoonosis list.; Spotted fever rickettsioses specific to Australia (Queensland tick typhus / Rickettsia australis; Flinders Island spotted fever / R. honei) — name the Australian species, not just generic 'rickettsia', all doxycycline-treated.; Penicillin allergy de-labelling governance: the national AURA surveillance system and NSQHS Antimicrobial Stewardship Standard underpin de-labelling, and validated risk-stratification tools (e.g. PEN-FAST) are the Australian-endorsed way to identify low-risk patients for direct oral challenge — tutorial cites de-labelling but not PEN-FAST/AURA framework.; Gentamicin and vancomycin therapeutic drug monitoring per Australian practice: vancomycin now dosed to AUC/MIC target (not just trough) per updated eTG/ASID guidance — relevant to the renal cross-topic cut.; HIV PrEP/PEP and U=U (undetectable=untransmissible) within the Australian testing framework, plus the recommendation to offer/start ART early — the tutorial mentions PrEP only in passing.; Nitrofurantoin caution: avoid in eGFR <45 mL/min and near term in pregnancy — important counterpoint now that nitrofurantoin is first-line for cystitis in Australia.

### Applied MCQs

**Q1.** A 58-year-old man with type 2 diabetes and hazardous alcohol use presents to Royal Darwin Hospital in February (wet season) after working in his flooded backyard. He has 3 days of fever, productive cough and pleuritic pain. BP 88/52, HR 122, RR 30, lactate 4.1 mmol/L. CXR shows a dense right upper lobe consolidation with early cavitation. Blood cultures are taken. Which empirical antibiotic regimen is most appropriate?

   - A. Ceftriaxone + azithromycin
   - B. Benzylpenicillin + gentamicin + azithromycin
   - C. Meropenem (and treat as melioidosis)
   - D. Amoxicillin + doxycycline
   - E. Piperacillin-tazobactam

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Meropenem (and treat as melioidosis)**

This is the archetypal melioidosis stem: Top End, wet season, soil/water exposure, diabetes plus hazardous alcohol, severe cavitating pneumonia with septic shock. In severe/ICU-level disease the intensive-phase agent is meropenem (ceftazidime is first-line for non-ICU severe disease, meropenem preferred if ICU/neurological/very severe). Standard severe-CAP regimens miss Burkholderia pseudomallei, which is intrinsically resistant to many agents. A and B are correct empirical regimens for severe CAP in southern Australia but do NOT cover melioidosis, so they are the seductive distractors here. D is mild-CAP therapy and grossly under-treats septic shock. E (pip-taz) covers Pseudomonas and is the febrile neutropenia answer, not melioidosis cover.

_Teaching point:_ Tropical north + wet season + diabetic/alcohol + severe pneumonia or sepsis = melioidosis: ceftazidime (or meropenem if ICU/neuro/severe), never plain ceftriaxone.
</details>

**Q2.** A previously well 29-year-old woman presents to her GP in suburban Melbourne with 2 days of dysuria, frequency and suprapubic discomfort. She is afebrile, haemodynamically normal, not pregnant, and has no flank pain. Urine dipstick shows leucocytes and nitrites. eGFR is normal. According to current Australian Therapeutic Guidelines, what is the most appropriate first-line empirical antibiotic?

   - A. Trimethoprim 300 mg orally daily for 3 days
   - B. Nitrofurantoin 100 mg orally 6-hourly for 5 days
   - C. Cefalexin 500 mg orally 12-hourly for 5 days
   - D. Ciprofloxacin 250 mg orally 12-hourly for 3 days
   - E. Amoxicillin-clavulanate 500/125 mg orally 8-hourly for 5 days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Nitrofurantoin 100 mg orally 6-hourly for 5 days**

Per the eTG Antibiotic March 2025 update, nitrofurantoin is now first-line empirical therapy for acute uncomplicated cystitis because of rising E. coli trimethoprim resistance. Trimethoprim (A) has been DEMOTED to a second-line/conditional option (cost/susceptibility-driven) and is the deadliest distractor here because older teaching and many memorised answers still say 3-day trimethoprim. C (cefalexin) and E (amoxicillin-clavulanate) are broader-spectrum alternatives, not first-line for uncomplicated cystitis (stewardship penalises them). D (ciprofloxacin) is reserved to spare fluoroquinolones. Her normal eGFR makes nitrofurantoin safe (avoid if eGFR <45).

_Teaching point:_ eTG March 2025: nitrofurantoin is first-line for uncomplicated cystitis; trimethoprim is now second-line. Check eGFR (avoid nitrofurantoin if <45) and avoid near term in pregnancy.
</details>

**Q3.** A 71-year-old woman is brought to ED with 12 hours of fever, headache, neck stiffness and increasing drowsiness (GCS 13). She has no focal neurological deficit, no papilloedema, no seizures and is not immunocompromised. Bacterial meningitis is suspected. What is the single most appropriate immediate management?

   - A. Urgent CT head, then LP, then antibiotics
   - B. LP first to confirm diagnosis, then antibiotics
   - C. Dexamethasone + ceftriaxone alone, immediately
   - D. Dexamethasone + ceftriaxone + benzylpenicillin, immediately
   - E. Ceftriaxone + vancomycin, immediately, withholding steroids

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Dexamethasone + ceftriaxone + benzylpenicillin, immediately**

Do not delay antibiotics for LP or imaging in suspected bacterial meningitis; give dexamethasone with/before the first antibiotic dose. At age >50 (here 71) Listeria monocytogenes must be covered, so benzylpenicillin (or amoxicillin) is ADDED to ceftriaxone. C is the classic trap: ceftriaxone alone covers pneumococcus and meningococcus but MISSES Listeria at the extremes of age. A wastes time with imaging she does not need (no focal deficit, seizures, GCS<10 or immunocompromise), and B delays treatment. E withholds dexamethasone, which improves outcomes in pneumococcal meningitis, and omits Listeria cover.

_Teaching point:_ Add benzylpenicillin/amoxicillin to ceftriaxone for Listeria cover when age >50, neonate, pregnant or immunocompromised; give dexamethasone with the first dose and never delay antibiotics for the LP.
</details>

**Q4.** A 64-year-old man presents with a tender, spreading erythematous, non-purulent cellulitis of the left lower leg. He is febrile (38.6°C) but haemodynamically stable. He reports a 'penicillin allergy.' Using PEN-FAST, you elicit: the reaction was a mild rash more than 10 years ago, no anaphylaxis, no angioedema, no mucosal involvement and no treatment was required. His PEN-FAST score is 0 (low risk). What is the most appropriate antibiotic strategy?

   - A. Vancomycin IV empirically
   - B. Clindamycin orally
   - C. Direct oral penicillin challenge / de-label, then flucloxacillin or cefazolin
   - D. Ciprofloxacin orally
   - E. Cefalexin orally without further allergy assessment

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Direct oral penicillin challenge / de-label, then flucloxacillin or cefazolin**

A PEN-FAST score of 0 identifies a low-risk history suitable for direct oral penicillin challenge and de-labelling, restoring first-line beta-lactams. This is both the correct clinical and stewardship answer. A (vancomycin) is the deadliest distractor: it FEELS safe because it sidesteps the allergy label, but it is unnecessarily broad, nephrotoxic, less effective for MSSA than a beta-lactam, and represents exactly the over-use de-labelling is designed to prevent. B (clindamycin) is reserved for severe/true penicillin allergy. D (ciprofloxacin) does not cover the likely Strep/Staph and is poor cellulitis cover. E reflexively uses cefalexin without de-labelling and forfeits the chance to restore first-line narrow therapy (flucloxacillin or cefazolin).

_Teaching point:_ PEN-FAST stratifies penicillin allergy: a low score (0-1) supports direct oral challenge and de-labelling; reflex vancomycin for a vague allergy label is a stewardship failure, not a safe default.
</details>

**Q5.** A 47-year-old man with poorly controlled diabetes presents with 18 hours of severe pain in the right thigh, far out of proportion to the modest overlying erythema. He is febrile, tachycardic, hypotensive and the skin shows early dusky discolouration with crepitus. He looks toxic. What is the single most important next step?

   - A. Urgent MRI of the thigh to confirm fascial involvement
   - B. IV flucloxacillin and mark the erythema margin for review
   - C. Urgent surgical exploration and debridement
   - D. CT thigh with contrast and inpatient observation
   - E. IV vancomycin alone and reassess in 6 hours

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Urgent surgical exploration and debridement**

Pain out of proportion, rapid progression, systemic toxicity, dusky skin and crepitus describe necrotising fasciitis, a surgical emergency. The keyed action is urgent debridement (source control) plus empirical antibiotics (vancomycin + piperacillin-tazobactam or meropenem + clindamycin or lincomycin for toxin suppression). A and D are the seductive traps: imaging feels thorough but DELAYS the only intervention that saves the limb and life; you operate, you do not wait for the scan. B treats this as simple cellulitis and dangerously under-covers. E gives incomplete cover and, again, delays surgery.

_Teaching point:_ Necrotising fasciitis is a clinical/surgical diagnosis: operate, do not image. Pain out of proportion + systemic toxicity = urgent debridement plus broad antibiotics including a lincosamide for toxin suppression.
</details>

**Q6.** A 34-year-old man with HIV (not on antiretroviral therapy, last CD4 unknown) presents with 3 weeks of progressive dry cough and exertional breathlessness. SpO2 is 91% at rest, falling to 84% on walking. CXR shows bilateral perihilar interstitial infiltrates. LDH is markedly elevated. Arterial PaO2 is 64 mmHg. Which treatment is most appropriate?

   - A. Ceftriaxone + azithromycin for community-acquired pneumonia
   - B. Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole alone
   - C. Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole plus corticosteroids
   - D. Aciclovir
   - E. Liposomal amphotericin B

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole plus corticosteroids**

Subacute dry cough, exertional desaturation, bilateral perihilar infiltrates and high LDH in an untreated HIV patient is Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP, CD4 typically <200). Treatment is high-dose TMP-SMX, and adjunctive corticosteroids are indicated when PaO2 <70 mmHg (or A-a gradient >35) — here PaO2 is 64, so steroids are required. B is the deadliest distractor: it is the right drug but omits steroids, which reduce mortality in hypoxaemic PJP; the examiner buries the qualifying PaO2 value precisely to catch you. A treats bacterial CAP and misses PJP. D (aciclovir) treats HSV encephalitis. E (amphotericin) treats cryptococcosis (CD4 <100-50), not PJP.

_Teaching point:_ PJP with PaO2 <70 mmHg (or A-a gradient >35): give corticosteroids WITH the TMP-SMX. The PaO2 in the stem is the trigger the examiner hides.
</details>

**Q7.** A 41-year-old abattoir worker in regional Queensland presents with 1 week of high fevers, drenching sweats, severe headache and myalgia. He has had no overseas travel. Examination is largely unremarkable. He was never offered any occupational vaccination. Serology later confirms the diagnosis. What is the most appropriate antibiotic?

   - A. Doxycycline
   - B. Ceftriaxone
   - C. Benzylpenicillin
   - D. Azithromycin
   - E. Ciprofloxacin

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Doxycycline**

Abattoir/livestock exposure with an undifferentiated febrile illness is Q fever (Coxiella burnetii), and the treatment is doxycycline. The Q-Vax vaccine is occupationally recommended but requires pre-screening (prior exposure can cause severe reactions), and he was never vaccinated. Doxycycline is also the unifying answer for most Australian intracellular zoonoses (Q fever, leptospirosis, scrub typhus, Australian spotted-fever rickettsioses). B and C are reasonable-looking 'serious infection' beta-lactams but do not treat an intracellular obligate organism. D (azithromycin) treats some atypicals but is not first-line for Q fever. E (ciprofloxacin) is not the preferred agent.

_Teaching point:_ Doxycycline is the default for Australian intracellular zoonoses: abattoir/livestock = Q fever; eschar = scrub typhus or Australian spotted fever; freshwater/floods + jaundice = leptospirosis.
</details>

**Q8.** A 26-year-old woman returns to Brisbane 8 days after a backpacking trip through rural Papua New Guinea and presents with high swinging fevers, rigors, headache and myalgia. She took no malaria prophylaxis. She is febrile and mildly jaundiced; platelets are 88. What is the single most important initial investigation?

   - A. Dengue NS1 antigen and serology
   - B. Thick and thin blood films for malaria
   - C. Blood cultures for enteric fever
   - D. Hepatitis serology
   - E. Leptospira serology

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Thick and thin blood films for malaria**

In any febrile returned traveller from an endemic area, malaria must be excluded FIRST because it can kill within days and is treatable; thick and thin films (with repeats if negative) are the key initial test. A is the seductive distractor: dengue is common, causes fever and thrombocytopenia, and looks like a perfect fit, but missing P. falciparum malaria is the lethal error, so films come first regardless of how 'dengue-like' the picture is. C, D and E are all part of the staged workup but are sequenced AFTER malaria has been excluded.

_Teaching point:_ Febrile returned traveller: exclude malaria first with thick and thin films (repeat if negative) before chasing dengue, enteric fever or lepto — malaria kills fastest and is curable.
</details>

### Schema recall — "What is your schema for X?"

**What is your schema for the immediate management of suspected sepsis / septic shock in an Australian setting?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Recognise: fever/hypothermia + tachycardia + hypotension or lactate >2 (>=4 = severe hypoperfusion). 2) Take blood cultures BEFORE antibiotics (plus septic screen / site-specific samples). 3) Give broad-spectrum IV antibiotics within 1 hour, chosen by SOURCE -> likely organism -> eTG empirical drug. 4) Resuscitate with TITRATED balanced crystalloid (Hartmann's/Plasma-Lyte) in 250-500 mL boluses, reassessing perfusion/lactate after each — NOT a fixed 30 mL/kg load. 5) Start noradrenaline EARLY (first-line vasopressor) rather than continuing large fixed-volume fluids. 6) Measure serial lactate, monitor urine output, seek and achieve source control. 7) De-escalate on cultures.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Two attack points: (a) writing the US '30 mL/kg fixed bolus' — Australian teaching (Australian Sepsis Network, ARISE-FLUIDS) uses small titrated boluses + early noradrenaline and would mark the fixed bolus as over-aggressive; (b) delaying antibiotics for an LP or CT — antibiotics and fluids come first, imaging never delays them.
</details>

**What is your schema for SOURCE -> BUG -> eTG DRUG across the common infective sites (current as of eTG March 2025)?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Skin/cellulitis (non-purulent): Strep>Staph -> flucloxacillin OR cefazolin (equal first-line for MSSA directed therapy; cefazolin lower AKI risk); cefalexin acceptable for paediatric cellulitis/impetigo. Purulent/MRSA risk -> doxycycline or TMP-SMX (community), vancomycin if severe. Bone/joint: S. aureus -> flucloxacillin or cefazolin (+ vancomycin if MRSA risk), sample before antibiotics. Cystitis: E. coli -> nitrofurantoin first-line (trimethoprim now second-line). Pyelonephritis: non-pregnant/non-septic -> ORAL first-line; severe/urosepsis -> gentamicin (+ amoxicillin/ampicillin only in pregnancy for GBS). CAP: severity-stratified (amoxicillin+doxycycline mild; ceftriaxone+azithromycin or benzylpen+gent+azithromycin severe; +meropenem if tropical melioidosis risk). Meningitis: ceftriaxone (+benzylpenicillin/amoxicillin for Listeria at extremes/pregnancy/immunocompromise) + dexamethasone.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Outdated anchors examiners exploit: defaulting to trimethoprim for cystitis (now second-line), naming flucloxacillin as the ONLY MSSA agent (cefazolin is equal first-line), giving IV amoxicillin/ampicillin for pyelonephritis in non-pregnant patients (removed for resistance), and using ceftriaxone alone for meningitis at the extremes of age (misses Listeria).
</details>

**What is your schema for the febrile returned traveller?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) ABCDE and sepsis screen if unwell. 2) EXCLUDE MALARIA FIRST — thick and thin films (rapid antigen as adjunct); repeat films if negative because it kills fastest and is treatable. 3) Anchor the differential on INCUBATION period + GEOGRAPHIC exposure + ACTIVITIES: short (<2 weeks) -> dengue, malaria, rickettsia, lepto; intermediate -> enteric fever (Salmonella Typhi), malaria; long -> TB, viral hepatitis, schistosomiasis. 4) Exposure clues: eschar -> scrub typhus/rickettsia (doxy); freshwater/floods + jaundice/AKI -> leptospirosis; abattoir -> Q fever. 5) Always test HIV; consider Strongyloides screen before any immunosuppression in at-risk patients. 6) Treat empirically for the lethal, treatable diagnoses while awaiting results.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The trap is pattern-matching to dengue (thrombocytopenia + fever) and skipping malaria films; the rule is malaria-first ALWAYS. Second attack point: forgetting Strongyloides screening before steroids/immunosuppression in returned travellers/Indigenous patients (hyperinfection risk).
</details>

**What is your schema for using the CD4 count to predict opportunistic infection in HIV?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Any CD4: TB, bacterial pneumonia, recurrent zoster, oral/oesophageal candida should raise suspicion of HIV. CD4 <200: Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP) — dry cough, exertional desaturation, high LDH -> TMP-SMX, ADD corticosteroids if PaO2 <70 mmHg or A-a gradient >35; start PJP prophylaxis. CD4 <100: cerebral toxoplasmosis (ring-enhancing lesions), cryptococcal meningitis (amphotericin + flucytosine). CD4 <50: disseminated MAC, CMV retinitis/colitis. Across all: start antiretroviral therapy early, counsel on U=U, offer PrEP to contacts.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Examiners hide the PaO2 value to catch you omitting steroids in hypoxaemic PJP, and they test whether you can map the CD4 number to the right organism (mixing up the <100 toxo/crypto tier with the <50 MAC/CMV tier). Forgetting to actually start ART early is the stewardship/management miss.
</details>

**What is your schema for melioidosis recognition and treatment in northern Australia?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Recognise: northern Australia (Top End/FNQ), WET SEASON, soil/water/mud exposure, risk factors diabetes, hazardous alcohol, chronic lung/renal disease, Indigenous. Presentations: severe pneumonia (often cavitating), sepsis, and abscesses (lung, liver, splenic, prostatic). Treat in TWO phases: INTENSIVE phase = ceftazidime first-line (meropenem if ICU/neurological/very severe), minimum 2 weeks but commonly 4-8 weeks for deep-seated/neuro/bone/osteomyelitis disease. ERADICATION phase = trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole for 3-6 months. Source control any collections.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The keyed distractor is plain ceftriaxone / standard severe-CAP cover, which misses B. pseudomallei. Second attack point: quoting only '2 weeks' intensive phase — contemporary Australian (Royal Darwin) practice extends to 4-8 weeks for deep-seated disease, and forgetting the months-long TMP-SMX eradication phase invites relapse.
</details>

**What is your schema for antimicrobial stewardship decision-making on any ID question?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Identify the SOURCE and the 2-3 likely organisms. 2) Choose the NARROWEST agent that reliably covers them (eTG empirical). 3) Apply modifiers: severity (escalate to IV/broad if septic), host (neutropenia -> anti-Pseudomonal pip-taz; immunocompromise -> add Listeria/opportunistic cover), geography (tropical -> melioidosis), allergy (risk-stratify with PEN-FAST; de-label low-risk for direct oral challenge). 4) Use the SHORTEST evidence-based duration. 5) De-escalate when cultures/susceptibilities return. 6) Achieve SOURCE CONTROL (drain abscess, remove line). 7) Monitor toxicity (vancomycin to AUC/MIC target; gentamicin TDM; nitrofurantoin avoid eGFR<45).

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Examiners reward the answer that is correct AND narrowest — the trap is the broad 'safe' choice (reflex vancomycin/meropenem) when a beta-lactam suffices. Also commonly attacked: defaulting to vancomycin for a vague penicillin allergy instead of applying PEN-FAST and de-labelling; and dosing vancomycin to trough only rather than the now-recommended AUC/MIC target.
</details>

### Distractor drills — "What's the deadliest distractor for X?"

**Most seductive wrong answer when an Australian patient presents with uncomplicated cystitis?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Trimethoprim 300 mg daily for 3 days as first-line empirical therapy.
- _Why it tempts you:_ For years this was the textbook first-line answer and is deeply embedded in memorised drug lists, OSCE scripts and older guidelines; it 'feels' like the canonical correct answer.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ The eTG Antibiotic March 2025 update DEMOTED trimethoprim to a second-line/conditional option because of high E. coli trimethoprim resistance. Nitrofurantoin 100 mg 6-hourly for 5 days is now first-line empirical therapy.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Date/guideline currency: if the question reflects current eTG (post-March 2025), nitrofurantoin beats trimethoprim — check eGFR (avoid nitrofurantoin <45) and avoid near term in pregnancy.**

**Deadliest distractor in the elderly or pregnant patient with bacterial meningitis?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Ceftriaxone (or cefotaxime) alone as empirical cover.
- _Why it tempts you:_ Ceftriaxone correctly covers the two most common pathogens — pneumococcus and meningococcus — so it looks complete and is the right answer for a healthy young adult.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ At age >50, in neonates, in pregnancy or immunocompromise, Listeria monocytogenes is in the differential, and cephalosporins do NOT cover Listeria. You must ADD benzylpenicillin or amoxicillin.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **The host modifier in the stem: any of age >50 / neonate / pregnant / immunocompromised forces added benzylpenicillin/amoxicillin for Listeria.**

**Most seductive wrong answer when a vague penicillin allergy label appears in a cellulitis/MSSA stem?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Reach straight for IV vancomycin to 'play it safe'.
- _Why it tempts you:_ It sidesteps the scary allergy label entirely and feels like the cautious, defensive choice that can't be faulted.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Vancomycin is unnecessarily broad, nephrotoxic, requires AUC/MIC monitoring and is LESS effective than a beta-lactam for MSSA; reflex vancomycin for a low-risk label is precisely the over-use that de-labelling targets.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **PEN-FAST risk score: a low score (0-1, e.g. distant mild rash, no anaphylaxis/mucosal involvement, no treatment needed) supports direct oral challenge/de-labelling and use of flucloxacillin or cefazolin.**

**Deadliest distractor in the diabetic Darwin patient with severe wet-season pneumonia and sepsis?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Ceftriaxone + azithromycin (standard severe-CAP cover).
- _Why it tempts you:_ It is the textbook-correct severe community-acquired pneumonia regimen for southern Australia and reads as the 'right' guideline answer.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ It misses Burkholderia pseudomallei (melioidosis), which is intrinsically resistant to ceftriaxone; the patient needs ceftazidime, or meropenem if ICU/neurological/very severe.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Geography + season + host: tropical north + wet season + diabetes/alcohol + severe pneumonia/sepsis = add melioidosis cover (ceftazidime/meropenem), not plain ceftriaxone.**

**Most seductive wrong answer in the toxic patient with thigh pain out of proportion to the rash?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Order an urgent MRI (or CT) to confirm fascial involvement before acting.
- _Why it tempts you:_ Imaging feels rigorous and 'evidence-based', and confirming the diagnosis before major surgery seems prudent.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Necrotising fasciitis is a clinical/surgical emergency; imaging DELAYS the only life- and limb-saving intervention (debridement). You operate, you do not wait for the scan.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Pain out of proportion + rapid progression + systemic toxicity (+/- crepitus/dusky skin) = go to theatre now; the surgical-emergency pattern overrides any imaging step.**

**Deadliest distractor in the febrile returned traveller with fever and thrombocytopenia?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Send dengue NS1/serology and treat as dengue.
- _Why it tempts you:_ Dengue is common in returned travellers, classically causes fever + thrombocytopenia, and the picture can look like a textbook dengue case.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Anchoring on dengue risks missing P. falciparum malaria, which also causes fever + thrombocytopenia but can be fatal within days and is treatable.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Exclude malaria FIRST with thick and thin films (repeat if negative) in any traveller from an endemic area, regardless of how dengue-like the presentation looks.**

**Most seductive wrong answer for adjunctive therapy in PJP pneumonia?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Give TMP-SMX alone and omit corticosteroids.
- _Why it tempts you:_ TMP-SMX is unmistakably the correct antimicrobial, so the answer 'feels' complete and the buried PaO2 value is easy to overlook.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ In hypoxaemic PJP (PaO2 <70 mmHg or A-a gradient >35) adjunctive corticosteroids reduce mortality; omitting them undertreats the patient.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Scan the stem for the PaO2/oxygenation value: PaO2 <70 mmHg (or A-a gradient >35) is the trigger to ADD steroids alongside TMP-SMX.**

### High-end review pearls

- If you see 'diabetic in Darwin/FNQ, wet season, soil or floodwater exposure, severe cavitating pneumonia or sepsis' think MELIOIDOSIS -> ceftazidime (meropenem if ICU/neuro), then TMP-SMX for 3-6 months. This is the single highest-probability Australian regional MCQ and the keyed answer is never plain ceftriaxone.
- Aunt-minnie: eschar + fever in northern Australia/SE Asia = scrub typhus (Orientia); conjunctival suffusion + jaundice + AKI after floodwater/mud = leptospirosis (Weil's); abattoir/livestock + undifferentiated fever = Q fever. All three converge on DOXYCYCLINE — when in doubt about an Australian intracellular zoonosis, the answer is doxycycline.
- Never-forget one-liner: in suspected bacterial meningitis, dexamethasone and antibiotics go in FIRST; the LP and CT never delay them. CT before LP only if focal deficit, seizures, GCS drop or immunocompromise.
- Classic Australian trap (post-2022): Japanese encephalitis virus (JEV) is now established and notifiable across the Murray-Darling basin/southeastern mainland, with a national at-risk vaccination program. A febrile encephalitis in an inland NSW/Victorian piggery worker or rural resident is no longer 'just' the old arboviruses — think JEV.
- Aunt-minnie: a painless nodule or slowly enlarging ulcer in someone from coastal Victoria (Mornington/Bellarine Peninsulas) = Buruli ulcer (Mycobacterium ulcerans) -> rifampicin + clarithromycin. A uniquely Australian mycobacterial item that catches people expecting a tropical-only answer.
- Stewardship pearl: cefazolin is now EQUAL first-line to flucloxacillin for directed MSSA therapy (CAMERA-2/SNAP) with LOWER acute kidney injury risk than high-dose IV flucloxacillin. If a question contrasts the two for serious MSSA infection with renal concern, cefazolin is the safety-conscious choice.
- eTG March 2025 dosing nuance: for community-acquired sepsis WITHOUT septic shock/ICU, reduced-frequency flucloxacillin/cefazolin dosing is now recommended; septic shock/ICU patients need MORE frequent dosing for adequate exposure. A subtle, current, examinable Australian-specific point.
- If you see a vague 'penicillin allergy' label, reach for PEN-FAST, not vancomycin: low score (0-1) = direct oral challenge and de-label. Reflex broad-spectrum cover for an unverified label is the stewardship failure examiners now actively test.
- Australian fluid resuscitation has moved on from '30 mL/kg': give small titrated balanced-crystalloid boluses (250-500 mL Hartmann's/Plasma-Lyte, reassess each time) and start noradrenaline EARLY. Quoting the US 30 mL/kg fixed bolus will be marked as outdated/over-aggressive in an Australian context.
- Remote/Indigenous health one-liners: Strep skin sores can lead to APSGN (and ARF/RHD) — secondary RHD prophylaxis is benzathine benzylpenicillin G IM every 21-28 days. Crusted (Norwegian) scabies needs oral ivermectin PLUS topical permethrin/benzyl benzoate plus keratolytics, and is notifiable in some jurisdictions.
- Always screen for Strongyloides stercoralis before starting steroids/immunosuppression in Indigenous, tropical-resident or returned-traveller patients — undetected infection plus corticosteroids causes fatal hyperinfection syndrome.
- Vancomycin is now dosed to an AUC/MIC target (not trough alone) per updated eTG/ASID guidance; nitrofurantoin (now first-line cystitis) must be AVOIDED if eGFR <45 and near term in pregnancy. Two renal cross-topic traps in one breath.
- Febrile neutropenia is a one-hour emergency: neutrophils <0.5 (or <1.0 and falling) + fever (single >=38.5 or sustained >=38.0) -> piperacillin-tazobactam within the hour (cefepime/ceftazidime are beta-lactam alternatives); add vancomycin only for specific indications, not routinely.
- Australian spotted-fever rickettsioses have NAMES worth knowing: Queensland tick typhus (Rickettsia australis) and Flinders Island spotted fever (R. honei) — eschar + fever + rash, all treated with doxycycline. Naming the Australian species, not generic 'rickettsia', signals a high-end answer.

> **Independent item audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[low]** MCQ 1 (melioidosis) — option C answer text 'Meropenem (and treat as melioidosis)': The keyed answer is correct because this patient is in septic shock (BP 88/52, lactate 4.1, ICU-level disease), so meropenem genuinely beats ceftazidime. BUT the explanation/teachingPoint repeatedly say 'ceftazidime is first-line for non-ICU severe disease' and frame ceftazidime as the default — which risks confusing the learner into thinking the stem could equally be answered by ceftazidime. Ceftazidime is not even an option, so the discriminator (shock/ICU forcing meropenem over ceftazidime) is never actually tested by the distractors. The real distractors (A/B) only test 'melioidosis vs standard CAP', not the meropenem-vs-ceftazidime decision. → Either tighten the teaching point to emphasise that the SHOCK/ICU features in THIS stem are what mandate meropenem (not just 'treat as melioidosis'), or add ceftazidime as a distractor option so the harder, more examinable discrimination (ceftazidime vs meropenem by severity) is actually drilled. As written the item is correct but easier than it advertises.
> - **[low]** MCQ 1 — option E rationale ('pip-taz is the febrile neutropenia answer, not melioidosis cover'): Minor inaccuracy: piperacillin-tazobactam has NO reliable activity against Burkholderia pseudomallei (it is intrinsically resistant), which is the correct reason to reject it — but the explanation dismisses it only as 'the febrile neutropenia answer' and says it 'covers Pseudomonas'. The clinically load-bearing reason (no melioidosis cover) is the right one and should lead; framing it as merely 'the wrong clinical scenario' understates why it is dangerous here. → State explicitly that pip-taz does not reliably cover B. pseudomallei, so it under-treats melioidosis sepsis.
> - **[low]** MCQ 2 (cystitis) — option B dosing '100 mg orally 6-hourly for 5 days': This is actually CORRECT for the Australian context (eTG recommends nitrofurantoin 100 mg 6-hourly because the 12-hourly slow-release Macrobid formulation is unavailable in Australia) — I verified this and it is NOT an error. Flagging only to confirm a likely reviewer suspicion: an examiner used to the international '100 mg BD' might wrongly think the 6-hourly dose is the trap. It is correct as keyed. No change needed to the answer. → No fix required; optionally add a one-line note that 6-hourly (not 12-hourly) is the Australian regimen because slow-release nitrofurantoin is unavailable here, to pre-empt challenge.
> - **[medium]** MCQ 3 (meningitis) — stem says 'no papilloedema, no seizures, not immunocompromised' and answer rejects CT: The stem describes increasing drowsiness with GCS 13. Standard IDSA/eTG pre-LP CT criteria include 'altered/declining level of consciousness' as an indication for CT before LP. A drowsy patient with a falling GCS is arguably exactly the patient many guidelines would CT before LP. The explanation hand-waves this by saying GCS<10 — but the cited threshold for CT is not uniformly GCS<10; reduced conscious state generally qualifies. This makes option A ('CT then LP then antibiotics') less clearly wrong than presented. The genuinely correct teaching (give dexamethasone + antibiotics FIRST, never delay for CT/LP) holds regardless, so the keyed answer D is still right — but the rationale for dismissing A is shaky. → Re-anchor the explanation: A is wrong NOT because she doesn't need a CT, but because antibiotics+dexamethasone must not be delayed for imaging — give drugs first, then CT, then LP. Avoid asserting she clearly doesn't meet CT criteria when a declining GCS arguably does.
> - **[low]** MCQ 4 (PEN-FAST) — answer text 'Direct oral penicillin challenge / de-label, then flucloxacillin or cefazolin': Clinically/stewardship correct and internally consistent (distant mild rash >10y, no anaphylaxis, no treatment = PEN-FAST 0, verified). However option C bundles TWO actions (de-label THEN treat) into one choice while the patient is febrile with spreading cellulitis NOW. In real practice you would start empirical therapy immediately and de-label in parallel/after; the option as worded could be read as 'delay antibiotics to do a challenge first'. The single-best-answer is still C, but the wording slightly muddies the timing. → Reword option C to make clear treatment is not delayed, e.g. 'Treat now with flucloxacillin/cefazolin and arrange direct oral penicillin challenge to de-label' — preserving that beta-lactam first-line therapy is appropriate immediately given PEN-FAST 0.
> - **[low]** MCQ 5 (necrotising fasciitis) — option C 'Urgent surgical exploration and debridement' as 'single most important next step': Correct and defensible (source control is the answer that saves life/limb). One subtlety: in a haemodynamically unstable, toxic, septic patient, empirical broad-spectrum antibiotics within the hour are given concurrently/immediately and arguably 'first' in the resus bay while theatre is mobilised. None of the distractors offer 'broad-spectrum antibiotics + urgent surgery together', so the item is clean, but the explanation should not imply antibiotics wait for surgery — they run in parallel. → In the explanation, state antibiotics (vancomycin + pip-taz or meropenem + clindamycin for toxin suppression) are given immediately AND in parallel with mobilising theatre; surgery is the definitive/most important step but does not delay antibiotics.
