> - **[low]** MCQ 6 (PJP) — option B 'TMP-SMX alone' as the keyed deadliest distractor: Verified correct: PaO2 64 mmHg (<70) triggers adjunctive corticosteroids, so B (omitting steroids) is wrong and C is right. Internally consistent and well-constructed. Minor: the stem gives SpO2 91% resting AND PaO2 64 — both already indicate hypoxaemia, so an alert candidate could pick C without specifically clocking the PaO2 threshold, slightly diluting the intended 'hidden PaO2' trap. Not an error. → Optional: raise the resting SpO2 to ~93-94% so the PaO2 of 64 is the decisive, less obvious trigger for steroids, sharpening the intended trap.
> - **[low]** MCQ 7 (Q fever) — option A 'Doxycycline' single best answer: Correct for acute Q fever. Clean item. Minor robustness point: the stem says 'serology later confirms the diagnosis' but asks for antibiotic choice up front — fine. Distractors B/C (beta-lactams) and D/E are appropriately weak. No correctness issue; azithromycin (D) is a legitimate alternative only in pregnancy, which is not this patient, so single-best-answer holds. → No fix required. Optionally note doxycycline is the answer for acute Q fever specifically (chronic Q fever endocarditis needs doxycycline + hydroxychloroquine long-term) to pre-empt a higher-order challenge.
> - **[low]** MCQ 8 (returned traveller) — option B 'thick and thin films' single most important INITIAL investigation: Correct and uncontroversial (malaria-first from PNG, a high P. falciparum + emerging chloroquine-resistant P. vivax area). Clean. The only quality issue: this is one of the easier items (the malaria-first rule is very well drilled and option A dengue is a classic strawman that most candidates will see through), so it adds little discrimination beyond MCQ schema reinforcement. → Consider making harder by giving a negative initial film and asking the next step (repeat films/RDT) — the 'repeat if negative' principle is the more examinable, more frequently-missed teaching point.
> - **[medium]** schemaRecall — sepsis schema, '30 mL/kg fixed bolus is over-aggressive / Australian practice uses titrated boluses + early noradrenaline': This reflects ARISE-FLUIDS/Australian Sepsis Network direction and is defensible as a teaching emphasis, but it is stated more categorically than the evidence supports. ARISE-FLUIDS did not show a fixed 30 mL/kg approach is harmful, and the Surviving Sepsis Campaign still suggests ~30 mL/kg as a starting guide. Presenting 'the 30 mL/kg bolus will be MARKED WRONG' as fact risks teaching candidates to reject a still-widely-accepted answer. → Soften to: Australian practice favours smaller titrated balanced-crystalloid boluses with reassessment and earlier noradrenaline rather than a reflexive fixed 30 mL/kg load — but a measured initial fluid challenge is still appropriate; avoid framing the fixed bolus as an automatic 'wrong answer'.
> - **[low]** highEndExtras — Buruli ulcer treatment 'rifampicin + clarithromycin': Correct first-line per current Australian (Victorian DH / eTG) guidance for Mycobacterium ulcerans. Verified as accurate. No error. Flagged only to confirm it is right, since reviewers sometimes expect rifampicin + ciprofloxacin/moxifloxacin (an alternative). Rifampicin + clarithromycin is the preferred oral regimen. → No fix required.
> - **[low]** highEndExtras — febrile neutropenia thresholds 'neutrophils <0.5 ... fever single >=38.5 or sustained >=38.0': Largely correct, but thresholds vary by guideline; common Australian/eTG definition is fever single oral temp >=38.5 OR >=38.0 sustained over 1 hour, neutrophils <0.5 (or <1.0 expected to fall). The 'or <1.0 and falling' is fine. Minor: pip-taz is correct first-line; the note that vancomycin is added only for specific indications is correct. No substantive error. → No fix required; thresholds are within accepted range.
> 
> _Quality notes:_ Overall the factual core is strong: I independently verified against 2025-2026 Australian sources the five highest-risk claims — nitrofurantoin now first-line for uncomplicated cystitis with trimethoprim demoted (eTG Mar 2025), nitrofurantoin 6-hourly being the correct Australian regimen (slow-release Macrobid unavailable here), meropenem over ceftazidime for ICU/septic-shock melioidosis, PJP adjunctive steroids triggered at PaO2 <70 / A-a >35, Listeria cover added at age >=50 in meningitis, and PEN-FAST 0 for a distant mild rash. All keyed answers are the single best answer and no distractor is accidentally also-correct.; Distractors are genuinely seductive in MCQs 1, 2, 3, 4 and 6 — these are the deadliest realistic traps (standard CAP cover that misses melioidosis; trimethoprim from outdated teaching; ceftriaxone-alone missing Listeria; reflex vancomycin for a vague allergy label; right-drug-wrong-adjunct in PJP). These are NOT strawmen and are well-targeted to current Australian exam blind spots.; Weaker / strawman-ish distractors: MCQ 5 option B (treat necrotising fasciitis as simple cellulitis with flucloxacillin) and MCQ 8 option A (dengue) are relatively easy to eliminate; MCQ 7's beta-lactam distractors are weak. MCQ 8 is the easiest item overall and adds limited discrimination beyond reinforcing the malaria-first schema.; Two items are easier than they advertise: MCQ 1's intended hard discrimination (ceftazidime vs meropenem by severity) is never tested because ceftazidime is not an option — only 'melioidosis vs not'; MCQ 6's intended hidden-PaO2 trap is undercut by an overtly low resting SpO2 (91%) that already signals hypoxaemia.; Best-constructed single-best-answer items: MCQ 4 (PEN-FAST) and MCQ 6 (PJP) — clean stems, internally consistent scoring/values, one defensible correct answer, no co-correct distractor.; Main rationale weaknesses (not answer-key errors): (a) MCQ 3 dismisses CT-before-LP too glibly given a drowsy GCS-13 patient who arguably meets imaging criteria — the correct teaching is 'drugs first, never delay for imaging', not 'she doesn't need a CT'; (b) the sepsis schema and one high-end extra overstate the '30 mL/kg is wrong' position more categorically than current evidence supports.; Internal consistency is high: schemaRecall, distractorDrills and highEndExtras align with the MCQ answers and with the stated tutorial core idea (SOURCE -> ORGANISM -> narrowest eTG drug, modulated by host/severity). No contradictions found between layers.; No fabricated or out-of-date drug names detected; Australian-specific currency (eTG Mar 2025, CAMERA-2/SNAP cefazolin equivalence, JEV establishment in the Murray-Darling basin, Buruli ulcer regimen, vancomycin AUC/MIC dosing) is accurate and appropriately contemporary for a 2026 sitting.

---

## Nephrology / Renal & Acid-Base  _(~9% of marks)_

### The one idea that unlocks the domain

> **The kidney is a single regulated tube: every renal question is solved by locating WHERE the lesion sits along the filtration-to-excretion axis (perfusion → glomerulus → tubule/interstitium → outflow) and then reading the urine, the bloods, and the acid-base as a downstream readout of that location.**

Almost every nephrology MCQ is a localisation problem disguised as a management or biochemistry question. If you anchor on the anatomical axis — renal perfusion, glomerular filtration, tubular handling, and urinary outflow — the data resolve themselves. AKI is just "which segment failed": pre-renal = perfusion (avid sodium retention, FeNa <1%, bland urine, urea-out-of-proportion), intrinsic = glomerulus/tubule (casts, FeNa >2%, ATN/GN), post-renal = outflow (hydronephrosis). Acid-base flows from the same tube: the kidney is the slow regulator of HCO3 and the excretor of fixed acid, so an anion gap tells you whether you are adding acid (lactate, ketones, toxic alcohols) or losing bicarbonate (RTA, diarrhoea). Hyperkalaemia is tubular potassium handling failing under low GFR or aldosterone blockade — and its ECG is a membrane-excitability story you can derive, not memorise. CKD is the chronic, irreversible version of the same axis, where the engine becomes "preserve nephrons": control intraglomerular pressure (ACEi/SGLT2i), avoid second hits (nephrotoxins, contrast, NSAIDs), and dose-adjust everything renally cleared. Glomerulonephritis splits cleanly into nephritic (inflamed, leaky-to-cells, hypertensive, haematuria + casts) vs nephrotic (podocyte/barrier failure, massive proteinuria, oedema, thrombosis). Once you see all of these as readouts of one tube, you stop memorising disconnected facts and start deriving the single best answer from first principles — which is exactly what Passmedicine-style single-best-answer questions reward.

### High-yield tutorial

## The engine: one tube, four segments

Read every renal stem by asking **"where on the tube is the lesion, and what should the urine/bloods look like if I'm right?"**

### AKI: localise before you treat

AKI is defined (KDIGO) as **creatinine rise ≥26.5 µmol/L in 48h, or ≥1.5× baseline in 7 days, or urine output <0.5 mL/kg/h for 6h.** Then localise:

- **Pre-renal** (perfusion): hypovolaemia, sepsis, cardiorenal, hepatorenal, or *haemodynamic* drugs. Kidney is structurally intact and **sodium-avid**: **FeNa <1%**, urine Na <20, **urea:creatinine ratio raised** (>100:1 in older units), bland urine. The classic exam triad of haemodynamic insult = **ACEi/ARB + diuretic + NSAID** ("triple whammy"), especially with intercurrent gastro. Reverses fast with perfusion.
- **Intrinsic**: most commonly **ATN** (the unreversed pre-renal, or contrast/aminoglycoside/myoglobin). Now FeNa >2%, **muddy brown granular casts**. Other intrinsic: **AIN** (drug — flucloxacillin, PPIs, NSAIDs; classically eosinophilia, white cell casts, sterile pyuria) and **GN** (red cell casts, see below).
- **Post-renal** (outflow): bladder outlet, stones, pelvic malignancy. **Always image early** — renal tract ultrasound looking for **hydronephrosis**; reversible with a catheter or nephrostomy.

**Management is generic and derivable**: restore perfusion (fluids if dry, treat sepsis), **stop nephrotoxins and renally-cleared drugs that accumulate**, relieve obstruction, and screen for emergency dialysis indications.

### When to dialyse — the AEIOU you can reconstruct
**A**cidosis (refractory metabolic), **E**lectrolytes (refractory hyperkalaemia), **I**ntoxication (toxic alcohols, lithium, salicylate), **O**verload (refractory pulmonary oedema), **U**raemia (pericarditis, encephalopathy). Note "refractory" — dialysis is for failure of medical therapy, not a number.

### Hyperkalaemia: a membrane story

K+ sets the resting membrane potential. As K+ rises the membrane depolarises, then inactivates sodium channels → **ECG progresses predictably**: **peaked T waves → PR prolongation/flat P → wide QRS → sine wave → arrest.** 

**Emergency management is mechanistic, not a list:**
1. **Stabilise the membrane** — **IV calcium gluconate** (cardioprotection; does NOT lower K+).
2. **Shift K+ intracellularly** — **insulin–dextrose** (10 units actrapid + 25g glucose) ± **salbutamol nebs**. Bicarbonate only if acidotic.
3. **Remove K+** — **resonium/SZC, diuresis, or dialysis** (the only definitive route).

### Acid-base: the gap is the engine

Compute **anion gap = Na − (Cl + HCO3)** (normal ~12). For a metabolic acidosis:

- **Raised AG** = you ADDED acid. **CAT MUDPILES** but Australian-weighted: **Lactate (sepsis, metformin in AKI), Ketones (DKA, alcoholic, starvation), Urate (renal failure), and toxic alcohols.**
- **Normal AG (hyperchloraemic)** = you LOST bicarbonate: **diarrhoea** or **RTA**.

**Toxic alcohols** are pure exam gold: **high AG metabolic acidosis + a raised OSMOLAR GAP** (measured − calculated osmolality >10). **Methanol → blindness; ethylene glycol → oxalate crystals + AKI.** Treat with **fomepizole (or ethanol) + dialysis.** Always check the **delta ratio / expected compensation** — Winters' formula: **expected pCO2 = 1.5×HCO3 + 8 (±2).**

### CKD: preserve nephrons

Stage by **eGFR (G1–G5)** AND **albuminuria (A1–A3)** — KHA "heat map": **the same eGFR is higher risk with more albuminuria.** Refer to nephrology (KHA cues): **eGFR <30, sustained ACR rise, eGFR drop >5/yr, refractory HTN, or unexplained haematuria/proteinuria.**

Slowing progression is the whole game and is fully derivable from "lower intraglomerular pressure + remove second hits":
- **ACEi/ARB** if **albuminuria** (dilate efferent arteriole → drop glomerular pressure → less proteinuria). Tolerate a **creatinine rise up to ~25–30%**.
- **SGLT2 inhibitors** now first-line nephroprotection in **diabetic AND non-diabetic CKD with albuminuria** (PBS — dapagliflozin for CKD). Expect an early **eGFR dip** — continue through it.
- BP target, glycaemic control, and **stop the second hit**: NSAIDs, iodinated contrast, gentamicin, hold metformin/SGLT2 during acute illness ("**sick day rules**").

### GN: nephritic vs nephrotic
- **Nephritic** = inflamed glomerulus, **haematuria + RBC casts + HTN + mild proteinuria + AKI**. Australian high-yield: **IgA nephropathy** (commonest, **synpharyngitic** haematuria), post-strep, and **rapidly progressive GN** (ANCA/anti-GBM — a renal emergency needing biopsy + immunosuppression).
- **Nephrotic** = barrier failure, **proteinuria >3.5g/day + hypoalbuminaemia + oedema + hyperlipidaemia + thrombosis risk**. Minimal change (kids), FSGS, membranous (anti-PLA2R).

**Urinalysis is your localiser**: RBC casts → GN; muddy brown casts → ATN; WBC casts/eosinophiluria → AIN; heavy protein, no cells → nephrotic.

### Highest mark-per-minute principles

- **Localise AKI with FeNa, urea:creatinine ratio, urine microscopy and renal ultrasound before reaching for management** — Every AKI vignette hides its answer in the urine and the ratio; localisation dictates the single best next step
  - _Exam angle:_ Stem gives FeNa <1% + bland urine + raised urea:Cr → pre-renal → answer is IV fluids, not dialysis
- **Hyperkalaemia management order: stabilise (calcium) → shift (insulin/dextrose, salbutamol) → remove (resonium/dialysis)** — Examiners test whether you know calcium protects the heart but does NOT lower potassium
  - _Exam angle:_ K 7.2 with broad QRS → first step is IV calcium gluconate, even before insulin-dextrose
- **Anion gap localises the acid; osmolar gap + high AG = toxic alcohol until proven otherwise** — Splits all metabolic acidosis into added-acid vs lost-bicarb and isolates the dialysis/antidote cases
  - _Exam angle:_ High AG acidosis + osmolar gap + oxalate crystals + AKI → ethylene glycol → fomepizole + dialysis
- **Triple whammy (ACEi/ARB + diuretic + NSAID) plus a dehydrating illness causes pre-renal AKI** — The single most reproduced Australian primary-care AKI scenario
  - _Exam angle:_ Elderly patient on these three with gastro and rising creatinine → withhold the offending drugs
- **ACEi/ARB and SGLT2i are nephroprotective via intraglomerular pressure; expect and tolerate an early creatinine/eGFR dip** — Distinguishes 'expected physiological dip, continue' from 'stop the drug', a favourite distractor
  - _Exam angle:_ 25% creatinine rise after starting ramipril in CKD → continue, recheck; >30% or hyperkalaemia → reassess
- **Stage CKD by eGFR AND albuminuria together (KHA heat map), not eGFR alone** — Albuminuria reclassifies risk and triggers ACEi/SGLT2i and referral
  - _Exam angle:_ eGFR 55 (G3a) but ACR 40 (A3) is high-risk and warrants nephroprotection and referral consideration
- **RBC casts = glomerular (nephritic); muddy brown casts = ATN; WBC casts/eosinophiluria = AIN; heavy protein no cells = nephrotic** — Urinalysis is the cheapest localiser and is directly examinable
  - _Exam angle:_ Haematuria + RBC casts + HTN + AKI post-URTI → IgA nephropathy
- **Avoid the second hit: hold nephrotoxins, contrast, NSAIDs, gentamicin and apply SGLT2i/metformin sick-day rules in acute illness** — AKI-on-CKD prevention is constantly tested and is pure decision-rule marks
  - _Exam angle:_ CT with contrast planned in eGFR 28 → hydrate, review nephrotoxins, hold metformin

### Must-know numbers (Australian where they differ)

- AKI (KDIGO): creatinine rise ≥26.5 µmol/L in 48h, or ≥1.5× baseline within 7 days, or urine output <0.5 mL/kg/h for ≥6h
- FeNa <1% (urine Na <20) = pre-renal; FeNa >2% = ATN/intrinsic
- Anion gap normal ≈ 8–12 mmol/L; >12 = raised
- Osmolar gap >10 mOsm/kg suggests toxic alcohol (methanol/ethylene glycol)
- Winters' formula: expected pCO2 = 1.5×HCO3 + 8 (±2)
- Hyperkalaemia: insulin–dextrose = 10 units actrapid + 25g glucose; IV calcium gluconate 10 mL 10% for cardioprotection
- Nephrotic-range proteinuria >3.5 g/day (or urine PCR/ACR equivalent); hypoalbuminaemia <30 g/L
- CKD eGFR stages: G1 ≥90, G2 60–89, G3a 45–59, G3b 30–44, G4 15–29, G5 <15
- Albuminuria (ACR): A1 <3, A2 3–30, A3 >30 mg/mmol (Kidney Health Australia)
- Nephrology referral cues (KHA): eGFR <30, eGFR fall >5/year, ACR persistently elevated, refractory HTN, unexplained haematuria
- ACEi/ARB: tolerate creatinine rise up to ~25–30% and K up to ~6.0 before stopping
- SGLT2i (dapagliflozin) PBS-listed for CKD with albuminuria; expect early eGFR dip of a few mL/min
- Dialysis indications (AEIOU): refractory Acidosis, Electrolytes (K), Intoxication, Overload, Uraemia (pericarditis/encephalopathy)

### Cross-topic cuts

- Endocrine: DKA is a high-AG ketoacidosis; SGLT2i causes euglycaemic DKA — test sick-day rules and the eGFR dip overlap with diabetic nephropathy
- Cardiology: cardiorenal syndrome, ACEi/ARB use post-MI/HF, and hyperkalaemia from MRAs (spironolactone) — diuretic resistance and contrast nephropathy at angiography
- Pharmacology/Toxicology: metformin-associated lactic acidosis in AKI, gentamicin/vancomycin dosing by eGFR, lithium toxicity needing dialysis, NSAID nephrotoxicity
- Infectious disease: sepsis as the leading cause of ATN; AIN from antibiotics (flucloxacillin); contrast and AKI on the septic patient
- Rheumatology/Immunology: ANCA vasculitis and anti-GBM as rapidly progressive GN; lupus nephritis; cryoglobulinaemia with hepatitis C
- Haematology: nephrotic syndrome and venous thromboembolism (renal vein thrombosis); myeloma cast nephropathy and hypercalcaemia-related AKI
- Respiratory: anti-GBM (Goodpasture) pulmonary-renal syndrome with haemoptysis plus AKI
- Acid-base ties everything: respiratory compensation (Winters'), mixed disorders in the sick patient, and the osmolar gap bridging toxicology

### Australian-specific (where the UK answer loses you marks)

- CKD is staged and referred using Kidney Health Australia (KHA) criteria and the eGFR/albuminuria 'heat map', not UK CKD or QOF cut-offs — know KHA referral triggers
- eGFR is reported routinely on every Australian biochemistry panel (CKD-EPI, not adjusted for Indigenous ethnicity in current practice) — a key prompt for opportunistic CKD detection
- Indigenous Australians carry a markedly higher burden of ESKD (rates several-fold the non-Indigenous population, highest in remote/Central Australia), driven by diabetic and post-infectious/proteinuric kidney disease — a high-yield epidemiology fact and an equity-of-access dialysis theme
- SGLT2 inhibitors (dapagliflozin) are PBS-listed for CKD with albuminuria; finerenone availability and PBS status differ from UK NICE pathways
- Australian fluid/resuscitation and sepsis pathways favour balanced crystalloids; local antivenom and rhabdomyolysis (e.g. crush, drug, snenvenomation) causes feature in Aus stems
- Toxic alcohol management: fomepizole may be less readily available than in the UK/US; ethanol infusion and early dialysis liaison via Poisons Information (13 11 26) is the Australian pathway
- Renal supportive/conservative (non-dialysis) care pathways and remote dialysis access (satellite units, return-to-country programs) are distinctly Australian service issues examiners may probe
- Post-streptococcal GN and APSGN outbreaks remain relevant in remote Indigenous communities, alongside high rates of rheumatic and skin (scabies/impetigo) disease feeding renal pathology

> **Independent accuracy audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[medium]** Remove K+ — resonium/SZC, diuresis, or dialysis (the only definitive route). → In Australia the binder used for K+ removal is Resonium A (sodium polystyrene sulfonate). SZC/patiromer are not PBS-subsidised as of 2026, so do not present them as routine Australian options. Note resonium is slow/of limited acute benefit — definitive removal is dialysis. _(PBS Public Summary Document, sodium zirconium cyclosilicate, PBAC March 2024 (recommended, not yet listed); TGA Lokelma registration April 2024; eTG Hyperkalaemia.)_
> - **[medium]** SGLT2 inhibitors now first-line nephroprotection in diabetic AND non-diabetic CKD with albuminuria (PBS — dapagliflozin for CKD). → Both dapagliflozin and empagliflozin are PBS-listed for CKD nephroprotection. Note the PBS albuminuria threshold (UACR >=22.6 mg/mmol when eGFR 45-90) and that SGLT2i are not initiated below eGFR ~20 mL/min/1.73m2. _(Australian Govt Dept of Health (Butler) PBS listing media release; Medicine Today 'Preventing diabetic kidney disease progression - 2025 update'; empagliflozin PBS listing effective 1 Nov 2025.)_
> - **[medium]** finerenone availability and PBS status differ from UK NICE pathways (australianSpecifics), and 'fomepizole may be less readily available than in the UK/US'. → State plainly: finerenone (Kerendia) is PBS-listed (since July 2023) for CKD with type 2 diabetes and albuminuria as an add-on nonsteroidal MRA to RASi +/- SGLT2i. The relevant nuance is hyperkalaemia monitoring, not availability. _(Bayer Australia / Australian Prescriber 'Finerenone for CKD associated with type 2 diabetes with albuminuria'; PBS listing 1 July 2023.)_
> - **[medium]** urea:creatinine ratio raised (>100:1 in older units) ... urea-out-of-proportion → In Australian SI units, an elevated urea(mmol/L):creatinine(micromol/L) ratio (normal ~40-100; >100 suggestive of pre-renal) supports pre-renal AKI. Do NOT quote the US BUN:Cr '>20:1' or '>100:1 BUN' figures. Also flag that the ratio is non-specific and should not override FeNa/urine microscopy/clinical volume assessment. _(LITFL Urea-Creatinine Ratio; OzEMedicine urea/creatinine; SI unit Australian pathology reporting.)_
> - **[medium]** ACEi/ARB: tolerate creatinine rise up to ~25-30% and K up to ~6.0 before stopping. → Tolerate creatinine rise up to ~25-30%; for potassium, manage (dietary/diuretic/dose review) once K+ >5.5 mmol/L and do not regard 6.0 as an acceptable steady-state on RASi. K+ >6.0 is hyperkalaemia needing treatment. _(KDIGO 2024 CKD guideline / CARI-ANZ commentary; eTG RAS inhibitor monitoring.)_
> - **[low]** insulin-dextrose (10 units actrapid + 25g glucose) → Add the safety rule: give 10 units short-acting insulin with adequate glucose (e.g. 50 mL 50% dextrose, plus an ongoing glucose infusion in those with eGFR low / frail / non-diabetic) and monitor BSL for up to 6 hours due to delayed hypoglycaemia. Omit if pre-treatment glucose already high. _(eTG / Australian hospital hyperkalaemia protocols; SESLHD/WACHS Management of Hyperkalaemia in Adults.)_
> - **[low]** Raised AG = CAT MUDPILES ... Urate (renal failure) → For uraemic high-AG acidosis attribute the gap to retained sulfate, phosphate and organic acids (renal failure), not 'urate'. Keep MUDPILES but correct the 'U' explanation. _(Standard acid-base physiology; eTG/derangedphysiology anion gap.)_
> - **[low]** eGFR is reported routinely on every Australian biochemistry panel (CKD-EPI, not adjusted for Indigenous ethnicity in current practice) → Specify: Australian labs report eGFR using the race-free CKD-EPI creatinine equation (2021 update); it has never applied an Indigenous coefficient. Note caveats: invalid in non-steady-state (AKI), pregnancy, extremes of body composition. _(RCPA/KHA eGFR reporting guidance; CKD-EPI 2021 race-free equation.)_
> 
> _Gaps flagged & folded in:_ IgA nephropathy management is now substantially updated (KDIGO 2025 IgAN/IgAV guideline): supportive 'CKD bundle' = maximally tolerated RASi PLUS an SGLT2i for proteinuric IgAN regardless of diabetes; add targeted-release budesonide (Nefecon) and the dual endothelin/angiotensin receptor antagonist sparsentan for progressive disease. The tutorial only labels IgA as 'commonest, synpharyngitic haematuria' and omits modern therapy.; Finerenone PBS-listed (since July 2023) as add-on nonsteroidal MRA for CKD + type 2 diabetes + albuminuria, layered on RASi and SGLT2i ('pillars' of diabetic kidney disease: RASi + SGLT2i + nsMRA + GLP-1 RA). High-yield 2026 Australian cross-topic point the tutorial does not teach.; GLP-1 receptor agonists (e.g. semaglutide; FLOW trial) now have a kidney-protective role in type 2 diabetes with CKD and feature in 2025-2026 ANZ/KDIGO diabetic kidney disease pathways — absent from the tutorial.; SGLT2i lower eGFR initiation threshold (~20 mL/min/1.73m2) and the PBS albuminuria threshold (UACR >=22.6 mg/mmol when eGFR 45-90) — specific, examinable Australian prescribing rules omitted.; Kidney Failure Risk Equation (KFRE) is increasingly used in Australia/KDIGO 2024 for risk-based nephrology referral (e.g. 5-year kidney failure risk >3-5%), complementing the eGFR/ACR heat map referral cues quoted.; CKD-MBD and anaemia of CKD: phosphate binders, active vitamin D, target Hb and the role of erythropoiesis-stimulating agents / IV iron, plus the newer HIF prolyl-hydroxylase inhibitors — entirely absent though commonly examined in CKD vignettes.; Cardiovascular risk dominates CKD outcomes: statin therapy is indicated in essentially all CKD (KHA/KDIGO) and the leading cause of death in CKD is cardiovascular, not progression to dialysis — a high-yield management/epidemiology point.; APSGN / acute post-streptococcal GN: beyond mention, the Australian high-burden context (remote Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander communities, skin sores/scabies-driven, RHD overlap, healthy skin programs) and the link to later CKD risk is a distinctly Australian exam theme worth expanding.; Contrast-associated AKI: current evidence/eTG position has shifted toward de-emphasising routine prophylaxis beyond IV isotonic fluid in high-risk patients and away from N-acetylcysteine; the tutorial's 'hydrate, hold metformin' is fine but should note metformin is withheld for contrast mainly when eGFR is low (e.g. <30, or AKI risk), not universally.; Pregnancy/obstetric and drug-specific caveats: ACEi/ARB and SGLT2i are contraindicated in pregnancy — relevant given reproductive-age CKD patients; a common safety distractor.; Hyperkalaemia work-up should include excluding pseudohyperkalaemia (haemolysed/old sample, thrombocytosis) before aggressive treatment — a frequently tested first step the tutorial omits.; Bicarbonate therapy for chronic metabolic acidosis in CKD (target serum HCO3, e.g. >=22 mmol/L) to slow progression and protect bone/muscle — a KDIGO/Australian management point not covered in the CKD section.

### Applied MCQs

**Q1.** A 78-year-old man from Bendigo presents to ED after 3 days of profuse watery diarrhoea following a community gastro outbreak. His regular medications are ramipril, indapamide and recently-started ibuprofen for knee pain. He looks dry: BP 96/58 lying, HR 104, mucous membranes parched. Bloods: creatinine 198 micromol/L (baseline 88), urea 22 mmol/L, Na 139, K 4.8, HCO3 20. Urine dipstick is bland with no blood or protein. A spot urine sodium is 14 mmol/L. What is the single best next step?

   - A. Urgent renal tract ultrasound looking for hydronephrosis
   - B. IV 0.9% sodium chloride bolus and withhold ramipril, indapamide and ibuprofen
   - C. Commence haemodialysis for acute kidney injury
   - D. Renal biopsy to confirm acute tubular necrosis
   - E. IV calcium gluconate and insulin-dextrose

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. IV 0.9% sodium chloride bolus and withhold ramipril, indapamide and ibuprofen**

This is textbook pre-renal AKI from the 'triple whammy' (ACEi + diuretic + NSAID) plus a dehydrating gastro illness. Every localising clue points pre-renal: clinically hypovolaemic, urine Na <20 (sodium-avid), bland urine, and a disproportionately raised urea relative to creatinine. The kidney is structurally intact, so the answer is restore perfusion AND remove the haemodynamic insult. C is wrong: there is no AEIOU dialysis indication (no refractory acidosis, K only 4.8, no overload/uraemia/intoxication) and pre-renal AKI reverses with fluid. A is the reflex for post-renal but nothing here suggests obstruction (no anuria, no LUTS, no single kidney). D over-investigates a reversible pre-renal picture and ATN/biopsy is not indicated when the cause is obvious. E treats hyperkalaemia that does not exist (K 4.8).

_Teaching point:_ In suspected pre-renal AKI, the urine sodium/FeNa, urea:creatinine pattern and volume exam localise the lesion BEFORE management; the answer is perfusion plus stopping the haemodynamic culprits, not dialysis or biopsy.
</details>

**Q2.** A 62-year-old woman with diabetic nephropathy presents with weakness. ECG shows broad QRS complexes with peaked T waves. Venous gas: K 7.3 mmol/L, HCO3 19, pH 7.31. The sample was processed promptly without haemolysis. Which single intervention should be given FIRST?

   - A. Insulin 10 units with 25 g dextrose IV
   - B. Salbutamol 10 mg nebulised
   - C. IV calcium gluconate 10% 10 mL
   - D. Sodium polystyrene sulfonate (Resonium A) orally
   - E. IV sodium bicarbonate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. IV calcium gluconate 10% 10 mL**

There are ECG changes of hyperkalaemia (broad QRS), so the immediate priority is membrane stabilisation with IV calcium to antagonise the membrane effects and prevent arrest. Calcium does NOT lower the potassium but buys time. Insulin-dextrose (A) and salbutamol (B) shift K+ intracellularly and come second, after cardioprotection. Resonium (D) removes K+ from the gut but is slow and of limited acute benefit, never the first step in a peri-arrest ECG. Bicarbonate (E) has a minor and inconsistent shifting effect and is reserved for significant acidosis, not first-line. The order is always stabilise -> shift -> remove.

_Teaching point:_ Hyperkalaemia with ECG changes: give IV calcium FIRST to protect the myocardium; it stabilises the membrane but does not lower potassium, so insulin-dextrose (the shift) and removal must follow.
</details>

**Q3.** A 34-year-old man is found drowsy in a Darwin share-house having reportedly drunk antifreeze. He is tachypnoeic. ABG: pH 7.18, HCO3 8, pCO2 22, Na 140, Cl 100, glucose 5. Measured serum osmolality 320 mOsm/kg; calculated osmolality 290 mOsm/kg. Urine microscopy shows envelope-shaped crystals and his creatinine is rising. Alongside calling the Poisons Information Centre, which is the single most appropriate management?

   - A. IV sodium bicarbonate alone
   - B. Activated charcoal
   - C. Fomepizole (or ethanol infusion) plus urgent haemodialysis
   - D. IV insulin-dextrose
   - E. N-acetylcysteine infusion

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Fomepizole (or ethanol infusion) plus urgent haemodialysis**

This is ethylene glycol poisoning: high anion gap metabolic acidosis (AG = 140 - (100+8) = 32), a raised osmolar gap (320 - 290 = 30), calcium oxalate crystals (envelope/needle-shaped) and AKI. Winters' confirms appropriate respiratory compensation (expected pCO2 = 1.5x8 + 8 = 20, +/-2; measured 22). The antidote blocks alcohol dehydrogenase (fomepizole, or ethanol where fomepizole is less readily available as in much of Australia) and dialysis removes the toxin and the acid load given the AKI and severe acidosis. A treats the number not the cause. B is useless for a rapidly absorbed alcohol presenting late. D and E are wrong toxidromes (E is for paracetamol). Always check the osmolar gap when a high-AG acidosis appears unexplained.

_Teaching point:_ High anion gap metabolic acidosis + raised osmolar gap = toxic alcohol until proven otherwise; oxalate crystals and AKI point to ethylene glycol, treated with ADH blockade (fomepizole/ethanol) plus dialysis.
</details>

**Q4.** A 58-year-old man with type 2 diabetes and CKD (baseline eGFR 38, urine ACR 55 mg/mmol) is reviewed in clinic. Two weeks ago his GP started ramipril. His creatinine has risen from 152 to 188 micromol/L (a 24% rise) and his potassium is 5.2 mmol/L. He is asymptomatic, normotensive and euvolaemic. What is the single most appropriate action?

   - A. Cease ramipril permanently and avoid all RAS inhibitors
   - B. Halve the ramipril dose and add a potassium-wasting diuretic
   - C. Continue ramipril at current dose and recheck bloods
   - D. Cease ramipril and arrange urgent renal artery imaging
   - E. Start sodium polystyrene sulfonate and refer for dialysis planning

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Continue ramipril at current dose and recheck bloods**

A creatinine rise up to ~25-30% after starting an ACEi/ARB is an expected haemodynamic effect (efferent arteriolar dilatation lowering intraglomerular pressure) and is the mechanism of nephroprotection, not nephrotoxicity. A 24% rise with K 5.2 (below the 5.5 action threshold) in an albuminuric diabetic - exactly the patient who benefits most - means continue and monitor. A and D over-react and discard a renoprotective drug; bilateral renal artery stenosis would typically cause a larger rise (>30%) and is not suggested here. B unnecessarily reduces an effective dose. E is grossly premature; there is no dialysis indication.

_Teaching point:_ After starting an ACEi/ARB in albuminuric CKD, tolerate a creatinine rise up to ~25-30% and potassium up to 5.5; this expected dip reflects the nephroprotective fall in intraglomerular pressure - continue and recheck rather than stop.
</details>

**Q5.** A 19-year-old man presents with cola-coloured urine that began the same day as a sore throat. BP is 156/94. Urinalysis shows blood +++ and protein +, with dysmorphic red cells and red cell casts on microscopy. Creatinine is mildly elevated. Complement (C3/C4) is normal and ASOT is not raised. What is the single most likely diagnosis?

   - A. Post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis
   - B. IgA nephropathy
   - C. Minimal change disease
   - D. Membranous nephropathy
   - E. Acute tubular necrosis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. IgA nephropathy**

The synpharyngitic pattern - macroscopic haematuria occurring WITH (not 1-2 weeks after) an upper respiratory infection - is the signature of IgA nephropathy, the commonest GN. The nephritic picture (haematuria, RBC casts, hypertension, mild proteinuria, AKI) with NORMAL complement and negative ASOT distinguishes it from post-strep GN (A), where haematuria follows the infection by 1-3 weeks, C3 is low, and ASOT/anti-DNase B are raised. C and D are nephrotic (heavy proteinuria, no RBC casts). E gives muddy brown casts, not RBC casts, and no hypertension/active sediment. RBC casts localise the lesion to the glomerulus.

_Teaching point:_ Synpharyngitic haematuria with RBC casts and NORMAL complement = IgA nephropathy; a 1-3 week post-infective lag with LOW C3 and raised ASOT = post-streptococcal GN.
</details>

**Q6.** A 67-year-old woman with type 2 diabetes, CKD (eGFR 36) and urine ACR 60 mg/mmol remains on maximally tolerated perindopril and dapagliflozin. BP and HbA1c are at target. Her potassium is 4.6 mmol/L. The team wants to further slow progression of her diabetic kidney disease. Which agent, PBS-listed in Australia for this indication, is the most appropriate add-on?

   - A. Spironolactone
   - B. Finerenone
   - C. Amlodipine
   - D. Indapamide
   - E. Sodium bicarbonate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Finerenone**

Finerenone (Kerendia) is a non-steroidal mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist, PBS-listed in Australia since July 2023 for CKD associated with type 2 diabetes with albuminuria, layered on top of a RAS inhibitor and SGLT2 inhibitor - the modern 'pillars' of diabetic kidney disease. She is the ideal candidate: persistent albuminuria despite ACEi + SGLT2i, K+ in range (4.6), allowing safe initiation with potassium monitoring. A (spironolactone) is a steroidal MRA without the same CKD-DM outcome/PBS framing and carries more hyperkalaemia/endocrine effects. C and D lower BP but are not nephroprotective add-ons in this pathway. E treats acidosis (target HCO3 >=22) but is not the antiproteinuric step asked for here.

_Teaching point:_ In diabetic kidney disease with residual albuminuria on RASi + SGLT2i and acceptable potassium, finerenone (PBS-listed since 2023) is the evidence-based non-steroidal MRA add-on - monitor potassium rather than worry about availability.
</details>

**Q7.** A 70-year-old man with eGFR 26 mL/min/1.73m2 is booked for a contrast-enhanced CT to investigate possible malignancy. He takes metformin and atorvastatin. He is euvolaemic. Which single management plan is most appropriate around the scan?

   - A. Administer N-acetylcysteine before and after contrast
   - B. Ensure IV isotonic fluid hydration and withhold metformin around the contrast load
   - C. Cancel the CT and request MRI with gadolinium instead
   - D. Give prophylactic sodium bicarbonate infusion and continue metformin
   - E. Commence prophylactic haemodialysis after the scan

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Ensure IV isotonic fluid hydration and withhold metformin around the contrast load**

In low eGFR, the evidence-based measure to reduce contrast-associated AKI is IV isotonic fluid in high-risk patients; metformin is withheld around iodinated contrast mainly when eGFR is low (e.g. <30) or AKI risk is high, because of lactic acidosis risk if AKI supervenes. A (NAC) is no longer recommended - the evidence does not support routine use. C is wrong because gadolinium carries its own risk (nephrogenic systemic fibrosis) in advanced CKD and may not answer the clinical question. D over-states bicarbonate prophylaxis (not superior to saline) and wrongly continues metformin. E is not a prophylactic strategy.

_Teaching point:_ Contrast-associated AKI prevention in low eGFR = IV isotonic hydration and sensible medication review (hold metformin when eGFR is low/AKI risk high); routine N-acetylcysteine and prophylactic bicarbonate/dialysis are not indicated.
</details>

**Q8.** A 24-year-old woman presents with periorbital and pedal oedema. Urinalysis shows protein ++++ with no blood and no casts. Urine PCR is markedly elevated (>3.5 g/day equivalent), serum albumin 22 g/L and cholesterol is high. She suddenly develops left flank pain and frank haematuria with a swollen left leg. Which complication best explains this deterioration?

   - A. Renal vein thrombosis
   - B. Obstructing ureteric calculus
   - C. Rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis
   - D. Acute tubular necrosis from hypovolaemia
   - E. Pyelonephritis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Renal vein thrombosis**

She has nephrotic syndrome (heavy proteinuria, hypoalbuminaemia, oedema, hyperlipidaemia, bland sediment with no cells/casts). Nephrotic syndrome is a hypercoagulable state - urinary loss of antithrombin III and other regulatory proteins drives venous thromboembolism, classically renal vein thrombosis presenting with flank pain and haematuria, and here also a DVT (swollen leg). B can cause flank pain/haematuria but does not explain the DVT or the nephrotic biochemistry. C would show RBC casts and an active nephritic sediment, absent here. D and E do not fit the bland sediment plus thrombotic picture.

_Teaching point:_ Nephrotic syndrome is prothrombotic (loss of antithrombin III); new flank pain + haematuria + a DVT points to renal vein thrombosis - bland heavy proteinuria with no casts localises the lesion to the podocyte barrier, not the glomerular inflammation of nephritic disease.
</details>

### Schema recall — "What is your schema for X?"

**What is your schema for the localisation and work-up of acute kidney injury (AKI)?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Confirm AKI by KDIGO: creatinine rise >=26.5 micromol/L in 48h, OR >=1.5x baseline in 7 days, OR urine output <0.5 mL/kg/h for >=6h. 2) Localise along the tube into pre-renal / intrinsic / post-renal. 3) Pre-renal (perfusion): volume exam, urine Na <20 and FeNa <1%, raised SI urea:creatinine ratio (urea mmol/L : creatinine micromol/L; normal ~40-100, >100 suggests pre-renal), bland urine; causes = hypovolaemia, sepsis, cardiorenal, hepatorenal, triple whammy. 4) Intrinsic: urine microscopy - muddy brown granular casts = ATN; RBC casts/dysmorphic RBCs = GN; WBC casts/eosinophiluria + drug = AIN; FeNa >2%. 5) Post-renal: renal tract ultrasound early for hydronephrosis. 6) Management: restore perfusion, treat sepsis, stop nephrotoxins/renally-cleared drugs, relieve obstruction, screen AEIOU for dialysis.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Students apply the US BUN:creatinine '>20:1' or '>100:1' figures, which are invalid in Australian SI units (urea mmol/L, creatinine micromol/L); the SI ratio is ~40-100 with >100 suggesting pre-renal. The ratio is also non-specific and must not override FeNa, microscopy and clinical volume status. Examiners also attack the failure to image early for post-renal causes.
</details>

**What is your schema for the emergency management of hyperkalaemia?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

0) FIRST exclude pseudohyperkalaemia (haemolysed/old sample, thrombocytosis, fist-clenching) - repeat if no ECG changes and the result is unexpected. 1) Get an ECG and continuous monitoring. 2) STABILISE the membrane: IV calcium gluconate 10% 10 mL (cardioprotection only; does NOT lower K+) if ECG changes or K+ high. 3) SHIFT intracellularly: insulin 10 units short-acting + adequate glucose (e.g. 50 mL 50% dextrose), monitor BSL for up to 6h due to delayed hypoglycaemia (omit dextrose if already hyperglycaemic); +/- salbutamol nebs; bicarbonate only if acidotic. 4) REMOVE: Resonium A (sodium polystyrene sulfonate) - slow, limited acute benefit; diuresis; haemodialysis is the only definitive route. 5) Treat the cause and review contributing drugs (RASi, MRA, NSAID).

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Two attack points: (a) believing calcium lowers potassium - it only stabilises the membrane; and (b) forgetting to exclude pseudohyperkalaemia before aggressive treatment. Also: do not quote SZC/patiromer as routine Australian binders - they are not PBS-subsidised as of 2026; Resonium A is the available exchanger, and it is slow so dialysis is definitive.
</details>

**What is your schema for the approach to a metabolic acidosis?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Confirm primary metabolic acidosis: low pH, low HCO3. 2) Calculate anion gap = Na - (Cl + HCO3) (normal ~8-12). 3) RAISED AG = added acid: MUDPILES - Methanol, Uraemia (retained sulfate/phosphate/organic acids), DKA/ketoacidosis, Propylene glycol, Iron/Isoniazid, Lactate (sepsis, metformin in AKI), Ethylene glycol, Salicylates. 4) If high AG and unexplained, CHECK THE OSMOLAR GAP (measured - calculated osmolality; >10 = toxic alcohol -> methanol = blindness, ethylene glycol = oxalate crystals + AKI). 5) NORMAL AG (hyperchloraemic) = lost bicarbonate: diarrhoea or RTA. 6) Check compensation with Winters': expected pCO2 = 1.5xHCO3 + 8 (+/-2); deviation = mixed disorder. 7) Consider delta ratio for a coexisting second acid-base process.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Students forget to compute the osmolar gap on an unexplained high-AG acidosis (missing the toxic alcohol/dialysis case), and mis-attribute the uraemic gap to 'urate' - it is retained sulfate, phosphate and organic acids; urate contributes negligibly. They also skip Winters' and miss a superimposed respiratory disorder.
</details>

**What is your schema for slowing progression of CKD?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Stage by eGFR (G1-G5) AND albuminuria (A1-A3) together - KHA heat map; same eGFR is higher risk with more albuminuria. 2) Lower intraglomerular pressure: ACEi/ARB if albuminuria (tolerate creatinine rise up to ~25-30%, manage K once >5.5). 3) SGLT2 inhibitor (dapagliflozin OR empagliflozin, both PBS-listed for CKD) in diabetic and non-diabetic albuminuric CKD; expect/continue through the early eGFR dip; not initiated below eGFR ~20. 4) In type 2 diabetes with albuminuria, add finerenone (PBS-listed) on top of RASi + SGLT2i, monitor K; consider GLP-1 RA (e.g. semaglutide). 5) BP and glycaemic targets. 6) Treat acidosis (target HCO3 >=22). 7) Cardiovascular risk: statin in essentially all CKD (CV death dominates). 8) Remove second hits: NSAIDs, contrast, gentamicin; SGLT2i/metformin sick-day rules. 9) Refer per KHA/KFRE cues.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Naming only dapagliflozin (empagliflozin is also PBS-listed for CKD from Nov 2025) and omitting the finerenone/GLP-1 pillars; quoting K 'up to 6.0' as acceptable on RASi (act at >5.5; 6.0 is hyperkalaemia needing treatment); forgetting statins (CV is the leading cause of death, not progression to dialysis); and forgetting ACEi/ARB and SGLT2i are contraindicated in pregnancy.
</details>

**What is your schema for distinguishing nephritic from nephrotic syndrome?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Use urinalysis as the localiser. 2) NEPHRITIC = inflamed glomerulus leaky to cells: haematuria + dysmorphic RBCs + RBC casts + hypertension + mild-moderate proteinuria + AKI. Causes: IgA nephropathy (commonest, synpharyngitic, normal complement), post-strep GN (1-3 weeks post, low C3, raised ASOT), and RPGN (ANCA/anti-GBM - renal emergency, biopsy + immunosuppression). 3) NEPHROTIC = podocyte/barrier failure: proteinuria >3.5 g/day + hypoalbuminaemia (<30) + oedema + hyperlipidaemia + thrombosis risk, bland sediment (no cells/casts). Causes: minimal change (kids), FSGS, membranous (anti-PLA2R). 4) Modern IgAN therapy: maximally tolerated RASi PLUS SGLT2i (CKD bundle) regardless of diabetes; targeted-release budesonide (Nefecon) or sparsentan for progressive disease.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Confusing the synpharyngitic IgA timing/normal complement with the post-strep 1-3 week lag and low C3; forgetting RPGN is a biopsy-and-immunosuppress emergency; and being unaware that nephrotic syndrome is prothrombotic (renal vein thrombosis). Examiners also probe the updated IgAN bundle (RASi + SGLT2i for proteinuric IgAN).
</details>

**What is your schema for the dialysis (or toxin-removal) indications in AKI/poisoning?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

AEIOU, each qualified by 'refractory to medical therapy', not a number: A = refractory metabolic Acidosis; E = refractory Electrolytes (hyperkalaemia); I = Intoxication (dialysable toxins - toxic alcohols/methanol/ethylene glycol, lithium, salicylate); O = fluid Overload (refractory pulmonary oedema); U = Uraemia (pericarditis, encephalopathy). Apply after maximal medical therapy and in liaison with nephrology/Poisons Information for toxins.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Students dialyse to a creatinine or potassium NUMBER rather than for refractory failure of medical therapy; and forget which toxins are dialysable (toxic alcohols, lithium, salicylate) versus those that are not. Examiners exploit the 'refractory' qualifier - a single high value without failed medical management is the wrong trigger.
</details>

### Distractor drills — "What's the deadliest distractor for X?"

**What is the most seductive wrong answer when a hyperkalaemic patient with ECG changes needs first-line treatment?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Insulin-dextrose given first to 'bring the potassium down'
- _Why it tempts you:_ It feels like the definitive fix because it actually lowers the serum potassium, whereas calcium does not change the number at all - candidates equate 'lowering K+' with treating the emergency.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ With ECG changes the immediate threat is a lethal arrhythmia; you must stabilise the myocardium FIRST. Shifting K+ takes time and does nothing to protect the membrane in the critical first minutes.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Presence of ECG changes (broad QRS, peaked T) mandates IV calcium FIRST - calcium protects the heart without lowering K+; insulin-dextrose follows.**

**What is the deadliest distractor when an Australian stem reports a 'raised urea:creatinine ratio of 22:1' favouring pre-renal AKI?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Applying the US BUN:creatinine '>20:1' (or '>100:1') threshold to confirm pre-renal AKI
- _Why it tempts you:_ It is the figure drilled into students from US textbooks and UpToDate, and it superficially 'confirms' the pre-renal call.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Australian labs report urea in mmol/L and creatinine in micromol/L, so the US BUN-based ratio is dimensionally invalid; the SI urea:creatinine ratio runs ~40-100, with >100 suggesting pre-renal.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Check the units: if urea is in mmol/L and creatinine in micromol/L, ignore US BUN ratios and use FeNa/urine Na/microscopy/volume status as the real localisers.**

**What is the most seductive wrong answer when a diabetic on a new ACEi shows a 24% creatinine rise and K 5.2?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Cease the ACEi (and avoid all RAS inhibitors) because of 'nephrotoxicity'
- _Why it tempts you:_ A rising creatinine after a drug looks causally damning, and stopping it feels safe and conservative.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ A rise up to ~25-30% is the expected haemodynamic effect of efferent dilatation and is the mechanism of nephroprotection in the albuminuric diabetic who benefits most; K 5.2 is below the 5.5 action threshold.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **The magnitude: <=25-30% creatinine rise with K <=5.5 = continue and recheck; only >30% rise or K>5.5-6.0 / symptoms triggers reassessment (and a big jump suggests bilateral renal artery stenosis).**

**What is the deadliest distractor for the 2026 Australian SGLT2i-for-CKD question that names only 'dapagliflozin'?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Stating dapagliflozin is the sole SGLT2 inhibitor PBS-listed for CKD nephroprotection
- _Why it tempts you:_ Dapagliflozin was first listed and is the agent most associated with the DAPA-CKD trial, so it is the reflex single answer.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ From 1 November 2025 empagliflozin is also PBS-listed for CKD (eGFR 20-90, with UACR threshold if eGFR 45-90), so 'dapagliflozin only' is incomplete.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **In 2026 both dapagliflozin AND empagliflozin are PBS-listed for CKD; know the eGFR ~20 initiation floor and the UACR >=22.6 mg/mmol threshold when eGFR 45-90.**

**What is the most seductive distractor when an unexplained high anion gap acidosis presents and you are asked for the next test?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Attributing the gap to lactate or uraemia and skipping the osmolar gap
- _Why it tempts you:_ Lactate and uraemia are the commonest high-AG causes, so it is statistically tempting to stop there and treat empirically.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Missing the osmolar gap misses toxic alcohols (methanol -> blindness, ethylene glycol -> oxalate crystals + AKI), which are time-critical and need antidote (fomepizole/ethanol) plus dialysis.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Any unexplained high-AG acidosis -> calculate the osmolar gap; >10 mOsm/kg flags a toxic alcohol that lactate/uraemia cannot explain.**

**What is the deadliest distractor when a child/young adult has cola-coloured urine with a recent sore throat?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Diagnosing post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis
- _Why it tempts you:_ Sore throat + haematuria is the classic post-strep association every student memorises, and APSGN is high-burden in remote Australian communities.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Synpharyngitic timing (haematuria WITH the URTI, not 1-3 weeks later) plus NORMAL complement and negative ASOT points to IgA nephropathy, not post-strep GN.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **The latency and complement: concurrent haematuria + normal C3 = IgA nephropathy; 1-3 week lag + low C3 + raised ASOT = post-streptococcal GN.**

**What is the most seductive distractor for contrast-associated AKI prophylaxis in a patient with low eGFR?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Giving N-acetylcysteine (and/or a sodium bicarbonate infusion) before contrast
- _Why it tempts you:_ NAC was widely taught and used for years and feels like a proactive, low-harm renoprotective measure.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Current evidence/eTG no longer support routine NAC, and bicarbonate is not superior to isotonic saline; the only robust measure is IV isotonic fluid in high-risk patients plus sensible drug review.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Effective prophylaxis = IV isotonic hydration (+ hold metformin when eGFR low/AKI risk high); NAC and prophylactic bicarbonate/dialysis are not indicated.**

### High-end review pearls

- Aunt-Minnie: high anion gap metabolic acidosis + raised osmolar gap + envelope/needle-shaped (calcium oxalate) crystals + AKI = ethylene glycol; methanol gives the gaps with visual loss and a normal-ish sediment.
- If you see synpharyngitic macroscopic haematuria with NORMAL complement, think IgA nephropathy; if the haematuria lags a throat/skin infection by 1-3 weeks with LOW C3, think post-streptococcal GN.
- Never-forget one-liner: IV calcium in hyperkalaemia protects the heart but does NOT lower the potassium - stabilise, then shift, then remove.
- Australian units trap: ignore US BUN:creatinine ratios (>20:1 / >100:1). In SI units the urea(mmol/L):creatinine(micromol/L) ratio is ~40-100, with >100 suggesting pre-renal - and it never overrides FeNa/microscopy/volume status.
- Binder trap (2026): the K+ exchanger available/affordable in Australia is Resonium A; SZC (Lokelma) and patiromer are NOT PBS-subsidised - don't quote them as routine, and remember resonium is slow so dialysis is the only definitive removal.
- The diabetic kidney disease 'four pillars' for 2026 Australia: RASi + SGLT2i + non-steroidal MRA (finerenone, PBS-listed since July 2023) + GLP-1 RA (FLOW data) - all layered, with potassium monitoring as you add finerenone.
- SGLT2i for CKD in 2026: BOTH dapagliflozin and empagliflozin are PBS-listed; initiate down to eGFR ~20, expect an early eGFR dip and continue through it, and respect the UACR >=22.6 mg/mmol threshold when eGFR is 45-90.
- If you see a creatinine jump >30% after starting an ACEi/ARB, think bilateral renal artery stenosis; a rise <=25-30% is the expected nephroprotective dip - continue.
- Never-forget: the leading cause of death in CKD is cardiovascular, NOT progression to dialysis - so a statin is indicated in essentially all CKD, and don't let the vignette distract you into only managing eGFR.
- Nephrotic syndrome is a hypercoagulable state (urinary antithrombin III loss) - new flank pain + haematuria + a DVT = renal vein thrombosis until proven otherwise.
- Triple whammy = ACEi/ARB + diuretic + NSAID; add an intercurrent gastro/dehydration and you have the single most reproduced Australian primary-care pre-renal AKI stem - the answer is withhold the three drugs and rehydrate.
- Pregnancy safety distractor: ACEi/ARB and SGLT2i are contraindicated in pregnancy - watch for the reproductive-age CKD patient where the 'best nephroprotection' answer is a trap.
- AEIOU dialysis indications all carry the silent word 'refractory' - dialyse for failure of medical therapy, not for a creatinine or potassium number.
- Uraemic high-AG acidosis: the retained anions are sulfate, phosphate and organic acids - not urate (urate contributes negligibly to the measured gap).
- Australian equity theme: Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Australians carry several-fold higher ESKD rates (highest in remote/Central Australia), driven by diabetic and post-infectious/proteinuric disease; APSGN outbreaks tie to skin sores/scabies and healthy-skin programs, and remote/return-to-country dialysis access is a service-level exam angle.
- Insulin-dextrose safety pearl: 10 units short-acting insulin can cause delayed hypoglycaemia in renal impairment - give adequate glucose and monitor BSL for up to 6 hours (omit dextrose only if already hyperglycaemic).
- Chronic metabolic acidosis in CKD: treat to a serum bicarbonate target (>=22 mmol/L) to slow progression and protect bone and muscle - an easily forgotten KDIGO/Australian management mark.
- eGFR caveat: Australian labs use the race-free 2021 CKD-EPI creatinine equation (never an Indigenous coefficient); it is invalid in non-steady-state (AKI), pregnancy and extremes of muscle mass - don't trust an eGFR in evolving AKI.

> **Independent item audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[low]** MCQ1 (pre-renal AKI, Bendigo gastro) — spot urine sodium clue with patient on a diuretic: The stem hangs a clean pre-renal call partly on a spot urine Na of 14 mmol/L (sodium-avid), but the patient is on indapamide, a thiazide-like diuretic. Diuretics natriuresis invalidates low urine Na / FeNa as a pre-renal marker — a patient on indapamide who is still excreting only 14 mmol/L Na is biochemically notable but a sharp examiner could argue the urine Na is uninterpretable here. The explanation leans on the urine Na as a 'localising clue' without acknowledging the diuretic caveat that the schema itself implies. The answer (B) is still unambiguously correct on clinical grounds (volume status, urea:creatinine, bland sediment, triple-whammy), so this does not break the item, but the urine-Na rhetoric is internally inconsistent with good FeNa teaching. → Either soften the explanation to note 'urine Na is less reliable on a diuretic but the clinical picture and urea:creatinine pattern remain pre-renal,' or drop the spot urine Na from the stem and rely on the clinical/urea clues. Optionally use FeUrea instead of FeNa as the more robust marker in the diuretic patient.
> - **[low]** MCQ1 — urea:creatinine framing in explanation: Explanation says 'disproportionately raised urea relative to creatinine.' Creatinine rose 88→198 (2.25x). Urea 22 with creatinine 198 gives an SI ratio of ~111, which by the schema's own cutoff (>100 suggests pre-renal) does support pre-renal — so the claim is defensible. But the stem does not give a baseline urea, so 'disproportionately raised' is asserted rather than shown. Minor. → Add a baseline urea (e.g. baseline urea 6) to make the disproportionate rise explicit, or rely on the absolute SI ratio >100 which is already consistent.
> - **[low]** MCQ7 (contrast CT, eGFR 26) — metformin/eGFR threshold precision: Answer B says withhold metformin 'around the contrast load,' explanation says held 'mainly when eGFR is low (e.g. <30) or AKI risk high.' This patient has eGFR 26, so metformin should be withheld — correct. But the stem's strongest defensible teaching is that at eGFR 26 with IV iodinated contrast, holding metformin is clearly indicated (eGFR <30), not merely 'sensible.' The item is correct; the only risk is that a student who knows ACR/eGFR contrast guidance might note routine hydration is debated (PRESERVE/AMACING showed IV saline benefit is modest/uncertain in moderate CKD). Single best answer is still B because every other option is wrong. → No change required to the answer. Could tighten explanation to: 'at eGFR <30 with iodinated contrast, withhold metformin; ensure isotonic IV hydration as the only measure with supportive evidence.'
> - **[low]** MCQ2 (hyperkalaemia first step) — distractor D dosing realism: Distractor D 'Resonium A orally' in a peri-arrest patient with broad QRS is a clean strawman — no examinee stabilising a peri-arrest ECG would reach for an oral binder. It is a weak distractor relative to the genuinely seductive trap (insulin-dextrose, which IS option A and well-constructed). D adds little discriminative value. → Consider replacing D with a more seductive trap such as 'Urgent haemodialysis' (tempting in dialysis-dependent diabetic with K 7.3) or 'IV calcium chloride via peripheral line' (route nuance). This raises ceiling difficulty.
> - **[low]** MCQ5 (IgA vs post-strep) — 'commonest GN' claim: Explanation calls IgA nephropathy 'the commonest GN.' This is the standard teaching (commonest primary glomerulonephritis worldwide and in Australia) and is correct, but stem could be read as steering. Not an error. Single-best-answer is clean: synpharyngitic timing + normal complement + negative ASOT excludes A decisively. → No fix needed; optionally specify 'commonest primary glomerulonephritis' for precision.
> - **[low]** Schema (CKD progression) — empagliflozin UACR threshold wording: Schema/highEndExtras state empagliflozin PBS UACR threshold ≥22.6 mg/mmol when eGFR 45-90, and subsidised regardless of albuminuria when eGFR 20-<45. Verified correct against the 1 Nov 2025 PBS expansion. No error — flagging as VERIFIED-CORRECT so it is not silently assumed wrong on re-audit. → None — claim confirmed accurate per PBS Nov 2025 listing.
> - **[low]** Cross-cutting — 'fomepizole less readily available in Australia' (MCQ3): Explanation states fomepizole 'is less readily available as in much of Australia,' implying ethanol is the typical Australian antidote. As of 2026 fomepizole availability has improved in Australian tertiary/Poisons-supported centres and is the preferred agent where stocked; the 'less readily available' framing is increasingly dated and could be marked down by a current toxicology examiner. The answer C (fomepizole OR ethanol + dialysis) is correct regardless. → Reword to 'fomepizole is first-line where available (stocked in major centres / via Poisons Information); ethanol infusion is the alternative,' removing the blanket availability claim.
> 
> _Quality notes:_ Verified against 2026 Australian sources: empagliflozin PBS-listed for CKD from 1 Nov 2025 (eGFR 20-90; UACR >=22.6 mg/mmol if eGFR 45-90; regardless of albuminuria if eGFR 20-<45) — CONFIRMED accurate. Finerenone PBS-listed from 1 July 2023 — CONFIRMED. KDIGO 30% creatinine-rise / K 5.5 action thresholds — CONFIRMED; the JSON's '~25-30%' framing is safe and consistent with the hard 30% line.; Single-best-answer integrity is strong across all 8 MCQs. No distractor is accidentally also-correct. The closest scrutiny is MCQ1 (urine Na on a diuretic) and MCQ7 (hydration evidence in moderate CKD), but in both the keyed answer remains the only defensible option because all distractors are clearly wrong or harmful.; The genuinely deadly distractors are well-built and correctly identified: insulin-dextrose-first in hyperkalaemia (MCQ2/drill1), US BUN ratio in SI units (schema/drill2), stopping ACEi for an expected creatinine dip (MCQ4/drill3), post-strep GN for synpharyngitic IgA (MCQ5/drill6), and NAC for contrast prophylaxis (MCQ7/drill7). These are the real traps, not strawmen.; Weakest distractors: Resonium (MCQ2 option D) and renal biopsy / dialysis options in MCQ1 (C, D) are near-strawman in a peri-arrest or obvious-pre-renal context — they lower ceiling difficulty slightly but do not compromise validity.; Arithmetic verified in MCQ3: anion gap 32, osmolar gap 30, Winters' expected pCO2 = 20 ±2 (measured 22 sits at the upper bound — technically appropriate compensation, consistent with the 'no additional respiratory disorder' read). All internally consistent.; Schemas are clinically sound and self-aware about their own failure points (SI urea:creatinine units, pseudohyperkalaemia, osmolar gap, AEIOU 'refractory' qualifier, race-free 2021 CKD-EPI). The 'whereItBreaks' sections are unusually high-quality and exam-accurate.; Equity/Australian-context content (ATSI ESKD burden, APSGN/scabies skin-sore link, race-free eGFR, Resonium-not-SZC PBS reality) is accurate and appropriately weighted for an Australian exam.; Minor dated claim: 'fomepizole less readily available in Australia' (MCQ3) is softening over time as fomepizole stocking improves; recommend rewording to avoid an examiner mark-down, though it does not affect the keyed answer.

---

## Neurology  _(~9% of marks)_

### The one idea that unlocks the domain

> **Neurology is the disciplined sequence: LOCALISE the lesion (where), then TIME-STAMP the deficit (how fast it evolved), then act on whichever EMERGENCY clock is running. Anatomy tells you what is broken; tempo tells you the mechanism; and a small set of time-critical clocks (recanalise the brain, stop the seizure, decompress the cord, ventilate the failing neuromuscular patient, give steroids before vision/cord is lost) tells you what to do RIGHT NOW.**

Almost every Neurology single-best-answer is solvable by answering three questions in order. (1) WHERE is the lesion? Cortex vs subcortex vs brainstem vs cord vs root vs nerve vs NMJ vs muscle each produce a signature (e.g. UMN below a sensory level = cord; areflexic ascending weakness = root/nerve; fatigable ptosis/diplopia = NMJ). (2) HOW FAST? Seconds-to-minutes = vascular or seizure; hours-days = inflammatory/infective; weeks-months = compressive/neoplastic; months-years = degenerative. Tempo + location nearly always names the diagnosis without a list. (3) WHICH CLOCK? Once you have a syndrome, the examiner is testing whether you start the time-critical intervention before the irreversible-damage window closes: thrombolysis/thrombectomy windows in stroke, ABC-then-benzo-then-load in status, urgent MRI + dexamethasone in cord compression, FVC monitoring + IVIg/plex in GBS/myasthenic crisis, high-dose steroids in giant cell arteritis to save the contralateral eye. Owning this engine means you do not memorise isolated facts; you reconstruct them. A "sudden worst-ever headache" localises to subarachnoid space, tempo says vascular, clock says non-contrast CT now then LP if negative. The same scaffold derives the right answer whether the stem is a stroke, a fitting patient, or a numb saddle.

### High-yield tutorial

## The engine: Localise → Time → Clock

**Step 1 — Localise.** Match the exam signature to a level:
- **Cortex:** higher-function loss (dysphasia, neglect, gaze deviation *towards* lesion), contralateral weakness face+arm>leg (MCA) or leg>arm (ACA).
- **Brainstem:** crossed signs (ipsilateral cranial nerve + contralateral limb), vertigo, diplopia, dysarthria, ataxia — **posterior circulation**.
- **Cord:** **a sensory level**, UMN signs below it, bladder involvement. Compression until proven otherwise.
- **Anterior horn/root/nerve:** LMN — wasting, fasciculation, areflexia, no sensory level.
- **NMJ:** **fatigable**, ptosis/diplopia/bulbar, normal reflexes/sensation.
- **Muscle:** symmetrical **proximal** weakness, no sensory loss.

**Step 2 — Time-stamp.** Seconds–minutes → vascular/seizure. Hours–days → inflammatory/infective (GBS, MS relapse, meningoencephalitis). Weeks–months → compressive/neoplastic. Years → degenerative (Parkinson's, dementia, MND).

**Step 3 — Run the clock.** Below are the five clocks finals love.

### Stroke clock
Sudden focal deficit → **non-contrast CT immediately** (its job is to exclude haemorrhage, not confirm infarct). Then:
- **IV thrombolysis (alteplase/tenecteplase) within 4.5 h** of known onset, if no haemorrhage and no contraindication. **Tenecteplase is now first-line in many Australian stroke services.**
- **Endovascular thrombectomy** for large-vessel occlusion (proximal MCA/ICA) **up to 24 h** when perfusion imaging (CTP) shows salvageable penumbra (mismatch).
- **BP must be <185/110 before lysis.** **Aspirin 300 mg is withheld for 24 h after lysis**, then started.
- **Haemorrhage** → reverse anticoagulation, lower BP (target SBP ~140), neurosurgery referral.
- **TIA:** **ABCD2 is de-emphasised** — treat all as high-risk. Give **aspirin 300 mg**, urgent specialist review/imaging, start dual antiplatelet (aspirin+clopidogrel 21 days) for high-risk TIA/minor stroke, then long-term single agent. **Carotid endarterectomy within 2 weeks** for symptomatic 70–99% (and selected 50–69%) ipsilateral stenosis. **AF → anticoagulate** (DOAC), not antiplatelet.

### Seizure / status clock
Status = seizure >5 min or recurrent without recovery. **ABC, glucose, thiamine** → then the ladder:
1. **Benzodiazepine:** IV **lorazepam** or **midazolam** (IM/buccal if no IV). Repeat once.
2. **Loading agent:** **levetiracetam, sodium valproate, or phenytoin** IV.
3. **Refractory → ICU, intubate, anaesthesia** (propofol/midazolam infusion).
Never give a second benzo beyond two doses without escalating — respiratory depression without controlling the seizure.

### Headache red-flag clock
- **Thunderclap, worst-ever, maximal at onset → SAH.** Non-contrast CT (near 100% if <6 h); **if negative, LP at ≥12 h for xanthochromia.**
- **Fever + headache + neck stiffness/photophobia → meningitis.** **Do not delay antibiotics for imaging/LP** — **ceftriaxone + dexamethasone** immediately; **add benzylpenicillin/amoxicillin if Listeria risk** (>50, immunosuppressed, pregnant).
- **>50 + temporal/jaw claudication + scalp tenderness → GCA.** **Start high-dose prednis(ol)one immediately to save the other eye**, then temporal artery biopsy (don't wait for it). ESR/CRP raised.
- **Postural headache, papilloedema, morning vomiting → raised ICP/mass.** Image; avoid LP until mass excluded.

### Neuromuscular respiratory clock
- **GBS:** ascending **areflexic** weakness days after GI/respiratory infection (*Campylobacter*). **Monitor FVC — intubate if <15 mL/kg or falling; not SpO₂.** Treat with **IVIg or plasma exchange** (steroids do NOT work). Watch **autonomic instability** (arrhythmia). CSF: albuminocytologic dissociation.
- **Myasthenic crisis:** fatigable weakness + respiratory failure, often triggered (infection, aminoglycosides, surgery). **IVIg/plasma exchange + ventilation.** Distinguish from **cholinergic crisis** (excess pyridostigmine: SLUDGE, miosis). Anti-AChR antibodies; check for **thymoma (CT chest)**.

### Cord / cauda equina clock
**Bilateral leg weakness + sensory level + bladder/bowel + back pain → urgent whole-spine MRI within hours.**
- **Cord compression** (metastatic) → **dexamethasone 16 mg now** + MRI + oncology/neurosurgery; radiotherapy or decompression.
- **Cauda equina** (below L1): **saddle anaesthesia, painless retention/incontinence, reduced anal tone** → **emergency MRI + surgical decompression**; delay = permanent. This is the classic "missed" stem.

### The slow clocks (pattern, not speed)
- **Parkinson's:** asymmetric resting tremor, bradykinesia, rigidity; **levodopa** responsive. Tremor *worse at rest*, improved by action (vs essential tremor: action/postural, **better with alcohol**, treat propranolol). **Beware drug-induced parkinsonism (metoclopramide, antipsychotics).**
- **Delirium vs dementia:** delirium = **acute, fluctuating, inattention, altered consciousness** (find/treat the cause — infection, drugs, metabolic). Dementia = chronic, stable consciousness.
- **MS:** young, female, **lesions separated in time and space**; optic neuritis, INO, Lhermitte's. MRI + oligoclonal bands. Acute relapse → **IV methylprednisolone** (shortens, doesn't change outcome).
- **Peripheral neuropathy:** length-dependent glove-and-stocking → **diabetes, alcohol, B12, drugs, renal**. Mononeuritis multiplex → vasculitis/diabetes.

**Exam reflex:** sensory level = cord; areflexia = nerve/root; fatigability = NMJ; thunderclap = SAH; sudden focal = stroke clock; >50 + headache + visual = steroids before biopsy.

### Highest mark-per-minute principles

- **Non-contrast CT in acute stroke is to EXCLUDE haemorrhage, not to confirm infarct (which is often invisible early).** — Tests whether you understand imaging purpose; correct answer is 'thrombolyse if CT shows no blood' not 'wait for infarct to appear'.
  - _Exam angle:_ Stem: dense MCA sign or normal CT at 2 h — best next step is thrombolysis, not repeat imaging.
- **Thrombectomy extends to 24 h with perfusion mismatch; thrombolysis stays 4.5 h.** — Two different windows for two different treatments is a classic trap.
  - _Exam angle:_ Patient at 10 h with LVO and salvageable penumbra → thrombectomy, NOT 'too late for treatment'.
- **In suspected meningitis, give ceftriaxone + dexamethasone before LP/CT if any delay.** — Antibiotic timing saves lives; examiners punish 'do LP first'.
  - _Exam angle:_ Febrile, drowsy, neck stiff → immediate IV ceftriaxone; imaging/LP after.
- **GCA: start steroids immediately to protect the second eye; biopsy can wait days.** — Sequence (treat-then-investigate) is the whole point.
  - _Exam angle:_ 75yo, jaw claudication, raised ESR, blurred vision → high-dose prednisolone now.
- **GBS respiratory failure is monitored by FVC, not oxygen saturation.** — SpO2 stays normal until catastrophic collapse; FVC <15 mL/kg triggers intubation.
  - _Exam angle:_ Ascending weakness with falling FVC, normal sats → ICU/ventilate.
- **Cauda equina and metastatic cord compression need MRI within hours and (cord) dexamethasone now.** — Time-to-decompression determines permanent deficit; the most commonly 'missed' emergency.
  - _Exam angle:_ Back pain + saddle numbness + urinary retention → emergency MRI + surgery.
- **Status epilepticus ladder: benzo (max 2 doses) → IV loading agent → ICU/anaesthesia.** — Repeated benzodiazepines without escalation cause respiratory arrest.
  - _Exam angle:_ Still fitting after 2 doses of midazolam → IV levetiracetam/valproate, not a third benzo.
- **AF-related TIA/stroke needs anticoagulation (DOAC), not antiplatelet.** — Mechanism dictates prevention: cardioembolic vs atherothrombotic.
  - _Exam angle:_ TIA + AF → DOAC; large-artery TIA → antiplatelet + statin + carotid assessment.
- **Resting tremor + asymmetry + bradykinesia = Parkinson's (levodopa); action tremor + alcohol relief = essential tremor (propranolol).** — The single most common movement-disorder discriminator.
  - _Exam angle:_ Tremor improved by a glass of wine, worse on action → essential tremor.
- **MS = dissemination in time AND space; treat relapse with IV methylprednisolone.** — Defines diagnosis and acute management in one rule.
  - _Exam angle:_ Young woman, prior optic neuritis, now INO → MS; steroids for the relapse.

### Must-know numbers (Australian where they differ)

- IV thrombolysis window: within 4.5 h of known symptom onset
- Endovascular thrombectomy: up to 24 h with perfusion mismatch (CTP)
- BP must be <185/110 mmHg before thrombolysis
- Aspirin 300 mg: withheld 24 h post-lysis, otherwise given immediately in non-haemorrhagic stroke/TIA
- Haemorrhagic stroke BP target: SBP ~140 mmHg
- SAH: non-contrast CT ~100% sensitive if <6 h; LP for xanthochromia at ≥12 h if CT negative
- Status epilepticus defined at >5 min continuous seizure; max 2 benzodiazepine doses before escalating
- GBS intubation trigger: FVC <15 mL/kg (or rapidly falling)
- Cord compression: dexamethasone 16 mg IV immediately; MRI within hours
- Carotid endarterectomy: symptomatic 70-99% stenosis within 2 weeks
- Dual antiplatelet (aspirin+clopidogrel) for 21 days after high-risk TIA/minor stroke, then single agent
- Listeria cover (add amoxicillin/benzylpenicillin) for meningitis if age >50, pregnant, or immunosuppressed
- GCA: high-dose prednisolone (e.g. 40-60 mg, up to 1 g IV methylprednisolone if visual loss)
- Tenecteplase 0.25 mg/kg is the increasingly preferred lytic in Australian stroke services

### Cross-topic cuts

- Cardiology: AF mandates anticoagulation for stroke prevention (CHA2DS2-VASc); endocarditis causes embolic stroke; long QT/arrhythmia complicate GBS autonomic instability and phenytoin loading.
- Endocrinology: diabetes is the leading cause of peripheral neuropathy and mononeuritis multiplex; hypoglycaemia mimics stroke/seizure (always check glucose first).
- Rheumatology: GCA overlaps polymyalgia rheumatica; vasculitis causes mononeuritis multiplex; SLE causes seizures/stroke.
- Haematology/Oncology: metastatic cord compression (breast, prostate, lung, myeloma); hyperviscosity; paraneoplastic syndromes (Lambert-Eaton vs myasthenia).
- Infectious disease: meningitis/encephalitis (HSV → aciclovir), Listeria cover, Campylobacter precedes GBS.
- Renal/Gastro: B12 deficiency (subacute combined degeneration), alcohol and thiamine (Wernicke) — give thiamine before glucose.
- Geriatrics: delirium vs dementia, drug-induced parkinsonism (metoclopramide, antipsychotics), polypharmacy lowering seizure threshold.
- Respiratory: neuromuscular respiratory failure (GBS, myasthenic crisis) — FVC monitoring bridges neuro and resp.

### Australian-specific (where the UK answer loses you marks)

- Tenecteplase (0.25 mg/kg) is now widely used as the first-line thrombolytic in Australian stroke services, often in preference to alteplase — UK Passmedicine still centres alteplase.
- Thrombectomy availability is organised through state stroke networks with telestroke (e.g. Victorian Stroke Telemedicine, NSW Telestroke) reaching regional/remote hospitals — expect stems involving transfer decisions.
- ABCD2 score is de-emphasised in current Australian/Stroke Foundation practice; all TIAs are treated as high-risk with urgent assessment, unlike older UK risk-stratification stems.
- Meningococcal empirical therapy in Australia follows eTG: ceftriaxone (+ dexamethasone), with benzylpenicillin/amoxicillin added for Listeria risk groups; notify public health (meningococcal is notifiable).
- DOACs (apixaban, rivaroxaban, dabigatran) are PBS-subsidised and first-line for AF-related stroke prevention over warfarin in most patients.
- Australian seizure guidelines (eTG) favour IV levetiracetam or sodium valproate as second-line after benzodiazepines, with midazolam (IM/buccal) prominent in prehospital and paediatric settings.
- Stroke Foundation (Australia) 'Clinical Guidelines for Stroke Management' is the reference standard, not NICE.
- Giant cell arteritis management follows local rheumatology pathways with fast-track temporal artery biopsy/ultrasound; tocilizumab is available for relapsing/steroid-sparing cases.

> **Independent accuracy audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[high]** IV thrombolysis (alteplase/tenecteplase) within 4.5 h of known onset... Must-know number: 'IV thrombolysis window: within 4.5 h of known symptom onset' → State: standard thrombolysis within 4.5 h of known onset; AND perfusion-imaging-selected thrombolysis up to 9 h (or from sleep midpoint in wake-up stroke) when CTP/MRP shows mismatch. Source: Australian and New Zealand Living Clinical Guidelines for Stroke Management, Chapter 3 (Stroke Foundation / InformMe, 2025 update; magicapp.org guideline_7233).
> - **[high]** Status epilepticus benzodiazepine: IV lorazepam or midazolam (IM/buccal if no IV) → First-line benzodiazepine in Australia: IV midazolam or IV clonazepam; if no IV access use IM/buccal/intranasal midazolam. Lorazepam is the UK/US first-line, not the Australian one. Source: eTG complete (Therapeutic Guidelines, Status epilepticus); Australian Prescriber, 'Midazolam for status epilepticus'.
> - **[medium]** Endovascular thrombectomy ... up to 24 h when perfusion imaging (CTP) shows salvageable penumbra → Thrombectomy 0-6 h for anterior LVO without mandatory perfusion mismatch; 6-24 h requires DAWN/DEFUSE-3 mismatch criteria on advanced imaging. Source: Australian and New Zealand Living Clinical Guidelines for Stroke Management, Chapter 3.
> - **[medium]** In suspected meningitis... ceftriaxone + dexamethasone immediately; add benzylpenicillin/amoxicillin if Listeria risk → Empirical: ceftriaxone (or cefotaxime) + dexamethasone; ADD vancomycin if resistant pneumococcus suspected; ADD benzylpenicillin (or amoxicillin/ampicillin) for Listeria risk (age >50, pregnant, immunocompromised). Notify public health (meningococcal disease is notifiable). Source: eTG complete (Acute bacterial meningitis).
