> - **[low]** Tenecteplase 0.25 mg/kg is the increasingly preferred / 'now first-line in many Australian stroke services' → Tenecteplase 0.25 mg/kg is now the first-line / preferred IV thrombolytic in Australia (alteplase is the alternative). Source: Australian clinical practice guidance on IV thrombolysis (Dec 2025); ANZ Living Clinical Guidelines for Stroke Management 2025 update.
> - **[low]** TIA: ABCD2 is de-emphasised... start dual antiplatelet (aspirin+clopidogrel 21 days) for high-risk TIA/minor stroke → Confirmed accurate; add loading doses (aspirin 300 mg + clopidogrel 300 mg, started <24 h, continue DAPT 21 days then single agent). Source: ANZ Living Clinical Guidelines for Stroke Management; Australian Prescriber, 'Drugs in secondary stroke prevention'.
> - **[low]** Cord compression (metastatic) -> dexamethasone 16 mg now → Dexamethasone 8-16 mg IV/PO as soon as MSCC suspected (commonly 8 mg in Australian palliative pathways; 16 mg acceptable) + urgent whole-spine MRI + oncology/neurosurgery/radiation oncology. Source: eTG; TROG 01.05 SuperDex (Australian trial).
> 
> _Gaps flagged & folded in:_ Extended thrombolysis window: Australia uses perfusion-selected lysis up to 9 h and for wake-up stroke (sleep-midpoint timing) — the single highest-yield Australian divergence from UK material; must be in mustKnowNumbers.; Vancomycin in empirical meningitis therapy (resistant pneumococcus cover) per eTG — currently absent.; Telestroke / state stroke networks (Victorian Stroke Telemedicine, NSW Telestroke, QLD) and drip-and-ship transfer decisions for thrombectomy — common Australian stem framing; mentioned in australianSpecifics but not as an exam reflex.; HSV encephalitis: empirical IV aciclovir for any suspected encephalitis (fever + altered mental state/seizures + focal/temporal features) BEFORE PCR confirmation — high-yield, only touched in crossTopicCuts, not in the headache/infective clock.; Thiamine before glucose in any malnourished/alcohol-dependent patient (Wernicke prevention) — flagged in crossTopicCuts but belongs in the status/altered-consciousness clock as an explicit reflex.; Functional/neurological assessment and driving: Austroads 'Assessing Fitness to Drive' rules after seizure, TIA/stroke, and syncope (non-commercial vs commercial standards) — a distinctly Australian exam/clinical point with no UK equivalent.; Meningococcal disease is a notifiable condition (public health notification + clearance antibiotics/chemoprophylaxis for close contacts with ciprofloxacin or rifampicin) — Australian ASID/eTG point.; Japanese encephalitis virus (JEV) now endemic in parts of mainland Australia (since 2022) and Murray Valley encephalitis — relevant Australian arboviral encephalitis differentials absent from the infective clock.; Tocilizumab is PBS-available for relapsing/steroid-refractory GCA (steroid-sparing) — mentioned in australianSpecifics but worth elevating; also GCA fast-track temporal artery ultrasound (halo sign) increasingly used alongside/before biopsy in Australia.; DOAC timing after cardioembolic stroke ('1-3-6-12 day' rule based on infarct size) and that DOACs are PBS first-line over warfarin for non-valvular AF — practical Australian prescribing detail.; MS disease-modifying therapy access via PBS and that acute optic neuritis/relapse steroids do not alter long-term disability — tutorial states the latter; PBS DMT pathway is the Australian add.; Idiopathic intracranial hypertension (acetazolamide, weight management, monitor visual fields) as a 'postural/raised ICP' differential in young obese women — absent from the raised-ICP clock.

### Applied MCQs

**Q1.** A 68-year-old man is brought to a metropolitan ED in Melbourne. His wife reports he developed sudden right-sided face and arm weakness and slurred speech 7 hours ago while gardening. He has no headache. NIHSS is 11. BP 168/92. BSL 7.2. Non-contrast CT shows no haemorrhage and an early hyperdense left MCA sign. CT angiography confirms a proximal left M1 occlusion, and CT perfusion shows a small infarct core with a large surrounding region of salvageable penumbra. What is the single most appropriate next step?

   - A. Withhold all reperfusion therapy as he is outside the 4.5-hour thrombolysis window
   - B. Proceed to endovascular thrombectomy on the basis of perfusion mismatch
   - C. Give IV tenecteplase 0.25 mg/kg only
   - D. Commence aspirin 300 mg and admit to the stroke unit for supportive care
   - E. Repeat the non-contrast CT in 24 hours to look for established infarct

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Proceed to endovascular thrombectomy on the basis of perfusion mismatch**

This is a large-vessel occlusion (proximal M1) at 7 hours with DAWN/DEFUSE-3-type mismatch (small core, large penumbra) on advanced imaging. In Australia, thrombectomy is offered 6-24 h when perfusion/clinical mismatch criteria are met, so he is firmly a thrombectomy candidate. A is the classic trap: it conflates the thrombolysis clock with the thrombectomy clock — being past 4.5 h does NOT close the reperfusion door for LVO. C is wrong because at 7 h he is outside the standard 4.5 h lytic window (and not described as perfusion-selected for lysis), and lysis alone does not reliably reopen a proximal M1; even if lysed he still needs thrombectomy. D and E abandon a salvageable brain.

_Teaching point:_ Two clocks, two treatments: thrombolysis ~4.5 h (or up to 9 h if perfusion-selected), but thrombectomy for anterior LVO runs to 24 h with mismatch. Being 'too late to lyse' is never the same as 'too late to treat'.
</details>

**Q2.** A 54-year-old woman wakes at 0700 with left facial droop, left arm weakness and neglect. She was last seen well when she went to sleep at 2300. She is brought to a regional Victorian hospital connected to the Victorian Stroke Telemedicine network. Non-contrast CT shows no haemorrhage; CT perfusion demonstrates a target mismatch with salvageable tissue. There is no large-vessel occlusion amenable to retrieval. It is now 0745. Which management is most appropriate?

   - A. No reperfusion therapy — onset time is unknown so thrombolysis is contraindicated
   - B. IV tenecteplase, with the treatment window calculated from the midpoint of sleep
   - C. Aspirin 300 mg and admission only, as she is a 'wake-up' stroke
   - D. Urgent transfer for thrombectomy despite no demonstrable occlusion
   - E. IV alteplase only if symptoms began within 4.5 hours of waking

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. IV tenecteplase, with the treatment window calculated from the midpoint of sleep**

Australian Living Guidelines support perfusion-selected thrombolysis in wake-up/unknown-onset stroke, with the window timed from the sleep midpoint (WAKE-UP/EXTEND evidence) when CTP/MRP shows mismatch. Tenecteplase 0.25 mg/kg is the preferred Australian lytic. A and C represent the outdated UK reflex that unknown onset bars lysis — this is exactly the high-yield Australian divergence. D is wrong: there is no occlusion to retrieve, so thrombectomy does not apply. E mistakenly anchors the window to wake-up time rather than sleep midpoint and prefers alteplase over the now-preferred tenecteplase.

_Teaching point:_ In Australia, unknown-onset/wake-up stroke is NOT an automatic exclusion: with perfusion mismatch you can thrombolyse, timing the window from the midpoint of sleep, up to 9 h.
</details>

**Q3.** A 23-year-old man is brought to a Brisbane ED having a generalised tonic-clonic seizure. Paramedics gave one dose of buccal midazolam 8 minutes ago without effect, then IV midazolam on arrival, and he is still convulsing 6 minutes later. ABC is maintained, BSL is 6.1, and he is on high-flow oxygen. What is the single most appropriate next step?

   - A. A third dose of IV midazolam
   - B. IV lorazepam
   - C. IV levetiracetam loading
   - D. Immediate intubation and propofol infusion
   - E. IV thiamine then 50% dextrose

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. IV levetiracetam loading**

He has had two adequate benzodiazepine doses without seizure control — this is established status, and the next rung is a second-line IV loading agent (levetiracetam, sodium valproate or phenytoin per eTG). A is the lethal trap: a third benzodiazepine adds respiratory depression without controlling the seizure — never give a third benzo before escalating. B is the UK answer and clinically pointless here — it is simply another benzodiazepine, and parenteral lorazepam is not the Australian first-line anyway. D (intubation/anaesthesia) is reserved for refractory status that has failed a loading agent. E is unnecessary — glucose is already normal, so empirical dextrose is not indicated.

_Teaching point:_ Status ladder: max TWO benzodiazepine doses, then a second-line IV loading agent (levetiracetam/valproate/phenytoin), then ICU/anaesthesia. A 'third benzo' is always wrong.
</details>

**Q4.** A 72-year-old man presents to a Perth ED febrile (38.9°C), drowsy (GCS 13), with neck stiffness and photophobia over 6 hours. He has a recent treated otitis media. There are no focal neurological signs and no papilloedema. Blood cultures are drawn. There will be a 40-minute wait for CT before LP. Which is the single most appropriate immediate management?

   - A. Defer all antibiotics until CT and LP are completed to avoid masking the culture
   - B. IV ceftriaxone + dexamethasone now
   - C. IV ceftriaxone + dexamethasone + vancomycin now, then proceed to CT/LP
   - D. IV benzylpenicillin alone now
   - E. IV aciclovir alone now pending PCR

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. IV ceftriaxone + dexamethasone + vancomycin now, then proceed to CT/LP**

Bacterial meningitis: antibiotics must NOT be delayed for imaging/LP. Per eTG, empirical therapy is ceftriaxone (or cefotaxime) + dexamethasone, and vancomycin is ADDED when resistant pneumococcus is a concern — here a recent otitis/beta-lactam exposure flags exactly that. B omits vancomycin, which is the Australian exam trap this stem is built on. A delays life-saving treatment. D (benzylpenicillin alone) is inadequate empirical cover. E treats only HSV and misses bacterial meningitis. Note Listeria cover (add benzylpenicillin/amoxicillin) would also be reasonable given age >50, but the question's discriminator is the vancomycin for resistant pneumococcus.

_Teaching point:_ Australian empirical meningitis = ceftriaxone + dexamethasone, ADD vancomycin if resistant pneumococcus suspected (recent beta-lactam, otitis/sinusitis, Gram-positive diplococci), ADD amoxy/benzylpenicillin for Listeria risk. Never delay antibiotics for the LP.
</details>

**Q5.** A 28-year-old previously well man presents to a Darwin hospital in February with 3 days of fever, headache, confusion and a focal seizure affecting the right arm. He is a rice farmer near the Murray-Darling. CT is unremarkable. CSF shows a lymphocytic pleocytosis with normal glucose. Which is the single most important immediate management step?

   - A. Await CSF viral PCR before starting any antiviral
   - B. IV aciclovir empirically now
   - C. IV ceftriaxone + dexamethasone for bacterial meningitis
   - D. IV ganciclovir for CMV encephalitis
   - E. Supportive care only as most viral encephalitides are self-limiting

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. IV aciclovir empirically now**

Fever + altered mental state + seizures + focal/temporal features = encephalitis, and empirical IV aciclovir must be started immediately to cover HSV, BEFORE PCR confirmation — delay markedly worsens outcome. A is the trap: waiting for PCR costs neurons. C addresses bacterial meningitis but the lymphocytic CSF with normal glucose and encephalopathic picture point to viral encephalitis (though concurrent antibacterial cover is often reasonable, aciclovir is the non-negotiable step here). D is wrong agent. E is dangerous. The arboviral context (Japanese encephalitis and Murray Valley encephalitis are now relevant in mainland Australia) is a differential, but you still cover HSV empirically because it is the treatable killer.

_Teaching point:_ Any suspected encephalitis (fever + encephalopathy/seizures + focal features) gets empirical IV aciclovir NOW, before PCR. In northern/inland Australia keep JEV and Murray Valley encephalitis on the differential.
</details>

**Q6.** A 31-year-old woman, 5 days after a diarrhoeal illness, presents to a Sydney ED with progressive ascending leg then arm weakness over 48 hours. Reflexes are absent. Sensation is largely intact. SpO2 is 98% on room air. Bedside FVC is 1.1 L (18 mL/kg) and was 1.6 L two hours earlier. Which is the single most appropriate immediate action?

   - A. Reassure and observe, as oxygen saturation is normal
   - B. Arrange ICU admission for close FVC monitoring and likely intubation, and start IVIg or plasma exchange
   - C. Start high-dose IV methylprednisolone
   - D. Give IV pyridostigmine
   - E. Perform urgent CT brain

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Arrange ICU admission for close FVC monitoring and likely intubation, and start IVIg or plasma exchange**

This is Guillain-Barre syndrome (post-Campylobacter ascending areflexic weakness). Respiratory failure is monitored by FVC, not SpO2 — her FVC is falling rapidly and approaching the 15 mL/kg intubation threshold, so she needs ICU-level monitoring/ventilation plus disease-modifying treatment with IVIg or plasma exchange. A is the deadly trap: normal sats falsely reassure because they collapse only at the very end. C is wrong — steroids do NOT work in GBS. D treats myasthenia, not GBS. E is irrelevant to this peripheral, areflexic syndrome.

_Teaching point:_ In GBS, a normal SpO2 is a liar — track the FVC. Intubate at <15 mL/kg or with a rapid downward trend, and treat with IVIg or plasma exchange (never steroids).
</details>

**Q7.** A 75-year-old woman presents to a rural NSW hospital with a 2-week history of new left temporal headache, scalp tenderness when combing her hair, and pain in her jaw when chewing. This morning she had transient blurring of vision in the left eye. ESR is 88 mm/h. Which is the single most appropriate immediate management?

   - A. Arrange temporal artery biopsy and start prednisolone only if it is positive
   - B. Start high-dose prednisolone (or IV methylprednisolone given visual symptoms) immediately, then arrange biopsy/ultrasound
   - C. Start low-dose aspirin and review in clinic in 2 weeks
   - D. Commence sumatriptan for presumed migraine
   - E. Arrange urgent CT brain before any treatment

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Start high-dose prednisolone (or IV methylprednisolone given visual symptoms) immediately, then arrange biopsy/ultrasound**

Classic giant cell arteritis (>50, temporal headache, jaw claudication, scalp tenderness, raised ESR, transient visual loss). Steroids must be started immediately to protect the contralateral/affected eye — visual symptoms warrant high-dose, often IV methylprednisolone. Biopsy (or temporal artery ultrasound for the halo sign, increasingly used in Australia) can follow within days; the histology stays positive despite steroids. A is the lethal sequencing trap — waiting for biopsy risks irreversible blindness. C, D, E all delay sight-saving treatment.

_Teaching point:_ GCA with any visual symptom: treat first (high-dose/IV steroids to save the other eye), investigate second. Biopsy/ultrasound can wait days; vision cannot.
</details>

**Q8.** A 64-year-old man presents to a Geelong ED with 24 hours of worsening lower back pain, bilateral leg heaviness, and difficulty passing urine; this morning he had an episode of incontinence. He has a history of prostate cancer. On examination there is reduced sensation over the perineum and reduced anal tone. Which is the single most appropriate immediate management?

   - A. Plain lumbar spine X-ray and analgesia, with outpatient MRI in 2 weeks
   - B. Urgent whole-spine MRI and dexamethasone, with neurosurgical/oncology referral
   - C. CT lumbar spine and discharge with urology follow-up
   - D. Bladder scan, insert catheter and reassess in the morning
   - E. Commence empirical antibiotics for presumed discitis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Urgent whole-spine MRI and dexamethasone, with neurosurgical/oncology referral**

Saddle anaesthesia, reduced anal tone, urinary symptoms and bilateral leg involvement with a cancer history = malignant cord/cauda equina compression — an emergency needing whole-spine MRI within hours and dexamethasone (8-16 mg; commonly 8 mg in Australian palliative pathways) plus urgent oncology/neurosurgery/radiation oncology. A, C and D all introduce the fatal delay that converts a salvageable cord into permanent paraplegia and incontinence — this is the classic 'missed' stem. E treats the wrong diagnosis.

_Teaching point:_ Back pain + saddle anaesthesia + bladder/bowel change + reduced anal tone = cord/cauda equina compression: MRI within hours and (for malignant cord compression) dexamethasone now. Time to decompression determines permanent deficit.
</details>

### Schema recall — "What is your schema for X?"

**What is your schema for the approach to ANY acute neurological presentation in an exam?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Three questions in strict order. (1) LOCALISE: cortex (higher-function loss, gaze towards lesion, face+arm>leg) vs brainstem (crossed signs, vertigo/diplopia/ataxia) vs cord (sensory LEVEL + UMN below + bladder) vs anterior horn/root/nerve (LMN: wasting, fasciculation, areflexia, no level) vs NMJ (fatigable, normal reflexes/sensation) vs muscle (symmetric proximal, no sensory loss). (2) TIME-STAMP: seconds-minutes = vascular/seizure; hours-days = inflammatory/infective; weeks-months = compressive/neoplastic; years = degenerative. (3) RUN THE CLOCK: match syndrome to its time-critical intervention (recanalise brain, stop seizure, decompress cord, ventilate failing neuromuscular patient, steroids before vision/cord lost).

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Students jump straight to a differential list without localising, so they cannot distinguish a cord lesion from a peripheral one, and they fail to start the time-critical intervention. Examiners attack the gap between recognising the syndrome and ACTING on its clock (e.g. knowing it is GBS but not monitoring FVC).
</details>

**What is your schema for the acute ischaemic stroke reperfusion decision in Australia?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Sudden focal deficit -> check glucose -> non-contrast CT IMMEDIATELY (job: exclude haemorrhage, not confirm infarct). If no blood: (a) THROMBOLYSIS — tenecteplase 0.25 mg/kg (preferred) within 4.5 h of known onset; OR perfusion-selected up to 9 h / from sleep midpoint in wake-up stroke if CTP/MRP mismatch; BP must be <185/110 first. (b) THROMBECTOMY — anterior LVO 0-6 h (no mandatory mismatch); 6-24 h if DAWN/DEFUSE-3 mismatch. Aspirin 300 mg unless lysed (then withhold 24 h). If haemorrhage: reverse anticoagulation, SBP ~140, neurosurgery. Use telestroke for regional/drip-and-ship transfer decisions.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The two-clock confusion: students apply the 4.5 h lytic ceiling to thrombectomy and wrongly declare LVO patients 'too late'. They also forget the extended 9 h/wake-up perfusion-selected lysis window (the key Australian divergence) and default to alteplase/UK reflexes instead of tenecteplase.
</details>

**What is your schema for managing status epilepticus?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Define: seizure >5 min or recurrent without recovery. STEP 0: ABC, high-flow O2, IV access, check glucose, give thiamine before glucose if malnourished/alcohol-dependent (Wernicke). STEP 1: benzodiazepine — IV midazolam or IV clonazepam (Australia); IM/buccal/intranasal midazolam if no IV. Repeat ONCE (max 2 doses total). STEP 2: IV loading agent — levetiracetam OR sodium valproate OR phenytoin. STEP 3: refractory -> ICU, intubate, anaesthetic infusion (propofol/midazolam). Throughout: identify and treat the cause.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Two failure points: (1) giving a THIRD benzodiazepine instead of escalating to a loading agent — causes respiratory arrest without seizure control; (2) naming lorazepam as Australian first-line (it is the UK answer; parenteral lorazepam is not routinely available here). Also forgetting thiamine-before-glucose in the at-risk patient.
</details>

**What is your schema for the red-flag headache?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Match the pattern to its emergency. (1) Thunderclap/worst-ever/maximal at onset -> SAH: non-contrast CT (~100% if <6 h); if negative, LP at >=12 h for xanthochromia. (2) Fever + neck stiffness/photophobia -> meningitis: ceftriaxone + dexamethasone IMMEDIATELY (do not wait for CT/LP); ADD vancomycin if resistant pneumococcus, ADD amoxy/benzylpenicillin for Listeria risk; notify public health. (3) Fever + altered mental state/seizures/focal features -> encephalitis: empirical IV aciclovir NOW before PCR. (4) >50 + temporal/jaw claudication + scalp tenderness + visual -> GCA: high-dose/IV steroids now, biopsy/ultrasound later. (5) Postural + papilloedema + morning vomiting -> raised ICP/mass: image, avoid LP until mass excluded (consider IIH in young obese women — acetazolamide, monitor visual fields).

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Students delay antibiotics in meningitis for the LP, forget vancomycin and aciclovir, and wait for biopsy in GCA before steroids. They also miss IIH as the 'raised ICP' mimic in young obese women and forget to time the SAH LP to >=12 h.
</details>

**What is your schema for distinguishing the neuromuscular respiratory failure emergencies (GBS vs myasthenic crisis)?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Shared engine: weakness threatening ventilation -> monitor FVC (NOT SpO2), intubate at <15 mL/kg or rapid fall, treat with IVIg OR plasma exchange (NOT steroids). GBS: ascending AREFLEXIC weakness days after Campylobacter/GI/resp infection; minimal sensory loss; watch AUTONOMIC instability (arrhythmia); CSF albuminocytologic dissociation. Myasthenic crisis: FATIGABLE weakness + bulbar/ptosis/diplopia, often triggered (infection, aminoglycosides, surgery); anti-AChR antibodies; CT chest for thymoma. Distinguish myasthenic from CHOLINERGIC crisis (excess pyridostigmine: SLUDGE + miosis).

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Trusting a normal SpO2 (it stays normal until catastrophic collapse) instead of trending FVC; giving steroids to GBS; and confusing myasthenic with cholinergic crisis (the discriminator is pupils/secretions: miosis + SLUDGE = cholinergic).
</details>

### Distractor drills — "What's the deadliest distractor for X?"

**When a patient with a large-vessel occlusion presents at 8-10 hours, what is the most seductive wrong answer?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ 'Outside the 4.5-hour window, so no reperfusion therapy — supportive care only.'
- _Why it tempts you:_ 4.5 h is drilled into every student as THE stroke number, so a stem at 8-10 h feels like a deliberate 'too late' cue, and choosing 'no treatment' feels disciplined.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ 4.5 h is the THROMBOLYSIS clock. Thrombectomy for anterior LVO runs to 24 h with DAWN/DEFUSE-3 mismatch — abandoning the patient discards salvageable penumbra and causes preventable disability.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Is there a proximal anterior occlusion WITH perfusion mismatch? If yes, the relevant clock is thrombectomy (24 h), not thrombolysis (4.5 h).**

**When a patient is still fitting after two benzodiazepine doses, what is the deadliest distractor?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ 'Give a third dose of the benzodiazepine.'
- _Why it tempts you:_ The first two doses were the right drug, the patient is still seizing, and 'more of what's working' feels intuitive — plus it avoids the scarier loading agents.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Each additional benzodiazepine compounds respiratory depression without adding seizure control, risking respiratory arrest. After two doses you have established status and must escalate to an IV loading agent.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Count the benzo doses: at two, the next correct step is ALWAYS a second-line loading agent (levetiracetam/valproate/phenytoin), never another benzodiazepine.**

**In suspected bacterial meningitis with a recent beta-lactam course or otitis, what is the most seductive Australian distractor?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ 'IV ceftriaxone + dexamethasone now' (and nothing more).
- _Why it tempts you:_ It is the headline empirical regimen everyone memorises, it correctly avoids delaying antibiotics for the LP, and it feels complete and confidently correct.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ It omits VANCOMYCIN, which eTG requires when resistant pneumococcus is a concern (recent beta-lactam, otitis/sinusitis, Gram-positive diplococci). Under-treating resistant pneumococcus risks treatment failure.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Scan the stem for resistant-pneumococcus flags (recent beta-lactam, otitis/sinusitis, Gram-positive diplococci) -> add vancomycin; and Listeria flags (>50, pregnant, immunocompromised) -> add amoxy/benzylpenicillin.**

**In a patient with ascending areflexic weakness and a normal SpO2, what is the deadliest distractor?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ 'Oxygen saturation is normal, so reassure and observe.'
- _Why it tempts you:_ SpO2 is the universal bedside surrogate for breathing, and a normal value pattern-matches to 'airway/breathing fine', making escalation feel like overkill.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ In neuromuscular respiratory failure SpO2 stays normal until sudden, catastrophic collapse. The valid early-warning measure is FVC — a falling FVC mandates ICU/intubation regardless of sats.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Ask for the FVC, not the SpO2: FVC <15 mL/kg or a rapid downward trend triggers intubation even with 98% sats.**

**In an elderly patient with temporal headache, jaw claudication and visual symptoms, what is the most seductive sequencing distractor?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ 'Arrange temporal artery biopsy and start steroids only if it is positive.'
- _Why it tempts you:_ It feels rigorous and evidence-based — confirm the diagnosis before committing to high-dose steroids in an elderly patient who could be harmed by them.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Waiting for histology risks irreversible blindness in the contralateral/affected eye within hours. Biopsy stays positive for days after steroids start, so treatment loses nothing by going first.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Any visual symptom in suspected GCA flips the order: treat first (high-dose/IV steroids), investigate second — biopsy/ultrasound can wait, the eye cannot.**

**In a tremor question, what is the most seductive distractor pulling you towards Parkinson's disease?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ 'It's a tremor in an older adult, therefore Parkinson's — start levodopa.'
- _Why it tempts you:_ Tremor + older patient is the prototypical Parkinson's image, and levodopa is the famous answer, so it pattern-matches instantly.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ An ACTION/postural tremor that improves with alcohol and is symmetrical is essential tremor (treat propranolol), not the asymmetric RESTING tremor with bradykinesia/rigidity of Parkinson's. Levodopa is the wrong drug.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Rest vs action: resting + asymmetric + bradykinesia = Parkinson's; action/postural + alcohol-responsive + family history = essential tremor.**

**In a young patient with fever, confusion and seizures, what is the seductive distractor that delays the life-saving step?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ 'Send CSF for HSV PCR and start aciclovir once the result is back.'
- _Why it tempts you:_ It feels like good stewardship — confirm the pathogen before committing to IV antiviral therapy — and PCR is the definitive test.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ HSV encephalitis outcomes depend on time-to-aciclovir; awaiting PCR (which can be falsely negative early) costs neurons and lives. Empirical aciclovir must start immediately.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Fever + encephalopathy/seizures + focal/temporal features = start empirical IV aciclovir NOW, before any PCR result.**

### High-end review pearls

- Two clocks, never one: thrombolysis ~4.5 h (or up to 9 h / sleep-midpoint if perfusion-selected); thrombectomy to 24 h with mismatch. 'Past 4.5 h' is never 'past treatment' if there is an anterior LVO.
- Aunt Minnie: wake-up stroke + target perfusion mismatch in Australia = thrombolyse from the sleep midpoint. This is the single highest-yield Australian divergence from UK/Passmedicine material.
- If you see 'still fitting after two benzos' -> the answer is a loading agent (levetiracetam/valproate/phenytoin), NEVER a third benzo. Two doses is the hard ceiling.
- Australian first-line status benzo is IV midazolam or IV clonazepam (or IM/buccal/intranasal midazolam) — lorazepam is the UK answer and parenteral lorazepam isn't routinely available here.
- Thiamine BEFORE glucose in any malnourished or alcohol-dependent patient — glucose first can precipitate Wernicke's. Build this into every altered-consciousness/status stem.
- If you see meningitis + recent beta-lactam/otitis/sinusitis or Gram-positive diplococci -> ADD vancomycin (resistant pneumococcus). Ceftriaxone + dex alone is the incomplete-answer trap.
- If you see meningitis + age >50 / pregnant / immunocompromised -> ADD amoxicillin or benzylpenicillin for Listeria. Cephalosporins do not cover Listeria.
- Never delay antibiotics for the LP in meningitis — and meningococcal disease is NOTIFIABLE: notify public health and give close-contact chemoprophylaxis (ciprofloxacin or rifampicin).
- If you see fever + altered mental state/seizures/temporal features -> empirical IV aciclovir NOW, before PCR. The treatable killer (HSV) doesn't wait for confirmation.
- Arboviral encephalitis is now an Australian reality: Japanese encephalitis (endemic in parts of mainland Australia since 2022) and Murray Valley encephalitis belong on the inland/northern differential — but you still cover HSV empirically.
- GBS lie-detector: a normal SpO2 is falsely reassuring — track the FVC. Intubate at <15 mL/kg or a rapid fall. Steroids do NOT work in GBS (IVIg or plasma exchange only).
- Watch autonomic instability in GBS (arrhythmias, labile BP) — it kills as readily as the respiratory failure, and phenytoin/QT issues compound it.
- Myasthenic vs cholinergic crisis: pupils and secretions break the tie — miosis + SLUDGE = cholinergic (too much pyridostigmine); normal/large pupils + clear trigger = myasthenic. Always CT chest for thymoma.
- GCA with any visual symptom: steroids first (high-dose oral, or IV methylprednisolone), biopsy/ultrasound later. The temporal artery halo sign on ultrasound is increasingly used in Australia, and tocilizumab is PBS-available for relapsing/steroid-refractory disease.
- The classic 'missed' emergency: back pain + saddle anaesthesia + painless retention/incontinence + reduced anal tone = cauda equina/cord compression -> MRI within HOURS. Plain X-ray and an outpatient MRI is the negligence trap.
- Malignant cord compression dexamethasone is 8-16 mg (commonly 8 mg in Australian palliative pathways per TROG SuperDex) — don't be wedded to a rigid '16 mg only'.
- Cancers that go to bone and crush cord: breast, prostate, lung, myeloma. A cancer history + new back pain + neurology = MSCC until proven otherwise.
- Hypoglycaemia mimics both stroke and seizure — 'check the glucose' is the free mark in every acute neuro stem.
- AF -> anticoagulate (DOAC, PBS first-line over warfarin for non-valvular AF), NOT antiplatelet. Time DOAC start after cardioembolic stroke by infarct size (the 1-3-6-12 day rule).
- Symptomatic carotid stenosis 70-99% -> endarterectomy within 2 WEEKS; high-risk TIA/minor stroke -> aspirin 300 mg + clopidogrel 300 mg loading, DAPT 21 days then monotherapy. ABCD2 is de-emphasised in Australia — treat all TIAs as high-risk.
- MS = dissemination in time AND space (optic neuritis, INO, Lhermitte's; oligoclonal bands). Treat acute relapse with IV methylprednisolone — it shortens the relapse but does NOT change long-term disability; long-term control is PBS-funded DMTs.
- Young obese woman + postural headache + papilloedema + visual obscurations but normal imaging = idiopathic intracranial hypertension -> acetazolamide, weight management, MONITOR VISUAL FIELDS. Don't mislabel as 'mass' or migraine.
- Drug-induced parkinsonism is symmetric and drug-linked (metoclopramide, antipsychotics) — review the medication chart before diagnosing idiopathic Parkinson's in the elderly.
- Austroads 'Assessing Fitness to Drive' is a distinctly Australian exam point: non-commercial vs commercial standards differ after a first seizure, TIA/stroke or syncope — counsel and document driving restrictions.
- Telestroke (Victorian Stroke Telemedicine, NSW Telestroke) underpins 'drip-and-ship' transfer decisions from regional hospitals — expect stems testing whether you lyse locally and transfer for thrombectomy.

> **Independent item audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[high]** MCQ 5 (Darwin rice farmer encephalitis) — stem geography: Internal geographic contradiction. The patient presents to a 'Darwin hospital' (Top End, NT) but is described as 'a rice farmer near the Murray-Darling.' The Murray-Darling Basin is ~3000 km away in south-eastern Australia (NSW/Vic/SA). A patient cannot present in Darwin yet farm near the Murray-Darling. This undermines the arboviral context the question is trying to build (Japanese encephalitis rice-paddy/piggery exposure was the 2022 Murray-Darling Basin outbreak; Murray Valley encephalitis is endemic to the Top End). The two clues point to different regions and different viruses. → Pick one geography. Either: (a) keep Darwin and reference Top End exposure (Murray Valley encephalitis / waterbird-Culex cycle, wet-season Feb timing is fine), dropping 'Murray-Darling'; or (b) relocate the stem to a Victorian/NSW Riverina hospital and keep the rice-farmer/Murray-Darling + Japanese encephalitis angle. February (wet season) fits both northern MVE and the JE season, so only the location/occupation clause needs harmonising.
> - **[medium]** MCQ 5 — answer defensibility vs concurrent bacterial cover: Answer B (empirical IV aciclovir now) is correct as the single non-negotiable step, but the explanation concedes 'concurrent antibacterial cover is often reasonable.' In a febrile, confused, seizing patient with CSF pleocytosis, standard Australian practice (eTG) is to start BOTH empirical aciclovir AND empirical ceftriaxone until bacterial meningitis is excluded — the two are not mutually exclusive. Option C (ceftriaxone + dexamethasone) is therefore not purely a strawman; a strict reader could argue both B and C are indicated. B is still the best single answer because aciclovir is the time-critical treatable-killer step, but the single-best-answer cleanliness is slightly soft. → Tighten the stem so HSV features dominate unambiguously (e.g., explicitly add temporal-lobe localisation such as the existing focal seizure plus 'MRI shows medial temporal signal change' or 'CSF lymphocytic with red cells'), and reword the explanation to state that empirical antibacterials are co-administered in practice but aciclovir is the step being tested. This removes any 'C is also right' ambiguity.
> - **[medium]** MCQ 6 / GBS schema — FVC intubation threshold: The explanation, teaching point and schema repeatedly cite '<15 mL/kg' as the intubation trigger. The classic, most widely taught and examined figure is the '20/30/40 rule' (intubate when FVC <20 mL/kg, OR maximal inspiratory pressure weaker than -30 cmH2O, OR maximal expiratory pressure <40 cmH2O). 15 mL/kg is a defensible lower/more-conservative cutoff, but stating the stem's FVC of 18 mL/kg is 'approaching the 15 mL/kg threshold' is misleading — 18 mL/kg is already BELOW the standard 20 mL/kg trigger, so by the conventional rule she already meets criteria, not merely approaching it. → Use the '20/30/40 rule' (FVC <20 mL/kg) as the primary cited threshold, noting the rapid downward trend (1.6→1.1 L) as the additional alarm. State she has ALREADY crossed the threshold. This is both more standard and more internally consistent with the numbers given.
> - **[low]** MCQ 1 — answer B vs bridging thrombolysis nuance: Answer B (thrombectomy) is the correct single best answer. However, the explanation asserts he is 'not described as perfusion-selected for lysis,' which is inaccurate: the stem explicitly gives a small core / large penumbra mismatch at 7 h, which IS the perfusion-selection criterion. Under the Australian extended-window (up to 9 h) pathway he could qualify for bridging tenecteplase IN ADDITION to thrombectomy. The teaching risk is implying lysis is off the table at 7 h when, with mismatch, it may be given as a bridge. B is still correct because thrombectomy is the definitive treatment for an M1 occlusion and is the 'single most appropriate next step,' but the rationale for excluding C overstates the case. → Reword the explanation for C: rather than 'not perfusion-selected,' say lysis ALONE is insufficient for a proximal M1 (poor recanalisation of large clots), and that even if bridging tenecteplase is given, the patient still requires thrombectomy — so thrombectomy is the answer. This preserves single-best-answer integrity without the factual misstatement.
> - **[medium]** MCQ 4 — Listeria cover omitted from the 'correct' answer: Patient is 72 (age >50), a Listeria risk group. eTG empirical meningitis cover for >50s includes benzylpenicillin/amoxicillin for Listeria IN ADDITION to ceftriaxone + dexamethasone (+/- vancomycin). The 'correct' answer C (ceftriaxone + dexamethasone + vancomycin) omits Listeria cover, and the explanation acknowledges this ('would also be reasonable') but waves it away as not the discriminator. Strictly, the most complete empirical regimen for this 72-year-old is C PLUS benzylpenicillin/amoxicillin. The question is salvageable because no option offers the full quad regimen, so C is the best AVAILABLE answer, but the stem pits two legitimate additions (vanc vs Listeria cover) and only rewards one. → Either lower the patient's age to <50 to remove the Listeria obligation and make vancomycin the clean sole discriminator, OR keep age 72 and add the Listeria agent to option C so the correct answer is genuinely the complete regimen. As written it is defensible but not airtight.
> - **[low]** MCQ 3 — clonazepam/midazolam dosing realism and 'two doses' counting: Clinically sound and answer C is correct (escalate to levetiracetam after two adequate benzo doses, never a third benzo). Minor realism issue: the timeline gives buccal midazolam at -8 min then IV midazolam on arrival, 'still convulsing 6 minutes later.' eTG/Australian practice allows repeating midazolam at ~5-minute intervals; the stem has effectively delivered two doses (pre-hospital buccal + IV), which correctly triggers second-line. This is fine. The only soft spot is that a purist could argue the buccal dose 8 min before the IV dose, with the IV dose only 6 min old, is borderline for declaring 'established status,' but the cumulative two-dose failure justifies escalation. No change to the answer needed. → Optional: state the buccal and IV doses were each adequate weight-based doses and that ≥5 min has elapsed since the second, to pre-empt any 'not yet two adequate doses' quibble. Answer key is correct as is.
> - **[low]** highEndExtras — DOAC timing '1-3-6-12 day rule': The extra states 'Time DOAC start after cardioembolic stroke by infarct size (the 1-3-6-12 day rule).' This older heuristic is being superseded; recent trials (ELAN, TIMING, OPTIMAS, START 2024-2025) support EARLIER DOAC initiation and the rigid 1-3-6-12 rule is no longer the dominant recommendation. Presented as current Australian fact, this risks teaching an outdated rule. → Reword to: 'start DOAC early after cardioembolic stroke; timing individualised by infarct size and haemorrhagic-transformation risk — recent evidence (ELAN/OPTIMAS) supports earlier initiation than the old 1-3-6-12 rule.'
> - **[low]** highEndExtras — symptomatic carotid endarterectomy timing: Extra says 'Symptomatic carotid stenosis 70-99% -> endarterectomy within 2 WEEKS.' Correct and high-yield, but it omits that symptomatic 50-69% stenosis also benefits from CEA (especially men), and benefit is time-critical within 2 weeks for that group too. As written it implies only 70-99% qualifies, which is an oversimplification. → Add: 'symptomatic 50-69% stenosis also benefits (selected patients, esp. men), still within 2 weeks; <50% no benefit.'
> 
> _Quality notes:_ Distractor quality is genuinely high across the set — these are deadly traps, not strawmen. The standout seductive distractors: 'past 4.5 h so no reperfusion' (Q1), 'third benzodiazepine' (Q3), 'ceftriaxone + dex without vancomycin' (Q4), 'normal SpO2 so reassure' (Q6), and 'biopsy before steroids in GCA' (Q7). Each is the exact error a strong-but-imperfect candidate makes. The distractorDrills section correctly identifies the discriminator for each.; Q2 (wake-up stroke) is the strongest single-best-answer item: option B (tenecteplase from sleep midpoint) is cleanly correct and well-differentiated, D (thrombectomy with no occlusion) and E (alteplase, wake-up-time anchoring) are precise traps. The 'no LVO amenable to retrieval' clause is doing important work to exclude D — well constructed.; Q7 (GCA) and Q8 (cord/cauda equina compression) are clean, unambiguous single-best-answer items with correct keys and time-critical logic. No issues beyond the noted general points.; The recurring weakness is 'incomplete-regimen' ambiguity in the infectious/emergency stems (Q4 Listeria, Q5 concurrent antibacterials): the questions test ONE correct addition while a second legitimate addition is also indicated but unrewarded. Defensible because no option offers the full regimen, but a top candidate may hesitate. Tightening the stems (age, localisation) would make them airtight.; Schemas are clinically sound and pedagogically strong. The 'two clocks' stroke framing, the status-epilepticus ladder, the red-flag-headache pattern-matcher, and the GBS-vs-myasthenic-crisis discriminator are all accurate and high-yield. The 'whereItBreaks' notes correctly target the realistic failure points.; No items are too easy; difficulty is well calibrated for a senior medical student / early-PGY Australian exam. The Australian-divergence emphasis (tenecteplase over alteplase, midazolam/clonazepam over lorazepam, 9 h/sleep-midpoint window, vancomycin/Listeria add-ons, Austroads driving, telestroke) is accurate and is the genuine high-yield delta from UK/Passmedicine material.; Two factual currency issues in highEndExtras (1-3-6-12 DOAC rule now dated; carotid 50-69% benefit omitted) — not wrong enough to mislead a careful reader, but worth updating to 2026 standards.; Minor terminology: explanations occasionally state hard numeric thresholds as if absolute ('15 mL/kg', '16 mg dexamethasone' which is actually correctly hedged) — the GBS FVC figure is the one that should be reconciled with the conventional 20/30/40 rule and with the stem's own numbers.

---

## Toxicology, Envenomation & Emergencies  _(~6% of marks)_

### The one idea that unlocks the domain

> **Every tox/envenomation emergency is a race between a TIME-DEPENDENT mechanism of harm and a TIME-DEPENDENT antidote/decontamination window — so the single engine is: "Identify the toxidrome/venom syndrome, map its mechanism to its specific reversible step, and act within that step's window." Resuscitation (ABC, airway, vasopressors, intubation) ALWAYS precedes the antidote, and the antidote is chosen by mechanism, not by name-recognition.**

Once you hold "mechanism → reversible step → window," the whole domain becomes derivable rather than memorised. Anaphylaxis = mast-cell mediator release causing vasodilation + bronchospasm → the reversible step is alpha/beta-adrenergic tone → IM adrenaline NOW, and everything else (fluids, steroids, antihistamine) is adjunct. Paracetamol = NAPQI depletes glutathione → reversible step is replenishing glutathione → NAC, and the nomogram exists only to tell you whether you're still in the window. Snake venom = consumptive coagulopathy + neurotoxicity + myotoxicity → the reversible step is binding free venom → antivenom only helps while venom is unbound and the lab markers are deranged; pressure-immobilisation buys time by trapping venom in lymphatics. Opioids = mu-receptor CNS/respiratory depression → naloxone; TCA = Na-channel blockade widening QRS → bicarbonate; beta-blocker/CCB = lost inotropy/chronotropy → high-dose insulin euglycaemia + calcium/glucagon. The engine also tells you what NOT to do: tourniquets and incision in snakebite (worsens local + sudden venom load), gastric lavage in most overdoses, and adrenaline by IV bolus in stable anaphylaxis (causes arrhythmia). The examiner's single-best-answer almost always rewards the candidate who picks the mechanism-matched first action within its window over a plausible-but-late or plausible-but-adjunct option.

### High-yield tutorial

# Toxicology, Envenomation & Emergencies — the Australian engine

**The master rule: resuscitate first (ABC), then give the mechanism-matched antidote within its window.** Almost every wrong MCQ answer is either a late antidote, an adjunct dressed up as primary therapy, or a contraindicated "classic" manoeuvre.

## Anaphylaxis — the most-tested emergency
**IM adrenaline is the first and only life-saving drug; everything else is adjunct.** Recognition = acute onset + (airway/breathing/circulation compromise) ± skin. Skin signs may be ABSENT (do not require urticaria).
- **Adult dose: 0.5 mg IM (0.5 mL of 1:1000) into the anterolateral thigh, repeat every 5 min.** Child: 0.01 mg/kg.
- IV adrenaline infusion only for refractory cases in monitored settings — **never push IV bolus 1:1000.**
- Position SUPINE (legs raised); sitting a hypotensive patient up can cause "empty ventricle" arrest.
- Adjuncts: high-flow O₂, IV fluids 20 mL/kg, salbutamol for bronchospasm. **Antihistamines/steroids do NOT treat anaphylaxis** and do not prevent biphasic reactions — they only ease cutaneous symptoms.
- Refractory + on a beta-blocker → **glucagon**.

## Snake envenomation — guaranteed Australian item
**Mechanism trio: venom-induced consumptive coagulopathy (VICC), neurotoxicity, myotoxicity (± renal).** First aid = **pressure-immobilisation bandage (PIB)** over the bite and the whole limb + splint; keep the patient still. **No tourniquet, no incision, no suction, no washing the site** (venom on skin helps the detection kit).
- The **snake venom detection kit (SVDK)** is done on the BITE-SITE SWAB or urine, NOT to decide whether to treat — clinical + lab envenomation decides treatment. SVDK guides antivenom CHOICE; geography and lab pattern often guide better.
- **Brown snake** (commonest serious bite, esp. urban/eastern AU): isolated VICC — INR up, fibrinogen low/absent, D-dimer high — usually **without** systemic neurotoxicity. **Tiger snake**: VICC + neurotoxicity + myotoxicity.
- Bloods: **INR/PT, aPTT, fibrinogen, D-dimer, CK, U&E, FBC** on arrival and serially. A normal coagulation panel + normal CK at 1, 6 and 12 hours with no symptoms = no envenomation.
- **Antivenom only for systemic envenomation** (coagulopathy, neurotoxicity, myotoxicity, collapse, AKI). It binds FREE venom — **it neither reverses established paralysis quickly nor instantly corrects clotting factors** (factors take hours to regenerate; do NOT chase the INR with repeat antivenom alone). Pre-treatment with prophylactic adrenaline/antihistamine is no longer routine; have adrenaline ready for reactions.

## Spiders & marine
- **Redback (latrodectism):** severe local/regional PAIN, sweating, hypertension — NOT life-threatening, NO coagulopathy. **First-line = analgesia + ice; PIB is NOT used** (slow-onset, want to localise pain relief). Antivenom is now controversial/second-line for refractory pain.
- **Funnel-web (Sydney):** rapid cholinergic crisis — salivation, lacrimation, sweating, fasciculation, hypertension, pulmonary oedema. **Apply PIB (like snakebite), give antivenom early.**
- **Box jellyfish (Chironex):** dermatonecrosis + cardiotoxicity/arrest. First aid = **douse liberally with VINEGAR** (inactivates undischarged nematocysts), CPR, antivenom.
- **Irukandji syndrome:** delayed (20–40 min) catecholamine storm — severe pain, hypertension, tachycardia, sense of impending doom, ± stress cardiomyopathy/APO. **Vinegar then; treatment is analgesia (opioids) + magnesium + BP control.**

## Core overdoses — match the antidote to the channel/receptor
- **Paracetamol:** NAPQI depletes glutathione → hepatocyte necrosis. **Plot a single 4-hour-or-later level on the nomogram; give NAC if above the line, or empirically if >8 h, staggered, or unknown time.** NAC works best <8 h but give it anyway later. Massive ingestion → higher-dose NAC regimens.
- **Opioid:** miosis + respiratory depression → **naloxone** (titrate to ventilation, beware withdrawal/short half-life vs long-acting opioids).
- **TCA:** anticholinergic + **Na-channel block → widening QRS, seizures, hypotension**. **QRS >100 ms or VT → IV sodium bicarbonate.** Avoid class I/III antiarrhythmics.
- **Beta-blocker / calcium-channel blocker:** bradycardia + hypotension. **High-dose insulin–euglycaemia (HIET)** is key inotrope; **calcium, glucagon (BB), atropine, vasopressors**. (CCB classically preserves consciousness; verapamil/diltiazem worst.)
- **Salicylate:** mixed **respiratory alkalosis + high-anion-gap metabolic acidosis**, tinnitus, hyperthermia. Treat with **urinary alkalinisation; dialysis if severe.**

## Hyperthermic neuro-syndromes
**Serotonin syndrome = fast onset (hours), clonus/hyperreflexia, agitation** (SSRI/MAOI/tramadol/triptan) → stop drug, benzos, **cyproheptadine**. **NMS = slow onset (days), lead-pipe RIGIDITY, hyporeflexia, very high CK** (dopamine antagonist/antipsychotic, or Parkinson's drug withdrawal) → stop drug, cool, **dantrolene/bromocriptine**. The discriminator the examiner wants: **clonus + rapid onset → serotonin; rigidity + bradyreflexia → NMS.**

### Highest mark-per-minute principles

- **IM adrenaline 0.5 mg (1:1000) into anterolateral thigh is the ONLY first-line drug in anaphylaxis; lay the patient flat.** — Most common exam emergency; distractors are antihistamine/steroid/IV adrenaline bolus.
  - _Exam angle:_ Stem with hypotension + wheeze ± no rash; correct answer = IM adrenaline, not 'sit up' or 'IV hydrocortisone'.
- **Snakebite first aid = pressure-immobilisation bandage + splint + stillness; NO tourniquet, incision, suction or washing the bite.** — Guaranteed Australian item; tests both the correct manoeuvre and the contraindicated 'classics'.
  - _Exam angle:_ Best next step in field management; trap answers are tourniquet/cut-and-suck.
- **Antivenom binds free venom only — give for SYSTEMIC envenomation; do not repeat-dose to chase a slow-correcting INR.** — Separates students who understand mechanism from rote-learners.
  - _Exam angle:_ INR still high 2 h post-antivenom → answer is 'wait/supportive', not 'more antivenom'.
- **Brown snake = isolated VICC (raised INR, low fibrinogen) usually without neurotoxicity; tiger snake adds neuro + myotoxicity.** — Lets you pick the snake/antivenom from the lab pattern.
  - _Exam angle:_ Urban Eastern-AU bite + coagulopathy alone → brown snake antivenom.
- **Redback = analgesia (no PIB); funnel-web = PIB + early antivenom (cholinergic crisis).** — The two spiders are managed oppositely — a clean single-best-answer split.
  - _Exam angle:_ Severe local pain, sweating, no coagulopathy → redback → analgesia, NOT a bandage.
- **Paracetamol: plot 4-hour level on nomogram; NAC if above line, or empirically if >8 h/staggered/unknown timing.** — Most common deliberate overdose; nomogram timing is heavily tested.
  - _Exam angle:_ Level taken at 2 h → answer is 'repeat at 4 h', a level before 4 h is uninterpretable.
- **Match antidote to channel: TCA → bicarbonate (wide QRS); BB/CCB → high-dose insulin + calcium/glucagon; opioid → naloxone; salicylate → alkalinise.** — One unifying decision rule covers four overdose stems.
  - _Exam angle:_ QRS 130 ms post-amitriptyline → IV sodium bicarbonate.
- **Serotonin syndrome = rapid onset + clonus/hyperreflexia; NMS = days + lead-pipe rigidity + hyporeflexia + very high CK.** — Classic discriminator pair examiners love.
  - _Exam angle:_ Tramadol added to SSRI, hours later clonus → serotonin syndrome → cyproheptadine.
- **Box jellyfish/Irukandji first aid = vinegar (inactivates nematocysts); Irukandji is a delayed catecholamine storm treated with opioids + magnesium.** — Distinctly Australian marine items.
  - _Exam angle:_ Tropical QLD swimmer, delayed severe pain + hypertension + doom → Irukandji.

### Must-know numbers (Australian where they differ)

- Adrenaline anaphylaxis: 0.5 mg IM (0.5 mL of 1:1000) adult, repeat q5min; child 0.01 mg/kg (max 0.5 mg)
- Anaphylaxis fluids: 20 mL/kg crystalloid bolus
- TCA: treat QRS >100 ms (and certainly VT) with IV sodium bicarbonate
- Paracetamol: interpret level only at ≥4 h post-ingestion; treatment line ~150 mg/L (1000 µmol/L) at 4 h on the AU nomogram; NAC empirically if >8 h or staggered/unknown
- Paracetamol toxic threshold ~200 mg/kg or 10 g (acute, single)
- Snake bloods serial at 0, 1, 6, 12 h: INR, aPTT, fibrinogen, D-dimer, CK
- Irukandji onset delayed ~20–40 min after sting
- Naloxone: titrate small aliquots (e.g. 100–400 mcg) to respiratory rate, beware short half-life vs methadone/sustained-release
- NMS: CK often >1000 (frequently markedly higher); serotonin syndrome more associated with clonus than extreme CK
- Salicylate: classic mixed respiratory alkalosis + high-anion-gap metabolic acidosis; consider dialysis at high levels/severe acidosis

### Cross-topic cuts

- Haematology: VICC mimics DIC (raised INR/D-dimer, low fibrinogen) — distinguish snakebite from sepsis-DIC; do NOT give FFP/factors as first move, antivenom + time is the answer
- Cardiology: TCA Na-channel block and salicylate/local anaesthetic toxicity all widen QRS — bicarbonate is the shared lever; BB/CCB overdose presents as the bradycardia-hypotension differential alongside inferior MI/hyperkalaemia
- Endocrine/metabolic: high-dose insulin-euglycaemia therapy for BB/CCB links to glucose monitoring; salicylate produces a high-anion-gap acidosis sitting in the MUDPILES differential
- Neurology: serotonin syndrome and NMS overlap with malignant hyperthermia, meningitis and status — discriminator is onset speed, reflexes (clonus vs rigidity) and drug history
- Respiratory: anaphylactic bronchospasm vs acute asthma — adrenaline (not just salbutamol) when there is circulatory collapse or angioedema
- Renal: snake myotoxicity → rhabdomyolysis/AKI; paracetamol and salicylate both threaten organ injury requiring level-guided escalation including dialysis
- Psychiatry: deliberate self-poisoning (paracetamol, TCA) ties to risk assessment and capacity

### Australian-specific (where the UK answer loses you marks)

- Snake envenomation is a core AU item absent from UK Passmedicine — know pressure-immobilisation bandage, SVDK, brown vs tiger snake patterns and CSL antivenoms
- First aid diverges sharply from old/overseas teaching: NO tourniquet, NO incision/suction, NO washing the bite (preserves venom for the detection kit) — UK sources may still mention suction devices
- SVDK is performed on bite-site swab/urine and guides antivenom CHOICE, not the decision to treat (clinical + lab envenomation decides)
- Australian paracetamol nomogram uses a single treatment line (~150 mg/L at 4 h) — no separate 'high-risk' line as in older UK practice; staggered/large ingestions get empirical and modified high-dose NAC per AU guidance
- Routine prophylactic adrenaline/premedication before snake antivenom is no longer recommended in AU — just have resuscitation ready
- Spider management is uniquely AU: redback (latrodectism) treated with analgesia and antivenom now reserved for refractory cases; funnel-web treated like snakebite with PIB + antivenom
- Marine envenomation is AU-flavoured: vinegar for box jellyfish/Irukandji, Irukandji syndrome (delayed catecholamine storm, magnesium + opioids) is essentially an Australian tropical exam item
- Resource to anchor to: Australian Resuscitation Council anaphylaxis guidance and the local Toxicology/Clinical Toxinology (e.g. WA/NSW Poisons Information 13 11 26) protocols

> **Independent accuracy audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[medium]** No washing the site (venom on skin helps the detection kit) ... SVDK guides antivenom CHOICE → Keep 'do not wash the bite' (still correct first aid) but reframe the reason as 'avoid disturbing the bite/PIB and avoid spreading venom', not 'to feed the SVDK'. State explicitly that SVDK is unreliable (high false-positive/false-negative rates), is never used to decide WHETHER to treat, and that antivenom choice in most jurisdictions is now driven by geography + clinical/lab syndrome. Source: NSW Health GL2024_007 Snake and Spider Bites Clinical Management (2024); CSL Antivenom Handbook; published SVDK accuracy data. _(NSW Health GL2024_007 (2024); CSL/Seqirus Antivenom Handbook; SVDK accuracy literature)_
> - **[medium]** Irukandji syndrome ... treatment is analgesia (opioids) + magnesium + BP control. → Reframe as: mainstay is IV opioid analgesia (e.g. fentanyl) titrated to pain; treat hypertension/APO actively (GTN is first-line for hypertensive APO); magnesium is adjunctive and of UNPROVEN benefit (negative RCT) — not a guaranteed correct answer. Source: McCullagh et al. randomised trial of magnesium in Irukandji syndrome; WA Country Health Irukandji Syndrome Management Guideline. _(WA Country Health Irukandji Syndrome Management Guideline; McCullagh RCT (magnesium negative))_
> - **[low]** Child: 0.01 mg/kg. (adrenaline in anaphylaxis) → State child dose as 0.01 mg/kg IM (1:1000) to a maximum of 0.5 mg per dose, repeat every 5 min. Adult 0.5 mg IM is correct per ASCIA 2024. Source: ASCIA Acute Management of Anaphylaxis Guidelines 2024. _(ASCIA Acute Management of Anaphylaxis Guidelines 2024)_
> - **[low]** QRS >100 ms or VT → IV sodium bicarbonate (TCA). → State: give IV sodium bicarbonate for QRS prolongation (commonly cited threshold >100 ms; many toxicologists act at >120 ms), and for hypotension/ventricular arrhythmia or seizures regardless. Target pH 7.5-7.55. Source: RCH TCA Poisoning CPG; Australian toxicology references. _(RCH Tricyclic Antidepressant Poisoning CPG)_
> - **[low]** Box jellyfish (Chironex): First aid = douse liberally with VINEGAR. → Add the boundary: vinegar (4-6% acetic acid) for TROPICAL stings (box jellyfish/Irukandji) to inactivate undischarged nematocysts; for non-tropical bluebottle stings use hot water immersion / remove tentacles, NOT vinegar. Source: ANZCOR Guideline 9.4.5 First Aid Management of Marine Envenomation (2024). _(ANZCOR Guideline 9.4.5 (2024))_
> 
> _Gaps flagged & folded in:_ Paracetamol two-bag NAC regimen (current AU/NZ standard): 200 mg/kg over 4 h then 100 mg/kg over 16 h (replaces the old three-bag regimen) — fewer anaphylactoid reactions; this is the heavily-tested current AU protocol and is missing from the tutorial.; Modified-release (e.g. Panadol Osteo) paracetamol: distinct AU pathway — take levels at 4 h AND ~8 h (a single 4 h level cannot exclude toxicity), treat if EITHER is above the line; full NAC course for MR ingestions >=10 g or >=200 mg/kg whichever is less. Up-scheduling of MR paracetamol to S3 is an Australian policy point.; Massive paracetamol ingestion (concentration >double the nomogram line, or >=30 g / >=500 mg/kg): increased-dose NAC regimen per AU/NZ guidelines — tutorial mentions 'higher-dose NAC' vaguely but should name the >2x-line / 500 mg/kg trigger.; Poisons Information Centre 13 11 26 (single national number) should be invoked as the routine first call for any significant overdose/envenomation — and Clinical Toxinology consult for envenomation. This is a real-world AU exam-safe answer.; Pressure bandage with immobilisation is now the preferred ANZCOR term (PBI), specifying a broad 10-15 cm elasticised bandage (preferred over crepe) applied over clothing, distal-to-proximal up the whole limb, plus splint — the bandage/splint must NOT be removed until in a hospital with antivenom available.; Anaphylaxis: position depends on symptom — lay flat / supine with legs raised if hypotensive, but sit up if predominantly respiratory distress, and left lateral if pregnant or vomiting. Tutorial says only 'supine' which is incomplete per ASCIA 2024.; Anaphylaxis: a single IM adrenaline device delivers 300 mcg (adult), and ASCIA now emphasises early adrenaline + that >=2-3 doses at 5 min intervals should prompt an adrenaline INFUSION in a monitored setting; observe at least 4 h post-resolution (biphasic risk).; Snakebite: collapse/cardiac arrest, thrombotic microangiopathy (TMA) and early VICC are recognised severe AU presentations; pre-hospital cardiac arrest from brown snake is a known killer — emphasise that the highest-acuity action is resuscitation, not antivenom.; Acute Anaphylaxis Clinical Care Standard (ACSQHC) exists as a national Australian standard — worth naming as the governance anchor alongside ASCIA.; Whole-bowel irrigation and activated charcoal: AU tox practice gives activated charcoal (50 g adult) within ~1-2 h for suitable agents in an alert/protected airway; the tutorial correctly damns gastric lavage but omits the role/limits of charcoal and WBI (e.g. for sustained-release/iron/lithium).; Digoxin toxicity (DigiFab/Fab fragments) and iron overdose (desferrioxamine) are common AU exam antidote pairs that fit the tutorial's 'mechanism-matched antidote' engine but are omitted.; Local anaesthetic systemic toxicity (LAST) → intravenous lipid emulsion (Intralipid 20%) is a classic AU emergency-medicine antidote pairing missing from the channel/receptor table.

### Applied MCQs

**Q1.** A 34-year-old woman in a Perth ED develops generalised flushing, audible wheeze and a BP of 78/44 mmHg about 10 minutes after IV flucloxacillin. There is no urticaria. She is on no regular medications. What is the single most important immediate action?

   - A. IV hydrocortisone 200 mg
   - B. IM adrenaline 0.5 mg (0.5 mL of 1:1000) into the anterolateral thigh
   - C. IV adrenaline 1 mg (1:10,000) bolus
   - D. IV chlorphenamine 10 mg
   - E. Sit her upright and give nebulised salbutamol

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. IM adrenaline 0.5 mg (0.5 mL of 1:1000) into the anterolateral thigh**

This is anaphylaxis (airway/breathing + circulation compromise) and the absence of a rash does not exclude it. IM adrenaline is the only first-line life-saving drug, given into the anterolateral thigh and repeated every 5 minutes. Hydrocortisone (A) and antihistamine (D) are adjuncts that ease cutaneous symptoms only and do not treat airway/circulatory compromise or prevent biphasic reactions. IV adrenaline bolus (C) is reserved for refractory cases in a monitored setting and risks arrhythmia/myocardial ischaemia when pushed. Sitting a hypotensive patient up (E) risks empty-ventricle arrest; salbutamol alone does not address vasodilatory shock.

_Teaching point:_ In anaphylaxis, IM adrenaline to the thigh is the ONLY first-line drug; rash may be absent and steroids/antihistamines are adjuncts, never the answer.
</details>

**Q2.** A 9-year-old boy (estimated weight 30 kg) develops stridor and hypotension after eating cashews at a Brisbane birthday party. You decide to give IM adrenaline. What is the correct dose?

   - A. 0.01 mg/kg (0.3 mg) IM of 1:1000, max 0.5 mg, repeat every 5 min
   - B. 0.1 mg/kg IM of 1:1000
   - C. 0.01 mg/kg IV of 1:10,000 bolus
   - D. 0.5 mg IM of 1:1000 (adult dose) regardless of weight
   - E. 0.15 mg IM of 1:1000 fixed paediatric dose

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. 0.01 mg/kg (0.3 mg) IM of 1:1000, max 0.5 mg, repeat every 5 min**

Per ASCIA 2024 the paediatric dose is 0.01 mg/kg of 1:1000 IM to a maximum of 0.5 mg per dose, repeated every 5 minutes; 30 kg x 0.01 = 0.3 mg. Option B is a 10-fold overdose. Option C uses the wrong route and concentration (IV bolus risks arrhythmia). Option D ignores weight (though here it would breach nothing, the principle is weight-based with a cap). Option E is an arbitrary fixed dose that under-doses this child.

_Teaching point:_ Paediatric anaphylaxis adrenaline = 0.01 mg/kg IM of 1:1000, capped at 0.5 mg per dose; always give IM, never IV bolus in the ward setting.
</details>

**Q3.** A 28-year-old man is bitten on the calf by a snake while gardening in suburban Brisbane. He is brought to ED 90 minutes later. INR is 6.0, fibrinogen undetectable, D-dimer markedly raised, aPTT prolonged; CK normal and there are no neurological signs. He has been given the appropriate antivenom. Two hours later his INR remains 5.5 and he feels well. What is the most appropriate next step?

   - A. Give a second dose of antivenom to correct the INR
   - B. Transfuse fresh frozen plasma to normalise the INR
   - C. Supportive care and serial monitoring; expect coagulation to recover over hours
   - D. Start an intravenous heparin infusion
   - E. Give vitamin K 10 mg IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Supportive care and serial monitoring; expect coagulation to recover over hours**

This is venom-induced consumptive coagulopathy (VICC), classically from a brown snake (urban eastern Australia, isolated coagulopathy without neuro/myotoxicity). Antivenom binds FREE venom; once given, the bound venom problem is addressed but clotting factors must regenerate over hours, so a persistently raised INR shortly after antivenom is expected and does NOT mandate more antivenom (A) — repeat dosing chases a lab number and adds reaction risk. FFP (B) is generally avoided as a first move and can fuel ongoing consumption while free venom remains; it is reserved for active bleeding. Heparin (D) is contraindicated. Vitamin K (E) is irrelevant — this is consumption, not a vitamin-K-dependent factor deficiency.

_Teaching point:_ Antivenom binds free venom only; do NOT chase a slowly-correcting INR with repeat antivenom — factor regeneration takes hours.
</details>

**Q4.** A farmer is brought to a regional Victorian ED after a snakebite to the hand 30 minutes ago. A bystander has applied a tourniquet above the elbow and made a small incision over the fang marks. The limb is otherwise normal and the patient is well. What is the most appropriate management of the first aid measures?

   - A. Leave the tourniquet, add suction to the incision
   - B. Remove the tourniquet immediately and apply a pressure-immobilisation bandage over the whole limb with a splint
   - C. Wash the bite thoroughly and apply the snake venom detection kit to the cleaned skin
   - D. Tighten the tourniquet and elevate the limb
   - E. Apply ice and elevate the limb above the heart

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Remove the tourniquet immediately and apply a pressure-immobilisation bandage over the whole limb with a splint**

Correct Australian first aid is pressure-immobilisation (now termed pressure bandage with immobilisation): a broad elasticised bandage applied over the whole limb plus a splint, keeping the patient still. Tourniquets and incision/suction are contraindicated — sudden tourniquet release can deliver a venom bolus, and incision/suction worsen local injury without benefit (A, D). You should not wash the bite (C) — but the modern reason is to avoid disturbing the site and spreading venom, not to feed the SVDK, which is unreliable. Ice and elevation (E) are not the recommended technique. The tourniquet must come off, but in a controlled hospital setting with resuscitation ready.

_Teaching point:_ Snakebite first aid = pressure bandage + immobilisation + splint + stillness; tourniquet, incision, suction and washing are all wrong.
</details>

**Q5.** A 19-year-old is found drowsy after a snakebite in the field; ambulance reports a PIB and splint are in place. On arrival she is GCS 8 with a systolic BP of 70 and a wide-complex bradycardia, then loses output. The team asks whether antivenom should be drawn up first. What is the single highest-priority action?

   - A. Give CSL polyvalent antivenom as the first intervention
   - B. Remove the pressure bandage to slow venom absorption
   - C. Commence resuscitation per ALS (airway, CPR, adrenaline) — antivenom is secondary
   - D. Send an urgent SVDK before any treatment
   - E. Give fresh frozen plasma for presumed coagulopathy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Commence resuscitation per ALS (airway, CPR, adrenaline) — antivenom is secondary**

Pre-hospital collapse and cardiac arrest (a recognised brown-snake killer) demand resuscitation first — ABC and ALS always precede the antidote. Antivenom (A) does not reverse arrest and is given once the patient is being actively resuscitated. Removing the PIB (B) would release trapped venom and worsen the situation. SVDK (D) never decides whether to treat and is unreliable; it certainly does not precede resuscitation. FFP (E) does not address the arrest. The unifying engine: resuscitate first, antidote second.

_Teaching point:_ In any tox/envenomation emergency, resuscitation (ABC/ALS) always precedes the antidote — collapse from brown snake is treated as an arrest, not as an antivenom decision.
</details>

**Q6.** A 22-year-old gardener in Sydney develops, within 20 minutes of a bite, profuse salivation, lacrimation, generalised muscle fasciculation, sweating, hypertension and frothy pulmonary oedema. What is the correct first aid and definitive treatment?

   - A. Analgesia and ice only; no bandage
   - B. Vinegar to the bite site, then antivenom
   - C. Pressure-immobilisation bandage + splint and early funnel-web antivenom
   - D. Atropine alone and observe
   - E. Pressure-immobilisation bandage but withhold antivenom unless coagulopathy develops

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Pressure-immobilisation bandage + splint and early funnel-web antivenom**

This is a cholinergic crisis from a Sydney funnel-web spider (SLUDGE-type features, fasciculation, hypertension, pulmonary oedema, rapid onset). Funnel-web bites are managed LIKE snakebite — pressure-immobilisation bandage plus splint — with early antivenom. Analgesia/ice with no bandage (A) is the management for redback (latrodectism), the opposite approach. Vinegar (B) is for tropical marine stings, not spiders. Atropine alone (D) is inadequate; antivenom is definitive. Withholding antivenom (E) is wrong — funnel-web envenomation is life-threatening and antivenom is given early, not gated on coagulopathy (which funnel-webs do not cause).

_Teaching point:_ Funnel-web = PIB + early antivenom (treat like a snake); redback = analgesia and no bandage — the two spiders are managed oppositely.
</details>

**Q7.** A 45-year-old presents 5 hours after ingesting an unknown number of immediate-release paracetamol tablets in a single impulsive act. She is well. A paracetamol level drawn now is plotted above the treatment line on the Australian nomogram. Which statement best reflects correct management?

   - A. Withhold NAC because she is asymptomatic and LFTs are normal
   - B. Start the two-bag NAC regimen: 200 mg/kg over 4 h then 100 mg/kg over 16 h
   - C. Give a single bolus of NAC and recheck the level in 4 hours
   - D. Wait for an 8-hour level before deciding, as a 5-hour level is uninterpretable
   - E. Perform gastric lavage now as it is within 6 hours

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Start the two-bag NAC regimen: 200 mg/kg over 4 h then 100 mg/kg over 16 h**

For a single immediate-release ingestion with a known time, a level at 4 hours or later that lies above the nomogram line mandates NAC; the current AU/NZ standard is the two-bag regimen (200 mg/kg over 4 h, then 100 mg/kg over 16 h), which causes fewer anaphylactoid reactions than the old three-bag protocol. Withholding because she is well (A) is dangerous — hepatotoxicity is delayed and NAC is preventive. A single bolus (C) is not the regimen. The 5-hour level is fully interpretable (only levels before 4 h are not), so D is wrong; an 8-hour wait would lose treatment window. Gastric lavage (E) is not indicated.

_Teaching point:_ Above-line paracetamol level at >=4 h after a single IR ingestion = start the two-bag NAC regimen (200 mg/kg/4 h then 100 mg/kg/16 h); a normal patient is still treated.
</details>

**Q8.** A 60-year-old man with osteoarthritis is brought in after ingesting roughly forty 665 mg modified-release (Panadol Osteo) paracetamol tablets. A level at 4 hours sits just below the nomogram line. He is asymptomatic. What is the most appropriate management?

   - A. Discharge with reassurance as the 4-hour level is below the line
   - B. Repeat the level at ~8 hours and commence NAC if either level is above the line; given the large MR ingestion, start NAC now
   - C. Plot only the 4-hour level and use a single nomogram point as for immediate-release
   - D. Give activated charcoal and discharge
   - E. Withhold NAC until LFTs rise

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Repeat the level at ~8 hours and commence NAC if either level is above the line; given the large MR ingestion, start NAC now**

Modified-release paracetamol follows a distinct Australian pathway: a single 4-hour level cannot exclude toxicity because absorption is prolonged, so levels are taken at 4 h AND ~8 h and you treat if EITHER is above the line. For large MR ingestions (>=10 g or >=200 mg/kg, whichever is less) a full NAC course is started empirically; this ingestion (~26 g) clearly qualifies. Treating the MR ingestion like IR with one 4-hour point (C) and discharging on a single below-line level (A) both miss delayed/rising levels. Charcoal-and-discharge (D) ignores the need for NAC. Waiting for LFTs (E) loses the preventive window.

_Teaching point:_ Modified-release paracetamol needs levels at 4 h AND 8 h (treat if either is above the line) and a full empirical NAC course for large ingestions — never trust a single 4-hour MR level.
</details>

**Q9.** A 30-year-old is brought in 1 hour after a deliberate overdose of amitriptyline. He is drowsy and hypotensive. ECG shows a QRS of 130 ms with a terminal R wave in aVR. What is the most appropriate specific treatment?

   - A. IV sodium bicarbonate boluses targeting pH ~7.5
   - B. IV flumazenil
   - C. IV amiodarone for the wide-complex rhythm
   - D. IV calcium gluconate
   - E. IV phenytoin for seizure prophylaxis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. IV sodium bicarbonate boluses targeting pH ~7.5**

TCA toxicity causes fast sodium-channel blockade producing QRS widening (a terminal R wave in aVR is the classic clue), hypotension and seizures. The mechanism-matched lever is IV sodium bicarbonate (sodium load + alkalinisation overcome the channel block), given for QRS prolongation (commonly >100 ms, many toxicologists act at >120 ms) and for hypotension/arrhythmia/seizures, targeting pH ~7.5–7.55. Flumazenil (B) is contraindicated (can precipitate seizures, wrong toxin). Class I/III antiarrhythmics like amiodarone (C) worsen sodium-channel blockade and are avoided. Calcium (D) is for CCB/hyperkalaemia, not TCA. Phenytoin (E) is a sodium-channel agent and is not used here.

_Teaching point:_ Wide QRS after a TCA overdose (look for the aVR terminal R wave) = IV sodium bicarbonate; avoid class I/III antiarrhythmics and flumazenil.
</details>

### Schema recall — "What is your schema for X?"

**What is your schema for the immediate management of anaphylaxis?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Recognise: acute onset + airway/breathing/circulation compromise +/- skin (skin may be ABSENT). 2) Call for help, remove trigger. 3) IM adrenaline immediately — adult 0.5 mg (0.5 mL 1:1000) anterolateral thigh; child 0.01 mg/kg to max 0.5 mg; repeat every 5 min. 4) Position by symptom: supine + legs up if hypotensive, sit up if respiratory distress predominates, left lateral if pregnant/vomiting. 5) High-flow O2, IV access, fluid bolus 20 mL/kg. 6) Adjuncts: salbutamol for bronchospasm; antihistamine/steroid only for cutaneous symptoms (they do NOT treat anaphylaxis or prevent biphasic reactions). 7) Refractory after >=2-3 doses -> adrenaline INFUSION in monitored setting; if on beta-blocker -> glucagon. 8) Observe >=4 h post-resolution (biphasic risk).

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Students sit a hypotensive patient up (empty-ventricle arrest) or reach for hydrocortisone/antihistamine as primary therapy. Examiners also attack the 'no rash' stem and the IV-bolus trap. The single point of attack: position is symptom-dependent, not always supine, and adjuncts are never the life-saving step.
</details>

**What is your schema for the assessment and management of a suspected Australian snakebite?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Resuscitate first — ABC/ALS; collapse or arrest (brown snake) is treated as an arrest, not an antivenom decision. 2) First aid: pressure bandage with immobilisation over the WHOLE limb + splint, applied over clothing, distal-to-proximal; keep patient still; do NOT remove until in a hospital with antivenom. No tourniquet/incision/suction/washing. 3) Bloods at 0, 1, 6, 12 h: INR/PT, aPTT, fibrinogen, D-dimer, CK, U&E, FBC. 4) Define envenomation clinically + by labs (VICC, neurotoxicity, myotoxicity, AKI, collapse). 5) Antivenom only for SYSTEMIC envenomation — choose by GEOGRAPHY + clinical/lab syndrome (SVDK is unreliable, never decides whether to treat). 6) Have adrenaline ready for reactions (no routine premedication). 7) Phone Poisons Information 13 11 26 / Clinical Toxinology. 8) Do NOT chase a slow INR with repeat antivenom — factors regenerate over hours.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The classic trap is repeat antivenom to 'correct' a persistently raised INR (factor regeneration takes hours), and using the SVDK to decide whether to treat. Examiners also attack the contraindicated 'classics' (tourniquet/cut-and-suck) and forgetting that resuscitation precedes antivenom.
</details>

**What is your schema for the channel/receptor-matched antidote in cardiotoxic overdoses?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Stabilise ABC. 2) Read the ECG/clinical pattern -> map to mechanism -> pick the lever: TCA (wide QRS, aVR R wave, Na-channel block) -> IV sodium bicarbonate. Beta-blocker/CCB (bradycardia + hypotension) -> high-dose insulin-euglycaemia therapy (HIET) as key inotrope + calcium + glucagon (BB) + atropine + vasopressors. Opioid (miosis + respiratory depression) -> naloxone titrated to ventilation. Salicylate (mixed resp alkalosis + HAGMA, tinnitus) -> urinary alkalinisation, dialysis if severe. Digoxin -> Fab fragments. Iron -> desferrioxamine. Local anaesthetic systemic toxicity -> IV lipid emulsion (Intralipid 20%). 3) Avoid mechanism-worsening drugs (class I/III antiarrhythmics in TCA; flumazenil where it unmasks seizures). 4) Charcoal within ~1-2 h if alert/airway protected for suitable agents; consider WBI for SR/iron/lithium. 5) Call 13 11 26.
