_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Students name-match instead of mechanism-match — e.g. giving amiodarone for the TCA wide-complex rhythm (worsens it), or treating BB/CCB shock with vasopressors alone while forgetting HIET. The discriminator is always 'which channel/receptor is blocked', not the drug's name.
</details>

**What is your schema for distinguishing and treating serotonin syndrome versus neuroleptic malignant syndrome?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Drug history: serotonergic agent recently added/increased (SSRI + tramadol/MAOI/triptan) vs dopamine antagonist/antipsychotic started or Parkinson's drug withdrawn. 2) Onset: serotonin = fast (hours); NMS = slow (days). 3) Neuromuscular sign: serotonin = clonus + hyperreflexia (lower-limb predominant), agitation; NMS = lead-pipe RIGIDITY + hyporeflexia. 4) CK: NMS markedly raised (often >>1000); serotonin more about clonus than extreme CK. 5) Treatment: both stop the drug + aggressive cooling + supportive/benzodiazepines; serotonin -> cyproheptadine; NMS -> dantrolene +/- bromocriptine. 6) Consider mimics: malignant hyperthermia (anaesthetic trigger), meningitis, status, sepsis.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Examiners attack the discriminator: clonus + rapid onset = serotonin; rigidity + bradyreflexia + days = NMS. Students confuse the antidotes (cyproheptadine vs dantrolene) and forget that onset speed and reflex pattern, not fever alone, separate them.
</details>

**What is your schema for the management of paracetamol overdose (including modified-release)?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) History: dose, time, single vs staggered, immediate-release (IR) vs modified-release (MR). 2) Toxic threshold ~200 mg/kg or 10 g acute. 3) Charcoal if <2 h and airway safe. 4) IR single ingestion known time: level at >=4 h, plot on AU nomogram (line ~150 mg/L at 4 h); treat if above line. 5) MR ingestion: levels at 4 h AND ~8 h, treat if EITHER above line; full empirical NAC for MR >=10 g or >=200 mg/kg. 6) Empirical NAC if >8 h, staggered, or unknown timing. 7) Regimen: two-bag NAC 200 mg/kg over 4 h then 100 mg/kg over 16 h. 8) Massive ingestion (>2x line, or >=30 g / >=500 mg/kg): increased-dose NAC. 9) Call 13 11 26.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The trap is interpreting a level drawn before 4 h, or trusting a single 4-hour level in an MR ingestion (needs the 8-hour level too). Students also forget that staggered/unknown-time/>8 h ingestions get empirical NAC regardless of a 'reassuring' level.
</details>

**What is your schema for Australian marine envenomation first aid?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Identify tropical vs non-tropical. 2) Box jellyfish (Chironex): tropical -> douse liberally with VINEGAR (4-6% acetic acid) to inactivate undischarged nematocysts, CPR for arrest, box jellyfish antivenom. 3) Irukandji: tropical, delayed (~20-40 min) catecholamine storm (severe pain, hypertension, tachycardia, sense of doom, +/- stress cardiomyopathy/APO) -> vinegar at the scene; mainstay is IV opioid analgesia (e.g. fentanyl) titrated to pain + active BP/APO control (GTN first-line for hypertensive APO); magnesium is adjunctive and of UNPROVEN benefit (negative RCT). 4) Non-tropical bluebottle (Physalia): remove tentacles + HOT WATER immersion, NOT vinegar. 5) Stonefish/penetrating: hot water immersion + antivenom for stonefish.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The examiner trap is over-applying vinegar — it is for TROPICAL stings only and is WRONG for bluebottle (use hot water). The second trap is stating magnesium as standard Irukandji therapy: it is adjunctive and unproven (negative RCT), so opioid analgesia + BP control is the safer 'best' answer.
</details>

### Distractor drills — "What's the deadliest distractor for X?"

**What is the deadliest distractor when a patient is hypotensive from anaphylaxis and the stem asks for the immediate action?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Sit the patient upright and give IV hydrocortisone (or IV antihistamine)
- _Why it tempts you:_ Steroids and antihistamines are reflexively associated with allergy, and sitting a breathless patient up feels intuitively right; they are commonly co-administered in real practice so they look 'complete'.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Antihistamines/steroids do not treat the vasodilation or bronchospasm and do not prevent biphasic reactions; sitting a hypotensive patient up can cause empty-ventricle cardiac arrest. The only life-saving step is IM adrenaline.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Ask: does this reverse the alpha/beta-adrenergic deficit RIGHT NOW? Only IM adrenaline does; everything else is adjunct, and position must be supine when hypotensive.**

**What is the most seductive wrong answer when an envenomated patient's INR stays elevated shortly after antivenom?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Give a second dose of antivenom to bring the INR down
- _Why it tempts you:_ It feels mechanistically logical ('more antidote = faster correction') and the abnormal lab number is anxiety-provoking; students equate normalising the number with treating the patient.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Antivenom binds only FREE/circulating venom; once given, the remaining coagulopathy reflects consumed factors that must regenerate over hours. Repeat antivenom adds anaphylaxis/serum-sickness risk without speeding factor recovery.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Has free venom been neutralised (appropriate dose given, no ongoing systemic progression)? If yes, a persistently high INR = wait and monitor, not more antivenom.**

**What is the deadliest distractor in snakebite field first aid?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Apply a tourniquet (or incise and suck the bite)
- _Why it tempts you:_ It is the 'classic' folk and old-textbook teaching, and arterial-occluding logic ('stop the venom spreading') sounds protective; overseas sources still mention suction devices.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Tourniquets cause ischaemia and a sudden venom bolus on release; incision/suction worsen local tissue injury and do not remove meaningful venom. The correct step is a pressure bandage with immobilisation over the whole limb plus a splint.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Does the manoeuvre slow LYMPHATIC spread without ischaemia? Only a broad pressure-immobilisation bandage does; anything occlusive or cutting is wrong.**

**What is the most seductive wrong answer for a swimmer stung by a bluebottle on a Sydney beach?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Douse the sting with vinegar
- _Why it tempts you:_ Vinegar is drilled as THE jellyfish first aid, so it generalises in students' minds to every jellyfish sting.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Vinegar is only for TROPICAL stings (box jellyfish/Irukandji); for non-tropical bluebottle (Physalia) stings the correct treatment is removing tentacles and HOT WATER immersion — vinegar can worsen bluebottle stings.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Is this a TROPICAL species (Chironex/Irukandji)? Vinegar yes. Bluebottle/temperate water = hot water immersion, not vinegar.**

**What is the deadliest distractor when a TCA-overdose patient has a wide-complex tachycardia?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Give amiodarone (or another class I/III antiarrhythmic) for the wide-complex rhythm
- _Why it tempts you:_ Wide-complex tachycardia triggers the rote 'amiodarone' reflex from ALS algorithms, and it superficially 'treats the arrhythmia'.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ The rhythm is caused by sodium-channel blockade; class I/III agents further block sodium/potassium channels and worsen conduction. The mechanism-matched treatment is IV sodium bicarbonate.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **What is the underlying CHANNEL lesion? Na-channel block (wide QRS, aVR terminal R) -> bicarbonate; never add another channel blocker.**

**What is the most seductive wrong answer when a redback (latrodectism) patient presents with severe local pain and sweating?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Apply a pressure-immobilisation bandage (treat like a snake/funnel-web)
- _Why it tempts you:_ PIB is the headline Australian envenomation manoeuvre, so it over-generalises to any bite with severe symptoms.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Latrodectism is not life-threatening, causes no coagulopathy, and has slow-onset regional pain; a PIB localises and worsens the pain and offers no benefit. Management is analgesia + ice, with antivenom only for refractory pain.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Is the venom rapidly life-threatening and lymphatically spread (snake/funnel-web)? Redback is neither -> analgesia, no bandage.**

**What is the deadliest distractor when interpreting a paracetamol level after a modified-release (Panadol Osteo) overdose?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Use a single 4-hour level on the nomogram to clear the patient if it is below the line
- _Why it tempts you:_ It mirrors the well-drilled immediate-release rule ('one level at >=4 h on the nomogram'), so students apply it identically.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ MR paracetamol has prolonged, erratic absorption, so a 4-hour level can be falsely reassuring while levels rise later; you need a 4 h AND ~8 h level, treating if either is above the line, plus empirical full NAC for large MR ingestions.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Is the preparation modified-release? If yes, a single 4-hour level cannot exclude toxicity — you must draw the second (~8 h) level.**

### High-end review pearls

- If you see anaphylaxis with NO rash, it is still anaphylaxis — up to ~20% lack cutaneous signs; the absence of urticaria is a classic distractor, not a rule-out.
- Aunt-minnie: urban/suburban Eastern-Australian bite with isolated coagulopathy (high INR, undetectable fibrinogen, high D-dimer) and NO neurotoxicity = brown snake. Add neurotoxicity + myotoxicity (raised CK) = tiger snake.
- Never-forget one-liner: antivenom binds FREE venom — it does not reverse established paralysis quickly nor instantly correct clotting factors; do not chase the INR with repeat antivenom.
- If you see VICC, think 'looks exactly like DIC' (high INR/D-dimer, low fibrinogen) — but the answer is antivenom + time, NOT FFP/factors as the first move (FFP can fuel consumption while free venom remains).
- Classic AU trap: SVDK does NOT decide whether to treat and is unreliable (high false-positive rate); antivenom choice is driven by geography + clinical/lab syndrome.
- If you see SLUDGE + fasciculation + pulmonary oedema after a Sydney bite, think funnel-web -> PIB + early antivenom (manage like a snake). Redback is the opposite: analgesia, no bandage.
- Vinegar is for TROPICAL jellyfish only (box jelly/Irukandji); a bluebottle (Physalia) sting wants HOT WATER immersion — over-applying vinegar is a deliberate exam trap.
- If you see a delayed (20-40 min) catecholamine storm in a tropical QLD swimmer — severe pain, hypertension, tachycardia, sense of impending doom +/- stress cardiomyopathy/APO — think Irukandji; treat with opioids + BP control. Magnesium is adjunctive and UNPROVEN (negative RCT), so it is not the guaranteed 'best' answer.
- Aunt-minnie ECG: wide QRS with a terminal R wave in aVR after an overdose = sodium-channel blockade (TCA) -> sodium bicarbonate.
- If you see bradycardia + hypotension with preserved consciousness and hyperglycaemia, think calcium-channel blocker overdose; if hypoglycaemia-prone with the same picture, think beta-blocker — either way HIET is the key inotrope, not vasopressors alone.
- Discriminator pearl: clonus + hyperreflexia + onset over hours = serotonin syndrome (cyproheptadine); lead-pipe rigidity + hyporeflexia + days + CK >>1000 = NMS (dantrolene/bromocriptine).
- The current AU/NZ paracetamol antidote is the TWO-bag NAC regimen (200 mg/kg over 4 h, then 100 mg/kg over 16 h) — it has replaced the three-bag regimen and causes fewer anaphylactoid reactions.
- Massive paracetamol (level >2x the nomogram line, or >=30 g / >=500 mg/kg) gets an INCREASED-dose NAC regimen — naming the >2x-line / 500 mg/kg trigger is the high-end answer.
- LAST (local anaesthetic systemic toxicity) -> IV lipid emulsion (Intralipid 20%); digoxin -> Fab fragments (DigiFab); iron -> desferrioxamine — round out the mechanism-matched antidote table.
- Exam-safe reflex: phone Poisons Information 13 11 26 (single national number) for any significant overdose/envenomation, and Clinical Toxinology for envenomation — it is almost never the wrong adjunct answer.
- Position in anaphylaxis is symptom-dependent: supine + legs up if hypotensive, sit up if respiratory distress predominates, left lateral if pregnant/vomiting — 'always supine' is incomplete.
- Resuscitation ALWAYS precedes the antidote: brown-snake pre-hospital cardiac arrest is run as an arrest (ALS), and a refractory anaphylaxis is an adrenaline infusion in a monitored bed before steroids ever matter.
- Charcoal (50 g adult) within ~1-2 h for suitable agents in an alert/airway-protected patient; whole-bowel irrigation for sustained-release, iron and lithium — but gastric lavage is essentially never the answer.

> **Independent item audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[medium]** MCQ #2 (paediatric adrenaline dose) — explanation of distractor D: The explanation of option D says ignoring weight 'here it would breach nothing.' This is internally WRONG and self-contradictory. For a 30 kg child the correct dose is 0.3 mg; the adult 0.5 mg dose is a ~67% overdose for this child, so it DOES breach the weight-based principle in a clinically meaningful way. The parenthetical undercuts the very teaching point of the item. → Reword to: 'Option D ignores weight — for this 30 kg child the adult 0.5 mg dose is a substantial (~67%) overdose; adrenaline is always weight-based with a 0.5 mg cap reached only at 50 kg.'
> - **[low]** MCQ #2 — option A wording bundles a worked answer with the general rule: Option A reads '0.01 mg/kg (0.3 mg) IM of 1:1000, max 0.5 mg, repeat every 5 min.' Embedding both the formula AND the pre-computed 0.3 mg AND the cap AND the repeat interval makes A markedly longer and more 'complete' than the distractors — a length/specificity cue that telegraphs the answer (the longest, most-qualified option is correct). Test-wise students pick it without reasoning. → Trim A to match distractor length (e.g. '0.01 mg/kg IM of 1:1000 (= 0.3 mg here), max 0.5 mg') and lengthen a distractor or two so the correct option is not conspicuously the most detailed.
> - **[low]** MCQ #6 (funnel-web) — toxidrome labelling in explanation: The explanation calls this 'a cholinergic crisis... (SLUDGE-type features).' Funnel-web (delta-hexatoxin) envenomation is primarily a catecholamine/autonomic storm with combined cholinergic AND adrenergic features (hence the HYPERTENSION, tachycardia and fasciculation in the stem), not a pure cholinergic/SLUDGE picture like organophosphates. Calling it 'cholinergic crisis / SLUDGE' is imprecise and could mislead a strong student toward an OP-poisoning (atropine) frame. → Reword to 'autonomic storm with mixed cholinergic and adrenergic features (salivation, lacrimation, fasciculation PLUS hypertension/tachycardia and pulmonary oedema)' and drop the bare 'SLUDGE/cholinergic crisis' label, or qualify it explicitly as mixed.
> - **[low]** MCQ #3 (VICC) — distractor B (FFP) defensibility: Option B (FFP) is correctly the wrong first move, but the stem patient 'feels well' with no bleeding, which makes B cleanly wrong — good. However the explanation states FFP 'can fuel ongoing consumption while free venom remains'; after appropriate antivenom there should be little/no free venom, so this rationale is slightly outdated/overstated. The real reason FFP is not indicated here is simply absence of bleeding (factor replacement is reserved for active haemorrhage). Minor rationale imprecision, not an answer error. → Adjust rationale to emphasise 'no active bleeding, so factor replacement is not indicated; FFP is reserved for clinically significant haemorrhage' rather than the 'fuels consumption' argument which applies mainly before adequate antivenom.
> - **[low]** MCQ #1 (anaphylaxis) — distractor C dose internal consistency: Option C reads 'IV adrenaline 1 mg (1:10,000) bolus.' 1 mg of 1:10,000 is 10 mL — this is the cardiac-arrest dose, not any anaphylaxis IV regimen (which is a titrated 50 microgram boluses/infusion). The distractor is correctly wrong, but framing it as a '1 mg bolus' makes it an obvious strawman to anyone who knows arrest dosing rather than the more seductive trap of a small IV adrenaline bolus. → Strengthen the trap: make C 'IV adrenaline 50 microgram boluses titrated' (the genuinely tempting but setting-inappropriate ward error) so it tests the IM-vs-IV-route discriminator rather than an obvious arrest dose.
> - **[low]** MCQ #4 (snakebite first aid) — option A 'add suction' as deadliest trap: Distractors A (leave tourniquet + suction) and D (tighten tourniquet + elevate) are both compound 'do two wrong things' options that are easy to eliminate. Only C (SVDK on cleaned skin) is a genuinely seductive single-error trap. The item is single-best-answer clean but several distractors are strawmen, lowering discrimination. → Replace one compound-wrong distractor with a more plausible single error, e.g. 'Remove the tourniquet and observe without a bandage' (tempting because it correctly removes the tourniquet but omits the PIB), forcing finer reasoning.
> 
> _Quality notes:_ All nine MCQ stated answers are correct per 2026 AU guidelines and I found NO accidental also-correct distractor in any item. Core facts independently verified: ASCIA adult IM adrenaline 0.5 mg / paeds 0.01 mg/kg cap 0.5 mg (confirmed); two-bag NAC 200 mg/kg/4h then 100 mg/kg/16h is current AU/NZ standard (confirmed); MR paracetamol dual 4 h + 8 h levels and empirical full NAC at >=10 g or >=200 mg/kg, increased-dose at >=30 g / >=500 mg/kg, and >2x-line trigger (all confirmed against ACI/MJA 2019 guideline still current 2026); funnel-web managed with PIB + early antivenom like snakebite (confirmed); Irukandji magnesium negative RCT / opioid + BP control mainstay (confirmed, Br J / EMA RCT); bluebottle = hot water not vinegar (standard); TCA wide-QRS/aVR-R = sodium bicarbonate (correct).; Distractor drills are genuinely the deadliest real-world traps, not strawmen: 'second dose of antivenom to correct INR', 'sit hypotensive anaphylaxis patient up + steroids', 'amiodarone for TCA wide-complex', 'vinegar on bluebottle', 'single 4-h level to clear MR paracetamol', and 'PIB on redback' are all high-yield, frequently-failed discriminators. Strong adversarial design here.; Schemas are clinically sound and well-structured. The paracetamol, snakebite, anaphylaxis and cardiotoxic-antidote schemas are exam-ready. The serotonin-vs-NMS and marine-envenomation schemas correctly nail the discriminators (clonus+hours vs rigidity+days; tropical-vinegar vs bluebottle-hot-water).; Minor over-engineering risk: several items (notably MCQ #2 and the redback drill) embed the full teaching rule INSIDE the correct option, creating a length/completeness cue. Across the set the correct answer is sometimes the most qualified option — worth balancing option lengths to avoid test-wise cueing.; Two items lean on compound 'two-wrong-things' distractors (MCQ #4 A and D; MCQ #1 C as an obvious arrest dose) that are easy eliminations and lower discrimination, though they do not threaten single-best-answer integrity.; One genuine accuracy nit (not an answer error): the funnel-web explanation labels the syndrome a 'cholinergic crisis / SLUDGE' when it is more accurately a mixed autonomic (cholinergic + adrenergic) storm — the stem itself lists hypertension, which is not cholinergic. Worth tightening so a strong candidate is not nudged toward an organophosphate/atropine frame.; No item is too easy at the targeted level; difficulty is appropriately calibrated for high-stakes AU clinical exams. Stems are unambiguous and each maps cleanly to one syndrome/mechanism, consistent with the tutorial's 'identify toxidrome -> mechanism -> time-windowed step' engine.

---

## Haematology  _(~5% of marks)_

### The one idea that unlocks the domain

> **Every haematology question is a "where is the lesion in the cell lifecycle, and does the clinical tempo demand emergency action?" problem. You localise the defect along one axis — production (marrow), maturation (substrate: iron/B12/folate), or destruction/loss (haemolysis, bleeding, consumption) — then ask whether a time-critical syndrome (TTP, febrile neutropenia, tumour lysis, HIT, massive haemorrhage, acute leukaemia) is layered on top demanding action BEFORE the diagnosis is confirmed.**

Almost every haem MCQ is solved by two sequential questions. First: localise. A cytopenia is either underproduction (low reticulocytes, marrow failure/infiltration/substrate lack), faulty maturation (MCV tells you the substrate — microcytic = iron/thalassaemia, macrocytic = B12/folate/myelodysplasia/alcohol), or loss/destruction (high reticulocytes, haemolysis screen, blood loss). This single axis derives anaemia classification, pancytopenia approach, myeloma (marrow crowding → CRAB), and leukaemia (clonal expansion crowding out normal lines). Iron studies are just the maturation-substrate readout: ferritin = stores (but acute-phase reactant), transferrin/TIBC rises when starved of iron, transferrin saturation = supply to marrow. Second question: tempo. The high-yield traps are not the diagnosis — they are recognising a syndrome where delay kills: TTP (don't transfuse platelets, start PLEX now), febrile neutropenia (piperacillin-tazobactam within 60 min, before cultures return), tumour lysis (rasburicase + fluids), HIT (stop heparin, start non-heparin anticoagulant — never platelets), and massive haemorrhage (ratio-based products, MTP). Once you hold "localise then check tempo," you derive rather than memorise: a febrile patient on chemo is FN until proven otherwise; thrombocytopenia + schistocytes + fever + neuro/renal = TTP not DIC (normal coags); high INR + bleeding = vitamin K + prothrombinex. The engine converts scattered facts into a reproducible decision tree that survives Australian-themed rewording.

### High-yield tutorial

# Haematology: Localise the lesion, then check the tempo

## 1. The anaemia engine — reticulocytes + MCV + iron studies

Start every anaemia with **reticulocyte count**: it splits underproduction (low retic) from loss/destruction (high retic). Then layer **MCV**.

**Microcytic (MCV <80):** iron deficiency vs thalassaemia vs anaemia of chronic disease (ACD). The discriminator is **iron studies**:
- **Iron deficiency:** ferritin LOW, transferrin/TIBC HIGH, transferrin saturation LOW. *Ferritin <15–30 µg/L is diagnostic.* Ferritin is an acute-phase reactant — a "normal" ferritin in an inflamed patient does NOT exclude deficiency; use **transferrin saturation <20%** or check CRP.
- **ACD:** ferritin NORMAL/HIGH, transferrin LOW, saturation low-normal. Iron is sequestered (hepcidin-driven), not absent.
- **Thalassaemia trait:** normal/high iron, **disproportionately low MCV for the degree of anaemia**, high RBC count, Mentzer index <13, raised HbA2 (β-trait). An Aus exam loves the patient of Mediterranean/SE-Asian background with lifelong mild microcytosis and normal ferritin.

**Iron-deficiency in any adult = exclude GI blood loss.** A man or postmenopausal woman with IDA needs colonoscopy/gastroscopy.

**Macrocytic:** megaloblastic (B12/folate — hypersegmented neutrophils, raised LDH/bilirubin from ineffective erythropoiesis) vs non-megaloblastic (alcohol, liver disease, hypothyroidism, **myelodysplasia**, reticulocytosis). **B12 deficiency with neuro signs (SACD) → replace B12 BEFORE folate** or you precipitate subacute combined degeneration.

**Normocytic:** ACD, renal (low EPO), early marrow failure, or **acute blood loss/haemolysis** (check retics).

## 2. Haemolysis & the microangiopathies

Haemolysis screen: **↑retics, ↑LDH, ↑unconjugated bilirubin, ↓haptoglobin.** Then **DAT/Coombs**: positive = immune (warm AIHA → steroids; cold → keep warm). DAT-negative with **schistocytes** = mechanical/microangiopathic.

**The killer pentad to recognise: TTP.** Microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia + thrombocytopenia + **normal coagulation (PT/APTT normal, fibrinogen normal)** ± fever, neuro, renal. Mechanism: ADAMTS13 deficiency → uncleaved vWF multimers → platelet microthrombi. **Do NOT transfuse platelets (fuels thrombosis) — urgent plasma exchange (PLEX) + steroids.** Contrast **DIC**: deranged coags, low fibrinogen, high D-dimer — treat the cause. **HUS** (Shiga-toxin, kids, bloody diarrhoea, renal-dominant) — supportive.

## 3. Marrow crowding: myeloma & acute leukaemia

**Myeloma = CRAB:** hyperCalcaemia, Renal impairment, Anaemia, Bone lesions (lytic, NOT sclerotic; **bone scan negative — use skeletal survey/whole-body MRI/low-dose CT**). Rouleaux, high ESR, paraprotein band on EPG/SPEP, Bence-Jones (free light chains) in urine. Suspect in older patient with back pain + anaemia + renal failure + high calcium.

**Acute leukaemia red flags:** pancytopenia with **blasts** on film, bruising/bleeding, infection, bone pain. **APML (AML M3, t(15;17)) is the emergency** — DIC and catastrophic bleeding; start **ATRA immediately** on suspicion. Blasts + Auer rods = AML.

## 4. The time-critical syndromes (tempo > diagnosis)

- **Febrile neutropenia:** neutrophils <0.5 (or <1.0 falling) + temp ≥38. **eTG: piperacillin-tazobactam 4.5 g IV stat, within 60 minutes**, after cultures but do NOT wait for results. Add **vancomycin** for line sepsis/MRSA risk/septic shock; use a carbapenem if penicillin-resistant risk. Gentamicin added if septic shock/resistant gram-negatives.
- **Tumour lysis syndrome:** ↑K, ↑phosphate, ↑urate, ↓Ca → AKI/arrhythmia. High risk in bulky/ high-WCC leukaemia/lymphoma post-chemo. **Hydration + rasburicase** (high risk) or allopurinol (lower risk). Avoid potassium.
- **HIT:** platelet fall **>50% at day 5–10** of heparin, **thrombosis not bleeding**. **Stop ALL heparin, start a non-heparin anticoagulant (e.g. argatroban/danaparoid/fondaparinux), never give platelets, never start warfarin until platelets recover** (skin necrosis risk). 4Ts score.

## 5. VTE, anticoagulation & reversal

**First-line for most DVT/PE = a DOAC** (apixaban/rivaroxaban), no bridging needed. **Exceptions favouring warfarin:** antiphospholipid syndrome, severe renal impairment (CrCl <30 caution; avoid dabigatran), mechanical valves (warfarin only). **LMWH** preferred in **cancer-associated** VTE and **pregnancy** (DOACs contraindicated).

**Reversal (must-know):**
- **Warfarin, major bleed:** **Prothrombinex-VF (4-factor PCC) + IV vitamin K** (+ FFP if Prothrombinex unavailable). High INR no bleed: hold + oral vit K.
- **Dabigatran:** **idarucizumab**.
- **Factor Xa inhibitors (ap/rivaroxaban):** andexanet alfa (limited Aus availability) or **Prothrombinex**.
- **Heparin:** **protamine**.

## 6. Transfusion

**Acute haemolytic (ABO mismatch):** fever, loin/back pain, hypotension during transfusion — **stop, IV fluids, check clerical error.** **TACO** (volume overload, hypertension, raised JVP) vs **TRALI** (hypoxia, normal/low filling pressures, within 6 h). **Febrile non-haemolytic** = common, slow/stop, antipyretic. Massive haemorrhage → **MTP, ~1:1:1 RBC:FFP:platelets**, tranexamic acid early in trauma.

### Highest mark-per-minute principles

- **Reticulocyte count is the first fork in any anaemia: low = production problem, high = loss/destruction.** — Instantly halves the differential before MCV is even considered, and stems are written to reward this split.
  - _Exam angle:_ Anaemia + high retics + high LDH + low haptoglobin + spherocytes → AIHA; do the DAT next.
- **Ferritin is an acute-phase reactant; a normal ferritin does not exclude iron deficiency in inflammation — use transferrin saturation.** — The classic distractor: inflamed patient with 'normal' ferritin who is actually iron deficient.
  - _Exam angle:_ IBD/RA patient, microcytic, ferritin 80, saturation 8% → iron deficient AND ACD; treat iron.
- **TTP = MAHA + thrombocytopenia with NORMAL coags; do not give platelets, start PLEX.** — Single highest-yield 'don't kill the patient' instinct in haem; separates TTP from DIC by coagulation profile.
  - _Exam angle:_ Schistocytes, platelets 15, normal PT/APTT/fibrinogen, confusion → urgent plasma exchange, NOT platelet transfusion.
- **Febrile neutropenia is a medical emergency: empirical IV antibiotics within 60 minutes after cultures, not after results.** — eTG-specific, time-bound, and Australian exams test the exact agent (pip-taz) and the 60-minute rule.
  - _Exam angle:_ Day 10 post-chemo, temp 38.5, neutrophils 0.3 → piperacillin-tazobactam 4.5 g IV stat; add vancomycin if line sepsis/shock.
- **HIT causes thrombosis not bleeding; stop heparin, give a non-heparin anticoagulant, never platelets, never warfarin first.** — Counterintuitive (low platelets but you anticoagulate), and the wrong answers (transfuse platelets/start warfarin) are dangerous.
  - _Exam angle:_ Platelets drop 60% at day 7 of heparin with a new DVT → stop heparin, start danaparoid/fondaparinux.
- **Match the anticoagulant to the indication: DOAC default, warfarin for valves/APS, LMWH for cancer and pregnancy.** — One stem can be answered purely by spotting the exception that forbids a DOAC.
  - _Exam angle:_ Pregnant woman with PE → therapeutic LMWH (enoxaparin), not a DOAC or warfarin.
- **Myeloma bone disease is lytic and bone-scan-NEGATIVE; image with skeletal survey/whole-body low-dose CT or MRI.** — The bone-scan trap is a favourite, plus CRAB ties the whole presentation together.
  - _Exam angle:_ Older patient, back pain, Ca 2.9, Cr high, anaemia, normal bone scan → myeloma; order SPEP + serum free light chains.
- **Warfarin major bleed is reversed with Prothrombinex-VF (4F-PCC) plus IV vitamin K, not FFP alone.** — Australian product name and the PCC-over-FFP preference are specifically examinable.
  - _Exam angle:_ INR 8 with GI bleed → Prothrombinex-VF + 5–10 mg IV vitamin K.
- **B12 deficiency with neurology: replace B12 before folate to avoid precipitating subacute combined degeneration.** — Sequence-dependent management point that is easy to get backwards.
  - _Exam angle:_ Macrocytic anaemia + paraesthesiae + absent ankle jerks → give B12 first.
- **Distinguish TACO from TRALI by filling pressures: overload/hypertension (TACO) vs hypoxia with normal/low pressures (TRALI).** — Both present with respiratory distress post-transfusion; management diverges (diuresis vs supportive/ventilation).
  - _Exam angle:_ SOB 2 h into transfusion, raised JVP, hypertension, responds to frusemide → TACO.

### Must-know numbers (Australian where they differ)

- Ferritin <15–30 µg/L = iron deficiency; transferrin saturation <20% supports deficiency even if ferritin normal
- Microcytic MCV <80 fL; macrocytic MCV >100 fL
- Febrile neutropenia threshold: neutrophils <0.5 ×10^9/L (or <1.0 and falling) plus temp ≥38.0°C (or ≥38.3 once)
- eTG empirical FN: piperacillin-tazobactam 4.5 g IV q6–8h, first dose within 60 minutes; vancomycin added for line/MRSA/shock
- HIT: platelet drop >50% from baseline, typically day 5–10 of heparin; 4Ts score for probability
- Warfarin reversal: Prothrombinex-VF (4F-PCC) ~25–50 IU/kg + vitamin K 5–10 mg IV for major bleeding
- DOAC renal cut-off: avoid dabigatran and use caution with apixaban/rivaroxaban if CrCl <30 mL/min; warfarin preferred
- Platelet transfusion threshold: <10 prophylactic, <20 if febrile/sepsis, <50 for procedures/active bleed, <100 for CNS/neurosurgery
- Massive transfusion protocol ratio ~1:1:1 RBC:FFP:platelets; tranexamic acid 1 g within 3 h of trauma
- Myeloma CRAB: corrected Ca >2.75 mmol/L, creatinine rise/eGFR <40, Hb <100, lytic bone lesions
- Tumour lysis: K >6, phosphate high, urate high, corrected Ca low; rasburicase for high-risk, allopurinol + hydration for lower risk
- VTE anticoagulation duration: 3 months if provoked; extended/indefinite if unprovoked with low bleed risk or ongoing risk factor

### Cross-topic cuts

- Renal medicine: CKD anaemia (low EPO, normocytic) and the renal limits on DOAC choice; myeloma cast nephropathy and TTP/HUS as causes of AKI
- Oncology: febrile neutropenia, tumour lysis and cancer-associated VTE (LMWH first-line) all sit at the chemo–haem interface
- Gastroenterology: iron-deficiency anaemia mandates GI source workup (coeliac, malignancy, angiodysplasia); B12 from pernicious anaemia/terminal ileal Crohn's
- Rheumatology/ID: anaemia of chronic disease and the inflamed-ferritin trap; warm AIHA with SLE; APS driving warfarin choice in VTE
- Cardiology: mechanical valves force warfarin; AF anticoagulation and the same reversal agents; protamine for heparin
- Endocrine: hypothyroidism as a macrocytosis cause; hypercalcaemia of myeloma overlapping with PTH-driven causes
- Emergency/critical care: massive transfusion protocol, TACO vs TRALI, and DIC in sepsis/obstetric haemorrhage
- Neurology: B12 deficiency causing subacute combined degeneration; TTP presenting as fluctuating confusion/stroke-like episodes

### Australian-specific (where the UK answer loses you marks)

- Empirical febrile neutropenia therapy follows eTG (Therapeutic Guidelines): piperacillin-tazobactam first-line, within 60 minutes — UK sources may quote different beta-lactams
- Warfarin reversal uses the Australian product Prothrombinex-VF (a 3/4-factor PCC) plus vitamin K; UK answers often say 'PCC' generically or default to FFP
- Andexanet alfa for Xa-inhibitor reversal has limited Australian availability/PBS access; Prothrombinex is the pragmatic local choice
- Idarucizumab (Praxbind) is available and PBS-listed for dabigatran reversal
- Thalassaemia trait is high-prevalence in Australia's Mediterranean and South-East/East Asian communities — a favourite microcytosis distractor against iron deficiency
- Blood product governance follows the National Blood Authority 'Patient Blood Management' guidelines, with restrictive transfusion thresholds (Hb 70 g/L, or 80 in cardiac disease)
- Anti-D and Rh management, and transfusion reaction reporting, run through Lifeblood (Australian Red Cross Lifeblood) haemovigilance
- DOAC access is PBS-streamlined for AF and VTE; cancer-associated VTE increasingly uses apixaban/rivaroxaban or LMWH per local guidance rather than UK NICE phrasing

> **Independent accuracy audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[high]** Warfarin, major bleed: Prothrombinex-VF (4-factor PCC) + IV vitamin K. Reinforced in mustKnowNumbers: 'Prothrombinex-VF (4F-PCC)' and in highYieldPrinciples 'Prothrombinex-VF (4F-PCC)'. → State: Prothrombinex-VF is a 3-factor PCC (II, IX, X, low VII); historically given WITH FFP if significant factor VII deficiency was a concern in major bleeding. As of 2025 Australia/NZ are transitioning to 4F-PCC (Beriplex). Dose 25-50 IU/kg (50 IU/kg life-threatening) + IV vitamin K 5-10 mg. Source: Tran H et al, 'Updated recommendations for warfarin reversal in the setting of four-factor prothrombin complex concentrate', MJA 2025;222(1); and 2013 MJA consensus warfarin reversal guidelines (Australasian Society of Thrombosis and Haemostasis). _(MJA 2025;222(1) Updated warfarin reversal recommendations (4F-PCC); MJA 2013;198(4) consensus warfarin reversal guidelines)_
> - **[medium]** Factor Xa inhibitors (ap/rivaroxaban): andexanet alfa (limited Aus availability) or Prothrombinex. Reinforced in australianSpecifics: 'Andexanet alfa for Xa-inhibitor reversal has limited Australian availability/PBS access; Prothrombinex is the pragmatic local choice.' → State that andexanet alfa is no longer provisionally registered in Australia (registration lapsed May 2026, removed from ARTG). For Xa-inhibitor major bleeding the practical Australian approach is cessation, PCC (Prothrombinex-VF, transitioning to Beriplex 4F-PCC) 25-50 IU/kg, tranexamic acid, supportive care; consider activated charcoal if recent ingestion. Source: TGA news article 'Lapse of provisional registration for Andexxa (andexanet alfa)', 2026; CATAG andexanet alfa guidance 2024. _(TGA: Lapse of provisional registration for Andexxa (andexanet alfa), 2026; CATAG Andexanet Alfa guidance Oct 2024)_
> - **[medium]** HIT: Stop ALL heparin, start a non-heparin anticoagulant (e.g. argatroban/danaparoid/fondaparinux)... exam angle: stop heparin, start danaparoid/fondaparinux. → In Australia the practical non-heparin anticoagulants for HIT are danaparoid (first-line) and fondaparinux; bivalirudin in cardiac/ECMO settings. Argatroban is NOT TGA-registered (Special Access Scheme only). Reference the THANZ HIT consensus statement (Thrombosis and Haemostasis Society of Australia and New Zealand). Source: THANZ HIT consensus statement, MJA 2019;210(11); Qld Health HIT guideline 2022. _(THANZ HIT consensus statement MJA 2019;210(11); Queensland Health HIT guideline 2022)_
> - **[low]** TTP... Do NOT transfuse platelets - urgent plasma exchange (PLEX) + steroids. → Add: front-line immune TTP = urgent PLEX + corticosteroids + caplacizumab, with rituximab to address the underlying ADAMTS13 autoantibody. ADAMTS13 activity <10% confirms the diagnosis. Source: 2025 focused update ISTH guidelines for management of TTP, J Thromb Haemost 2025. _(2025 focused update of ISTH guidelines for TTP, J Thromb Haemost 2025 (PMID 40533296))_
> - **[low]** Febrile neutropenia threshold: ... temp >=38.0C (or >=38.3 once); eTG empirical FN: piperacillin-tazobactam 4.5 g IV q6-8h. → Use the Australian fever definition: single temp >=38.5C or >=38.0C persisting over 1 hour, with neutrophils <0.5 (or <1.0 and falling). eTG: piperacillin-tazobactam 4.5 g IV q8h (q6h if severe), first dose within 60 min; add vancomycin/gentamicin per shock/line/resistance. Source: eTG Therapeutic Guidelines, Antibiotic / Febrile neutropenia. _(eTG Therapeutic Guidelines (Antibiotic): Febrile neutropenia)_
> 
> _Gaps flagged & folded in:_ Caplacizumab and rituximab now belong in the front-line TTP algorithm in Australia (PLEX + steroids + caplacizumab + rituximab); ADAMTS13 activity <10% with inhibitor confirms immune TTP. The tutorial stops at 'PLEX + steroids'.; Australia/NZ 2025 transition from 3-factor PCC (Prothrombinex-VF) to 4-factor PCC (Beriplex) for warfarin/Xa reversal - a current, high-yield Australian-specific change worth an explicit callout.; Idarucizumab (Praxbind) detail: it is TGA-registered for dabigatran reversal - the tutorial mentions it but should note it is the specific reversal agent and that dabigatran (renally cleared) is the DOAC to avoid in CrCl <30.; DOAC renal cut-offs deserve numbers: apixaban dose-reduce 2.5 mg BD if 2 of (age >=80, weight <=60 kg, creatinine >=133); avoid all DOACs if CrCl <25-30 (apixaban sometimes used with caution); dabigatran contraindicated CrCl <30. Tutorial gives only a vague '<30 caution'.; Andexanet alfa registration lapse (May 2026) should be stated explicitly as a recent Australian change rather than 'limited availability'.; Iron deficiency: oral iron is first-line in Australia, but IV ferric carboxymaltose (Ferinject) is widely used and PBS-relevant for intolerance/malabsorption/ongoing loss/CKD - a common Australian exam point absent from the tutorial.; Coeliac disease as a leading cause of IDA in younger Australian adults (serology + duodenal biopsy) - the GI workup section mentions it only in cross-cuts, not the main text.; HIT: 4Ts score components and the platelet-transfusion caveat (permitted only for active bleeding/high-risk procedure, not prophylactically) could be made explicit; THANZ consensus is the Australian reference.; G6PD deficiency and hereditary spherocytosis as named haemolysis causes relevant to Australia's migrant populations (alongside thalassaemia) - only thalassaemia is emphasised.; JAK2 V617F and the myeloproliferative neoplasms (polycythaemia vera, essential thrombocythaemia, primary myelofibrosis) - a common haematology exam cluster entirely absent; PV management includes venesection to Hct <0.45 plus aspirin.; CLL/lymphoma basics and the warm AIHA association with CLL/SLE could anchor the 'destruction' axis better.; Massive transfusion: confirm tranexamic acid dosing (1 g over 10 min then 1 g over 8 h, within 3 h of trauma per CRASH-2/Australian MTP templates) and mention hypocalcaemia/hyperkalaemia/hypothermia complications of massive transfusion.

### Applied MCQs

**Q1.** A 34-year-old woman of Maltese background is referred from her Geelong GP for lifelong mild anaemia. She is well, with no menorrhagia and a vegetarian-inclusive diet. FBC: Hb 108 g/L, MCV 68 fL, RBC 5.9 ×10^12/L, RDW normal. Ferritin 95 µg/L, transferrin saturation 32%. CRP <5.

   - A. Iron deficiency anaemia — start oral ferrous sulfate and arrange gastroscopy/colonoscopy
   - B. Anaemia of chronic disease — investigate for occult inflammation
   - C. Beta-thalassaemia trait — order haemoglobin electrophoresis for HbA2
   - D. Sideroblastic anaemia — check lead level and order a bone marrow biopsy
   - E. Lead poisoning — arrange chelation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Beta-thalassaemia trait — order haemoglobin electrophoresis for HbA2**

The signature here is a markedly low MCV (68) that is disproportionate to a only mildly reduced Hb, a HIGH RBC count (5.9), normal RDW, and entirely normal iron studies (ferritin 95, saturation 32%). Mentzer index (MCV/RBC = 68/5.9 ≈ 11.5, <13) points to thalassaemia trait, confirmed by raised HbA2 on electrophoresis. A is the seductive trap because microcytosis screams iron deficiency, but the normal ferritin/saturation excludes it and there is no indication for GI scoping in a young woman with a clear non-iron cause. B fails because ACD usually gives a normocytic/mildly microcytic picture with LOW transferrin saturation and a NORMAL/low RBC count, plus CRP is normal here. D and E are red herrings with no supporting features.

_Teaching point:_ Microcytosis with a disproportionately high RBC count, low Mentzer index and NORMAL iron studies = thalassaemia trait, not iron deficiency — do not scope the gut or start iron.
</details>

**Q2.** A 52-year-old woman presents to a Sydney ED with 2 days of confusion and bruising. Bloods: Hb 78 g/L, platelets 14 ×10^9/L, blood film shows abundant schistocytes, LDH 1450 U/L, bilirubin raised, haptoglobin undetectable. PT, APTT and fibrinogen are all NORMAL. Creatinine mildly raised, temp 37.9°C. DAT negative.

   - A. Transfuse platelets to a target >50 before any procedure
   - B. Commence broad-spectrum antibiotics for presumed DIC and replace fibrinogen
   - C. Urgent plasma exchange with corticosteroids, and add caplacizumab
   - D. Start unfractionated heparin for a microvascular thrombotic process
   - E. Give IV immunoglobulin and observe

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Urgent plasma exchange with corticosteroids, and add caplacizumab**

Microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia (schistocytes, high LDH, low haptoglobin) + severe thrombocytopenia + NORMAL coagulation + neuro/renal features = TTP (ADAMTS13 <10%). The modern Australian front-line is PLEX + corticosteroids + caplacizumab, with rituximab for the autoantibody. A is the deadliest answer: platelet transfusion fuels microvascular thrombosis and can precipitate stroke/death — platelets are withheld unless life-threatening bleeding. B describes DIC, excluded here by NORMAL PT/APTT/fibrinogen. D is wrong — anticoagulation does not address the platelet microthrombi and risks bleeding with platelets of 14. E is inadequate as monotherapy.

_Teaching point:_ MAHA + thrombocytopenia + NORMAL coags = TTP: start PLEX/steroids/caplacizumab urgently and never reflexively transfuse platelets.
</details>

**Q3.** A 68-year-old man on warfarin for AF (target INR 2–3) presents to a regional Victorian ED with melaena and haematemesis. He is tachycardic and hypotensive. INR is 6.8.

   - A. Hold warfarin and give 5 mg oral vitamin K, then recheck INR in 24 hours
   - B. Fresh frozen plasma 4 units alone
   - C. Prothrombinex-VF 50 IU/kg plus IV vitamin K 5–10 mg, with FFP only if a 3-factor PCC leaves residual factor VII concern
   - D. Idarucizumab 5 g IV
   - E. Andexanet alfa bolus and infusion

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Prothrombinex-VF 50 IU/kg plus IV vitamin K 5–10 mg, with FFP only if a 3-factor PCC leaves residual factor VII concern**

This is life-threatening warfarin-associated haemorrhage: reverse with PCC plus IV vitamin K immediately. Note the corrected Australian detail: Prothrombinex-VF is a THREE-factor PCC (II, IX, X, low VII), so FFP may be co-administered to cover factor VII in major bleeding — and Australia/NZ are transitioning to 4F-PCC (Beriplex), which removes that need. A is dangerously slow for active major bleeding. B (FFP alone) is inferior to PCC and is not the standard of care for rapid reversal. D (idarucizumab) reverses dabigatran, not warfarin. E is wrong on two counts: andexanet targets Xa inhibitors, and its provisional Australian registration lapsed in May 2026.

_Teaching point:_ Warfarin major bleed = PCC + IV vitamin K now; remember Prothrombinex-VF is a 3-factor PCC (II, IX, X) with Australia transitioning to 4F-PCC (Beriplex).
</details>

**Q4.** A 24-year-old man receiving induction chemotherapy for AML is reviewed on the haematology ward in a Brisbane hospital on day 9. Temp 38.6°C, BP 118/72, HR 92. Neutrophils 0.2 ×10^9/L. He has a tunnelled central line that looks clean.

   - A. Take blood cultures and wait for results before starting antibiotics
   - B. Piperacillin-tazobactam 4.5 g IV after cultures, given within 60 minutes
   - C. Piperacillin-tazobactam plus vancomycin plus gentamicin as initial triple therapy
   - D. Oral amoxicillin-clavulanate and discharge with GP follow-up
   - E. Paracetamol and reassess the temperature in 4 hours

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Piperacillin-tazobactam 4.5 g IV after cultures, given within 60 minutes**

Neutrophils <0.5 + fever ≥38.5 = febrile neutropenia, a time-critical emergency. eTG: piperacillin-tazobactam 4.5 g IV, first dose within 60 minutes, taking cultures but NOT waiting for results. A breaches the 60-minute rule. C over-treats: vancomycin and gentamicin are escalation agents reserved for septic shock, suspected line sepsis (this line looks clean), MRSA risk or resistant gram-negatives — not routine first-line, and he is haemodynamically stable. D is unsafe oral/outpatient management in a high-risk inpatient. E delays life-saving antibiotics.

_Teaching point:_ Febrile neutropenia = pip-taz IV within 60 minutes after (not after the results of) cultures; reserve vancomycin/gentamicin for shock, line sepsis or resistance.
</details>

**Q5.** A 70-year-old woman is on a treatment-dose heparin infusion for a PE. On day 7 her platelet count falls from a baseline of 245 to 95 ×10^9/L. She develops a swollen, painful right calf with new Doppler-confirmed DVT. There is no bleeding.

   - A. Transfuse platelets and continue heparin
   - B. Stop heparin and start warfarin immediately
   - C. Stop all heparin and start danaparoid or fondaparinux
   - D. Stop heparin, start argatroban as the registered Australian first-line agent
   - E. Reduce the heparin infusion rate and recheck platelets in 24 hours

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Stop all heparin and start danaparoid or fondaparinux**

A >50% platelet fall at day 5–10 of heparin with NEW thrombosis (not bleeding) is HIT — calculate the 4Ts score. Management: stop ALL heparin and start a non-heparin anticoagulant. The Australian (THANZ) agents are danaparoid (traditional first-line) and fondaparinux. A is lethal: platelets fuel thrombosis and heparin must stop. B is wrong — warfarin acutely drops protein C and can cause venous limb gangrene/skin necrosis; defer until platelets recover. D names argatroban, which is NOT TGA-registered (Special Access Scheme only) — the seductive UK/US answer. E continues the culprit drug.

_Teaching point:_ HIT causes thrombosis: stop all heparin, start danaparoid/fondaparinux (the Australian agents), never give platelets or start warfarin first — argatroban is not registered here.
</details>

**Q6.** A 74-year-old man presents to a Perth GP with 6 weeks of progressive lower back pain and fatigue. Bloods: Hb 92 g/L, corrected calcium 2.92 mmol/L, creatinine 198 µmol/L, ESR 110 mm/hr. Blood film shows rouleaux. A whole-body bone scan is reported as normal.

   - A. Reassure — the normal bone scan excludes bony malignancy
   - B. Order serum protein electrophoresis, serum free light chains and a skeletal survey/low-dose whole-body CT
   - C. Arrange a DEXA scan and start a bisphosphonate for osteoporosis
   - D. Diagnose primary hyperparathyroidism and request a sestamibi scan
   - E. Request prostate-specific antigen and a bone biopsy of the most painful vertebra

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Order serum protein electrophoresis, serum free light chains and a skeletal survey/low-dose whole-body CT**

CRAB (hyperCalcaemia, Renal impairment, Anaemia, Bone pain) plus rouleaux and a very high ESR is multiple myeloma until proven otherwise. The pivotal trap is A: myeloma bone disease is purely LYTIC and bone-scan NEGATIVE (osteoblastic activity is needed to light up a bone scan), so a normal bone scan does NOT reassure — image with skeletal survey, low-dose whole-body CT or MRI. Confirm with SPEP and serum free light chains. C, D and E miss the unifying paraprotein diagnosis; hypercalcaemia here is myeloma-driven, not PTH-driven.

_Teaching point:_ In suspected myeloma a NORMAL bone scan is expected, not reassuring — lytic lesions are bone-scan negative; order SPEP, free light chains and a skeletal survey/low-dose CT.
</details>

**Q7.** A 31-year-old woman who is 26 weeks pregnant presents to a Melbourne ED with pleuritic chest pain and dyspnoea. CTPA confirms a segmental pulmonary embolus. She is haemodynamically stable.

   - A. Apixaban 10 mg BD for 7 days then 5 mg BD
   - B. Rivaroxaban 15 mg BD then 20 mg daily
   - C. Warfarin with LMWH bridging
   - D. Therapeutic-dose enoxaparin (LMWH)
   - E. Unfractionated heparin infusion lifelong

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Therapeutic-dose enoxaparin (LMWH)**

In pregnancy the anticoagulant of choice for VTE is therapeutic LMWH (enoxaparin): it does not cross the placenta and is safe for the fetus. A and B (DOACs) are CONTRAINDICATED in pregnancy — they are the seductive default for any other adult PE, which is exactly why they are the trap. C (warfarin) is teratogenic, especially in the first trimester, and crosses the placenta causing fetal bleeding/embryopathy. E is wrong: an indefinite UFH infusion is impractical and inappropriate as definitive therapy.

_Teaching point:_ Match the anticoagulant to the indication: pregnancy and cancer-associated VTE favour LMWH; DOACs are the default ONLY when no exception (pregnancy, mechanical valve, APS, severe renal impairment) applies.
</details>

**Q8.** A 58-year-old man receives a packed red cell transfusion for symptomatic anaemia at a rural Australian hospital. Two hours into the second unit he becomes acutely dyspnoeic and hypoxic (SpO2 88%). BP 168/95, JVP elevated, bibasal crackles. CXR shows bilateral interstitial infiltrates.

   - A. TRALI — stop transfusion, supportive ventilation, avoid diuretics
   - B. TACO — stop transfusion, sit up, give IV frusemide and oxygen
   - C. Acute haemolytic reaction — stop transfusion and recheck the patient's ABO group
   - D. Anaphylaxis — give IM adrenaline
   - E. Febrile non-haemolytic reaction — slow the transfusion and give paracetamol

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. TACO — stop transfusion, sit up, give IV frusemide and oxygen**

Hypertension, raised JVP and a volume-overload picture point to TACO (transfusion-associated circulatory overload), which responds to stopping the transfusion, upright posture, oxygen and diuresis. The key discriminator from TRALI is the filling pressures: TACO has HIGH filling pressures (raised JVP, hypertension); TRALI has hypoxia with NORMAL/LOW filling pressures and you would NOT diurese (A is the seductive mirror-image trap). C requires fever/loin pain/hypotension and a clerical error. D lacks urticaria/wheeze/hypotension. E does not explain the hypoxia, infiltrates and overload signs.

_Teaching point:_ Post-transfusion respiratory distress: raised JVP/hypertension = TACO (diurese); hypoxia with normal/low filling pressures = TRALI (support, do not diurese).
</details>

### Schema recall — "What is your schema for X?"

**What is your schema for the approach to anaemia?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Two forks, in order. FORK 1 — reticulocyte count: LOW retics = underproduction (marrow failure/infiltration, substrate lack, renal/EPO, ACD); HIGH retics = loss or destruction (bleeding or haemolysis — run a haemolysis screen). FORK 2 — MCV: MICROCYTIC (<80) = iron deficiency vs thalassaemia trait vs ACD, discriminated by iron studies; NORMOCYTIC = ACD, renal, early marrow failure, acute blood loss/haemolysis; MACROCYTIC (>100) = megaloblastic (B12/folate, hypersegmented neutrophils) vs non-megaloblastic (alcohol, liver, hypothyroid, myelodysplasia, reticulocytosis). Then act: any adult with IDA needs a GI source workup (including coeliac serology in younger adults); B12 with neuro signs is replaced BEFORE folate.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Students jump straight to MCV and skip reticulocytes, so they miss that a normocytic anaemia with high retics is haemolysis. The second classic break: calling a normal ferritin 'not iron deficient' in an inflamed patient — ferritin is an acute-phase reactant, so use transferrin saturation <20%.
</details>

**What is your schema for a microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia with thrombocytopenia (the schistocyte + low platelet picture)?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Confirm MAHA: schistocytes on film + high LDH + low haptoglobin + raised unconjugated bilirubin, with thrombocytopenia and a NEGATIVE DAT (mechanical, not immune). Then split by COAGULATION PROFILE: NORMAL PT/APTT/fibrinogen → TTP (ADAMTS13 <10%) → urgent PLEX + corticosteroids + caplacizumab + rituximab, and do NOT transfuse platelets. DERANGED coags with low fibrinogen and high D-dimer → DIC → treat the underlying cause and replace products. Renal-dominant in a child with bloody diarrhoea → HUS (Shiga toxin) → supportive. Pregnancy/HTN/deranged LFTs → consider HELLP/pre-eclampsia.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The exam attacks at two points: (1) reflexively transfusing platelets in TTP — this fuels thrombosis and can kill; (2) failing to use the coagulation profile to separate TTP (normal coags) from DIC (deranged coags) — they look identical on the film but diverge entirely in management.
</details>

**What is your schema for warfarin reversal?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Stratify by bleeding and INR. MAJOR/LIFE-THREATENING bleed (any INR): Prothrombinex-VF (3-factor PCC, II/IX/X) 25–50 IU/kg, 50 IU/kg if life-threatening, PLUS IV vitamin K 5–10 mg — add FFP if residual factor VII is a concern with a 3F-PCC (and note Australia/NZ are moving to 4F-PCC Beriplex which removes this need). INR high, NO bleed: INR 4.5–10 hold doses ± low-dose oral vitamin K; INR >10 give oral vitamin K and hold. Minor bleeding: vitamin K + hold, consider PCC if significant.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Two examiner traps: (1) calling Prothrombinex-VF a 4-factor PCC — it is a 3-factor PCC, the FFP co-administration caveat exists precisely because of low factor VII; (2) giving FFP ALONE for major bleeding, which is slower and inferior to PCC + vitamin K. Also: forgetting vitamin K (PCC alone wears off and INR rebounds).
</details>

**What is your schema for thrombocytopenia in a patient on heparin (suspected HIT)?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Apply the 4Ts: Thrombocytopenia magnitude (>50% fall scores highest), Timing (day 5–10, or rapid if recent heparin exposure), Thrombosis (new arterial/venous clot), and oTher causes excluded. Intermediate/high probability → STOP all heparin (including flushes/LMWH), START a non-heparin anticoagulant — in Australia danaparoid (first-line) or fondaparinux (bivalirudin in cardiac/ECMO). Do NOT transfuse platelets (only for active bleeding/high-risk procedure). Do NOT start warfarin until platelets recover (≥150) due to venous limb gangrene/skin necrosis risk. Confirm with anti-PF4/heparin antibody and functional assay.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Examiners attack with the impulse to (1) transfuse platelets because the count is low — wrong, HIT is a prothrombotic state; (2) start warfarin early — wrong, risks limb gangrene; and (3) name argatroban as first-line — wrong in Australia, where it is Special Access Scheme only and danaparoid/fondaparinux are the practical agents.
</details>

**What is your schema for febrile neutropenia?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

DEFINE: neutrophils <0.5 (or <1.0 and falling) PLUS single temp ≥38.5°C or ≥38.0°C sustained over 1 hour. ACT within 60 minutes: take blood cultures (peripheral + each lumen) but do NOT wait — give piperacillin-tazobactam 4.5 g IV (q8h, q6h if severe). ESCALATE by risk: add vancomycin for suspected line sepsis/MRSA/skin-soft tissue/septic shock; add gentamicin and/or switch to a carbapenem for septic shock or resistant gram-negative risk. Assess with MASCC score for low-risk outpatient candidates. Source the fever, but never let the workup delay the first dose.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The break is timing and agent: waiting for culture results before antibiotics (must be within 60 minutes), and either under-treating (oral/discharge in a high-risk inpatient) or over-treating (routine triple therapy when stable). Vancomycin is escalation, not default.
</details>

**What is your schema for the time-critical haematology emergencies (the 'tempo' layer)?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Scan every haem stem for an overlaid emergency demanding action BEFORE diagnosis is confirmed: TTP (MAHA + low platelets + normal coags → PLEX/steroids/caplacizumab, no platelets); FEBRILE NEUTROPENIA (neutropenia + fever → pip-taz within 60 min); TUMOUR LYSIS (post-chemo bulky disease, high K/phosphate/urate, low Ca → hydration + rasburicase, avoid potassium); HIT (>50% platelet fall day 5–10 + thrombosis → stop heparin, danaparoid/fondaparinux); APML/acute leukaemia with DIC (blasts ± Auer rods, bleeding → ATRA immediately on suspicion); MASSIVE HAEMORRHAGE (MTP ~1:1:1, tranexamic acid 1 g within 3 h of trauma).

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Students lock onto reaching the diagnosis and miss that the mark is for the time-critical action. The hardest version embeds the syndrome in a long stem (e.g. confusion blamed on sepsis when it is TTP), so anchor on the trigger features, not the headline complaint.
</details>

**What is your schema for iron studies interpretation in microcytic anaemia?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Read three numbers as a story of supply. FERRITIN = stores (but an acute-phase reactant — falsely normal/high in inflammation). TRANSFERRIN/TIBC = the truck, which UP-regulates when iron-starved. TRANSFERRIN SATURATION = iron actually reaching the marrow. IRON DEFICIENCY: ferritin LOW, transferrin/TIBC HIGH, saturation LOW (<20%). ACD: ferritin NORMAL/HIGH, transferrin LOW, saturation low-normal (hepcidin sequestration). THALASSAEMIA TRAIT: iron studies NORMAL/high, disproportionately low MCV, high RBC, raised HbA2. Then treat: oral iron first-line in Australia, IV ferric carboxymaltose (Ferinject) for intolerance/malabsorption/ongoing loss/CKD; and investigate the GI tract in any adult with IDA.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The break is the inflamed patient with a 'normal' ferritin who is genuinely iron deficient — use the transferrin saturation, not the ferritin. Second break: mistaking thalassaemia trait (normal iron studies, high RBC) for iron deficiency and starting unnecessary iron/endoscopy.
</details>

### Distractor drills — "What's the deadliest distractor for X?"

**A patient has schistocytes, platelets of 15, fever and confusion. What is the deadliest distractor?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Transfuse platelets to raise the count before they have a major bleed.
- _Why it tempts you:_ The platelet count is alarmingly low and the patient is bruising — every reflex says 'replace the missing component', and most thrombocytopenias do get platelets.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ In TTP the platelets are being consumed into microvascular thrombi; adding platelets pours fuel on the fire and can precipitate stroke, MI or death. Treatment is PLEX + steroids + caplacizumab, withholding platelets unless there is life-threatening bleeding.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **A NORMAL coagulation profile (PT/APTT/fibrinogen) with MAHA + thrombocytopenia defines TTP — that single normal-coags finding flips the management from 'transfuse' to 'never transfuse, start PLEX'.**

**A microcytic anaemia in a young adult of Mediterranean or South-East Asian background. What is the most seductive distractor?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Diagnose iron deficiency and start oral iron (± arrange endoscopy).
- _Why it tempts you:_ Low MCV is the textbook hallmark of iron deficiency, and iron deficiency is genuinely the commonest microcytic anaemia, so the pattern-match is strong.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ In thalassaemia trait the iron studies are normal/high; giving iron is futile (and iron overload is a long-term risk), and endoscopy is an unnecessary invasive workup. The microcytosis is structural, not from iron lack.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Normal/high ferritin and transferrin saturation with a HIGH RBC count and a Mentzer index <13 — disproportionate microcytosis with preserved iron stores breaks the iron-deficiency trap.**

**A patient on warfarin with a major GI bleed and INR 7. What is the most seductive wrong reversal?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Give FFP (4 units) as the reversal agent, or rely on vitamin K alone.
- _Why it tempts you:_ FFP 'replaces clotting factors' and is widely available; vitamin K 'reverses warfarin', so each feels like a complete answer.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ FFP is slow, volume-heavy and gives sub-therapeutic factor levels compared with PCC; vitamin K alone takes 6–12 hours to work — neither rapidly reverses life-threatening bleeding. The standard is PCC (Prothrombinex-VF) PLUS IV vitamin K together.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **'Major/life-threatening bleed' is the trigger word — it mandates PCC + IV vitamin K immediately; FFP is only an adjunct (notably to cover factor VII with the 3-factor Prothrombinex-VF) or a fallback when PCC is unavailable.**

**Suspected HIT with thrombocytopenia. What is the most seductive distractor agent/action?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Start argatroban (or transfuse platelets, or switch straight to warfarin).
- _Why it tempts you:_ Argatroban is the canonical HIT answer in UK/US textbooks and question banks; transfusing platelets feels right with a low count; warfarin seems like sensible ongoing anticoagulation.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Argatroban is NOT TGA-registered in Australia (Special Access Scheme only), so it is the wrong best-answer locally. Platelet transfusion fuels thrombosis. Early warfarin drops protein C and causes venous limb gangrene/skin necrosis.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **In an Australian stem the registered non-heparin anticoagulants are danaparoid (first-line) and fondaparinux — choosing the locally available agent, and deferring warfarin until platelets recover, breaks all three traps.**

**An older patient with back pain, anaemia, hypercalcaemia and renal failure, and a NORMAL bone scan. What is the most seductive distractor?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Use the normal bone scan to reassure/exclude bony malignancy.
- _Why it tempts you:_ A bone scan is the intuitive test for bony pathology, and a normal result feels reassuring — it is easy to assume 'normal imaging = no bone disease'.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Myeloma lesions are purely lytic; a bone scan detects osteoBLASTIC reaction, which myeloma lacks, so it is characteristically NEGATIVE. A normal scan does not exclude (and is expected in) myeloma.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **The CRAB constellation with rouleaux/high ESR plus the specific fact that myeloma is bone-scan negative — image instead with skeletal survey/low-dose whole-body CT/MRI and order SPEP + free light chains.**

**Respiratory distress 2 hours into a transfusion. What is the deadliest mirror-image distractor?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Call it TRALI and give supportive care while avoiding diuretics.
- _Why it tempts you:_ TRALI and TACO both present with post-transfusion hypoxia and bilateral CXR infiltrates, and TRALI is the 'exam-favourite' answer, so it is easy to pick reflexively.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ If the picture is actually TACO (volume overload), withholding diuresis and treating it as TRALI lets the patient stay overloaded — the correct move (frusemide, upright, oxygen) is omitted.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Filling pressures: raised JVP + hypertension = TACO (diurese); hypoxia with NORMAL/LOW filling pressures = TRALI (support, do not diurese).**

**A young adult with iron-deficiency anaemia and no obvious bleeding source. What is the most seductive distractor in the workup?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Attribute it to diet/menstruation and simply start iron without further investigation.
- _Why it tempts you:_ Dietary or menstrual iron loss is common and plausible, and starting iron 'treats the problem', so it feels efficient and reasonable.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ This misses occult GI pathology — in younger Australian adults coeliac disease is a leading cause of IDA, and malignancy/angiodysplasia must be excluded in older adults; iron alone treats the number, not the cause.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Adult IDA mandates a source search — coeliac serology (± duodenal biopsy) in younger adults and endoscopic GI workup in men/postmenopausal women — before settling on a benign explanation.**

### High-end review pearls

- If you see schistocytes + low platelets + NORMAL coags, think TTP and reach for PLEX, not platelets; if the coags are deranged with low fibrinogen, think DIC instead. The coagulation profile is the entire fork.
- Aunt-minnie: blasts with Auer rods + DIC/bleeding in a young patient = APML (AML M3, t(15;17)) — start ATRA immediately on clinical suspicion, before cytogenetics confirm, because the bleeding kills first.
- Australian product trap: Prothrombinex-VF is a THREE-factor PCC (II, IX, X, trace VII) — not a 4-factor PCC. Australia/NZ are transitioning to 4F-PCC (Beriplex) in 2025, which removes the need for co-administered FFP. Knowing the factor count is the higher-tier mark.
- Andexanet alfa is effectively off the Australian table for 2026 — its provisional TGA registration LAPSED in May 2026 and it was removed from the ARTG. For Xa-inhibitor major bleeding: stop the drug, give PCC (Prothrombinex-VF → Beriplex), tranexamic acid, supportive care; charcoal if very recent ingestion.
- HIT agent trap: argatroban is the textbook answer but is NOT registered in Australia (SAS only). The Australian (THANZ) agents are danaparoid (first-line) and fondaparinux; bivalirudin in cardiac/ECMO. Never platelets, never warfarin until platelets recover.
- Modern TTP triplet is PLEX + corticosteroids + caplacizumab (+ rituximab for the ADAMTS13 autoantibody); ADAMTS13 activity <10% confirms immune TTP. 'PLEX + steroids' alone is now the lower-tier answer.
- Ferritin is an acute-phase reactant: in an inflamed patient (IBD, RA, infection) a 'normal' ferritin can still be iron deficiency — use transferrin saturation <20%. The IBD patient with ferritin 80 and saturation 8% is iron deficient AND has ACD; treat the iron.
- Never-forget sequence: in B12 deficiency with neuro signs (SACD), replace B12 BEFORE folate — giving folate first can precipitate/worsen subacute combined degeneration.
- Myeloma is bone-scan NEGATIVE because the lesions are lytic (no osteoblastic reaction); a normal bone scan is expected, not reassuring. Image with skeletal survey/low-dose whole-body CT/MRI.
- If you see microcytosis with a HIGH RBC count and normal iron studies in a Mediterranean/SE-Asian patient, think thalassaemia trait — do not start iron or scope the gut. Mentzer index <13 is your quick discriminator.
- Match the anticoagulant to the exception, not the rule: DOAC is default, but pregnancy and cancer-associated VTE → LMWH; mechanical valve and antiphospholipid syndrome → warfarin only; dabigatran is the DOAC to avoid in CrCl <30 (renally cleared, reversed by idarucizumab).
- DOAC numbers worth carrying: apixaban dose-reduce to 2.5 mg BD if 2 of (age ≥80, weight ≤60 kg, creatinine ≥133 µmol/L); avoid DOACs broadly if CrCl <25–30; dabigatran contraindicated CrCl <30.
- Febrile neutropenia is a 60-MINUTE disease: cultures then pip-taz 4.5 g IV within the hour — do not wait for results. Vancomycin/gentamicin are escalation for line sepsis/MRSA/shock/resistant gram-negatives, not the default.
- Tumour lysis aunt-minnie: post-chemo bulky/high-WCC malignancy with high K, high phosphate, high urate and LOW calcium → hydrate + rasburicase (high risk) or allopurinol (lower risk); avoid giving potassium and watch for AKI/arrhythmia.
- Massive transfusion never-forget: MTP ratio ~1:1:1 RBC:FFP:platelets, tranexamic acid 1 g over 10 min then 1 g over 8 h within 3 h of trauma; watch for the lethal metabolic complications — hypocalcaemia, hyperkalaemia and hypothermia.
- Australian transfusion governance one-liner: restrictive thresholds per National Blood Authority Patient Blood Management (Hb 70 g/L, or 80 in cardiac disease); reactions reported through Lifeblood haemovigilance.
- Haemolysis in Australia's migrant populations: beyond thalassaemia, remember G6PD deficiency (episodic haemolysis with bite cells/Heinz bodies after oxidant drugs/fava beans/infection) and hereditary spherocytosis (DAT-negative, spherocytes, splenomegaly, raised MCHC).
- MPN cluster examiners love: JAK2 V617F links polycythaemia vera, essential thrombocythaemia and primary myelofibrosis. PV management = venesection to Hct <0.45 plus low-dose aspirin; suspect PV with itch after a hot shower (aquagenic pruritus) and thrombosis.
- Acute haemolytic (ABO mismatch) transfusion reaction = fever + loin/back pain + hypotension during the transfusion: STOP immediately, IV fluids, and check for a clerical/labelling error — it is a 'wrong blood in tube/patient' event until proven otherwise.
- Iron deficiency treatment in Australia: oral iron is first-line, but IV ferric carboxymaltose (Ferinject) is the go-to for intolerance, malabsorption, ongoing loss or CKD — a frequent local exam point.

> **Independent item audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[high]** MCQ5 (HIT) — option D 'argatroban as the registered Australian first-line agent' and explanation/distractor claiming argatroban is NOT TGA-registered / SAS-only / 'wrong best-answer locally': The factual basis for marking D wrong is shaky and partly incorrect. The THANZ 2019 consensus statement (MJA) explicitly lists argatroban as one of four acceptable non-heparin anticoagulants for HIT (danaparoid, argatroban, fondaparinux, bivalirudin) and specifically PREFERS argatroban in severe renal impairment. Multiple Australian hospital medicine guidelines exist for argatroban in HIT (SESLHD SESLHDMG/116, Qld Health 2022 HIT guideline). The blanket claim 'argatroban is the canonical UK/US answer and the wrong best-answer locally' overstates the case. Clinically, this patient has a NEW DVT (HITT) — a direct thrombin inhibitor like argatroban is a guideline-endorsed treatment, so D is arguably also clinically correct, undermining single-best-answer integrity. → Either (a) reword D so it is unambiguously wrong on a non-contested basis (e.g. 'continue heparin but add argatroban'), or (b) recast the discriminator: the defensible teaching point is that danaparoid is the practical THANZ first-line agent and argatroban is reserved for renal impairment/critical care — NOT that argatroban is unavailable or wrong. Remove/soften the 'NOT TGA-registered, SAS only' assertion unless verified against current ARTG; it is presented with false certainty. If argatroban's TGA status cannot be confirmed, do not hinge the answer on it.
> - **[medium]** MCQ3 (warfarin reversal) option C and the schema/highEndExtras on Prothrombinex-VF being a 3-factor PCC with FFP co-administration: Outdated/anachronistic for a 2026 stem. Australia transitioned from Prothrombinex-VF (3F-PCC) to Beriplex (4F-PCC) during 2024-2025 (NBA confirms Prothrombinex-VF availability only ~to mid-2024, then Beriplex P/N then Beriplex AU). MJA 2025 has updated warfarin-reversal recommendations FOR the 4F-PCC setting. Presenting Prothrombinex-VF as current standard and teaching 'add FFP to cover factor VII' is historical, not 2026 practice. Option C's wording ('with FFP only if a 3-factor PCC leaves residual factor VII concern') is also convoluted and bundles a contested rationale into the keyed answer, which is poor item-writing. → Update to 2026 reality: keyed answer should be '4F-PCC (Beriplex) 50 IU/kg + IV vitamin K 5-10 mg', with FFP only as a fallback when PCC unavailable. Drop the factor-VII/FFP caveat from the correct option (move it to a historical teaching footnote at most). The 'knowing the factor count is the higher-tier mark' framing should be reframed as historical context.
> - **[medium]** Schema 'febrile neutropenia' and MCQ4 — fever threshold defined as 'single temp ≥38.5°C or ≥38.0°C sustained over 1 hour': The ≥38.5°C single-reading threshold is non-standard. eTG / IDSA / common Australian definitions use a single oral temp ≥38.3°C (or ≥38.0°C sustained ≥1 hour). The schema's '≥38.5' is inconsistent with the literature and with itself across the document (the stem uses 38.6, which clears any threshold, so the MCQ answer is unaffected, but the schema teaches a wrong number). → Change schema definition to 'single temp ≥38.3°C, or ≥38.0°C sustained over 1 hour'. Align distractor-drill and schema wording.
> - **[low]** MCQ4 (febrile neutropenia) explanation phrase 'taking cultures but NOT waiting for results' / teachingPoint 'after (not after the results of) cultures': Minor wording clumsiness ('after (not after the results of)') that can confuse; also the pip-taz dose 4.5 g q8h is correct but eTG expresses dosing as the piperacillin component (100 mg/kg, max 4 g piperacillin = 4.5 g pip-taz) — fine, but the item could be misread as a fixed 4.5 g regardless of weight. → Reword teachingPoint to 'give pip-taz within 60 minutes; take cultures first but do not wait for results.' Optionally note dose is weight-based (max 4 g piperacillin component).
> - **[low]** highEndExtras — 'Andexanet alfa ... provisional TGA registration LAPSED in May 2026 and removed from ARTG': This is CORRECT (verified: Andexxa provisional registration lapsed 20 May 2026, removed from ARTG, AstraZeneca discontinued for commercial reasons). No error — but flagging as a fragile, time-sensitive fact that anchors MCQ3 option E. It is accurate as of the 2026-06-28 stem date. → No fix needed; retain. Just ensure this stays current in future revisions as it is a moving regulatory fact.
> - **[low]** MCQ1 (thalassaemia trait) — explanation typo 'a only mildly reduced Hb': Grammatical typo only; clinical content is sound. Mentzer index 68/5.9 = 11.5 (<13) correctly supports thalassaemia trait; normal iron studies correctly exclude IDA. Answer C is unambiguously the single best answer. → Fix typo to 'an only mildly reduced Hb'. No clinical change.
> - **[low]** MCQ7 (pregnancy PE) option A 'Apixaban 10 mg BD for 7 days then 5 mg BD': Distractor dosing detail is slightly off — apixaban VTE loading is 10 mg BD for 7 days then 5 mg BD, which is actually CORRECT dosing, so this is fine as a seductive (correctly-dosed but wrong-in-pregnancy) distractor. No error; noting it survives scrutiny as a genuine trap (correct drug regimen, wrong because pregnancy). Answer D (therapeutic LMWH) is unambiguously correct. → No fix needed.
> 
> _Quality notes:_ MCQ2 (TTP) is excellent and clean: MAHA + thrombocytopenia + NORMAL coags + neuro/renal + DAT-negative is unambiguous TTP; PLEX+steroids+caplacizumab is the correct 2026 Australian front-line; the platelet-transfusion trap (A) is genuinely the deadliest distractor, not a strawman. Single-best-answer integrity intact.; MCQ6 (myeloma, bone-scan-negative) is a strong, high-yield item — the 'normal bone scan is reassuring' trap (A) is a real and seductive cognitive error, and the CRAB + rouleaux + high ESR framing is clinically sound. Distractors C/D/E are plausible-but-clearly-inferior, not strawmen.; MCQ8 (TACO vs TRALI) is well constructed — raised JVP + hypertension cleanly discriminates TACO from TRALI, and the TRALI 'mirror-image' distractor (A) with its 'avoid diuretics' hook is a genuine deadly trap. Good item.; Overall distractor quality is high across the set — most distractors are guideline-plausible (correctly-dosed apixaban in pregnancy, FFP-alone in warfarin bleed, TRALI mirror of TACO) rather than strawmen. This is a well-above-average bank.; Main systemic weakness: over-reliance on contested/fragile Australian regulatory micro-facts (argatroban SAS-only; Prothrombinex 3F-PCC + FFP) as the load-bearing discriminator. These are the two items most likely to be wrong or out-of-date, and they are framed as 'higher-tier marks', which amplifies the risk of teaching a falsehood. Prefer discriminators grounded in stable clinical reasoning over volatile registration status.; No MCQ is too easy; difficulty is appropriately pitched at advanced trainee level. Stems are well-localised geographically and clinically without being cluttered. No ambiguous stems found except the convoluted wording inside MCQ3 option C (the keyed answer should not contain a conditional rationale).

## Rheumatology & Immunology  _(~4% of marks)_

### The one idea that unlocks the domain

> **Every rheumatology question is a triage decision: the examiner is asking "is this a must-not-miss emergency (joint, eye, kidney, lung) that needs an intervention NOW, or a chronic immune-mediated disease I manage with a pattern-recognition antibody/clinical algorithm?" — so you derive answers by first asking what tissue is about to be destroyed and how fast.**

Rheumatology MCQs feel like list-memorisation but are actually built on a single time-critical triage engine. For every stem, the highest-yield reflex is: "What irreversible end-organ damage is threatened, and what is the one action that prevents it within hours?" Septic arthritis threatens cartilage destruction in hours → aspirate then antibiotics. GCA threatens the retinal/ciliary circulation → steroids BEFORE biopsy. ANCA/anti-GBM vasculitis threatens glomeruli and alveoli → immunosuppress before titres return. This engine instantly sorts the "do it now" stems from the "investigate and pattern-match" stems (gout, RA, SLE, PMR).
