The second layer is mechanism-based pattern recognition: crystals (negatively birefringent needles = urate; positively birefringent rhomboids = calcium pyrophosphate), and antibodies as disease fingerprints (anti-CCP for RA, anti-dsDNA/Sm for SLE, anti-PR3 for GPA, anti-MPO for MPA/EGPA, anti-GBM for Goodpasture). Because antibodies map to specific organ patterns, knowing the antibody lets you predict the clinical scenario and vice versa — you never memorise pairs, you derive them. The third layer is the eTG/Australian drug algorithm, which is rules-based: acute crystal arthritis = NSAID or colchicine or steroid; never start or stop allopurinol during an attack but never interrupt it if already established. Owning these three engines — threat-triage, crystal/antibody pattern, and the rules-based drug ladder — lets you reason to the single best answer rather than recall it, which is exactly how Passmedicine-style distractors are defeated.

### High-yield tutorial

## The triage engine: what tissue dies, and how fast?

Open every stem by asking **"what irreversible damage is threatened and what is the one action in the next few hours that prevents it?"** This sorts the whole domain.

### 1. The hot joint — septic until proven otherwise

A single acutely hot, swollen, painful joint is **septic arthritis until the aspirate says otherwise**, because untreated it destroys cartilage within days and carries ~10% mortality. **Joint aspiration (arthrocentesis) BEFORE antibiotics** is the single must-not-miss action. Send synovial fluid for **Gram stain, culture, cell count, and polarised microscopy**.

Decision rules from the fluid:
- **WCC >50,000/mm³ (especially >50% neutrophils)** → treat as septic even with negative Gram stain. **Crystals and infection can coexist** — finding crystals does NOT exclude sepsis.
- **Negatively birefringent needle-shaped crystals = monosodium urate = gout** (yellow when parallel to the compensator).
- **Positively birefringent rhomboid crystals = calcium pyrophosphate = pseudogout (CPPD)**.

Empirical therapy: **flucloxacillin IV**; add vancomycin if MRSA risk. In a young sexually active patient with migratory polyarthralgia, tenosynovitis and pustules, think **disseminated gonococcal infection** → ceftriaxone. **Prosthetic joint** → refer to orthopaedics, do not aspirate blindly on the ward.

### 2. Gout — the rules-based ladder

**Acute attack (eTG):** choose ONE — **NSAID** (e.g. indomethacin/naproxen), **colchicine**, or **prednisolone** (15–30 mg/day) — based on comorbidities. **Colchicine dosing is now LOW: 1 mg stat then 0.5 mg one hour later, then stop (or 0.5 mg bd next day).** High-dose colchicine is wrong and causes diarrhoea. Use steroid/colchicine when NSAIDs are contraindicated (CKD, heart failure, anticoagulation).

**The allopurinol rules** (classic MCQ trap):
- **Do NOT start urate-lowering therapy during an acute attack** (precipitates/prolongs it) — wait ~2–4 weeks.
- **NEVER stop allopurinol the patient is already taking** during an attack.
- When you start allopurinol, **co-prescribe colchicine 0.5 mg daily prophylaxis for up to 6 months** to prevent mobilisation flares.
- **Target serum urate <0.36 mmol/L** (<0.30 if tophi). Titrate slowly.
- Check **HLA-B*5801** in high-risk groups (Han Chinese, Thai, Korean) for allopurinol hypersensitivity/SJS. **Febuxostat** is second-line (caution in CVD).

### 3. The eye-threatening engine — GCA and PMR

**Giant cell arteritis is an ophthalmic emergency.** New headache, scalp tenderness, jaw claudication, age >50, raised ESR/CRP, with visual symptoms = **start high-dose steroids IMMEDIATELY — do NOT wait for the temporal artery biopsy.** Biopsy stays positive for ~2 weeks despite steroids (skip lesions mean a negative biopsy doesn't exclude it).
- **No visual symptoms: prednisolone 40–60 mg/day.**
- **Visual loss or amaurosis: IV methylprednisolone** then high-dose oral. Add **low-dose aspirin** and PPI/bone protection.
- **PMR** = proximal shoulder/hip girdle pain and stiffness, age >50, raised ESR, **dramatic response to prednisolone 15 mg/day**. PMR and GCA overlap; always screen PMR patients for GCA symptoms.

### 4. Antibodies as fingerprints — derive, don't memorise

Antibodies map onto organ patterns:
- **Anti-CCP** → RA (specific, prognostic for erosions). **RF** sensitive but non-specific.
- **ANA** = screening gate for connective tissue disease; positive ANA then sub-type.
- **Anti-dsDNA / anti-Sm** → SLE (dsDNA tracks lupus nephritis activity; **low C3/C4** = active consumption).
- **Anti-Ro/La** → Sjögren, and **neonatal lupus/congenital heart block** in pregnancy.
- **Anti-Scl-70** → diffuse scleroderma; **anti-centromere** → limited (CREST).
- **Anti-Jo-1** → polymyositis with ILD.

### 5. Vasculitis red flags — kidney + lung = immunosuppress now

The killer combination is **renal failure + pulmonary haemorrhage/haemoptysis** (pulmonary-renal syndrome).
- **c-ANCA / anti-PR3 → GPA** (granulomatosis with polyangiitis): **saddle-nose, sinusitis, haemoptysis, glomerulonephritis**.
- **p-ANCA / anti-MPO → MPA and EGPA** (EGPA = asthma + eosinophilia + mononeuritis multiplex).
- **Anti-GBM → Goodpasture**: linear IgG on biopsy, haemoptysis + RPGN. Treatment includes **plasma exchange** plus steroids/cyclophosphamide.

**Rule: in any rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis with haemoptysis, send ANCA + anti-GBM and start pulsed methylprednisolone — don't wait for serology to act.** This closes the loop back to the triage engine: the threatened tissue (glomerulus, alveolus) dictates urgency over diagnostic certainty.

### Highest mark-per-minute principles

- **A single hot joint is septic arthritis until the aspirate proves otherwise — aspirate BEFORE antibiotics.** — The most heavily tested must-not-miss; missing it is a guaranteed fail-the-station scenario.
  - _Exam angle:_ Stem gives a hot knee with raised inflammatory markers; correct answer is 'urgent joint aspiration', not 'start antibiotics' or 'start NSAID'.
- **Crystals and infection coexist — finding urate crystals does NOT exclude septic arthritis.** — Classic distractor: stem shows crystals AND high WCC/fever to bait a 'gout' answer.
  - _Exam angle:_ Known gout patient with one very hot joint and fever — still aspirate and treat as septic.
- **In GCA, start high-dose steroids immediately and biopsy later.** — Tests whether you prioritise sight-saving treatment over diagnostic confirmation.
  - _Exam angle:_ New headache + jaw claudication + visual blurring, age 70; answer is 'commence prednisolone now', not 'arrange temporal artery biopsy first'.
- **Never start allopurinol during an acute gout attack, but never stop it if already established.** — Single most common gout MCQ trap in Australian exams.
  - _Exam angle:_ Patient on long-term allopurinol presents with a flare — continue allopurinol AND treat the flare.
- **Colchicine is now low-dose: 1 mg then 0.5 mg one hour later.** — Old high-dose regimens are wrong answers; eTG changed this.
  - _Exam angle:_ Distractors offer '0.5 mg every 2 hours until diarrhoea' — that is the outdated, incorrect option.
- **Pulmonary haemorrhage + renal failure = pulmonary-renal syndrome; send ANCA and anti-GBM and immunosuppress urgently.** — High-mortality vasculitis pattern examiners love for derivation.
  - _Exam angle:_ Haemoptysis + rising creatinine + haematuria; answer differentiates GPA (PR3) vs Goodpasture (anti-GBM, needs plasma exchange).
- **Antibody = disease fingerprint mapped to organ pattern.** — Lets you derive the diagnosis from serology or predict serology from the scenario.
  - _Exam angle:_ Anti-dsDNA + low complement = active lupus nephritis; anti-Ro in pregnancy = congenital heart block risk.
- **Low C3/C4 indicates active complement consumption in SLE.** — Distinguishes active disease and flares.
  - _Exam angle:_ Lupus patient with rising dsDNA and falling complement = flare/nephritis.
- **PMR responds dramatically to low-dose prednisolone (15 mg); GCA needs high-dose (40–60 mg).** — Dose discrimination is a frequent single-best-answer point.
  - _Exam angle:_ Proximal girdle stiffness, ESR high, no headache — answer is prednisolone 15 mg, and screen for GCA.

### Must-know numbers (Australian where they differ)

- Synovial WCC >50,000/mm³ (with neutrophil predominance) → treat as septic arthritis
- Septic arthritis mortality ~10%; affects native large joints (knee most common)
- Acute gout colchicine (eTG): 1 mg stat, then 0.5 mg 1 hour later — NOT high-dose
- Allopurinol flare prophylaxis: colchicine 0.5 mg daily for up to 6 months when initiating
- Serum urate target <0.36 mmol/L (<0.30 mmol/L if tophi present)
- Do not start urate-lowering therapy until ~2–4 weeks after the acute attack settles
- GCA without visual loss: prednisolone 40–60 mg/day; with visual loss: IV methylprednisolone
- PMR: prednisolone 15 mg/day with dramatic response within days
- GCA/PMR/temporal arteritis: age >50, ESR often >50 mm/hr, raised CRP
- Temporal artery biopsy remains positive for up to ~2 weeks after starting steroids
- HLA-B*5801 testing before allopurinol in Han Chinese/Thai/Korean (SJS/TEN risk)
- Acute lupus flare markers: rising anti-dsDNA, falling C3/C4

### Cross-topic cuts

- Nephrology: lupus nephritis (anti-dsDNA, low complement), ANCA-associated and anti-GBM rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis — overlaps directly with the renal RPGN/nephritic syndrome topic
- Respiratory: pulmonary-renal syndrome (haemoptysis + GN), EGPA presenting as adult-onset asthma + eosinophilia, ILD with anti-Jo-1/anti-Scl-70
- Ophthalmology/Neurology: GCA causing amaurosis fugax/AION and stroke — sight and brain emergency overlap
- Infectious disease: septic arthritis organisms (S. aureus, gonococcus), and immunosuppression-related infection risk from steroids/biologics (screen for latent TB, hepatitis B before TNF inhibitors)
- Obstetrics: anti-Ro/La and congenital heart block; antiphospholipid syndrome causing recurrent miscarriage and thrombosis; SLE flares in pregnancy
- Cardiology: APLS thrombosis, accelerated atherosclerosis in SLE/RA; aortitis in GCA/Takayasu
- Endocrine/Pharmacology: long-term steroid harms — osteoporosis (bone protection), hyperglycaemia, adrenal suppression — recurring in PMR/GCA management
- Haematology: anaemia of chronic disease in RA, eosinophilia in EGPA, cytopenias in SLE

### Australian-specific (where the UK answer loses you marks)

- Use eTG (Therapeutic Guidelines) drug choices and doses, not NICE — colchicine low-dose 1 mg then 0.5 mg at 1 hour is the eTG-aligned answer
- Allopurinol remains first-line urate-lowering therapy in Australia; febuxostat is second-line and PBS-restricted with CVD caution
- HLA-B*5801 screening is emphasised given Australia's large East/Southeast Asian population before allopurinol
- Gout is highly prevalent in Aboriginal, Torres Strait Islander, Māori and Pacific Islander Australians — consider earlier urate-lowering therapy and screen for renal disease
- Disseminated gonococcal arthritis is a relevant cause of acute monoarthritis in sexually active young Australians — treat with ceftriaxone and notify/contact-trace
- Biologic DMARDs (TNF inhibitors, rituximab) are PBS-authority listed and require pre-treatment screening for latent TB (QuantiFERON) and hepatitis B/C
- Rheumatology and ophthalmology referral pathways: GCA suspected → start steroids and arrange urgent temporal artery biopsy/ultrasound without delaying treatment
- Methotrexate is first-line conventional DMARD for RA in Australia, with folic acid co-prescription and monitoring of FBC/LFTs

> **Independent accuracy audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[medium]** GCA management: 'Add low-dose aspirin and PPI/bone protection.' → Do NOT routinely add aspirin in new GCA. Core GCA actions: immediate high-dose glucocorticoid (IV methylprednisolone if visual loss/amaurosis, otherwise oral prednisolone ~1 mg/kg up to 60 mg/day), urgent temporal artery biopsy and/or vascular ultrasound without delaying steroids, plus glucocorticoid-sparing/adverse-effect prophylaxis (bone protection, PPI, glucose monitoring). Aspirin only if an independent vascular indication exists. _(Cochrane review (no RCT evidence for aspirin in GCA); contemporary GCA reviews 2022-2025; Medicine Today (Australia) GCA 2025; eTG Rheumatology.)_
> - **[medium]** Vasculitis: 'Treatment includes plasma exchange plus steroids/cyclophosphamide' (for the ANCA/anti-GBM cluster); cyclophosphamide presented as the induction agent for GPA/MPA. → GPA/MPA induction: rituximab OR cyclophosphamide plus glucocorticoids (PEXIVAS reduced-dose taper). Plasma exchange is NOT routine in AAV post-PEXIVAS, reserved for severe renal disease or pulmonary haemorrhage on a case-by-case basis. Plasma exchange remains standard for anti-GBM/Goodpasture (plus steroids + cyclophosphamide). _(KDIGO 2024 ANCA-Associated Vasculitis Guideline; RAVE/RITUXVAS; PEXIVAS (NEJM); Chua et al. Internal Medicine Journal 2024 (ANZ practice).)_
> - **[low]** Allopurinol initiation: 'co-prescribe colchicine 0.5 mg daily prophylaxis for up to 6 months' / mustKnowNumbers: 'colchicine 0.5 mg daily for up to 6 months when initiating'. → Co-prescribe colchicine 500 microgram once or twice daily (renally adjusted) as flare prophylaxis when starting/up-titrating urate-lowering therapy, continued for at least 3-6 months and until target urate is reached and stable; longer if tophi or ongoing flares. NSAID or low-dose prednisolone are alternatives if colchicine contraindicated. _(eTG (Therapeutic Guidelines) Gout; Australian Prescriber 'The management of gout'; ACR 2020 / EULAR gout guidelines.)_
> - **[low]** Acute gout: 'prednisolone (15-30 mg/day)'. → Acute gout flare oral steroid: prednisolone approximately 30 mg/day (or 0.5 mg/kg) for several days, then stop/taper. Reserve 15 mg/day as the characteristic PMR dose. _(eTG Gout; ACP/ACR/EULAR acute gout corticosteroid dosing (20-35 mg/day).)_
> - **[low]** Empirical septic arthritis therapy: 'flucloxacillin IV; add vancomycin if MRSA risk.' → Per eTG: empirical IV flucloxacillin for likely S. aureus/streptococcal native-joint septic arthritis; use vancomycin if MRSA risk or severe beta-lactam allergy; add Gram-negative cover (e.g. ceftriaxone) if immunocompromised, IVDU, or Gram-negative organisms seen. Tailor to Gram stain and local susceptibility. _(eTG Antibiotic (Therapeutic Guidelines), septic arthritis topic; updated March 2025.)_
> 
> _Gaps flagged & folded in:_ GCA steroid-sparing therapy: tocilizumab (IL-6 inhibitor) is now PBS-listed in Australia for GCA and is a high-yield steroid-sparing/relapse agent (GiACTA trial). Its complete omission is a major Australian currency gap, especially as Australian rheumatologists increasingly use it for relapsing/refractory GCA and visual loss.; ANCA-associated vasculitis induction: rituximab is first-line (alongside/instead of cyclophosphamide) for remission induction in GPA/MPA, and is the preferred agent in relapsing disease and where fertility preservation matters; rituximab is also used for maintenance. The tutorial only names cyclophosphamide.; EGPA biologic therapy: mepolizumab (anti-IL-5) is a key add-on for relapsing/refractory EGPA and is increasingly examined; the tutorial treats EGPA purely as a pattern-recognition diagnosis with no management.; PEXIVAS-era plasma exchange and steroid dosing: plasma exchange is now SELECTIVE (not routine) in AAV, and reduced-dose glucocorticoid tapering is preferred - a frequently updated exam point that contradicts older 'always plasmapheresis' teaching.; RA biologic pre-screening detail: before starting TNF inhibitors/biologics, screen for latent TB (QuantiFERON-Gold/IGRA), hepatitis B and C, and consider VZV/vaccination status; the tutorial mentions TB/HBV briefly under cross-topic but should make IGRA explicit as the Australian standard.; Antiphospholipid syndrome specifics: the tutorial mentions APLS only in cross-cuts; high-yield Australian points are testing (lupus anticoagulant, anti-cardiolipin, anti-beta2-glycoprotein-I) confirmed 12 weeks apart, and that warfarin (not DOACs) is preferred for thrombotic APLS, particularly triple-positive - a common MCQ trap.; Allopurinol starting dose and renal titration: start low (e.g. 50-100 mg/day, lower in CKD) and titrate to urate target; not a fixed dose. Renal-dose colchicine and avoidance of colchicine-clarithromycin/strong CYP3A4-P-gp interactions (fatal toxicity) are high-yield Australian safety points.; Methotrexate safety counselling (Australia): once-WEEKLY dosing (fatal if taken daily - a recurring Australian quality-and-safety alert), folic acid on non-MTX days, strict contraception/teratogenicity, and pneumonitis/hepatotoxicity monitoring.; CPPD/pseudogout association screening: in younger patients with CPPD, screen for metabolic causes (haemochromatosis, hyperparathyroidism, hypomagnesaemia, hypophosphatasia) - relevant in Australia given high haemochromatosis prevalence.; Gonococcal arthritis is a notifiable infection in Australia - emphasise public health notification and partner contact tracing, and that a urethral/cervical/throat swab + NAAT plus blood cultures are needed since synovial cultures are often negative.; Gout in First Nations, Maori and Pacific Islander Australians: very high prevalence with earlier onset and renal disease - lower threshold for urate-lowering therapy and renal screening (the tutorial flags this but it warrants prominence as an Australian equity/exam point).

### Applied MCQs

**Q1.** A 68-year-old man with a background of well-controlled gout (on allopurinol 300 mg daily for 3 years) presents to a Melbourne ED with a single exquisitely hot, swollen, erythematous right knee over 24 hours. He is febrile at 38.6 degrees C. Bloods: WCC 16, CRP 180, urate 0.31 mmol/L. He cannot bear weight. What is the single most appropriate next step?

   - A. Commence oral prednisolone 30 mg daily for an acute gout flare
   - B. Urgent joint aspiration before antibiotics
   - C. Start IV flucloxacillin empirically and review in the morning
   - D. Increase allopurinol and add colchicine prophylaxis
   - E. Arrange an urgent ultrasound of the knee to confirm an effusion

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Urgent joint aspiration before antibiotics**

A single acutely hot joint with fever and high inflammatory markers is septic arthritis until the aspirate proves otherwise, because cartilage is destroyed within days and mortality is ~10%. Aspiration must precede antibiotics so culture/Gram stain/cell count/polarised microscopy are not sterilised. The known-gout history (A, D) is the seductive trap: crystals and infection coexist, and a normal urate during a flare does not exclude either gout OR sepsis. Starting antibiotics before aspiration (C) destroys diagnostic yield and delays the procedure. Ultrasound (E) confirms an effusion you already know is there and wastes time — you need the fluid analysed, not imaged.

_Teaching point:_ A single hot joint is septic until the aspirate says otherwise — aspirate BEFORE antibiotics, and a gout history never buys an exemption because crystals and sepsis coexist.
</details>

**Q2.** A 72-year-old woman presents to a Brisbane GP with a new left-sided temporal headache, scalp tenderness when brushing her hair, and pain in her jaw when chewing. This morning she had a transient curtain of greyness over her left eye that resolved. Examination is normal; ESR is 92 mm/hr. What is the single most appropriate immediate management?

   - A. Arrange an urgent temporal artery biopsy and start steroids once the result confirms the diagnosis
   - B. Commence oral prednisolone 15 mg daily and refer to rheumatology
   - C. Commence IV methylprednisolone now, then high-dose oral steroids, and arrange urgent biopsy/ultrasound without delaying treatment
   - D. Commence oral prednisolone 60 mg daily plus low-dose aspirin and a PPI
   - E. Start tocilizumab as first-line therapy and arrange temporal artery ultrasound

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Commence IV methylprednisolone now, then high-dose oral steroids, and arrange urgent biopsy/ultrasound without delaying treatment**

This is giant cell arteritis with amaurosis fugax — an ophthalmic emergency where the threatened tissue is the retinal/ciliary circulation. With visual symptoms you give IV methylprednisolone, NOT merely oral, and you NEVER delay steroids for the biopsy (which stays positive ~2 weeks and may miss skip lesions). A is the classic 'wait for the biopsy' trap. B uses the PMR dose (15 mg), which is grossly under-treating GCA. D is tempting and the right oral dose, but (i) it is oral not IV despite visual loss, and (ii) routine aspirin is no longer recommended in new GCA — no RCT evidence and bleeding risk on high-dose steroids. E: tocilizumab is a steroid-sparing/relapse agent, not first-line for acute sight-threatening GCA.

_Teaching point:_ GCA with visual symptoms = IV methylprednisolone immediately, biopsy later; do not add routine aspirin, and 15 mg is the PMR dose, not the GCA dose.
</details>

**Q3.** A 55-year-old man presents with 6 weeks of haemoptysis and progressive breathlessness, now with frank haematuria. Creatinine has risen from 90 to 410 micromol/L over two weeks. Urine microscopy shows dysmorphic red cells and red cell casts. CXR shows bilateral alveolar shadowing. Which result, if positive, would most specifically mandate plasma exchange in addition to steroids and an induction immunosuppressant?

   - A. Anti-PR3 (c-ANCA)
   - B. Anti-MPO (p-ANCA)
   - C. Anti-glomerular basement membrane antibody
   - D. Anti-dsDNA with low complement
   - E. Anti-CCP

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Anti-glomerular basement membrane antibody**

This is a pulmonary-renal syndrome. Anti-GBM (Goodpasture) gives linear IgG on biopsy and plasma exchange remains standard of care (plus steroids and cyclophosphamide) to remove pathogenic antibody. PR3 (A) and MPO (B) cause ANCA-associated vasculitis, where post-PEXIVAS plasma exchange is now SELECTIVE — reserved case-by-case for severe renal disease or pulmonary haemorrhage — not automatically mandated, and rituximab or cyclophosphamide is induction. dsDNA/low complement (D) is lupus, not this picture. Anti-CCP (E) is RA and irrelevant to pulmonary-renal syndrome. The discriminator is that anti-GBM is the one antibody where plasma exchange is unambiguously standard rather than selective.

_Teaching point:_ In pulmonary-renal syndrome, anti-GBM mandates plasma exchange; for ANCA vasculitis, PEXIVAS made plasma exchange selective, not routine.
</details>

**Q4.** A 64-year-old man with CKD stage 3 (eGFR 38), heart failure and atrial fibrillation on warfarin presents with an acute, hot, swollen first metatarsophalangeal joint. Aspiration shows negatively birefringent needle-shaped crystals and no organisms; WCC 8000. He is not on any urate-lowering therapy. What is the single most appropriate treatment for the acute attack?

   - A. Naproxen 500 mg twice daily
   - B. Colchicine 1 mg stat, then 0.5 mg one hour later
   - C. Allopurinol 300 mg daily started now
   - D. Colchicine 0.5 mg every two hours until symptoms settle or diarrhoea occurs
   - E. Prednisolone 15 mg daily for five days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Colchicine 1 mg stat, then 0.5 mg one hour later**

This is acute gout. NSAIDs (A) are contraindicated by CKD, heart failure AND anticoagulation — a triple bait. Allopurinol (C) must NOT be started during an acute attack (precipitates/prolongs it). D is the outdated high-dose colchicine regimen ('until diarrhoea') — now wrong per eTG. E uses the PMR dose; the correct acute-gout steroid dose is ~30 mg/day, so 15 mg is under-dosed. The correct answer is low-dose colchicine (1 mg then 0.5 mg at one hour), with renal dose-adjustment and awareness of CYP3A4/P-gp interactions. Prednisolone ~30 mg would also be acceptable, but at the offered dose it is wrong.

_Teaching point:_ When NSAIDs are contraindicated, use low-dose colchicine (1 mg then 0.5 mg at 1 hr) or prednisolone ~30 mg — never start allopurinol mid-flare, and 'until diarrhoea' colchicine is the outdated wrong answer.
</details>

**Q5.** A 29-year-old Aboriginal woman, 16 weeks pregnant, has SLE with anti-Ro/SSA antibodies. She is clinically well. Beyond standard antenatal care, which fetal complication is most specifically associated with her antibody profile and warrants targeted monitoring?

   - A. Neural tube defects
   - B. Congenital complete heart block
   - C. Intrauterine growth restriction from placental insufficiency
   - D. Neonatal thrombocytopenia from antiphospholipid antibodies
   - E. Fetal hydrops from parvovirus

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Congenital complete heart block**

Anti-Ro/SSA (and anti-La) cross the placenta and damage the fetal cardiac conduction system, causing neonatal lupus and congenital complete heart block — the textbook antibody-to-organ map. Serial fetal echocardiography/heart rate monitoring is indicated. A is unrelated to her serology. C and D can occur in lupus/APLS pregnancies but are not specific to anti-Ro. E is an infectious cause unrelated to her antibodies. The point is that the antibody fingerprint predicts the organ threatened.

_Teaching point:_ Anti-Ro/La in pregnancy = congenital heart block and neonatal lupus — let the antibody predict the threatened organ.
</details>

**Q6.** A 38-year-old woman from a Sydney clinic has had three first-trimester miscarriages and a prior DVT. Lupus anticoagulant and anti-cardiolipin antibodies are positive, confirmed again 12 weeks later; anti-beta2-glycoprotein-I is also positive. She is now planning anticoagulation for her thrombotic history outside pregnancy. Which is the single most appropriate long-term anticoagulant?

   - A. Apixaban
   - B. Rivaroxaban
   - C. Warfarin with target INR 2-3
   - D. Low-dose aspirin alone
   - E. Dabigatran

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Warfarin with target INR 2-3**

This is triple-positive antiphospholipid syndrome with prior thrombosis. Warfarin (a vitamin K antagonist) is preferred for thrombotic APLS, especially triple-positive disease, because DOACs (A, B, E) have higher recurrent-thrombosis rates in trials (notably rivaroxaban in TRAPS) and are the seductive 'modern' trap. Aspirin alone (D) is inadequate secondary prevention after a venous thrombotic event in APLS. Diagnosis also requires the antibodies confirmed 12 weeks apart, which is satisfied here.

_Teaching point:_ Thrombotic APLS — especially triple-positive — gets warfarin, NOT a DOAC; the 'modern oral anticoagulant' is the trap.
</details>

**Q7.** A 47-year-old man with adult-onset asthma develops a foot drop and patchy numbness. Bloods show eosinophilia of 4.5 x10^9/L. p-ANCA/anti-MPO is positive. He has had recurrent sinusitis and migratory pulmonary infiltrates. Which single feature best distinguishes his condition from granulomatosis with polyangiitis?

   - A. The presence of glomerulonephritis
   - B. A positive ANCA
   - C. Adult-onset asthma with marked eosinophilia
   - D. Sinus involvement
   - E. Mononeuritis multiplex

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Adult-onset asthma with marked eosinophilia**

This is eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis (EGPA, Churg-Strauss): the triad of asthma, eosinophilia and vasculitis (here mononeuritis multiplex as foot drop), typically anti-MPO/p-ANCA. The discriminator from GPA is asthma plus marked eosinophilia — GPA is PR3/c-ANCA with saddle-nose and necrotising granulomas but NOT the asthma-eosinophilia phenotype. Glomerulonephritis (A) and sinus disease (D) occur in both. ANCA (B) is present in both (though MPO favours EGPA/MPA, PR3 favours GPA). Mononeuritis multiplex (E) occurs in both vasculitides. Mepolizumab (anti-IL-5) is an increasingly examined add-on for relapsing EGPA.

_Teaching point:_ Asthma + eosinophilia + vasculitis = EGPA (MPO/p-ANCA); the asthma-eosinophilia phenotype is the discriminator from PR3-positive GPA.
</details>

**Q8.** A 32-year-old Han Chinese woman in Perth is diagnosed with tophaceous gout and her rheumatologist plans to start urate-lowering therapy after the acute flare settles. Which single step is most important before commencing allopurinol?

   - A. Load with allopurinol 300 mg daily immediately to reach target faster
   - B. Check HLA-B*5801 genotype
   - C. Switch directly to febuxostat as first-line
   - D. Confirm serum urate is below 0.36 mmol/L before starting
   - E. Start a high-protein diet to lower urate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Check HLA-B*5801 genotype**

HLA-B*5801 strongly predisposes to allopurinol-induced SJS/TEN and testing is recommended in high-risk groups (Han Chinese, Thai, Korean) — directly relevant given Australia's large East/Southeast Asian population. A is wrong: start low (50-100 mg, lower in CKD) and titrate to target, not load. Febuxostat (C) is second-line and PBS-restricted with CVD caution, not a default substitute. D is backwards — you start ULT because urate is high; the target <0.36 (<0.30 with tophi) is what you titrate toward, not a precondition. E is ineffective and a high-protein/purine load would worsen urate.

_Teaching point:_ Before allopurinol in Han Chinese/Thai/Korean patients, check HLA-B*5801 to avoid SJS/TEN; start low and titrate to urate target (<0.30 if tophi).
</details>

### Schema recall — "What is your schema for X?"

**What is your schema for the acute hot single joint?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1. Triage assumption: septic arthritis until aspirate proves otherwise (cartilage dies in days, ~10% mortality). 2. Aspirate the joint BEFORE antibiotics (native joint); send Gram stain, culture, cell count, polarised microscopy. Prosthetic joint = do not aspirate on the ward, refer ortho. 3. Interpret fluid: WCC >50,000 with neutrophil predominance = treat as septic even if Gram-negative; remember crystals and infection coexist. Negatively birefringent needles = urate (gout); positively birefringent rhomboids = CPPD (pseudogout). 4. Empirical antibiotics per eTG: IV flucloxacillin; vancomycin if MRSA risk/severe beta-lactam allergy; add ceftriaxone (Gram-negative cover) if immunocompromised/IVDU or Gram-negatives on stain. 5. Special cases: young sexually active + tenosynovitis + pustules + migratory arthralgia = disseminated gonococcal (ceftriaxone, NAAT swabs + blood cultures, notify and contact-trace).

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Students see crystals or a known-gout history and call it gout, missing coexisting sepsis. Examiners also bait 'start antibiotics first' (sterilises cultures) and forget that a normal urate during a flare excludes nothing. The other classic miss: aspirating a prosthetic joint on the ward instead of referring.
</details>

**What is your schema for suspected giant cell arteritis?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1. Recognise the pattern: age >50, new headache, scalp tenderness, jaw claudication, raised ESR/CRP. 2. Stratify by visual involvement. No visual symptoms = oral prednisolone ~1 mg/kg up to 60 mg/day. Visual loss/amaurosis = IV methylprednisolone then high-dose oral. 3. Treat FIRST, investigate second: never delay steroids for temporal artery biopsy/ultrasound (biopsy stays positive ~2 weeks; skip lesions mean a negative biopsy doesn't exclude GCA). 4. Steroid-sparing/relapse: tocilizumab (IL-6 inhibitor, PBS-listed, GiACTA) for relapsing/refractory disease. 5. Steroid harm prophylaxis: bone protection, PPI, glucose monitoring. 6. Always screen for/with PMR overlap.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Two attacks. First, 'arrange biopsy before steroids' — wrong, you start steroids immediately. Second, routine low-dose aspirin — this is outdated EULAR-legacy teaching with no RCT support and is now NOT recommended in new GCA (bleeding risk on steroids). Also confusing the PMR dose (15 mg) with the GCA dose (40-60 mg / 1 mg/kg).
</details>

**What is your schema for the gout drug ladder (acute and chronic)?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

Acute flare (choose ONE based on comorbidity): NSAID (e.g. naproxen/indomethacin) if no CKD/HF/anticoagulation; OR low-dose colchicine 1 mg stat then 0.5 mg one hour later (renally adjusted, beware CYP3A4/P-gp interactions e.g. clarithromycin = fatal); OR prednisolone ~30 mg/day (0.5 mg/kg) for several days. Allopurinol rules: do NOT start ULT during a flare (wait ~2-4 weeks); NEVER stop established allopurinol during a flare. Starting ULT: check HLA-B*5801 in Han Chinese/Thai/Korean; start low (50-100 mg, lower in CKD) and titrate to urate target <0.36 (<0.30 if tophi); co-prescribe colchicine 500 microgram once/twice daily flare prophylaxis for at least 3-6 months AND until target urate reached and stable (longer if tophi/flares). Febuxostat second-line (CVD caution, PBS-restricted).

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The allopurinol start/stop rule is the single most baited point. Examiners also offer the OLD high-dose colchicine ('0.5 mg every 2 hrs until diarrhoea') as a distractor, use 15 mg prednisolone (the PMR dose) for acute gout, or imply a flat 6-month prophylaxis cap (it continues until target urate is sustained).
</details>

**What is your schema for the pulmonary-renal syndrome / rapidly progressive GN with haemoptysis?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1. Recognise the killer combination: haemoptysis/pulmonary haemorrhage + rising creatinine + nephritic urine (dysmorphic RBCs, red cell casts). 2. Act before serology returns: send ANCA (PR3, MPO) AND anti-GBM, and start pulsed methylprednisolone. 3. Map the antibody: PR3/c-ANCA = GPA (saddle-nose, sinusitis); MPO/p-ANCA = MPA or EGPA (asthma + eosinophilia); anti-GBM = Goodpasture (linear IgG on biopsy). 4. Induction: ANCA vasculitis (GPA/MPA) = rituximab OR cyclophosphamide + glucocorticoids with PEXIVAS reduced-dose taper; plasma exchange is SELECTIVE (severe renal disease or pulmonary haemorrhage), not routine. Anti-GBM = plasma exchange (standard) + steroids + cyclophosphamide. 5. EGPA relapse add-on: mepolizumab (anti-IL-5).

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Old teaching says 'always plasmapheresis for ANCA vasculitis' — PEXIVAS made it selective. Students also name cyclophosphamide as the only induction agent and forget rituximab (first-line, preferred for relapse/fertility preservation). The discriminator examiners attack: plasma exchange is unambiguous for anti-GBM but case-by-case for AAV.
</details>

**What is your schema for the antibody-to-disease map in connective tissue disease?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1. ANA = the screening gate; if positive, subtype. 2. SLE: anti-dsDNA and anti-Sm (specific); dsDNA tracks lupus nephritis activity; low C3/C4 = active complement consumption/flare. 3. RA: anti-CCP (specific, predicts erosions); RF sensitive but non-specific. 4. Sjogren/pregnancy: anti-Ro/La → sicca, and neonatal lupus/congenital heart block in pregnancy. 5. Scleroderma: anti-Scl-70 → diffuse; anti-centromere → limited/CREST. 6. Myositis: anti-Jo-1 → polymyositis with ILD. 7. APLS: lupus anticoagulant, anti-cardiolipin, anti-beta2-glycoprotein-I, confirmed 12 weeks apart. Derive the scenario from the antibody and vice versa.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Students memorise pairs instead of mapping antibody→organ, so they can't reason backwards. Common attacks: confusing Scl-70 (diffuse) with centromere (limited); forgetting low complement signifies ACTIVE lupus; and missing that APLS needs repeat confirmation at 12 weeks and warfarin (not DOAC) for thrombosis.
</details>

**What is your schema for safe DMARD/biologic initiation in Australia?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1. Conventional first-line RA: methotrexate — ONCE WEEKLY (fatal if taken daily — Australian safety alert), folic acid on non-MTX days, strict contraception (teratogenic), monitor FBC/LFTs, watch for pneumonitis/hepatotoxicity. 2. Before TNF inhibitors/biologics: screen latent TB with QuantiFERON-Gold/IGRA, hepatitis B and C serology, check VZV/vaccination status. 3. Biologics are PBS-authority listed (TNF inhibitors, rituximab). 4. Steroid course alongside = bone protection, glucose monitoring, PPI as needed.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The once-weekly methotrexate rule is a recurring Australian quality-and-safety trap (daily dosing = fatal). Examiners also test forgetting IGRA/hepatitis screening before a TNF inhibitor (reactivation of latent TB/HBV), and omitting folic acid.
</details>

### Distractor drills — "What's the deadliest distractor for X?"

**Most seductive wrong answer when a known gout patient presents with one hot joint, fever and visible urate crystals on aspirate?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Diagnose a gout flare and treat with an NSAID/colchicine/steroid.
- _Why it tempts you:_ The crystals are right there, the patient has documented gout, and the picture 'fits' — it feels like confirmed pattern recognition.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Crystals and infection coexist; finding urate does not exclude septic arthritis, which destroys cartilage in days. A normal/low urate during a flare excludes nothing either.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Fever + high synovial WCC (especially >50,000 with neutrophil predominance) overrides the crystals — treat as septic, send culture, give empirical antibiotics after aspiration.**

**Deadliest distractor in suspected GCA with visual symptoms?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Arrange an urgent temporal artery biopsy and start steroids once it confirms the diagnosis.
- _Why it tempts you:_ It feels rigorous and evidence-based to confirm before committing to high-dose steroids in an elderly patient.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Every hour of delay risks irreversible blindness; the biopsy stays positive for ~2 weeks after steroids start and skip lesions mean a negative result doesn't exclude GCA.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Visual symptoms = give IV methylprednisolone NOW; the biopsy/ultrasound follows and never gates treatment.**

**Most seductive wrong answer for the acute gout drug choice in a patient with CKD, heart failure and on warfarin?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Naproxen (or any NSAID).
- _Why it tempts you:_ NSAIDs are the reflex first-line for gout and the 'default' acute treatment in most students' minds.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ CKD, heart failure and anticoagulation are each NSAID contraindications — this patient has all three (renal harm, fluid overload, GI bleeding on warfarin).
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Comorbidity profile redirects you to low-dose colchicine (renally adjusted) or prednisolone ~30 mg, NOT an NSAID.**

**Classic Australian trap distractor when choosing anticoagulation for thrombotic antiphospholipid syndrome?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ A DOAC such as rivaroxaban or apixaban.
- _Why it tempts you:_ DOACs are the modern default for VTE/AF — no monitoring, convenient, 'first-line' in most other thrombosis settings.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ DOACs have higher recurrent-thrombosis rates in APLS (rivaroxaban failed in TRAPS), especially triple-positive disease.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Triple-positive or thrombotic APLS = warfarin (vitamin K antagonist), target INR 2-3 — not a DOAC.**

**Most seductive wrong answer for colchicine dosing in acute gout?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Colchicine 0.5 mg every 2 hours until symptoms resolve or diarrhoea develops.
- _Why it tempts you:_ It's the regimen many older textbooks and senior clinicians still quote, so it 'sounds authoritative'.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ High-dose 'until diarrhoea' regimens cause toxic diarrhoea and are no longer recommended; eTG changed to low-dose.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Correct eTG dose is 1 mg stat then 0.5 mg one hour later, then stop — anything escalating 'until diarrhoea' is the outdated wrong answer.**

**Deadliest distractor in ANCA-associated vasculitis (GPA/MPA) management?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Routine plasma exchange for everyone, with cyclophosphamide as the only induction agent.
- _Why it tempts you:_ Older teaching paired 'pulmonary-renal syndrome' reflexively with plasmapheresis, and cyclophosphamide is the classic textbook induction drug.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ PEXIVAS made plasma exchange SELECTIVE in AAV (severe renal disease or pulmonary haemorrhage, case-by-case), and rituximab is now first-line induction (non-inferior to cyclophosphamide, preferred for relapse/fertility).
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Plasma exchange is unambiguously standard ONLY for anti-GBM/Goodpasture; in AAV it is selective, and rituximab belongs alongside cyclophosphamide.**

**Most seductive wrong answer for steroid dose in PMR vs GCA vs acute gout?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Prednisolone 15 mg/day for whichever condition is in the stem.
- _Why it tempts you:_ 15 mg is a familiar 'rheumatology steroid dose' so it feels safe to apply broadly.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ 15 mg is specifically the PMR dose. GCA needs 40-60 mg (1 mg/kg) or IV methylprednisolone if visual; acute gout needs ~30 mg.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Match dose to disease: PMR 15 mg, GCA 40-60 mg/IV, acute gout ~30 mg — a single number distinguishes the right answer.**

### High-end review pearls

- Aunt-minnie: saddle-nose deformity + sinusitis + haemoptysis + glomerulonephritis = GPA (PR3/c-ANCA). Asthma + eosinophilia + mononeuritis multiplex (foot drop) = EGPA (MPO/p-ANCA).
- If you see haemoptysis + rising creatinine + red cell casts, think pulmonary-renal syndrome — send ANCA AND anti-GBM and pulse methylprednisolone BEFORE serology returns.
- Never-forget one-liner: aspirate before antibiotics. Antibiotics first sterilise the culture and you lose the diagnosis.
- Crystals never exclude sepsis — the two coexist. A hot joint with crystals AND fever/high WCC is still septic until proven otherwise.
- GCA pearl: start steroids first, biopsy later. Biopsy stays positive ~2 weeks on steroids, and skip lesions mean a negative biopsy doesn't exclude GCA.
- Australian update trap: do NOT add routine aspirin in new GCA — it is outdated EULAR-legacy teaching with no RCT support and is dangerous on high-dose steroids.
- Tocilizumab (IL-6 inhibitor, GiACTA, PBS-listed) is the Australian steroid-sparing/relapse agent for GCA — high-yield currency point.
- PEXIVAS one-liner: plasma exchange in ANCA vasculitis is now SELECTIVE, not routine — but remains standard for anti-GBM/Goodpasture.
- Rituximab is first-line induction in GPA/MPA (RAVE/RITUXVAS), preferred when fertility preservation matters or in relapsing disease — not just cyclophosphamide.
- Methotrexate is ONCE WEEKLY — daily dosing is fatal (a recurring Australian quality-and-safety alert); folic acid on non-MTX days, strict contraception.
- Before any TNF inhibitor/biologic: QuantiFERON (IGRA) for latent TB plus hepatitis B/C serology — reactivation kills.
- Colchicine + clarithromycin (or other strong CYP3A4/P-gp inhibitors) = fatal colchicine toxicity. Renally dose-adjust colchicine.
- Allopurinol mantra: never START during a flare, never STOP an established prescription during a flare. Check HLA-B*5801 in Han Chinese/Thai/Korean first.
- Urate targets: <0.36 mmol/L standard, <0.30 mmol/L if tophi. Start allopurinol low (50-100 mg, lower in CKD) and titrate — don't load.
- SLE flare fingerprint: rising anti-dsDNA + FALLING C3/C4 (active complement consumption) = lupus nephritis activity.
- Anti-Ro/La in pregnancy = congenital complete heart block and neonatal lupus — arrange fetal cardiac monitoring.
- Scl-70 = diffuse scleroderma (worse, ILD/renal crisis); anti-centromere = limited/CREST. Don't swap them.
- Thrombotic APLS, especially triple-positive (lupus anticoagulant + anti-cardiolipin + anti-beta2-GPI), gets WARFARIN not a DOAC; antibodies confirmed 12 weeks apart.
- Young patient with CPPD/pseudogout: screen for metabolic causes — haemochromatosis (high Australian prevalence), hyperparathyroidism, hypomagnesaemia, hypophosphatasia.
- Sexually active young Australian with migratory arthralgia + tenosynovitis + pustules = disseminated gonococcal arthritis: ceftriaxone, NAAT/throat-cervical-urethral swabs + blood cultures (synovial cultures often negative), and it is NOTIFIABLE with contact tracing.
- Equity pearl: gout in First Nations, Maori and Pacific Islander Australians has high prevalence, earlier onset and renal disease — lower threshold for urate-lowering therapy and renal screening.
- EGPA relapse add-on = mepolizumab (anti-IL-5) — increasingly examined alongside the pattern-recognition diagnosis.
- Prosthetic joint with possible infection: do NOT aspirate blindly on the ward — refer to orthopaedics.

> **Independent item audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[high]** Q4 (CKD/HF/warfarin gout) explanation + Schema 'gout drug ladder' + extras: 'NEVER start allopurinol mid-flare' / 'never START during a flare': This absolute rule is OUTDATED per current Australian guidance. Australian Prescriber ('The management of gout', tg.org.au, the eTG-aligned source the content itself cites) explicitly states: 'Allopurinol therapy should not be stopped in the event of an acute gout flare and CAN BE SAFELY STARTED during an acute attack' (provided flare cover is co-prescribed). The content treats 'never start ULT during a flare' as a load-bearing examinable fact and even brands it 'the single most baited point' and a 'mantra' in highEndExtras. For Q4 itself, option C (allopurinol alone) is still correctly WRONG because it does not treat the acute flare and omits flare prophylaxis — but the stated REASONING ('precipitates/prolongs it', 'must NOT be started') is wrong by 2026 Australian guidelines. → Reframe to the defensible modern position: 'Do not start ULT alone as flare treatment, and traditionally ULT was deferred ~2-4 weeks; however current Australian guidance (Australian Prescriber/eTG) permits commencing allopurinol DURING a flare provided effective anti-inflammatory flare cover is given. Never STOP established allopurinol during a flare.' Remove the absolute 'never start' mantra from the schema and highEndExtras.
> - **[medium]** Q4 option E + explanation: 'Prednisolone 15 mg daily for five days' labelled wrong because 'the correct acute-gout steroid dose is ~30 mg/day, so 15 mg is under-dosed': 15 mg is NOT clearly wrong in Australian practice. Australian Prescriber states 'Oral prednisolone 35 mg daily has been shown to effectively treat acute gout, however 15-20 mg daily is OFTEN RECOMMENDED.' So 15 mg sits at the bottom of the accepted Australian range, not below it. This weakens single-best-answer cleanliness: a candidate could legitimately defend E as adequate gout therapy (and E, unlike C, IS an anti-inflammatory appropriate for this comorbid patient). The intended single best answer (B, low-dose colchicine) is still the best choice, but the justification for excluding E is factually shaky. → Either (a) change option E's dose to an unambiguously wrong steroid framing (e.g. 'prednisolone 5 mg daily' or a clearly PMR-mismatched scenario), or (b) keep E but justify its exclusion on a sounder axis — e.g. colchicine is the cleanest single best answer; if using steroids the typical acute-gout course is ~30-35 mg, and 15 mg/5 days is a short under-treating course — while acknowledging 15-20 mg is within accepted Australian range. Do NOT teach that 15 mg is simply 'the PMR dose' and therefore wrong for gout.
> - **[medium]** Q2 GCA explanation + Schema 'suspected GCA' + highEndExtras: 'routine aspirin is no longer recommended... no RCT evidence... dangerous... outdated EULAR-legacy teaching': Overstated as an absolute. The aspirin-in-GCA question is genuinely contested, but it is not settled that aspirin is 'not recommended' — multiple guidance sources and reviews still suggest low-dose aspirin SPECIFICALLY in GCA patients presenting with visual loss or stroke (the exact scenario in Q2, amaurosis fugax). Presenting 'do NOT add aspirin' as a definite Australian-update fact is an over-claim. For Q2 itself this does not change the answer (C is correct because D is oral not IV despite visual symptoms — that alone makes D wrong), so the aspirin line is a redundant secondary justification carrying overstated certainty. → Soften to: 'Routine aspirin for ALL GCA is no longer universally endorsed (evidence is retrospective/conflicting); some guidance still suggests low-dose aspirin in ischaemic/visual-loss presentations. The decisive reason D is wrong is that visual symptoms mandate IV methylprednisolone, not oral.' Remove the categorical 'no RCT, dangerous, not recommended' framing from the extras.
> - **[low]** Q7 (EGPA vs GPA) option A 'presence of glomerulonephritis' as a distractor: Weak/strawman distractor as written, and the explanation slightly overstates symmetry. The explanation says GN 'occurs in both' — true, but GN is markedly LESS common and less severe in EGPA (more in MPO+ subset) than in GPA, so a knowledgeable candidate would never pick A regardless. It is not a 'deadliest trap'; it is an easy eliminate. This is acceptable for single-best-answer but does not meet the brief's bar of 'genuinely seductive' distractors. → Consider replacing A with a sharper near-miss, e.g. 'PR3/c-ANCA positivity' (forces the candidate to know EGPA is usually MPO/p-ANCA but CAN be PR3) or 'upper-airway/sinus granulomas with saddle-nose' (a true GPA-discriminator that tempts those who conflate the two). Keep the correct answer C.
> - **[low]** Q3 stem + Schema: anti-GBM 'mandates plasma exchange... unambiguously standard': Largely correct and well-supported (KDIGO/anti-GBM disease: urgent PLEX standard). Minor nuance: in the rare anti-GBM patient who is dialysis-dependent at presentation with 100% crescents and no pulmonary haemorrhage, PLEX benefit for renal recovery is limited — so 'unambiguously' is a slight over-generalisation. Does not affect Q3's answer (the stem has active pulmonary haemorrhage + recoverable renal function, where PLEX is clearly indicated). → Add a one-line caveat in the schema: 'PLEX is standard for anti-GBM with pulmonary haemorrhage or potentially-recoverable renal function; benefit is limited if already dialysis-dependent with 100% crescents and no lung involvement.' Optional/low priority.
> - **[low]** Q8 / Schema / extras: HLA-B*5801 testing presented as 'recommended' before allopurinol in Han Chinese/Thai/Korean: Directionally correct and a legitimate best-answer for Q8 (it is the single most important pre-step among the offered options, all others being clearly wrong). Nuance: the cited Australian Prescriber source identifies HLA-B*5801 as a recognised RISK FACTOR/association but does not mandate ROUTINE testing in Australia for all such patients; routine genotyping is guideline-endorsed in some jurisdictions and increasingly considered cost-effective in high-risk groups but is not yet universal Australian standard-of-care. The MCQ is still answerable and B is still the best option. → No change needed to the answer. In the explanation/schema, phrase as 'consider/check HLA-B*5801 in high-risk ancestry groups (recommended in several guidelines, increasingly used in Australia)' rather than implying it is mandated universal Australian practice.
> 
> _Quality notes:_ Q1 (hot joint), Q3 (anti-GBM/PLEX discriminator), Q5 (anti-Ro/heart block), Q6 (triple-positive APS warfarin INR 2-3 for a VENOUS DVT — correctly uses 2-3 not 3-4) are all clean, single-best-answer, with genuinely seductive distractors. These are the strongest items.; The recurring teaching device — 'match the steroid dose to the disease (PMR 15, GCA 40-60/IV, gout 30)' — is pedagogically neat but factually brittle for gout, where 15-20 mg is an accepted Australian dose. The mnemonic should not be used to mark a 15 mg gout option 'wrong'.; The content is impressively CURRENT on several high-yield points that are often stale in question banks: PEXIVAS making PLEX selective in AAV (verified vs KDIGO 2024), rituximab as first-line induction, DOAC failure in APS (TRAPS), tocilizumab/GiACTA for GCA, mepolizumab for EGPA relapse. These are correct and well-judged.; Crystal nomenclature is precise and correct: negatively birefringent needles = urate; positively birefringent rhomboids = CPPD. Verified.; Distractor quality is mostly strong (the NSAID-in-CKD/HF/warfarin triple-bait, the 'wait for biopsy' GCA trap, the DOAC-for-APS trap, the high-dose 'until diarrhoea' colchicine trap are all deadly real-world errors). The weakest distractor is Q7 option A (glomerulonephritis), which is an easy eliminate rather than a trap.; Two items lean on absolute claims ('never start allopurinol mid-flare'; 'never add aspirin in GCA') that the content itself elevates to 'high-yield currency points' — but both are actually CONTESTED/outdated relative to 2026 Australian sources. Because the broader item set repeatedly reinforces these as dogma (schema + highEndExtras), the error is amplified across the layer rather than isolated to one stem. Fixing the schema/extras wording matters more than fixing any single MCQ.; No item is dangerously mis-keyed: every stated correct answer (B, C, C, B, B, C, C, B) remains the best available option even after the critiques. The issues are in the JUSTIFICATIONS and in two distractors' defensibility, not in the keys themselves — hence 'minor-fixes' overall.; Stems are well-constructed with appropriate Australian context (named cities, First Nations/Han Chinese ancestry where epidemiologically relevant). None are too easy; difficulty is well-pitched for an applied/registrar-level layer.

---

## Geriatrics, Palliative & General/Preventive Medicine  _(~5% of marks)_

### The one idea that unlocks the domain

> **Every question in this domain is decided by asking "What is this patient's goal, and does this intervention serve more good than harm given their trajectory?" — i.e. weigh benefit against burden across a finite, often shortening, time horizon, then act with the least-restrictive, most-reversible option first.**

Geriatrics, palliative and preventive medicine look like three syllabi but run on one engine: net benefit over a defined time horizon. In geriatrics the horizon is shortened by frailty, so the equation tips toward deprescribing, fall prevention and avoiding restraint. In palliation the horizon is days-to-months, so the goal is symptom relief, not survival — you titrate opioids to symptom not number, and you accept sedation as a trade. In prevention the horizon is years, so you only screen/treat when the patient will live long enough to benefit (a 90-year-old gets no mammogram; a 50-year-old does). The same engine resolves ethics: capacity is decision- and time-specific, you start with the least restrictive option, and a valid advance care directive made with capacity binds even when the patient now lacks it. It resolves Indigenous health: closing the gap means front-loading prevention because the burden and the horizon both arrive early. Once you ask "goal + benefit-vs-burden over what horizon, least-restrictive first," you can derive the right answer to a delirium, a syringe driver, a screening interval, a deprescribing or a substitute-decision-maker question without memorising each separately. The distractors are almost always the option that ignores the horizon (screening the dying), the goal (curative thinking in palliation) or proportionality (sedating/restraining first).

### High-yield tutorial

## The engine: goal × (benefit − burden) over a defined horizon, least-restrictive first

### Delirium — the geriatric emergency that masquerades as dementia
**Delirium = acute, fluctuating disturbance of attention + altered awareness, with an organic cause.** Inattention is the screening pillar (CAM: acute onset/fluctuation **AND** inattention **AND** [disorganised thinking **OR** altered consciousness]). Key exam trap: **hypoactive delirium is more common and more lethal** than hyperactive — the quiet, drowsy elderly patient is the missed one. Dementia is chronic and stable; delirium fluctuates and has a trigger.

**Decision rule: find and fix the cause; non-pharmacological management is first-line, always.** Mnemonic causes — **PINCH ME**: Pain, Infection (UTI/pneumonia), Nutrition, Constipation, Hydration, Medications, Environment. The single commonest precipitant in finals is **drugs (anticholinergics, opioids, benzodiazepines) and urinary retention/constipation**. Reorientation, sleep-wake cues, glasses/hearing aids, mobilisation, hydration come before any drug. **Pharmacological sedation is a last resort for severe distress/risk only** — low-dose haloperidol, **but NEVER in Parkinson's or Lewy body dementia (use quetiapine)**. Benzodiazepines worsen delirium except in **alcohol/benzo withdrawal**, where they are the treatment.

### Falls & deprescribing — subtract before you add
**Falls are multifactorial; the highest-yield modifiable cause is medication.** The drug classes that fall and fracture patients: **benzodiazepines, antipsychotics, antidepressants, antihypertensives/diuretics (postural drop), opioids, anticholinergics** — the "FRIDs" (fall-risk-increasing drugs). After any fall: **lying-and-standing BP** (postural drop = ≥20 systolic or ≥10 diastolic), medication review, vision, feet/footwear, vitamin D, and a **multifactorial intervention** is the evidence-based answer. **Deprescribing engine: does each drug still serve a goal the patient will live to benefit from?** Stop the statin in the frail dying; stop the bisphosphonate after 5 years; review anticholinergic burden.

### Palliative symptom control — titrate to symptom, not to number
**Opioid conversion is the most tested calculation.** Anchor: **oral morphine 10 mg = SC morphine 5 mg (÷2); oral morphine to oral oxycodone ÷1.5; oral morphine to SC/IV ÷2–3.** **Breakthrough dose = 1/6 of the total 24-hour background dose**, given as immediate-release, PRN. Example: 60 mg sustained-release morphine BD = 120 mg/24h → breakthrough = **20 mg IR**. When converting a patient in renal failure, **morphine accumulates (active metabolites) — switch to hydromorphone or fentanyl**.

**Nausea is receptor-mapped — match drug to mechanism:** opioid/chemical/metabolic → **haloperidol** (D2, central); gastric stasis → **metoclopramide** (prokinetic, but avoid in complete bowel obstruction); raised ICP/vestibular → **cyclizine**; broad/unknown → **levomepromazine**. **A syringe driver (CSCI) is for the dying patient who can't swallow** — common combination: morphine + metoclopramide/haloperidol + an antisecretory (**hyoscine butylbromide/glycopyrronium for terminal secretions**). **Always co-prescribe a laxative with any opioid; always have anticipatory ("just in case") meds**: opioid, antiemetic, antisecretory, midazolam (for agitation/terminal restlessness).

### Prevention — only screen those who live to benefit
Screening is benefit-over-years: **stop screening when life expectancy < the lead time to benefit.** Australian programs are the exam targets (numbers below). The horizon rule kills most distractors: don't FOBT-screen the patient with metastatic disease; don't start a statin for primary prevention in advanced frailty.

### Capacity, ACP & end-of-life — least restrictive, decision-specific
**Capacity is presumed, decision-specific and time-specific.** The functional test (4 limbs): can the patient **Understand, Retain, Weigh/use, and Communicate** the decision? Fail any one = lacks capacity *for that decision now*. **A capacitous adult may refuse any treatment, even life-saving — even "irrationally."** **A valid Advance Care Directive made with capacity binds clinicians** when the patient later lacks it. If no directive, the **substitute decision-maker hierarchy** applies (guardian → enduring guardian/attorney for medical → spouse/carer → relative/friend — varies by state). The engine: **least restrictive option, patient's prior expressed wishes/best interests, proportionate to the decision.**

### Frailty, pressure injury, continence — the substrate
**Frailty = reduced physiological reserve → small insult, big decompensation.** It is the variable that *shortens the horizon* and so changes every other answer. **Pressure injuries: prevent with repositioning, pressure-redistributing surfaces, skin care, nutrition; stage by depth** (Stage 1 non-blanching erythema → Stage 4 bone/tendon). **Continence: identify reversible causes first (DIPPERS — Delirium, Infection, atrophic vaginitis, Pharmaceuticals, Psychological, Excess output, Restricted mobility, Stool impaction)** before pads or catheters.

### Indigenous health — front-load prevention because burden and horizon arrive early
**Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples carry chronic disease earlier and heavier; "Closing the Gap" = prevention shifted younger.** Hence **CVD risk assessment from 30 (vs 45 general), and diabetes/kidney screening earlier.** **Cultural safety is defined by the recipient, not the provider** — it means care free of racism, with the patient's culture respected; ask, don't assume. Always offer the **715 Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Health Check.**

### Highest mark-per-minute principles

- **Inattention is the cardinal feature of delirium, and hypoactive delirium is commoner and deadlier than hyperactive.** — Single highest-yield geriatrics discriminator; separates delirium from dementia and from depression.
  - _Exam angle:_ Drowsy, fluctuating elderly post-op patient — the answer is 'screen for delirium / CAM', not 'dementia' or 'sedate'.
- **Treat delirium by finding and fixing the cause (PINCH ME); non-pharmacological measures first, sedation only for severe risk.** — Most delirium MCQs offer haloperidol as a tempting wrong first step.
  - _Exam angle:_ Best initial management = reorientation/treat UTI/stop offending drug; haloperidol only if danger — and quetiapine if Parkinson's/LBD.
- **Medications are the most modifiable fall and fracture cause; after a fall do lying-standing BP and a multifactorial review.** — Deprescribing FRIDs is the evidence-based, examinable intervention.
  - _Exam angle:_ Recurrent faller on a benzo/antihypertensive — answer is medication review + multifactorial assessment, not just a walking aid.
- **Breakthrough opioid dose = 1/6 of the total 24-hour background dose, as immediate-release.** — The most frequently tested palliative calculation.
  - _Exam angle:_ Give the SR total/24h, divide by 6, pick the IR option in the right units.
- **Match the antiemetic to the receptor/cause: haloperidol (chemical/opioid), metoclopramide (gastric stasis), cyclizine (ICP/vestibular).** — Palliative nausea is never 'give ondansetron for everything'.
  - _Exam angle:_ Opioid-induced nausea → haloperidol; bowel-obstruction nausea → avoid prokinetic, use haloperidol/cyclizine.
- **Capacity is presumed, decision-specific, time-specific; a capacitous adult can refuse even life-saving treatment, and a valid ACD binds later.** — The ethics stem turns on these four facts every time.
  - _Exam angle:_ Competent Jehovah's Witness refusing blood, or honouring a prior ACD — respect the refusal; least restrictive.
- **Only screen/prevent when life expectancy exceeds the time-to-benefit; frailty shortens the horizon and cancels the intervention.** — Resolves both preventive and deprescribing stems with one rule.
  - _Exam angle:_ Frail 88-year-old with dementia — the answer is NOT to add a statin or do a screening mammogram.
- **In renal failure, morphine's active metabolites accumulate — switch to fentanyl or hydromorphone.** — Classic safety-pivot in opioid prescribing.
  - _Exam angle:_ Confused, myoclonic palliative patient with low eGFR on morphine → rotate opioid, not increase dose.
- **Indigenous chronic-disease burden arrives early, so prevention is front-loaded: CVD risk from 30, earlier diabetes/renal screening, 715 health check.** — Closing-the-Gap age cut-offs are directly examinable Australian specifics.
  - _Exam angle:_ Aboriginal patient aged 32 — start absolute CVD risk assessment now.

### Must-know numbers (Australian where they differ)

- Breakthrough opioid dose = 1/6 of total 24-hour background dose (IR formulation)
- Oral morphine → SC morphine: divide by 2; oral morphine → oral oxycodone: divide by ~1.5; oral → SC/IV: divide by 2–3
- Oral codeine/tramadol → oral morphine: divide by ~10 and ~5 respectively
- Postural hypotension = fall ≥20 mmHg systolic or ≥10 mmHg diastolic on standing
- BreastScreen Australia: free mammogram every 2 years, target age 50–74 (invited from 50; available from 40)
- National Bowel Cancer Screening: iFOBT every 2 years — now from age 45 to 74 (eligible to request from 45; auto-invited from 50)
- National Cervical Screening: HPV test every 5 years, ages 25–74 (replaced 2-yearly Pap)
- Absolute CVD risk assessment (2023 Aus CVD guideline): from age 45 general population; from age 30 for Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people; diabetes from 35
- 715 Health Check: annual Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Health Assessment (any age)
- AAA screening: one-off ultrasound offered to men ~65 (opportunistic in Australia)
- Pressure injury staging: Stage 1 non-blanchable erythema → Stage 4 exposed bone/muscle/tendon (plus unstageable and deep tissue injury)
- Bisphosphonate primary course ~5 years then review (drug holiday); Vitamin D + calcium for falls/fracture risk
- Reposition at-risk patients every 2 hours; Waterlow/Braden for pressure-injury risk
- CAM delirium: acute onset/fluctuation + inattention + (disorganised thinking OR altered consciousness)

### Cross-topic cuts

- Delirium ↔ Infectious disease/Renal: UTI, pneumonia, AKI and hyponatraemia are top precipitants — the 'confused elderly' stem is often a sepsis or electrolyte question in disguise.
- Opioids ↔ Nephrology: low eGFR mandates morphine→fentanyl/hydromorphone rotation; same logic as renal drug dosing.
- Falls/postural drop ↔ Cardiology & Endocrine: autonomic neuropathy (diabetes), antihypertensive load, and arrhythmia/syncope overlap with 'fall' presentations.
- Deprescribing ↔ Pharmacology: anticholinergic burden, QT-prolonging combinations, and statin/bisphosphonate cessation tie into adverse-drug-reaction stems.
- Capacity/ACP ↔ Psychiatry & Ethics: depression can impair the 'weigh/use' limb; capacity assessment overlaps with involuntary treatment and consent across the curriculum.
- Frailty ↔ Perioperative/Surgery: frailty predicts post-op delirium and poor outcomes, driving conservative/palliative decisions in surgical stems.
- Prevention/CVD risk ↔ Cardiology & Endocrinology: absolute CVD risk calculation, lipid and BP targets, and diabetes screening reappear as primary-prevention questions.
- Indigenous health ↔ Renal/Endocrine/Cardiology: earlier-onset diabetic nephropathy, rheumatic heart disease and CKD shape both screening age and management.
- Continence ↔ Urology/Gynae & Neurology: overflow (retention/anticholinergics), urge (overactive bladder), stress and neurogenic bladder map onto the same DIPPERS reversible-cause screen.
- Palliative nausea/bowel obstruction ↔ Gastroenterology & Oncology: malignant bowel obstruction management (avoid prokinetics, use hyoscine/octreotide) bridges palliative and GI topics.

### Australian-specific (where the UK answer loses you marks)

- Screening ages differ from UK: Australian bowel screening uses iFOBT now from 45 (UK FIT 50/54+); cervical = 5-yearly HPV 25–74; BreastScreen 50–74 — quote the Australian numbers, not NHS ones.
- CVD risk uses the 2023 Australian CVD risk calculator (Aus CVD Risk), not QRISK; Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander assessment starts at 30.
- Substitute-decision-maker law and advance care directive terminology are STATE-based (e.g. Enduring Guardian in NSW, Medical Treatment Decision Maker in Victoria, Enduring Power of Attorney for medical in QLD) — the hierarchy concept is examinable, the exact title varies by jurisdiction.
- Closing the Gap framework, the MBS 715 Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Health Check, and cultural safety (defined by the recipient) are uniquely Australian content with no UK equivalent.
- Immunisation follows the National Immunisation Program (NIP): e.g. herpes zoster (Shingrix) funded ~65 and from 50 for Aboriginal/Torres Strait Islander and immunocompromised; pneumococcal and influenza schedules per NIP, not the UK schedule.
- Prescribing is PBS-driven and guidance follows the Australian Therapeutic Guidelines and palliAGED/CareSearch rather than NICE — opioid conversion ratios and anticipatory prescribing reflect Australian Therapeutic Guidelines: Palliative Care.
- Indigenous chronic disease screening (diabetes, CKD with earlier ACR/eGFR monitoring, rheumatic heart disease) is front-loaded relative to the general population — an Australia-specific exam theme.
- Voluntary Assisted Dying is now legal in all Australian states (with strict eligibility/residency and conscientious-objection provisions) — distinct from UK law; a capacity/end-of-life stem may hinge on this.

> **Independent accuracy audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[medium]** In renal failure, morphine accumulates — switch to hydromorphone or fentanyl. (Highlighted in tutorial body, highYieldPrinciples, and crossTopicCuts as the safety pivot.) → In severe renal impairment the preferred opioids are FENTANYL (and methadone) — no renally-cleared active metabolites. Hydromorphone is better than morphine but still accumulates an active metabolite (H3G) and should be used with caution, not presented as equivalently safe to fentanyl. For the classic 'confused/myoclonic, low eGFR, on morphine' stem, rotate to fentanyl. Source: eTG Therapeutic Guidelines: Palliative Care; Australian Prescriber 'Opioids — clinical applications in palliative care' and 'Prescribing in renal supportive care'.
> - **[low]** mustKnowNumbers: 'Absolute CVD risk assessment (2023 Aus CVD guideline): from age 45 general population; from age 30 for Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people; diabetes from 35.' Tutorial body separately states 'CVD risk assessment from 30 (vs 45 general), and diabetes/kidney screening earlier' for Indigenous. → State the full ranges: general 45–79, First Nations 30–79 (with individual risk-factor review at 18–29), diabetes from 35. Source: 2023 Australian Guideline for assessing and managing cardiovascular disease risk (Heart Foundation/cvdcheck.org.au); MJA 2024;220(9).
> - **[low]** highYieldPrinciples: 'CVD risk from 30' framed as the Indigenous Closing-the-Gap cutoff, and australianSpecifics: 'Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander assessment starts at 30.' → Reinforce: use the 2023 Australian CVD risk calculator (cvdcheck.org.au), a recalibrated PREDICT-derived equation, NOT Framingham/QRISK. Source: cvdcheck.org.au; MJA 2025;223(4).
> - **[low]** Delirium: 'low-dose haloperidol, but NEVER in Parkinson's or Lewy body dementia (use quetiapine).' Body presents haloperidol as the go-to sedative for severe delirium. → Keep haloperidol contraindication in Parkinson's/Lewy body (use quetiapine) — correct. But emphasise antipsychotics do not treat delirium and are for severe risk/distress only; low-dose haloperidol, risperidone or olanzapine are all acceptable. Source: ACSQHC Delirium Clinical Care Standard; Australian Prescriber 'Managing delirium in older patients'.
> - **[low]** National Cervical Screening: HPV test every 5 years, ages 25–74 (replaced 2-yearly Pap). → Add: since 1 July 2022, self-collected vaginal HPV swab is available universally to all eligible screeners (25–74) as an equally valid option to clinician-collected sampling. Source: National Cervical Screening Program; Dept of Health, Disability and Ageing.
> 
> _Gaps flagged & folded in:_ Cervical screening self-collection: since July 2022, ALL eligible people (25-74) can choose a self-collected vaginal HPV swab as a first-line, equally valid option — high-yield Australian-specific point, currently omitted.; Opioid renal-failure pivot should name FENTANYL (and methadone) as the metabolite-free choices; hydromorphone still accumulates active H3G and is only a partial improvement over morphine — not equivalent to fentanyl.; Lung cancer screening: Australia's National Lung Cancer Screening Program commenced July 2025 — low-dose CT every 2 years for high-risk people aged 50-70 with significant smoking history (≥30 pack-years, current smoker or quit ≤10 years). This is brand-new, uniquely Australian, and directly examinable; tutorial has no lung screening at all.; Diabetes screening tool: AUSDRISK every 3 years from age 40 (and from 18 for Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people) per RACGP Red Book — concrete Australian screening specific not stated.; CKD detection in Indigenous/diabetes: Kidney Health Australia recommends Kidney Health Check (eGFR + uACR + BP) and earlier/more frequent ACR monitoring; the tutorial gestures at 'earlier renal screening' but gives no concrete interval or the uACR component.; Osteoporosis/bisphosphonate: tutorial says 'stop after 5 years (drug holiday)' — should specify this applies to oral bisphosphonates ~5 years / IV zoledronic acid ~3 years, then reassess fracture risk; high-risk patients continue. Denosumab must NOT be simply stopped (rebound vertebral fractures) — a dangerous omission for a deprescribing-focused tutorial.; Anticholinergic burden tools: name the Drug Burden Index / anticholinergic cognitive burden scale and explicitly flag oxybutynin, amitriptyline, promethazine, and 'PIMs' via the Beers/STOPP-START frameworks used in Australian deprescribing.; Advance care directive / SDM: tutorial correctly flags state variation but should note that a common-law refusal and a statutory ACD both bind, and that VAD is self-requested only and can NEVER be authorised by a substitute decision-maker or an advance care directive — a frequently tested distinction.; Postural BP measurement standard: specify measure supine after 5 min rest, then standing at (1 and) 3 minutes — the timing is commonly tested alongside the ≥20/≥10 mmHg thresholds.; Falls/fracture: add FRAX or Garvan fracture risk calculator and the PBS criteria context for anti-resorptive therapy (e.g. after minimal-trauma fracture or T-score ≤ -2.5 / -3.0), plus vitamin D and calcium, and exercise/strength + balance training (the single best-evidenced falls intervention) which the tutorial under-weights versus medication review.; Pneumococcal NIP update: for completeness alongside zoster, note current NIP gives a single dose of 13vPCV (or per schedule) at 70 for non-Indigenous and earlier/repeat dosing for Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander adults and at-risk groups — schedules differ from UK.; Terminal secretions/anticipatory meds: tutorial lists hyoscine butylbromide/glycopyrronium and midazolam — good — but should note hyoscine BUTYLbromide (Buscopan) does not cross BBB whereas hyoscine HYDRObromide does (sedating/anticholinergic delirium risk); the bromide/hydrobromide distinction is a classic Australian exam trap.

### Applied MCQs

**Q1.** An 84-year-old man on a Brisbane surgical ward is reviewed two days after a hemiarthroplasty for a fractured neck of femur. Nursing staff note he has become withdrawn, drowsy and slow to answer, dozing through meals. His daughter says yesterday he was chatty and oriented. He scores poorly when asked to recite the months of the year backwards. Observations: afebrile, HR 82, BP 138/80, SpO2 95% on air. He has not opened his bowels for four days and the bladder scan shows 480 mL. Which feature is most central to making the diagnosis?

   - A. The four-day history of constipation
   - B. Inability to recite the months backwards (inattention)
   - C. The post-operative timing
   - D. The reduced oral intake
   - E. The change from his usual chatty self

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Inability to recite the months backwards (inattention)**

This is hypoactive delirium — the quiet, drowsy, missed presentation. Inattention is the cardinal, diagnosis-defining feature of delirium (CAM requires acute onset/fluctuation AND inattention AND [disorganised thinking OR altered consciousness]). The months-backwards failure operationalises inattention, so B is the answer. Constipation and urinary retention (480 mL) are precipitants under PINCH ME — they explain WHY he is delirious and must be treated, but they are not how you make the diagnosis (A is a cause, not the defining feature). Post-op timing (C) and reduced intake (D) are context/consequences. The acute fluctuating change (E) is one CAM limb but is non-specific; without demonstrated inattention you cannot diagnose delirium. The trap is choosing a precipitant or the fluctuation over the single discriminating sign.

_Teaching point:_ Inattention is the pillar that makes the delirium diagnosis; hypoactive (drowsy, quiet) delirium is commoner and deadlier than hyperactive and is the one that gets missed.
</details>

**Q2.** A 79-year-old woman with idiopathic Parkinson's disease is admitted to a Melbourne hospital with a urinary tract infection. On day 2 she becomes acutely agitated, pulling at her cannula and trying to climb out of bed, placing herself at genuine risk of harm. Non-pharmacological measures (reorientation, family at bedside, treating the infection, a quiet single room) have been tried and the distress is escalating. She is not in alcohol or benzodiazepine withdrawal. Which is the most appropriate pharmacological agent if sedation is now required?

   - A. Haloperidol
   - B. Olanzapine
   - C. Quetiapine
   - D. Diazepam
   - E. Risperidone

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Quetiapine**

She has Parkinson's disease, so dopamine-blocking antipsychotics (haloperidol, risperidone, olanzapine) are contraindicated — they worsen rigidity and can precipitate a Parkinsonian crisis. The agent of choice when antipsychotic cover is genuinely needed in Parkinson's or Lewy body dementia is quetiapine (or clozapine). Haloperidol (A) is the standard first-line in non-Parkinsonian delirium but is the classic trap here. Olanzapine (B) and risperidone (E) are reasonable in ordinary delirium but still D2-active and unsafe in Parkinson's. Benzodiazepines (D) worsen delirium and are reserved for alcohol/benzo withdrawal, which is explicitly excluded. The stem has already exhausted non-pharmacological steps and established true risk, so 'continue non-pharmacological measures' would be the wrong reflex here.

_Teaching point:_ Antipsychotics do not treat delirium and are for severe distress/risk only — but when used in Parkinson's or Lewy body dementia, avoid D2 blockers (haloperidol, risperidone, olanzapine) and use quetiapine.
</details>

**Q3.** A 68-year-old man with metastatic prostate cancer is managed at home by a palliative care service in regional NSW. His bony pain is well controlled on sustained-release oral morphine 60 mg twice daily, plus occasional breakthrough doses. The team wants to confirm the correct immediate-release breakthrough dose. What is the appropriate breakthrough dose of oral immediate-release morphine?

   - A. 10 mg
   - B. 20 mg
   - C. 30 mg
   - D. 60 mg
   - E. 5 mg

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. 20 mg**

Total 24-hour background = 60 mg BD = 120 mg/24h. Breakthrough = 1/6 of the total 24-hour dose = 120 ÷ 6 = 20 mg immediate-release. So B is correct. A (10 mg) is 1/12 — a common error from halving the single SR dose rather than taking 1/6 of the daily total. C (30 mg) wrongly uses 1/4. D (60 mg) confuses the single SR dose with the breakthrough.
