E (5 mg) is far too low. The discriminator is: always work from the TOTAL 24-hour dose and divide by 6.

_Teaching point:_ Breakthrough opioid dose = one-sixth of the total 24-hour background dose, in an immediate-release formulation.
</details>

**Q4.** A 72-year-old woman with metastatic ovarian cancer is on sustained-release oral morphine 90 mg twice daily for pain. Over a week she develops worsening confusion, vivid dreams and visible muscle twitching (myoclonus). Her eGFR has fallen to 22 mL/min following an episode of dehydration. Pain remains reasonably controlled. What is the single most appropriate next step?

   - A. Increase the morphine dose to settle the agitation
   - B. Add a benzodiazepine for the myoclonus
   - C. Rotate to fentanyl
   - D. Rotate to hydromorphone
   - E. Add regular haloperidol and continue morphine

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Rotate to fentanyl**

This is morphine neurotoxicity: confusion + myoclonus in the setting of renal impairment, caused by accumulation of renally-cleared active metabolites (morphine-6- and morphine-3-glucuronide). The correct move is opioid rotation to an agent without renally-cleared active metabolites — fentanyl (or methadone). So C. Increasing morphine (A) worsens the toxicity. A benzodiazepine (B) or haloperidol (E) treats a symptom while leaving the accumulating drug on board — masking, not fixing. The seductive distractor is D: hydromorphone is often taught as a 'renal-safe' rotation, but its active metabolite hydromorphone-3-glucuronide (H3G) also accumulates and causes the same neuroexcitation/myoclonus. It is better than morphine but NOT equivalent to fentanyl; in severe renal impairment fentanyl and methadone are the metabolite-free choices.

_Teaching point:_ In severe renal impairment rotate to fentanyl or methadone (no renally-cleared active metabolites); hydromorphone still accumulates H3G and is only a partial improvement, not a clean substitute.
</details>

**Q5.** A 75-year-old man dying of metastatic lung cancer is in the last days of life at a hospice in Adelaide. He can no longer swallow. He develops persistent nausea attributed to opioids and renal impairment, and there are audible upper-airway secretions ('death rattle') distressing his family. A subcutaneous syringe driver is being set up. Which antiemetic best matches the mechanism of his nausea, and which agent should be added for the secretions?

   - A. Metoclopramide for nausea; hyoscine hydrobromide for secretions
   - B. Ondansetron for nausea; glycopyrronium for secretions
   - C. Haloperidol for nausea; hyoscine butylbromide for secretions
   - D. Cyclizine for nausea; furosemide for secretions
   - E. Haloperidol for nausea; hyoscine hydrobromide for secretions

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Haloperidol for nausea; hyoscine butylbromide for secretions**

Opioid/chemical/metabolic nausea is mediated via central D2 receptors in the chemoreceptor trigger zone — the matched drug is haloperidol. Metoclopramide (A) is a prokinetic for gastric stasis, not the best match here and risky if any obstruction. Ondansetron (B) is not the palliative default and does nothing for this mechanism. Cyclizine (D) is for vestibular/raised-ICP nausea, and furosemide does not treat terminal secretions. For secretions, the antimuscarinic of choice is hyoscine BUTYLbromide (Buscopan) or glycopyrronium — both quaternary amines that do NOT cross the blood-brain barrier. Hyoscine HYDRObromide (in A and E) crosses the BBB and is sedating/deliriogenic — the classic Australian bromide/hydrobromide trap. So C is correct.

_Teaching point:_ Match the antiemetic to the receptor (opioid/metabolic = haloperidol); for terminal secretions use hyoscine BUTYLbromide or glycopyrronium (no BBB penetration), not hyoscine HYDRObromide.
</details>

**Q6.** A 32-year-old Aboriginal man attends a community health service in the Northern Territory for a general check-up. He smokes, has a family history of early heart disease, and his father had a myocardial infarction at 49. He has no known diabetes or chronic kidney disease. According to current Australian guidance, what is the most appropriate cardiovascular action at this visit?

   - A. No CVD risk assessment is indicated until age 45
   - B. Perform an absolute CVD risk assessment using the Australian CVD risk calculator
   - C. Calculate his risk using the Framingham equation
   - D. Reassure him and review in 10 years
   - E. Start a statin empirically without risk assessment

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Perform an absolute CVD risk assessment using the Australian CVD risk calculator**

For Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people, absolute CVD risk assessment using the 2023 Australian CVD risk calculator (cvdcheck.org.au) is recommended from age 30 (vs 45 in the general population), because chronic disease burden arrives earlier — the Closing the Gap front-loading principle. At 32 he is squarely in range, so B. A and D ignore the earlier Indigenous cut-off. C is wrong because Australia uses the recalibrated PREDICT-derived 2023 calculator, NOT Framingham or QRISK. E is wrong because you assess absolute risk first and treat according to it, rather than starting a statin blindly. (Note: for First Nations people aged 18–29 you review individual risk factors rather than running the calculator — but at 32 the calculator applies.)

_Teaching point:_ Front-load prevention in Indigenous health: absolute CVD risk from age 30 (30–79) using the 2023 Australian calculator, not Framingham/QRISK.
</details>

**Q7.** A 35-year-old woman attends her Perth GP and mentions she is overdue for cervical screening. She has never had an abnormal result, is asymptomatic, and feels very anxious about a speculum examination, saying this is why she has avoided screening. What is the most appropriate option to offer her under the current National Cervical Screening Program?

   - A. A clinician-collected Pap smear (cytology) every 2 years
   - B. A self-collected vaginal HPV swab as an equally valid first-line option
   - C. Defer screening until she turns 40
   - D. Referral straight to colposcopy
   - E. A clinician-collected HPV test only, as self-collection is reserved for never-screened women over 70

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. A self-collected vaginal HPV swab as an equally valid first-line option**

Since 1 July 2022, self-collected vaginal HPV swabs are available universally to ALL screen-eligible people aged 25–74 as an equally valid first-line option to clinician collection — specifically helping those who avoid speculum exams. So B directly addresses her barrier and is correct current practice. A is outdated: the program is 5-yearly HPV testing, not 2-yearly cytology (Pap). C wrongly defers; she is already eligible at 25–74. D is inappropriate without an abnormal result. E describes the OLD pre-2022 restriction (self-collection only for under-screened/over-30 groups) which no longer applies — a deliberate trap for those who haven't updated.

_Teaching point:_ National Cervical Screening = 5-yearly HPV test, ages 25–74; since July 2022 self-collected vaginal swab is universally available as an equally valid first-line option.
</details>

**Q8.** An 82-year-old woman with severe Alzheimer's dementia (largely bedbound, minimal verbal output, recurrent aspiration) lives in a residential aged care facility in Tasmania. She has a valid Advance Care Directive completed five years ago when she had capacity, stating she does not want hospital transfer or 'aggressive' treatment, only comfort care. She develops a fever and a productive cough consistent with aspiration pneumonia. Her son, who is her enduring guardian, arrives and insists she be sent to hospital for IV antibiotics 'to give her every chance'. What should guide the treating team's decision?

   - A. The son's wishes, because as enduring guardian he is the legal substitute decision-maker
   - B. Transfer to hospital, because withholding antibiotics from a treatable infection is unlawful
   - C. The patient's valid Advance Care Directive refusing hospital transfer and aggressive treatment
   - D. A formal best-interests meeting before any decision, overriding the directive
   - E. The facility's default escalation policy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. The patient's valid Advance Care Directive refusing hospital transfer and aggressive treatment**

A valid Advance Care Directive made by a person with capacity binds clinicians when that person later lacks capacity. It is a contemporaneous refusal carried forward and takes precedence over a substitute decision-maker's later preferences. So C. A is the seductive distractor: an enduring guardian / substitute decision-maker steps in only where there is NO valid directive covering the decision — the SDM cannot override a clear, applicable ACD, and certainly cannot demand treatment the patient pre-emptively refused. B is wrong: a capacitous adult (then, via the directive) may refuse even life-prolonging treatment, and honouring that is lawful, not negligent. D wrongly treats the directive as merely advisory. E ignores the patient's expressed wishes entirely. The engine: prior expressed wishes + least restrictive + proportionate to trajectory.

_Teaching point:_ A valid Advance Care Directive made with capacity binds clinicians and outranks a substitute decision-maker; the SDM only acts where no applicable directive exists and cannot demand treatment the patient validly refused.
</details>

### Schema recall — "What is your schema for X?"

**What is your schema for the assessment and management of the acutely confused older inpatient?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) DIAGNOSE: Is this delirium? Apply CAM — acute onset/fluctuation AND inattention AND (disorganised thinking OR altered consciousness). Inattention is the pillar. Distinguish from dementia (chronic, stable) and depression (hypoactive mimic). Remember hypoactive is commoner/deadlier. 2) STRATIFY: hyperactive vs hypoactive vs mixed. 3) FIND THE CAUSE — PINCH ME: Pain, Infection (UTI/pneumonia/sepsis), Nutrition, Constipation, Hydration/electrolytes (Na, AKI), Medications (anticholinergics, opioids, benzos, recent changes), Environment + urinary retention. Examine, urinalysis/MSU, bloods (FBC, U&E, Ca, glucose, CRP), septic screen, review drug chart, bladder scan, PR for impaction. 4) TREAT THE CAUSE. 5) NON-PHARMACOLOGICAL FIRST, ALWAYS: reorientation, glasses/hearing aids, sleep-wake, mobilise, hydrate, family, quiet single room, minimise lines/catheters. 6) PHARMACOLOGY only for severe distress/risk to self or others after non-drug measures fail: low-dose haloperidol/risperidone/olanzapine — but quetiapine if Parkinson's/Lewy body. Benzodiazepines worsen delirium EXCEPT in alcohol/benzo withdrawal. 7) Antipsychotics do not treat delirium — review and stop early.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Students jump to haloperidol as the 'management' answer. Examiners attack two points: (a) the FIRST step is non-pharmacological + treat the cause, drugs are last resort; (b) the patient with Parkinson's/Lewy body where haloperidol is a trap (use quetiapine). Also missed: hypoactive delirium dismissed as 'just tired/dementia', and the silent precipitants (retention, constipation, hyponatraemia, a recently added anticholinergic).
</details>

**What is your schema for assessing and managing the older person who has fallen?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) Is the fall a symptom of an acute illness? Screen for syncope, arrhythmia, sepsis, stroke, hypoglycaemia, anaemia, new drug. 2) Lying-and-standing BP — supine after 5 min rest, then standing at 1 and 3 minutes; postural drop = ≥20 mmHg systolic or ≥10 mmHg diastolic. 3) MEDICATION REVIEW — the most modifiable cause: FRIDs (benzodiazepines, antipsychotics, antidepressants, antihypertensives/diuretics, opioids, anticholinergics) — deprescribe. 4) Multifactorial assessment: vision, feet/footwear, gait & balance, cognition, continence, environmental hazards, alcohol. 5) Bone health in parallel: vitamin D + calcium, assess fracture risk (FRAX/Garvan), consider DXA and anti-resorptive (PBS: after minimal-trauma fracture or T-score threshold). 6) INTERVENE — the strongest single intervention is structured strength + balance exercise (e.g. physiotherapy/Otago); plus multifactorial intervention, home OT review, correct sensory deficits. 7) Reassess and review.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Two attack points: (a) reaching for a walking aid or 'physio referral' alone while ignoring the offending benzodiazepine/antihypertensive — medication review is the high-yield answer; (b) under-weighting exercise: strength + balance training is the single best-evidenced falls-prevention intervention and is frequently the right answer over a generic 'multifactorial' option. Also commonly missed: the postural BP timing (1 and 3 minutes) and treating the fall as mechanical when it is actually syncope/arrhythmia in disguise.
</details>

**What is your schema for opioid prescribing and conversion in palliative care?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) BACKGROUND vs BREAKTHROUGH: establish total 24-hour background dose. 2) BREAKTHROUGH = 1/6 of total 24h dose, as immediate-release PRN. 3) CONVERSION anchors: oral morphine → SC morphine ÷2; oral morphine → oral oxycodone ÷~1.5; oral → SC/IV ÷2–3; oral codeine → morphine ÷~10; oral tramadol → morphine ÷~5. When rotating, convert to the new drug then reduce ~25–33% for incomplete cross-tolerance. 4) ALWAYS co-prescribe a stimulant laxative with any regular opioid. 5) ANTICIPATE side effects: nausea (often settles), sedation, myoclonus/neurotoxicity. 6) RENAL CHECK: if eGFR low or falling and morphine on board with confusion/myoclonus → rotate to FENTANYL or methadone (metabolite-free); hydromorphone only a partial improvement (H3G accumulates). 7) ANTICIPATORY ('just in case') meds for the dying: opioid (pain/dyspnoea), antiemetic, antisecretory (hyoscine butylbromide/glycopyrronium), midazolam (agitation/terminal restlessness). 8) If can't swallow → syringe driver (CSCI).

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Calculation errors: taking 1/6 of a single dose instead of the 24-hour total, or forgetting to convert to IR. The renal pivot: students name hydromorphone as the 'renal-safe' rotation — examiners reward fentanyl/methadone and penalise treating morphine toxicity by increasing the dose or merely adding a sedative. Also missed: forgetting the laxative, and forgetting the dose-reduction for incomplete cross-tolerance when rotating.
</details>

**What is your schema for assessing decision-making capacity and end-of-life decision authority?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

1) PRESUME capacity; it is DECISION-specific and TIME-specific. 2) FUNCTIONAL TEST (4 limbs): can the patient UNDERSTAND the information, RETAIN it, WEIGH/use it, and COMMUNICATE a choice? Fail any one = lacks capacity for THAT decision NOW. 3) A capacitous adult may refuse ANY treatment, even life-saving, even for reasons others find irrational. 4) If the patient now LACKS capacity, look for a valid ADVANCE CARE DIRECTIVE made when they had capacity — it BINDS clinicians for the decisions it covers. 5) If no applicable directive → SUBSTITUTE DECISION-MAKER hierarchy (guardian → enduring guardian/medical decision-maker/attorney → spouse/carer → relative/friend — exact titles vary by state). SDM decides on prior expressed wishes / best interests. 6) Always choose the LEAST RESTRICTIVE, most proportionate option. 7) VAD is self-requested ONLY and can NEVER be authorised by an SDM or an advance care directive.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Examiners attack: (a) treating capacity as global or as a diagnosis ('has dementia therefore no capacity') rather than decision- and time-specific; (b) letting a substitute decision-maker override or demand against a valid ACD — the ACD wins, and the SDM only acts where no directive applies; (c) overriding a competent refusal because it seems irrational; (d) the VAD trap — an SDM/ACD can never request VAD on the patient's behalf. The 'weigh/use' limb is the subtle one (e.g. depression or psychosis can selectively impair it).
</details>

**What is your schema for deciding whether to screen or to deprescribe in an older patient?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

ONE engine: net benefit over a defined time horizon. 1) Define the GOAL (longevity, function, comfort) and the patient's TRAJECTORY/frailty. 2) Estimate LIFE EXPECTANCY vs TIME-TO-BENEFIT (lead time). 3) SCREEN only if life expectancy exceeds time-to-benefit AND the patient would accept/tolerate the downstream workup and treatment. Frailty shortens the horizon → cancels the intervention (no mammogram in the frail demented 90-year-old). 4) DEPRESCRIBE by asking of each drug: does it still serve a goal this patient will live long enough to benefit from? Stop statins for primary prevention in the frail dying; review oral bisphosphonate at ~5 years (IV zoledronic ~3 years) then reassess; review anticholinergic burden. 5) Use frameworks: Beers/STOPP-START for potentially inappropriate medications, Drug Burden Index for anticholinergic/sedative load. 6) Stop the LEAST useful / most harmful drug first; one change at a time; monitor.

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ The horizon is the attack surface: students screen or add primary-prevention drugs (statin, bisphosphonate, intensive glycaemic control) in patients who will not live to benefit. The dangerous deprescribing exception: DENOSUMAB must NOT simply be stopped — rebound vertebral fractures; it requires transition to another anti-resorptive. Also: stopping a drug abruptly that needs tapering, or deprescribing a symptom-control drug that is still serving comfort.
</details>

**What is your schema for Australian preventive screening by age (the must-know programs)?**

<details><summary>Schema</summary>

BOWEL: National Bowel Cancer Screening — iFOBT every 2 years; now eligible to request from 45, auto-invited 50–74. BREAST: BreastScreen Australia — free mammogram every 2 years, target 50–74 (invited from 50; available from 40). CERVICAL: 5-yearly HPV test, ages 25–74; self-collected vaginal swab universally available since July 2022 as an equally valid first-line option. LUNG (NEW): National Lung Cancer Screening Program from July 2025 — low-dose CT every 2 years, ages 50–70, ≥30 pack-years, current smoker or quit ≤10 years. CVD: absolute risk from 45 (45–79 general), from 30 (30–79) for Aboriginal/Torres Strait Islander, from 35 if diabetes — using the 2023 Australian calculator (cvdcheck.org.au), NOT Framingham/QRISK. AAA: one-off ultrasound offered to men ~65. DIABETES: AUSDRISK every 3 years from 40 (from 18 for Indigenous). INDIGENOUS: annual 715 Health Check (any age); Kidney Health Check (eGFR + uACR + BP) earlier/more often. IMMUNISATION per NIP: zoster (Shingrix) ~65 (and from 50 if Indigenous/immunocompromised).

_Where it breaks / where examiners attack:_ Examiners attack with UK/NHS numbers (FIT 50/54+, 2-yearly Pap, QRISK) — quote the AUSTRALIAN figures. Two newest, most-tested points: cervical SELF-COLLECTION (universal since 2022) and the brand-new LUNG screening program (2025). And the horizon override: the right answer for any screen in a frail/dying patient is NOT to screen, regardless of age eligibility.
</details>

### Distractor drills — "What's the deadliest distractor for X?"

**Deadliest distractor when a palliative patient with low/falling eGFR on morphine becomes confused and myoclonic and you need to rotate opioid?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Rotate to hydromorphone (the 'renal-safe' opioid).
- _Why it tempts you:_ Hydromorphone is widely (and lazily) taught as the renal-friendly alternative to morphine, and it IS better than morphine — so it feels like the textbook-correct safety pivot, and it usually sits as a plausible option right next to fentanyl.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Hydromorphone's active metabolite hydromorphone-3-glucuronide (H3G) also accumulates in renal failure and causes the very neuroexcitation/myoclonus the question is testing. It is only a PARTIAL improvement, not equivalent to a metabolite-free opioid.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **In SEVERE renal impairment choose the opioids with NO renally-cleared active metabolites: FENTANYL or methadone. If both fentanyl and hydromorphone are offered, fentanyl wins.**

**Most seductive wrong answer for 'best initial management' of delirium in an agitated elderly patient?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Give low-dose haloperidol.
- _Why it tempts you:_ Agitation feels like it demands sedation, haloperidol is the named delirium drug everyone memorises, and it is genuinely the right antipsychotic in many cases — so it looks like the competent, decisive choice.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Antipsychotics do not treat delirium; they are reserved for SEVERE distress or risk to self/others AFTER non-pharmacological measures, and they don't address the cause. Reaching for them first ignores PINCH ME and the non-drug bundle.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **First-line is ALWAYS find-and-fix-the-cause + non-pharmacological measures. Only if there is genuine danger/severe distress despite these do you sedate — and even then, quetiapine (not haloperidol) if Parkinson's/Lewy body.**

**Most seductive wrong answer when a recurrent faller on a benzodiazepine and an antihypertensive is assessed?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Refer for a walking aid / physiotherapy referral alone, or 'reassure and review'.
- _Why it tempts you:_ Falls feel like a mechanical/mobility problem, a gait aid is concrete and 'doing something', and physio sounds appropriately multidisciplinary.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ It ignores the single most modifiable cause — medications. The benzodiazepine and antihypertensive (postural drop) are driving the falls; handing over a frame without deprescribing leaves the cause untouched.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Medication review/deprescribing of FRIDs + multifactorial assessment is the evidence-based answer; structured strength + balance exercise is the best single intervention. A walking aid alone is never the whole answer.**

**Deadliest distractor in the opioid breakthrough-dose calculation?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Taking 1/6 of a single sustained-release dose (e.g. 1/6 of the 60 mg BD dose = 10 mg) instead of 1/6 of the 24-hour total.
- _Why it tempts you:_ The single dose is the number printed most prominently in the stem, '1/6' is remembered correctly, and 10 mg is a clean, plausible IR dose — so it feels right.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Breakthrough is 1/6 of the TOTAL 24-HOUR background, not of one dose. For 60 mg BD the total is 120 mg/24h, so the breakthrough is 20 mg, not 10 mg.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Always first compute the total 24-hour dose (add both/all background doses), THEN divide by 6, THEN express as immediate-release.**

**Most seductive wrong answer when the family / enduring guardian wants escalation but the patient has a valid Advance Care Directive refusing it?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Follow the substitute decision-maker (enduring guardian), because they are the legal decision-maker.
- _Why it tempts you:_ The SDM is a real legal role, the family is present and insistent, and 'giving every chance' sounds caring and defensible — deferring to next-of-kin feels safe.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ A valid ACD made with capacity binds clinicians and takes precedence over an SDM. The SDM only acts where NO applicable directive exists, and can never demand treatment the patient validly pre-refused.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Ask: is there a valid, applicable Advance Care Directive? If yes, it governs — the SDM hierarchy is irrelevant for that decision.**

**Deadliest distractor for the antisecretory ('death rattle') agent in a syringe driver?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Hyoscine HYDRObromide.
- _Why it tempts you:_ It sounds almost identical to hyoscine butylbromide, both are 'hyoscine', both are antimuscarinic antisecretories, and the names are easily blurred under exam pressure.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Hyoscine HYDRObromide is a tertiary amine that CROSSES the blood-brain barrier — sedating and deliriogenic. Hyoscine BUTYLbromide (Buscopan) and glycopyrronium are quaternary amines that do NOT cross the BBB.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **For secretions choose the agent that does NOT cross the BBB: hyoscine BUTYLbromide or glycopyrronium. 'Butyl/glyco = stays out of the brain.'**

**Most seductive wrong answer when asked which patients still benefit from a screening test or primary-prevention statin?**
- _Seductive wrong answer:_ Offer it because the patient meets the AGE eligibility for the program.
- _Why it tempts you:_ Guideline age cut-offs are crisp and memorisable, and 'eligible by age = offer it' feels like correct guideline-adherent practice.
- _Why it's dead wrong:_ Eligibility by age is necessary but not sufficient. If life expectancy (shortened by frailty/advanced illness) is less than the time-to-benefit, the screen/prevention causes net harm (workup, anxiety, complications) with no chance of payoff.
- _The discriminator that breaks the trap:_ **Apply the horizon rule: screen/prevent only if life expectancy exceeds time-to-benefit. Frailty or a terminal trajectory cancels the intervention regardless of age.**

### High-end review pearls

- Aunt Minnie: drowsy, fluctuating, inattentive older patient who 'just seems quiet/tired' post-op or with a UTI = HYPOACTIVE DELIRIUM, the commonest and deadliest form — never write it off as dementia, depression or fatigue.
- If you see Parkinson's or Lewy body dementia + needs sedation for delirium, think QUETIAPINE (or clozapine) — haloperidol, risperidone and olanzapine are all D2 traps that worsen rigidity.
- If you see confusion + MYOCLONUS + low eGFR on morphine, think MORPHINE NEUROTOXICITY → rotate to FENTANYL. Do not increase the dose; do not just add a benzo. Hydromorphone is a trap (H3G still accumulates).
- Hyoscine BUTYLbromide / glycopyrronium STAY OUT of the brain (quaternary, no BBB); hyoscine HYDRObromide gets IN and sedates/delirifies. The one-letter difference is a classic Australian exam landmine.
- Breakthrough opioid = 1/6 of the TOTAL 24-hour dose, as immediate-release. The trap is dividing a single dose by 6 — always sum the day first.
- Never-forget one-liner: antipsychotics DO NOT treat delirium — they treat danger. First-line is always find-and-fix-the-cause (PINCH ME) plus the non-pharmacological bundle.
- DENOSUMAB is the deprescribing landmine: it must NEVER simply be stopped — rebound vertebral (often multiple) fractures. Transition to another anti-resorptive. Oral bisphosphonate review ~5 years, IV zoledronic ~3 years, then reassess.
- A valid Advance Care Directive made with capacity BINDS clinicians and OUTRANKS the substitute decision-maker. The SDM only acts where no applicable directive exists — and neither an SDM nor an ACD can ever request Voluntary Assisted Dying (self-request only).
- Australian screening numbers, not NHS: bowel iFOBT 2-yearly (request from 45, invited 50–74); cervical 5-yearly HPV 25–74 with UNIVERSAL self-collection since July 2022; BreastScreen 50–74; CVD risk from 45 (30 if Indigenous, 35 if diabetes) on the 2023 Aus calculator — NOT Framingham/QRISK.
- Brand-new and very examinable: National Lung Cancer Screening Program (from July 2025) — low-dose CT every 2 years, ages 50–70, ≥30 pack-years, current smoker or quit within 10 years.
- Indigenous front-loading rule: CVD risk from 30, AUSDRISK from 18, earlier/more frequent Kidney Health Check (eGFR + uACR + BP), zoster vaccine from 50, annual 715 Health Check at any age — burden and horizon both arrive early.
- Postural BP technique is examinable, not just the threshold: supine 5 min rest, then stand and measure at 1 and 3 minutes; positive = drop ≥20 mmHg systolic OR ≥10 mmHg diastolic.
- Cultural safety is defined by the RECIPIENT, not the provider — ask, don't assume; it means care free of racism with the patient's culture respected. Always offer the 715 health check.
- If you see 'confused elderly' in a vignette, the hidden question is usually SEPSIS (UTI/pneumonia), an ELECTROLYTE (hyponatraemia), AKI, or a NEW DRUG (anticholinergic) — delirium stems are frequently infection/electrolyte questions in disguise.
- Continence and incontinence: screen reversible causes first with DIPPERS (Delirium, Infection, atrophic vaginitis, Pharmaceuticals, Psychological, Excess output, Restricted mobility, Stool impaction) before reaching for pads or a catheter — the catheter is the trap.
- Metoclopramide is a PROKINETIC — never the answer in complete/malignant bowel obstruction (risk of perforation/colic); use haloperidol ± hyoscine ± octreotide instead. Match the antiemetic to the receptor, not 'ondansetron for everyone'.
- Strength + balance (exercise) training is the single best-evidenced falls-prevention intervention and is under-weighted by students who default to medication review alone — the best answer is often BOTH, but don't forget exercise.

> **Independent item audit — verdict: minor-fixes**
> - **[low]** MCQ1 (post-op hypoactive delirium; 'Which feature is most central to making the diagnosis?'): The CAM algorithm requires BOTH acute onset/fluctuation (option E) AND inattention (option B) as mandatory features. The stem asks which is 'most central to making the diagnosis' — strictly, you cannot diagnose delirium on inattention alone; you also need the acute/fluctuating change. A strong candidate could argue E is equally diagnosis-defining, creating a defensible second-best that edges toward two-correct-answers territory. → Tighten the stem to remove ambiguity, e.g. 'Which single feature best DISCRIMINATES delirium from its mimics (dementia, depression)?' — inattention is then unambiguously the single best answer because the acute change is non-specific. Alternatively keep the stem but ensure the explanation explicitly states why inattention outranks the (also-required) acute change: inattention is the specific discriminating sign, whereas acute fluctuation is sensitive but non-specific.
> - **[low]** MCQ3 (breakthrough morphine dose; answer 20 mg): The 'breakthrough = 1/6 of 24h total' rule is the standard teaching, but several Australian/UK sources quote a 1/6 to 1/12 range. A pedant could argue 10 mg (1/12, option A) is also 'appropriate.' The item is keyed to the 1/6 convention, which is fine, but the stem says 'the appropriate breakthrough dose' (definite article) rather than acknowledging the conventional 1/6 rule. → No change to the key (20 mg via 1/6 is the correct single-best per standard Australian palliative teaching). Optionally phrase as 'Using the standard one-sixth rule, what is the breakthrough dose?' to pre-empt the 1/12 objection. Distractor A (10 mg) is correctly framed in the explanation as the 1/12 / single-dose-halving error.
> - **[low]** MCQ4 (morphine neurotoxicity in renal failure; answer C rotate to fentanyl): Clinically sound and well-keyed. Minor note: real-world best practice would also reduce dose for incomplete cross-tolerance and address hydration when rotating, but as a single-best 'next step' among the options, rotation to a metabolite-free opioid is unambiguously correct. Hydromorphone distractor (D) is genuinely seductive and correctly explained via H3G accumulation — verified accurate. → No fix required. The item is solid. The H3G/renal claim is confirmed against the literature (H3G accumulates ~4x in renal insufficiency; only fentanyl and methadone lack renally-cleared active metabolites).
> - **[low]** MCQ2 (Parkinson's + delirium; answer C quetiapine): Factually correct and verified (olanzapine and risperidone both carry significant D2 antagonism and are contraindicated/avoided in PD/LBD; quetiapine or clozapine preferred). The only subtle point: the stem pre-empts the 'continue non-pharmacological' reflex well. No accidental second-correct answer. → No fix required. Strong item.
> - **[low]** MCQ6 (Indigenous CVD risk; answer B assess from age 30): Verified correct: 2023 Australian guideline recommends absolute CVD risk assessment for Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people from age 30 using cvdcheck.org.au, NOT Framingham. The parenthetical note that 18–29 First Nations people get individual risk-factor review rather than the calculator is accurate. No issue. → No fix required.
> - **[low]** Schema 'opioid prescribing and conversion' — conversion ratios: The schema lists oral morphine to oral oxycodone as '÷~1.5'. This is the most commonly cited ratio but Australian sources (eviQ, ANZCA) vary between 1.5 and 2. The oral-to-SC morphine ÷2 and oral-to-IV ÷2–3 are correct. Not an MCQ answer, so low stakes, but worth a caveat since conversion ratios are a known exam minefield. → Add a one-line caveat that conversion ratios vary by source/jurisdiction and that the safest practice is to convert, then reduce 25–33% for incomplete cross-tolerance (already stated) — so candidates don't treat ÷1.5 as gospel.
> - **[low]** MCQ5 (terminal nausea + secretions; answer C haloperidol + hyoscine butylbromide): Verified correct on both limbs: opioid/metabolic nausea is CTZ D2-mediated (haloperidol is the matched agent), and hyoscine BUTYLbromide/glycopyrronium are quaternary amines that do not cross the BBB whereas HYDRObromide is a tertiary amine that does and is sedating/deliriogenic. The two-part stem is answered cleanly by only one option. No accidental second-correct. → No fix required. Excellent discriminating item.
> 
> _Quality notes:_ Distractor quality is genuinely high across the board — these are deadliest-trap distractors, not strawmen. Standouts: hydromorphone as the 'renal-safe' opioid (MCQ4/drill 1), hyoscine HYDRObromide vs BUTYLbromide one-letter landmine (MCQ5/drill 6), enduring-guardian-overrides-ACD (MCQ8/drill 5), and 1/12-vs-1/6 breakthrough error (MCQ3/drill 4). All are the errors strong candidates actually make.; All eight MCQs are single-best-answer clean with the sole minor exception of MCQ1, where the acute-fluctuating-change distractor (E) is itself a mandatory CAM feature and edges close to defensibility — recommend tightening the stem to 'best discriminates' framing.; Factual accuracy verified against 2026 Australian guidance for every load-bearing claim: olanzapine/risperidone D2 risk in PD (confirmed), Indigenous CVD risk from age 30 on 2023 calculator (confirmed), National Lung Cancer Screening Program July 2025 / 50–70 / 30 pack-years / quit within 10 yrs (confirmed exactly), universal cervical self-collection since July 2022 for all 25–74 (confirmed), H3G renal accumulation and fentanyl/methadone as metabolite-free (confirmed), hyoscine hydrobromide BBB crossing (confirmed), denosumab rebound fractures requiring antiresorptive transition (confirmed).; No item is too easy for a final-year/early-postgrad Australian audience; each hinges on a discriminating point rather than recall. The ACD-vs-SDM item (MCQ8) and the Parkinson's antipsychotic item (MCQ2) are particularly well-calibrated.; Stems are clinically realistic with appropriate Australian context (Brisbane, Melbourne, NT community health, regional NSW palliative service). Vignettes pre-empt the obvious reflex answers (e.g., MCQ2 explicitly exhausts non-pharmacological measures and excludes withdrawal; MCQ8 establishes the ACD was made with capacity), which is good MCQ hygiene that closes off 'it depends' escape hatches.; The schemaRecall and distractorDrills are internally consistent with the MCQ keys — no contradictions found between the teaching layer and the applied layer. The denosumab 'never simply stop' landmine in the deprescribing schema and highEndExtras is accurate and high-yield, though notably it is taught but not tested by any MCQ (a potential gap if full coverage is desired, but not an error).

---

# Cross-topic integrators — where silo thinkers die

**1. A 58-year-old man on amiodarone for AF for 18 months presents with weight loss, tremor, anxiety and worsening palpitations. TSH is suppressed, free T4 and T3 elevated. Thyroid uptake scan shows low uptake; colour-flow Doppler shows absent vascularity.**
- _Domains linked:_ Cardiology × Endocrinology & Metabolic
- _The pivot:_ **Low/absent thyroid vascularity on Doppler = Type 2 amiodarone-induced thyrotoxicosis (destructive thyroiditis) → treat with glucocorticoids, NOT thionamides.**
- _The cross-topic trap:_ Silo thinkers reflexively start carbimazole for any thyrotoxicosis and stop amiodarone abruptly. Type 2 AIT is a destructive thyroiditis responding to steroids; Type 1 (increased vascularity, underlying nodular goitre) responds to thionamides. Distinguishing them by vascularity is the whole question, and stopping amiodarone can worsen the underlying arrhythmia.

**2. A 67-year-old woman with newly diagnosed type 2 diabetes is started on an SGLT2 inhibitor. Two weeks later she presents unwell with vomiting, abdominal pain, Kussmaul breathing and ketones 4.2. Her capillary glucose is 9.8 mmol/L. Venous pH 7.18, bicarbonate 12.**
- _Domains linked:_ Endocrinology & Metabolic × Nephrology / Renal & Acid-Base
- _The pivot:_ **Near-normal glucose with a high-anion-gap ketoacidosis on an SGLT2 inhibitor = euglycaemic DKA.**
- _The cross-topic trap:_ Silo thinkers exclude DKA because glucose is not high, and the acid-base story gets mislabelled. The pivot is recognising the high-anion-gap metabolic acidosis with ketosis despite euglycaemia, driven by the drug. Management still requires insulin plus dextrose, and the agent must be withheld perioperatively/when fasting.

**3. A 34-year-old woman presents with pleuritic chest pain, dyspnoea and a malar rash. She is 8 weeks postpartum. CT confirms bilateral PE. She has a history of two prior miscarriages. APTT is prolonged, platelets mildly low. Anti-cardiolipin and lupus anticoagulant are positive.**
- _Domains linked:_ Rheumatology & Immunology × Haematology × Respiratory
- _The pivot:_ **Prolonged APTT that does NOT correct on mixing study + positive lupus anticoagulant = antiphospholipid syndrome (paradoxically prothrombotic).**
- _The cross-topic trap:_ Silo thinkers see a prolonged APTT and worry about bleeding, or stop anticoagulation. In APS the lupus anticoagulant is an in-vitro artefact; the patient is hypercoagulable and needs lifelong warfarin (not a DOAC for triple-positive/arterial APS). The prolonged APTT is a clue to thrombophilia, not a contraindication to anticoagulation.

**4. A 72-year-old man with metastatic small cell lung cancer presents with confusion, nausea and a seizure. Sodium is 116 mmol/L. He is clinically euvolaemic, urine osmolality 580, urine sodium 60, serum osmolality low. He is not on diuretics.**
- _Domains linked:_ Endocrinology & Metabolic × Respiratory × Neurology
- _The pivot:_ **Euvolaemic hyponatraemia with concentrated urine and high urine sodium in SCLC = SIADH (ectopic ADH).**
- _The cross-topic trap:_ Silo thinkers give normal saline, which can worsen SIADH hyponatraemia (saline excreted, free water retained). The cross-link is the paraneoplastic source. Correct too fast and you cause osmotic demyelination; the pivot fact (urine osm > serum osm, euvolaemia) drives fluid restriction +/- tolvaptan, not saline boluses.

**5. A 45-year-old man with known alcohol-related cirrhosis presents with increasing ascites, fever and abdominal pain. Ascitic tap shows neutrophils 480/mm3, single organism on culture. He is also oliguric with creatinine rising from 90 to 210 over 5 days, urine sodium <10, no proteinuria, bland sediment.**
- _Domains linked:_ Gastroenterology & Hepatology × Infectious Diseases & Antimicrobial Stewardship × Nephrology / Renal & Acid-Base
- _The pivot:_ **Ascitic PMN >=250/mm3 = spontaneous bacterial peritonitis, which is precipitating hepatorenal syndrome; very low urine sodium with bland sediment distinguishes HRS from ATN.**
- _The cross-topic trap:_ Silo thinkers treat the AKI with more diuretics or nephrotoxic gentamicin. The renal failure is functional (HRS type 1) triggered by SBP; management is IV albumin + terlipressin and a non-nephrotoxic cephalosporin, plus albumin at SBP diagnosis to prevent HRS. Missing the SBP-HRS link kills the patient.

**6. A 60-year-old woman presents with fatigue, polyuria, constipation and confusion. Calcium is 3.1 mmol/L, phosphate low. She has had recurrent renal stones. PTH is inappropriately normal-to-high. She also has a gastrinoma and a pituitary microadenoma noted incidentally.**
- _Domains linked:_ Endocrinology & Metabolic × Nephrology / Renal & Acid-Base × Gastroenterology & Hepatology
- _The pivot:_ **Hypercalcaemia with non-suppressed PTH + gastrinoma + pituitary adenoma = primary hyperparathyroidism within MEN1.**
- _The cross-topic trap:_ Silo thinkers anchor on malignancy/hypercalcaemia (PTHrP) and miss that the PTH is the discriminator (PTH-mediated vs not). The three-gland clustering signals MEN1, mandating genetic counselling and family screening. Treating the calcium without recognising the syndrome misses a curable, heritable cause and the gastrinoma.

**7. A 28-year-old man returns from the Northern Territory with high fevers, jaundice, dark urine and confusion. Haemoglobin has dropped from 140 to 78 over 48 hours; LDH high, haptoglobin undetectable, blood film shows fragments and intraerythrocytic ring forms. Creatinine is 280.**
- _Domains linked:_ Infectious Diseases & Antimicrobial Stewardship × Haematology × Nephrology / Renal & Acid-Base
- _The pivot:_ **Intraerythrocytic ring forms on the film = falciparum malaria; the haemolysis + AKI + impaired consciousness define severe malaria.**
- _The cross-topic trap:_ Silo thinkers work up the haemolytic anaemia (Coombs, MAHA) and AKI separately and delay the film. Severe falciparum needs IV artesunate immediately, not oral therapy. The blood film is both the haematology test and the ID diagnosis; parasitaemia, AKI and cerebral involvement upstage management.

**8. A 70-year-old man with rheumatoid arthritis on methotrexate and recently started trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole for a UTI presents with mucositis, pancytopenia and fever. WCC 1.1, platelets 22, neutrophils 0.3.**
- _Domains linked:_ Rheumatology & Immunology × Infectious Diseases & Antimicrobial Stewardship × Haematology
- _The pivot:_ **Trimethoprim is a second antifolate stacked on methotrexate → severe folate-deficient pancytopenia/marrow suppression.**
- _The cross-topic trap:_ Silo thinkers prescribe the antibiotic without checking the rheumatology drug chart, treating the two domains in isolation. The drug-drug interaction (two antifolates) is the killer. Management is folinic acid (leucovorin) rescue, stopping both, plus neutropenic sepsis cover — not just G-CSF. Always screen the immunosuppressant list before adding antimicrobials.

**9. A 55-year-old woman presents with palpitations, heat intolerance and weight loss. ECG shows AF at 140. She is also profoundly weak with bilateral leg paralysis that came on overnight. Potassium is 1.9 mmol/L. She is of East Asian background. TSH suppressed.**
- _Domains linked:_ Endocrinology & Metabolic × Cardiology × Neurology × Nephrology / Renal & Acid-Base
- _The pivot:_ **Thyrotoxicosis + acute hypokalaemic paralysis = thyrotoxic periodic paralysis (intracellular K shift, not total-body depletion).**
- _The cross-topic trap:_ Silo thinkers aggressively replace potassium IV and risk rebound hyperkalaemia when the thyroid state is treated, because the potassium is shifted, not lost. The pivot is recognising the thyroid-paralysis link; cautious K replacement plus non-selective beta-blockade (propranolol) treats both the arrhythmia and the paralysis, and definitive antithyroid therapy prevents recurrence.

**10. An 80-year-old man with Parkinson's disease and a recent admission is brought in febrile, rigid, confused, with a creatine kinase of 9,800 and AKI. His levodopa was withheld two days ago because he was nil-by-mouth for a procedure.**
- _Domains linked:_ Neurology × Toxicology, Envenomation & Emergencies × Nephrology / Renal & Acid-Base × Geriatrics
- _The pivot:_ **Abrupt withdrawal of dopaminergic therapy in Parkinson's = neuroleptic malignant syndrome-like (parkinsonism-hyperpyrexia) syndrome.**
- _The cross-topic trap:_ Silo thinkers chase sepsis or label it NMS from an added antipsychotic, missing that stopping the drug caused it. The fix is to restart dopaminergic therapy urgently (NG/transdermal rotigotine), plus rhabdomyolysis-driven AKI management. Never abruptly cease levodopa; the medication-reconciliation error is the whole point.

**11. A 62-year-old man with type 2 diabetes and CKD (eGFR 28) presents with severe lactic acidosis (lactate 9, pH 7.1) after a vomiting illness. He takes metformin, an ACE inhibitor and an NSAID for gout. He is hypotensive.**
- _Domains linked:_ Endocrinology & Metabolic × Nephrology / Renal & Acid-Base × Toxicology, Envenomation & Emergencies
- _The pivot:_ **Metformin accumulation in worsening renal failure (the 'triple whammy' of ACEi + NSAID + intercurrent illness precipitating AKI) = metformin-associated lactic acidosis.**
- _The cross-topic trap:_ Silo thinkers attribute the lactic acidosis to sepsis alone and continue the offending drugs. The renal-tox-endo link is that AKI causes metformin accumulation; severe MALA may need haemodialysis (which also corrects acidosis and clears metformin). Withhold metformin during any acute illness and avoid the nephrotoxic 'triple whammy'.

**12. A 38-year-old woman with SLE on long-term prednisolone presents with worsening dyspnoea, dry cough and hypoxia. CXR shows bilateral infiltrates. She was started on azathioprine 6 weeks ago. LDH is high; she desaturates on exertion. Beta-D-glucan is positive.**
- _Domains linked:_ Rheumatology & Immunology × Infectious Diseases & Antimicrobial Stewardship × Respiratory
- _The pivot:_ **Immunosuppression (steroid + azathioprine) with hypoxia, raised LDH and positive beta-D-glucan = Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia.**
- _The cross-topic trap:_ Silo thinkers attribute the infiltrates to a lupus flare (pneumonitis) and escalate immunosuppression, which is catastrophic in PJP. The pivot is recognising the opportunistic infection in an immunosuppressed host; treat with high-dose co-trimoxazole + steroids, and remember PJP prophylaxis should have been prescribed. Flare vs infection is the classic cross-topic fork.

**13. A 75-year-old woman presents after a fall with confusion. She is on warfarin for AF, started a course of clarithromycin for a chest infection 5 days ago. INR is 8.5. CT head shows a small subdural.**
- _Domains linked:_ Cardiology × Infectious Diseases & Antimicrobial Stewardship × Haematology × Geriatrics
- _The pivot:_ **Clarithromycin is a CYP3A4/P-glycoprotein inhibitor that potentiates warfarin → supratherapeutic INR and bleeding.**
- _The cross-topic trap:_ Silo thinkers prescribe the macrolide for the chest infection without reviewing anticoagulation, and the cross-domain drug interaction causes the bleed. Management is prothrombinex + vitamin K (not just stopping warfarin) for major bleeding, and choosing a non-interacting antibiotic. The interaction, not the fall alone, is the examiner's target.

**14. A 50-year-old man with haemochromatosis presents with new diabetes, fatigue, arthralgia of the 2nd/3rd MCP joints, and worsening exertional dyspnoea with peripheral oedema. Ferritin and transferrin saturation are very high. Echo shows a restrictive/dilated picture.**
- _Domains linked:_ Gastroenterology & Hepatology × Endocrinology & Metabolic × Cardiology × Rheumatology & Immunology
- _The pivot:_ **Transferrin saturation >45% with high ferritin and HFE mutation = iron overload causing multi-organ deposition (pancreas, heart, joints, liver).**
- _The cross-topic trap:_ Silo thinkers treat the diabetes, cardiomyopathy and arthropathy as separate problems and miss the unifying iron-overload diagnosis. The MCP arthropathy + bronze diabetes + cardiomyopathy points to haemochromatosis; venesection (and screening relatives) modifies all organ outcomes. One pivot fact (transferrin saturation) ties four domains together.

**15. A 65-year-old man on a thiazide for hypertension presents with gout flare. He is started on allopurinol but a renal-impaired dose is used; weeks later he develops fever, rash, eosinophilia, deranged LFTs and AKI. He is of Han Chinese descent.**
- _Domains linked:_ Rheumatology & Immunology × Toxicology, Envenomation & Emergencies × Nephrology / Renal & Acid-Base
- _The pivot:_ **Allopurinol hypersensitivity syndrome (DRESS), strongly associated with HLA-B*58:01 (higher prevalence in Han Chinese, Thai, Korean populations).**
- _The cross-topic trap:_ Silo thinkers see rash + AKI + eosinophilia and chase infection or vasculitis. The drug-reaction link (DRESS from allopurinol, potentiated by thiazide and renal impairment) is the pivot; stop the drug, give systemic steroids, and recognise HLA-B*58:01 screening is recommended before starting allopurinol in at-risk ancestries. Never start allopurinol during an acute flare without cover.

**16. A 70-year-old man with COPD on long-term oral steroids presents drowsy. ABG: pH 7.31, pCO2 high, bicarbonate high. Glucose 32, no ketones, serum osmolality 340, sodium 150. He has had polyuria and poor intake for a week.**
- _Domains linked:_ Respiratory × Endocrinology & Metabolic × Nephrology / Renal & Acid-Base
- _The pivot:_ **Marked hyperglycaemia, high osmolality, minimal ketosis = hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state (steroid-induced), superimposed on chronic respiratory acidosis with metabolic compensation.**
- _The cross-topic trap:_ Silo thinkers read the ABG as a single primary disorder and miss the mixed picture: a compensated chronic respiratory acidosis PLUS the metabolic derangement of HHS. The endocrine-respiratory overlap matters because aggressive fluid and insulin are needed for HHS, while the high bicarbonate is appropriate compensation, not a primary alkalosis to correct. Steroids precipitated the HHS.

---

# Adversarial review of the gamble itself

**Verdict:** "Conditionally sound as a PASS strategy, dangerous as written. The 20/80 logic is correct for maximising EXPECTED marks across a broad curriculum in <2 weeks — breadth-first high-yield is genuinely the right macro-bet, and trying to cover everything to equal depth would be worse. BUT the gamble is dangerous in one specific, fixable way: applying 80/20 to the must-not-miss tail. Finals are not graded on expected value alone; OSCE/viva stations have high variance and Australian examiners deliberately seed envenomation, tropical ID, Indigenous health, and emergency 'kill questions' that the low-weight domains (Tox=6, Heme=5, Rheum=4) and the rare-but-lethal items inside big domains are exactly priced to drop. Pure high-yield optimises the average and leaves you exposed on the tail that actually fails people. The fix costs little: ring-fence a ~1.5-day must-not-miss + Australia-specific overlay that sits OUTSIDE the weighting, apply 80/20 only to DEPTH on common/chronic disease, protect acid-base/electrolytes/sepsis as cross-cutting force-multipliers, and reserve the final 3 days for active recall over the full weighted set rather than new content. With those guardrails the gamble is reasonable. Without them it is an avoidable single-point-of-failure bet, and the honest downside is a fail driven not by the 80% you knew but by the 20% you deliberately discarded."

### Where the 80/20 rigidity is dangerous

- **[high risk]** Toxicology/Envenomation/Emergencies (w=6) treated as 'low-yield' and cut. In Australia this is a uniquely high-stakes, exam-favoured AND clinically must-not-miss block: snake bite (brown/tiger), spider, paracetamol overdose nomogram + NAC, tricyclic/serotonin syndrome, anticholinergic, organophosphate, salicylate, anaphylaxis, sepsis, DKA, status epilepticus. The weight (6) understates exam frequency because Aussie finals over-test envenomation and OD relative to global curricula. An 80/20 cut here can directly fail a station via a single 'kill question'.
  - _Mitigation:_ NEVER cut toxicology/emergencies. Carve out a fixed 'must-not-miss' sheet that lives OUTSIDE the 20/80 weighting: anaphylaxis, sepsis/qSOFA + empirical abx, DKA/HHS, status epilepticus, paracetamol/NAC, snake bite + antivenom approach, hyperkalaemia, massive PE, tension pneumo, GI bleed resus. ~1 day, ring-fenced.
- **[high risk]** Pure high-yield favours classic/common presentations and silently drops the long tail of 'common-enough emergencies' inside big domains. Within Cardiology (w=15) a 20/80 pass nails ACS/HF/AF but may skip aortic dissection, tamponade, complete heart block, long-QT/torsades. Within Respiratory: tension pneumothorax, massive haemoptysis, type-2 respiratory failure. These are low-prevalence but binary-fail items.
  - _Mitigation:_ Apply 80/20 to KNOWLEDGE DEPTH, not to the must-not-miss list within each domain. For every top-6 domain, append the 3-4 'will-kill-the-patient' diagnoses even if statistically rare. Treat as flashcard tier, not deep study.
- **[high risk]** Tail domains collapsed together: Haematology (5), Rheumatology/Immunology (4), Geriatrics/Palliative/Preventive (5) = 14% combined but easy to round to zero under a falling-behind gamble. Each hides a killer: heme = febrile neutropenia, TTP, DIC, APTT/INR reversal, massive transfusion; rheum = GCA (vision loss, steroids NOW), septic arthritis, SLE/vasculitis crisis; geriatrics = delirium screen, falls, polypharmacy, opioid titration/palliative emergencies (cord compression, hypercalcaemia of malignancy). Aussie finals love GCA, septic joint, febrile neutropenia, malignant spinal cord compression.
  - _Mitigation:_ Do NOT zero these. One-page each of the named killers. Febrile neutropenia, GCA, septic arthritis, malignant cord compression, hypercalcaemia, TTP are high-frequency exam items disguised as 'low-weight' domains.
- **[medium risk]** Nephrology/Acid-Base (w=9) is mis-classed as low-yield reasoning-heavy and deprioritised. Acid-base and electrolytes (hyperK, hypoNa, AKI) are cross-cutting: they appear inside cardiology, tox, endo, and resus stations. Cutting them degrades performance ACROSS domains, not just in the 9% renal block — a hidden correlated failure the weights don't capture.
  - _Mitigation:_ Keep a compact acid-base/electrolyte/AKI core. It is a force-multiplier touching ~40% of acute scenarios. Highest leverage per hour on the whole list.
- **[medium risk]** Australian-specific epidemiology blind spot under generic high-yield. Finals examiners reward local context: melioidosis & tropical infections (northern QLD/NT), Indigenous health (rheumatic heart disease, CKD, diabetes, bronchiectasis), Q fever, leptospirosis, Ross River, hydatid, strongyloides hyperinfection, antimicrobial stewardship per Therapeutic Guidelines / eTG, and envenomation. Generic UK/US-style high-yield revision misses these and they are deliberately tested.
  - _Mitigation:_ Add a half-day 'Australia-specific' overlay: RHD + Indigenous chronic disease, tropical/zoonotic IDs, eTG empirical antibiotic choices, envenomation. Cheap, high marginal return because it is predictably examined and rarely revised.
- **[high risk]** All-or-nothing single-pass means no spaced repetition and no buffer for illness/burnout. A 20/80 sprint with zero consolidation loop tends to produce recognition-level (not recall-level) memory that collapses under SAQ/viva pressure and time decay over a <2-week window. The gamble assumes linear retention; retention is exponential-decay without review.
  - _Mitigation:_ Reserve last 2-3 days for active recall/past-paper drilling over the whole weighted set rather than new material. Front-load breadth in week 1, switch to retrieval+gaps in the final 3 days. Protect sleep — sleep deprivation in a 2-week sprint nets negative.

### Blind spots of pure high-yield

- The weights encode EXAM PREVALENCE, not CONSEQUENCE-OF-MISSING. 80/20 on prevalence systematically under-protects rare-but-binary-fail content (must-not-miss emergencies). The single most dangerous structural flaw of the gamble.
- Correlated failure across domains: acid-base, electrolytes, sepsis, and resuscitation are cross-cutting primitives. Cutting them as 'low-weight' silently degrades many high-weight domains — the weights treat domains as independent when they are not.
- Assessment FORMAT is ignored. OSCE/viva stations sample a small number of scenarios with high variance; one unlucky must-not-miss station can fail the whole exam regardless of how well the 80% common material was learned. High-yield optimises the mean; finals can be killed by the tail.
- Australian-specific killers (envenomation, melioidosis/tropical, Indigenous RHD/CKD, eTG stewardship) are predictably examined yet are exactly what generic high-yield revision drops — a known, avoidable blind spot.
- Retention dynamics: a single-pass 20/80 with no spaced repetition yields fragile recognition memory; under time pressure and decay over 2 weeks, recall fails even for 'covered' material. The plan budgets coverage but not consolidation.
- Human-factor downside: an aggressive sprint risks burnout/sleep debt that produces negative marginal returns; the all-or-nothing framing has no slack for a sick day.

### Contingency triage — if you fall behind

"CUT NEXT (in order), if falling behind — cut DEPTH first, then breadth on low-consequence common chronic disease: (1) Deep mechanism/physiology and edge-case zebra diagnoses everywhere — learn recognition + first action only. (2) Fine pharmacology detail (exact dosing, second-/third-line agents) — keep first-line + the antidote/emergency drugs. (3) Chronic outpatient optimisation nuance in lower-weight domains (e.g. detailed DMARD selection in rheum, fine titration in stable HF) — keep the diagnosis + initial management. (4) Rare chronic syndromes and long differential lists in the tail domains — keep only the named killer per domain. The principle: sacrifice completeness of MANAGEMENT and DEPTH on stable/chronic disease before you sacrifice any acute recognition. NEVER CUTTABLE (the ring-fenced floor, study these even if everything else falls apart): the A-to-E resuscitation approach; anaphylaxis; sepsis recognition + empirical antibiotics (eTG); DKA/HHS; hyperkalaemia management; status epilepticus; acute coronary syndrome; massive PE; tension pneumothorax; major GI bleed; paracetamol overdose + NAC; Australian snake-bite envenomation; febrile neutropenia; giant cell arteritis; septic arthritis; malignant spinal cord compression; hypercalcaemia of malignancy; stroke/thrombolysis time-critical pathway; acute asthma/COPD exacerbation; meningitis/encephalitis empirical treatment. These are format-independent must-not-miss items where a single question can fail the exam or kill a patient — they sit OUTSIDE the 20/80 budget."

### Independent review of the cross-cutting material

All three artifacts are strong and clinically accurate on the claims I verified (amiodarone-induced thyrotoxicosis type 1/2 Doppler vascularity split and steroid-vs-thionamide pivot confirmed against Bogazzi/Bartalena; euglycaemic DKA on SGLT2i at glucose 9.8 confirmed). The integrators are genuinely high-yield, the pivots are correct, and the strategy review is the most intellectually honest of the three — it correctly identifies the core structural flaw (weights encode prevalence not consequence-of-missing) rather than just listing topics. This is publishable-quality material. However, the critique brief asked the right adversarial questions and there ARE real weaknesses: (1) the MCQ technique is ~80% genuinely discriminating but contains 2-3 rules that are generic exam-prep filler dressed up, and one piece of actively risky advice; (2) two integrator pivots overstate certainty and one is subtly oversimplified in a way that could mislead; (3) the strategy review is honest about risk but under-states two specific downsides and contains an internal time-budget inconsistency. Verdict: ship with the concrete fixes below; none are fatal, but the risky-advice items (especially the warfarin/DOAC and the saline-in-SIADH framing) should be corrected before students rely on them.

- **TECHNIQUE — rule 'On second-guessing, change an answer only with a concrete reason' + 'First instinct is usually right':** This is the single weakest, most generic rule and it is also mildly evidence-contrary. The 'first instinct is usually right' folklore is not supported by the answer-changing literature: meta-analyses of medical/standardised exams consistently show that when students DO change answers, wrong-to-right changes outnumber right-to-wrong roughly 2:1. The rule as written discourages a behaviour that on net gains marks, and it is the kind of confidence-soothing filler that appears in every generic test-prep guide without discriminating value. → Reframe to the evidence: 'Reviewing flagged items is net-positive; the data show changes are more often wrong-to-right than right-to-wrong. Do NOT cling to first instinct as a rule. The discipline is to change only when you can NAME the missed clue (lab, negation, interaction) — but actively hunt for one on review rather than defaulting to no-change.' This keeps the genuine signal (need a concrete reason) while killing the unsupported 'first instinct wins' framing.
- **TECHNIQUE — rules 1 ('read the stem backwards'), 2 ('single discriminating detail'), 3 ('decode the phrasing'), 5 (negative stems), 6 (lab anchoring), 7 (epidemiological flags), 8 (textbook-as-trap), 9 (Australian localisation):** None — flagging these as the load-bearing, genuinely discriminating core. They are NOT filler: each names a specific, testable behaviour with a concrete tell. Rules 7 and 9 in particular are correctly Australia-specific (RHD/melioidosis/eTG) and are the highest-value rules in the set. The critique brief asked 'generic filler or discriminating' — the honest answer is most are discriminating. → No change to content. Only structural fix: rules 1 and 11 ('eliminate to a 2-option duel') overlap heavily with rule 10's time-budget and the distractorArchetypes section; consider merging the elimination mechanics so the rule list reads as ~9 distinct techniques rather than 13 with redundancy, which makes the genuine discriminators stand out instead of being diluted by near-duplicates.
- **INTEGRATOR — APS case: 'needs lifelong warfarin (not a DOAC for triple-positive/arterial APS). The prolonged APTT is a clue':** Two issues. (1) The blanket 'not a DOAC' is stated too absolutely for a teaching pivot — the evidence (TRAPS trial) specifically contraindicates rivaroxaban in TRIPLE-positive APS; for single/double-positive venous APS the picture is less categorical, though warfarin remains preferred. As written a student will mark 'DOAC always wrong in any APS' which is an over-generalisation. (2) More importantly: a lupus anticoagulant can spuriously elevate the BASELINE INR, making warfarin monitoring unreliable — this is a genuine exam-favoured nuance the integrator omits while spending words on the APTT. → Tighten to: 'Warfarin is standard; avoid DOACs in triple-positive and arterial APS (TRAPS trial — excess arterial events with rivaroxaban). Note LA can spuriously raise baseline INR, so target-INR monitoring may need chromogenic factor X.' Keep the prothrombotic-despite-prolonged-APTT pivot, which is correct and the high-yield point.
- **INTEGRATOR — SIADH case: 'give normal saline, which can worsen SIADH':** Oversimplified to the point of being a half-truth that examiners exploit in BOTH directions. Isotonic saline worsening hyponatraemia is only true when urine osmolality EXCEEDS the osmolality of the infused fluid (the 'electrolyte-free water' / desalination phenomenon, urine osm here 580 > 308). The integrator states the rule without the condition, so a student can't reproduce the reasoning and will mis-apply it. Also, this patient has a SEIZURE and Na 116 — the correct immediate step is hypertonic (3%) saline, NOT fluid restriction/tolvaptan, which the pivot buries. Severe symptomatic hyponatraemia is the one time you DO give saline (hypertonic). → Split the teaching: 'Symptomatic (seizing, Na 116) = hypertonic 3% saline bolus NOW for the emergency, with controlled correction (<8-10 mmol/L/24h to avoid ODS). Once stable, treat the cause: fluid restriction +/- tolvaptan. Isotonic saline worsens SIADH ONLY because urine osm (580) > infusate osm (~308), so free water is retained — state the condition or the rule is unusable.' This is the single most important integrator fix because as written it could drive a dangerous answer in an emergency stem.
- **INTEGRATOR — overall certainty calibration on Doppler/B-D-glucan/anti-cardiolipin 'positive' phrasings:** Several pivots present a single confirmatory finding as definitive when finals examiners deliberately blur them: beta-D-glucan is positive in PJP but ALSO candida/aspergillus/other fungi (not specific); anti-cardiolipin must be persistently positive on REPEAT testing 12 weeks apart for APS classification (single positive in an acute thrombotic/postpartum setting can be transient); AIT type 1/2 frequently coexist as 'mixed' forms in practice. The integrators teach clean prototypes, which is fine for exams, but the artifact's own TECHNIQUE rule 8 warns against exactly this — internal inconsistency. → Add a one-line 'real-world caveat' tag to the 3-4 pivots that rest on a single non-specific marker (B-D-glucan non-specific; APS antibodies need persistence at 12 weeks; mixed AIT exists). This makes the integrators robust to the 'plausible-but-rarer mimic' and 'too-definitive option' archetypes the technique sheet itself flags.
- **STRATEGY — internal time-budget inconsistency:** The mitigations don't sum coherently against a <2-week window. Ring-fenced must-not-miss sheet = ~1 day; Australia-specific overlay = half-day; final 'last 2-3 days' reserved for active recall over the FULL weighted set; plus 'front-load breadth in week 1'. That is ~1.5 days carved out + 3 days retrieval = ~4.5 of ~14 days committed before any new high-yield depth, yet the plan still assumes a 20/80 breadth pass across ~10 domains in the remainder. The arithmetic is tighter than the prose admits, and the verdict calls the gamble 'reasonable with guardrails' without showing the day-budget closes. → Add an explicit day-by-day skeleton (e.g. Days 1-7 breadth high-yield + ring-fenced sheets interleaved, Days 8-11 gaps + Australia overlay, Days 12-14 past-paper retrieval) so the guardrails are shown to FIT the window. Right now the mitigations are individually sound but not reconciled against the same 14-day budget they're competing for.
- **STRATEGY — two under-stated downsides:** The review is honest about the prevalence-vs-consequence flaw (its best insight) but under-states: (1) OPPORTUNITY COST of the ring-fence — it asserts the must-not-miss + Australia overlay 'costs little (~1.5 days)' without acknowledging that 1.5 of 14 days is ~11% of total budget pulled from the 80% common material that actually carries most marks; for a borderline candidate that trade can lower the EXPECTED pass probability even as it lowers tail variance. The review presents the ring-fence as nearly free, which under-states its cost. (2) It never quantifies or bounds how many must-not-miss items there are — the NEVER-CUTTABLE list runs to ~20 items, which is no longer a 'sheet' but a substantial syllabus; calling it format-independent floor while it quietly grows to 20 conditions undermines the 'cheap' framing. → State the trade honestly: 'The ring-fence trades expected marks for reduced tail variance — rational for a pass/fail finals where one station can fail you, but it is NOT free; it is ~11% of budget. Cap the must-not-miss list at recognition+first-action depth ONLY (one line each), or the ~20-item floor silently becomes a second curriculum.' Also add the missing human-factor honesty: the plan says 'protect sleep' but offers no concrete slack mechanism (e.g. a designated drop-day) if illness hits — name one.
- **STRATEGY — one genuine omission in the must-not-miss floor:** The ring-fenced NEVER-CUTTABLE list is good but has gaps for Australian finals specifically: it omits (a) thyroid storm and myxoedema coma, (b) addisonian/adrenal crisis, (c) acute liver failure / variceal bleed as distinct from 'major GI bleed', and (d) serotonin syndrome / NMS differentiation — the latter two are directly tested by the artifact's OWN integrators (Parkinson's-hyperpyrexia case) yet absent from the floor. Internal inconsistency: an integrator teaches a killer the strategy doesn't ring-fence. → Add adrenal crisis, thyroid storm/myxoedema coma, and the NMS/serotonin-syndrome/parkinsonism-hyperpyrexia cluster to the NEVER-CUTTABLE list so the strategy floor is consistent with the conditions the integrators already flag as cross-topic killers.

---

# Final completeness critic — read this before you walk in

**Verdict:** CONDITIONAL FAIL-RISK. The blueprint is internally coherent and the non-negotiables list is genuinely strong (it has already absorbed most of the "rare-but-binary-fail" emergencies — anaphylaxis, hyperkalaemia, status, ACS, massive PE, tension pneumo, variceal bleed, paracetamol, snake-bite, febrile neutropenia, GCA, septic arthritis, cord compression, hypercalcaemia, stroke, meningitis, ARF/RHD, melioidosis). The gamble is survivable. But it is NOT yet pass-safe, for three structural reasons the plan itself half-confesses and then does not fix: (1) the non-negotiables are a recognition+first-move list, not a managed-to-disposition list — finals OSCE/viva/long-case marks the SECOND and THIRD moves (titration, reassessment, escalation, complication, communication) that the manifesto never commits to building; (2) several predictably-examined Australian and cross-cutting items are named but unverified as actually drilled to dose/protocol depth; (3) the plan budgets coverage but explicitly not consolidation, so under 2-week decay the "covered" tail is exactly what fails in the high-variance station. The verdict is pass-likely on the written/MCQ paper, materially fail-exposed on the clinical/viva tail. Close the gaps below and it becomes pass-safe.

### Highest-risk gaps that could cost you a pass

- THE SECOND-MOVE GAP (most likely single cause of a fail). Every non-negotiable is coded as 'recognition + first action'. Finals viva/long-case fails candidates on the next steps: after IM adrenaline — what if no response (IV adrenaline infusion, fluids, refractory adjuncts); after benzo in status — the exact second-line (levetiracetam/valproate/phenytoin) and the call to ICU/anaesthetics; after first-dose insulin in DKA — the potassium-gated insulin rule, glucose-bridging dextrose, and what defines resolution. A list that stops at the first drug is MCQ-pass but viva-fail. This is the highest-probability killer.
- THE FORMAT/TAIL GAP the plan names but does not close. OSCE/viva samples ~6-10 stations with high variance; one communication/ethics or one must-not-miss procedural station (DKA management plan, deteriorating patient/MET call, breaking bad news, capacity) can fail the whole exam. The build has near-zero coverage of communication, capacity, ethics, ceiling-of-care, and the structured 'deteriorating patient' ISBAR/escalation station — precisely the high-variance tail the manifesto admits can kill it. Coverage of the mean does not insure the tail.
- eTG DOSE-PRECISION UNVERIFIED. Australian finals reward the candidate who can state the actual empirical regimen, dose, route and duration to eTG, because that is what distinguishes safe-to-graduate. The artifact asserts 'per eTG' repeatedly but there is no evidence the precise strings (meningitis ceftriaxone + dexamethasone + pre-hospital benzylpenicillin; febrile neutropenia pip-taz within 1h; melioidosis intensive ceftazidime/meropenem then eradication co-trimoxazole; snakebite — pressure-immobilisation + which antivenom by syndrome/region + lab triggers; sepsis empirical by source) are committed to memory. If these are topic-labels not drilled prescriptions, the stewardship/ID weighting (11%+11%) and the Australian-killer edge collapse.
- CONSOLIDATION FOR THE EMERGENCY TAIL. A single-pass 20/80 with no spaced repetition means the rare-but-binary emergencies (the very content the non-negotiables exist to protect) decay fastest because they are seen once and not reinforced. Over 2 weeks under stress, recognition memory for adrenal crisis, hyperkalaemia ECG sequence, snakebite pathway, and second-line status drugs degrades below recall threshold at the exact moment a station demands them. The plan budgets coverage but not the retrieval loop that keeps the tail accessible — its own listed blind spot, unmitigated, applied to the highest-consequence items.
- INDIGENOUS / RURAL-REMOTE HEALTH BREADTH. ARF/RHD is in, but the broader guaranteed Australia-specific cluster — CKD and ESKD in Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander populations, rheumatic heart disease secondary prophylaxis (benzathine penicillin regimen), trachoma/scabies/APSGN, and culturally-safe communication / interpreter / 'Close the Gap' framing — is under-built relative to how reliably it is examined. This is a predictable, avoidable Australian blind spot that generic high-yield drops, and it intersects the communication-station gap above, compounding the risk.

### What this doc does not fully cover

- MODALITY GAP — the clinical long case / viva is unaddressed. The whole artifact is recognition-and-first-drug shaped (MCQ-native). Australian finals are dominated by long case (full history, exam, problem list, management plan, social/discharge/safety-netting) and structured viva. No unit listed builds: structured problem-list synthesis, multi-morbidity prioritisation, a management plan spoken aloud, or 'what would you do next / and then / and if that fails' escalation laddering. This is the single largest modality hole.
- MODALITY GAP — communication/ethics/consent/capacity/breaking-bad-news/end-of-life-discussion stations. Geriatrics+Palliative is only 5% by prevalence but communication stations are near-guaranteed in Australian finals and are pass/fail gates. ACP, substitute decision-maker, MET-call/NFR/ceiling-of-care, voluntary assisted dying framing (state-dependent), and delirium-vs-dementia capacity assessment are not in the non-negotiables.
- UNVERIFIED CLAIM — 'per eTG' is asserted for sepsis, anaphylaxis, febrile neutropenia, meningitis, melioidosis but the actual drug+dose+route+duration strings are not shown to be memorised. eTG-citable empirical regimens (e.g. ceftriaxone dose for meningitis + dexamethasone timing + benzylpenicillin for suspected meningococcaemia pre-transfer; pip-taz vs cefepime for febrile neutropenia; ceftazidime/meropenem + the eradication phase for melioidosis; adrenaline 0.5mg IM 1:1000 anterolateral thigh repeat q5min) are exactly what gets marked. Naming the topic is not the same as drilling the prescription.
- DOMAIN UNDER-SERVED — Nephrology/acid-base at 9% plus the cross-cutting 'anion gap multiplier' claim, but AKI staging/workup, indications for urgent dialysis (AEIOU), contrast/nephrotoxin avoidance, and CKD-in-Indigenous-Australians (a named killer) are not in the non-negotiables despite being high-frequency exam fodder. Acid-base is claimed as a multiplier but only listed once — no evidence it is drilled as a repeatable algorithm across datasets.
- DOMAIN UNDER-SERVED — Endocrine beyond DKA/HHS/hypercalcaemia: thyroid storm and myxoedema coma, adrenal crisis / sick-day steroid rules, and severe hypoglycaemia management are absent from non-negotiables. Adrenal crisis is a classic binary-fail viva.
- MODALITY GAP — data interpretation as a live skill: ECG (beyond STEMI — complete heart block, hyperK morphology, long QT, WPW), CXR, ABG, and basic spirometry/iron-studies/CSF-pattern interpretation. The plan references findings but does not list a drilled image/tracing interpretation modality, which OSCE 'spot' and MCQ both sample heavily.
- UNVERIFIED CLAIM — spaced repetition / consolidation is named as a blind spot but no remediation modality is in the build. There is a manifesto+timetable but no described retrieval-practice loop, no interleaving schedule, no 48-hour-pre-exam high-yield emergency flashcard pass. Coverage without a forgetting-curve countermeasure is the plan's own admitted hole, left open.
- DOMAIN THINNESS — Haematology (5%) emergencies beyond febrile neutropenia: TTP/TMA (PLASMIC, plasma exchange not platelets), DIC, massive transfusion protocol + reversal of warfarin/DOAC (Prothrombinex, idarucizumab, andexanet), and tumour lysis. Anticoagulation reversal is a guaranteed, examinable, must-not-miss that is currently invisible.

---

_This core doc acknowledges full scope and forthrightly takes on the 80/20 gamble. It is a weapon, not a safety net. The audits above are your honesty layer — where an audit flagged a high-severity correction, trust the correction over the original._
